Chernoff Bounds and Reverse Hypercontractivity on HDX
Yotam Dikstein, Max Hopkins

Abstract
We prove optimal concentration of measure for lifted functions on high dimensional expanders (HDX). Let X X be a k k -dimensional HDX. We show for any i ⩽ k i\leqslant k and function f : X ⁡ ( i ) → [ 0 , 1 ] f:X{\left(i\right)}\to[0,1] : ℙ s ∈ X ⁡ ( k ) [ | 𝔼 t ⊆ s [ f ( t ) ] − μ | ⩾ ε ] ⩽ exp ( − ε 2 k i ) . \ProbOp_{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}\left[\left|\underset{{t\subseteq s}}{\mathbb{E}}[f(t)]-\mu\right|\geqslant\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right). Using this fact, we prove that high dimensional expanders are reverse hypercontractive , a powerful functional inequality from discrete analysis implying that for any sets A , B ⊂ X ⁡ ( k ) A,B\subset X{\left(k\right)} , the probability a ρ \rho -correlated pair passes between them is at least ℙ s , s ′ ∼ T ρ [ s ∈ A , s ′ ∈ B ] ⩾ ℙ [ A ] O ⁡ ( 1 ) ℙ [ B ] O ⁡ ( 1 ) . \ProbOp_{s,s^{\prime}\sim T_{\rho}}[s\in A,s^{\prime}\in B]\geqslant\ProbOp[A]^{O(1)}\ProbOp[B]^{O(1)}. Our results hold under weak spectral assumptions on X X . Namely we prove exponential concentration of measure for any complex below the ‘Trickling-Down Threshold’ (beyond which concentration may be arbitrarily poor), and optimal concentration for k \sqrt{k} -skeletons of such complexes. We also show optimal bounds for the top dimension of stronger HDX among other settings. We leverage our inequalities to prove several new agreement testing theorems on high dimensional expanders, including a new 99 % 99\% -regime test for subsets , and a variant of the ‘Z-test’ achieving inverse exponential soundness under the stronger assumption of ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -expansion. The latter gives rise to the first optimal testers beyond the complete complex and products, a stepping stone toward the use of HDX in strong soundness PCPs. We also give applications within expansion, analysis, combinatorics, and coding theory, including a proof that two-sided HDX have optimal geometric overlap (giving the first explicit bounded-degree construction), near-optimal double samplers, new super-exponential degree lower bounds for certain HDX, distance-amplified list-decodable and locally testable codes, a Frankl–Rödl Theorem, and more.



1
Introduction

Recent years have seen the emergence of
high dimensional expanders
(HDX) as a core tool in theoretical computer science, with breakthrough applications in approximate sampling
[
Ana+19
,
ALG21
]
, coding theory
[
Din+22
,
PK22
]
, and quantum complexity
[
ABN23
]
, and strong promise toward longstanding problems in hardness of approximation and probabilistically checkable proofs
[
DK17
,
DD19
,
KM20
,
GK22
,
HL22
,
BLM24
,
DDL24
]
. One central force behind the success of HDX in application is their
concentration of measure
. Consider (as a warmup) the following fundamental question: given a
k
-uniform hypergraph
X
and a function
f:X{\left(1\right)}\to[0,1]
on its vertices,
1
1

1

We denote by
X{\left(i\right)}
the subsets of size
i
in (the downward closure of)
X
.
how concentrated is
f
to its mean across a random hyperedge?

\ProbOp_{\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X(k)}\left[\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})-\mu\right|>\varepsilon\right]\overset{?}{\leqslant}\beta(\varepsilon,k)

(1.1)

When
X
is the complete hypergraph, this was classically resolved by Chernoff and Hoeffding
[
Che52
,
Hoe94
]
who showed
\beta(\varepsilon,k)\leqslant\exp(-\varepsilon^{2}k)
. Over the years, near-matching bounds have been shown for more general hypergraph families (see e.g.
[
Pau14
,
KS18
,
KKS21
]
), and even for certain bounded degree systems like walks on expanders
[
AKS87
,
Gil98
]
. These objects, called
sampler graphs
or
extractors
, are by now a core tool in the field with applications in de-randomization, complexity, and cryptography
[
Gol97
,
Wig09
]
.

High dimensional expanders are known to satisfy a ‘Chebyshev-type’ bound
\beta(\varepsilon,k)\leqslant\frac{1}{k\varepsilon^{2}}
for (
1.1
)
[
DK17
]
, but this is of course not what makes them powerful. Rather, many modern applications in complexity require concentration of
X
against
broader classes of functions
. Of particular interest is the extension of (
1.1
) to functions
f:X(i)\to[0,1]
sitting on
i
-sets
of
X
. Complexes satisfying such a bound are called
inclusion samplers
; they arise naturally in the study of agreement and PCPs (forming consistency checks), and play a critical role in low soundness constructions toward the Sliding Scale Conjecture
[
IKW12
,
DM11
,
Mos17
,
DL17
]
. Unfortunately, inclusion samplers are notoriously difficult to construct, and no bounded degree families were known for many years.

This changed with the advent of spectral HDX
[
Gar73
,
LSV05a
,
EK16
,
KM17
,
DK17
,
Opp18
]
. A key motivation behind the modern incarnation of these objects
[
KM17
,
DK17
,
Opp18
]
was the fact that their ‘inclusion graphs’ (the bipartite adjacency matrix between
k
-sets and
i
-sets) are spectral expanders, and therefore satisfy ‘Chebyshev-type’ concentration
\beta(\varepsilon,i,k)\leqslant\frac{i}{\varepsilon^{2}k}
. This observation played a central role in recent progress in agreement testing
[
DK17
,
Din+18
,
DD19
,
KM20
,
GK22
,
DD23
,
BM23
]
, but left an exponential gap from the Chernoff-type concentration needed for other applications.

In this work we take a major step toward closing this gap, resolving the problem completely in many regimes of interest. In particular, drawing on ideas from the concentration of measure
[
BLM13
]
, PCP
[
IKW12
]
, and HDX
[
DD19
,
AJT19
]
literature, we prove HDX are optimal samplers between every two levels.

Theorem 1.1

(HDX are Optimal Inclusion Samplers (Informal))
.

Let
X
be a nice enough
d
-dimensional HDX.
2
2

2

This includes, e.g.,
d
-skeletons of
\frac{1}{d^{2}}
-one-sided
d^{2}
-dimensional HDX or the top level of any
2^{-d}
-two-sided HDX. See
Section
2.1
and
Definition
4.26
for a formal description of ‘nice enough’ complexes.
Then for any
i\leqslant k\leqslant d
and any
f:X(i)\to[0,1]
of expectation
\mu
:

\ProbOp_{s\in X(k)}\left[\left\lvert\underset{t\subset s}{\mathbb{E}}[f(t)]-\mu\right\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right).

Moreover, this is essentially tight—no inclusion sampler achieves better than
\exp\left(-O\left(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right)
error.

Theorem
1.1
is powerful when
i=o(k)
, but our corresponding lower bounds show non-trivial inclusion sampling is essentially impossible in the critical regime
i=\Theta(k)
. In fact this exact issue stood as a barrier for many years on the complete complex, where Impagliazzo, Kabanets, and Wigderson
[
IKW12
]
proved
Theorem
1.1
and used it to construct sub-optimal testers and PCPs by taking
i=\Theta(\sqrt{k})
. In 2017, Dinur and Livni-Navon
[
DL17
]
resolved this problem by appealing to a somewhat surprising player: an underutilized tool from boolean function analysis called
reverse hypercontractivity
(RHC).

Reverse hypercontractivity is a classic functional inequality due to Borell
[
Bor82
]
that lower bounds the correlation between sets on the noisy hypercube. Roughly speaking, it implies that for any two subsets
A,B\subseteq\{0,1\}^{d}
, the probability a
\rho
-correlated edge crosses
A
and
B
is at least some power of their measure:

\ProbOp_{(s,s^{\prime})\sim T_{\rho}}[s\in A,s^{\prime}\in B]\geqslant\ProbOp[A]^{O_{\rho}(1)}\ProbOp[B]^{O_{\rho}(1)}.

Reverse hypercontractivity is an even rarer phenomenon than inclusion sampling—while there are some moderately de-randomized inclusion graphs (namely the Grassmann
[
IKW12
]
) satisfying Chebyshev-type variants of
Theorem
1.1
, none are known to admit reverse hypercontractivity. At outset this may even seem necessary. The inequality is closely tied to tensorization and (modified) Log-Sobolev Inequalities
[
MOS13
]
, which inherently fail on sparse hypergraphs.

In this work, we circumvent this issue by a new combinatorial argument showing any hypergraph satisfying
Theorem
1.1
‘locally’ is also reverse hypercontractive. This leads to the following corollary for HDX:

Theorem 1.2

(Reverse Hypercontractivity for HDX (Informal))
.

let
X
be a nice enough
d
-dimensional HDX. Then for any
\rho\in(0,1)
,
k\leqslant d
, and
A,B\subset X(k)
:

\ProbOp_{(s,s^{\prime})\sim T_{\rho}(X)}[s\in A,s^{\prime}\in B]\geqslant\ProbOp[A]^{O_{\rho}(1)}\ProbOp[B]^{O_{\rho}(1)}.

Here
T_{\rho}(X)
is the natural generalization of the noise operator to hypergraphs which, roughly speaking, generates
s^{\prime}
by re-sampling each vertex in
s
with probability
1-\rho
. We also prove a more standard analytical version of reverse hypercontractivity for all functions by reduction to the above, as well as analog statements for the heavily studied ‘down-up’ walks which play a critical role in almost all HDX applications.

Applications

The remainder of our work is devoted to proving a variety of applications of
Theorem
1.1
and
Theorem
1.2
including new families of agreement tests, bounded degree complexes with optimal geometric overlap, double samplers with near-optimal overhead, new degree lower bounds for HDX, large distance list-decodable and locally-testable codes, and several extensions of classic combinatorial and analytic results to HDX. Here we choose to highlight just two of these applications, agreement testing and geometric overlap. We refer the reader to
Section
2.5
and
Section
2.6
for a more in-depth discussion of our applications.

Agreement testing
is a general property testing paradigm that generalizes classical subroutines in PCP theory like the plane vs. plane test (e.g.
[
AS97
,
GS97
,
Din07
]
). Roughly speaking, an agreement test consists of a universe
U
and a family of overlapping subsets
S\subset\mathbb{P}(U)
(think of
U
as the vertices of
X
, and
S
as its hyperedges). Given a family of
local
assignments
\{f_{s}:s\to\{0,1\}\}_{s\in S}
, we’d like to test whether
f_{s}
actually agrees with a
global
function
g:U\to\{0,1\}
in the sense that
f_{s}=g|_{s}
for many
s
. These systems occur in PCP reductions to ensure the provers (who answer on subsets) can’t ‘cheat’ by using answers not corresponding to a real solution of the initial problem (corresponding to global functions on
U
).

Agreement testing has two major regimes, the ‘
99\%
-regime’ (where we’d like to infer global structure only if the test passes with high probability) and the ‘
1\%
-regime’ (where we’d like to infer global structure even when the test only passes with
non-trivial
probability). Leveraging reverse hypercontractivity, we give new tests in both regimes. In the
99\%
-regime, we give a new
2
-query test where the universe
U
corresponds to
i
-sets
of a high dimensional expander, extending a similar result of
[
DFH19
]
on the complete complex. Our test, along with
Theorem
1.1
, has since appeared as an important sub-routine in the construction of the first bounded-degree
1\%
agreement testers in
[
DD23a
,
DDL24
]
.

In the
1\%
-regime, we give a new
3
-query tester for a variant of
\ell_{\infty}
-expanders (a strengthened notion of HDX
[
KKS21
]
) with
optimal soundness
, meaning that if the test passes with probability asymptotically better than a random function (
2^{-\Theta(k)}
for a
k
-dimensional
\ell_{\infty}
-expander), we are able to infer global structure. In the context of PCPs, such a soundness guarantee is critical to ensure the alphabet size stays polynomial in the soundness. Our result gives rise to the first families of optimal testers beyond the complete complex and products
[
DL17
]
, including (dense) random complexes, skeletons of many well-studied spin systems, the full linear matroid, and more. We remark that while all such examples are dense, we prove an optimal ‘local’ agreement theorem only under the assumption of local spectral expansion. We lift this guarantee to the general result using
\ell_{\infty}
-expansion, but it is possible this requirement could be relaxed using recent techniques based on coboundary expansion
[
GK22
,
BM23a
,
DD23a
,
BLM24
,
DDL24
]
to give sparse optimal testers.

The
Geometric Overlap Property
is one of the earliest notions of high dimensional expansion
[
Gro10
,
Lub18
]
. A
d
-dimensional complex
X
has
c
-geometric overlap if for any embedding of
X
into
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there is a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that is covered by a
c
-fraction of
X
’s hyperedges. Geometric overlap was first proven for the complete complex in 2-dimensions by Boros and Füredi
[
BF84
]
, and later extended to all dimensions by Bárány
[
Bár82
]
. Gromov
[
Gro10
]
asked whether there are bounded degree complexes satisfying geometric overlap. This was resolved in
[
Fox+12
]
, who gave both an optimal random construction and several explicit constructions with bounded but sub-optimal overlap (including one from high dimensional expanders). Several later works
[
PRT16
,
Evr17
,
Opp18
]
continued to build the connection between high dimensional expanders and geometric overlap, but it remained open whether a construction achieving the best of both worlds (explicit and optimal) could be achieved. Leveraging a variant of
Theorem
1.1
, we resolve this problem, showing under mild assumptions that
any
sufficiently strong high dimensional expander has near-optimal geometric overlap.

The Trickling-Down Threshold

High dimensional expanders exhibit a
phase transition
at a certain expansion parameter called the ‘Trickling-Down (TD)-Threshold’
[
Opp18
]
. Any complex breaking this barrier immediately has ‘local-to-global’ structure, meaning properties such as global expansion and fast-mixing can be inferred just from
local
structure of the complex
[
Gar73
,
KM17
,
Opp18
,
AL20
]
. This barrier shows up as a major point in constructions of HDX as well. It is relatively easy to build complexes
at
the threshold by tensoring an expander with the complete complex
[
LMY20
,
Gol21
]
, but
breaking
the barrier requires complicated algebraic machinery and is considered the ‘gold standard’ for HDX—indeed only three such constructions are known
[
LSV05a
,
KO18
,
Dik22
]
.

Theorem
1.1
and
Theorem
1.2
hold for
\sqrt{d}
-dimensional skeletons of any complex breaking the TD-Threshold. In fact, we prove that the top level of such complexes satisfy exponential concentration for all Lipschitz functions, corresponding to
\beta(\varepsilon,i,k)\leqslant\exp(-\varepsilon\frac{\sqrt{k}}{i})
in the inclusion sampling setting. On the other hand, we give examples of complexes
at
the TD-threshold that exhibit arbitrarily poor concentration. We view our results in this sense as a significant strengthening of HDX’s ‘local-to-global’ phase transition, and a partial explanation to the great difficulty of constructing hypergraphs beyond the TD-Threshold. Whereas graph expansion and mixing are ‘common’ phenomenon,
Theorem
1.1
and
Theorem
1.2
imply as soon as one breaks the TD-Threshold
X
not only satisfies exponential concentration of measure, its skeletons satisfy optimal concentration and reverse hypercontractivity—rare properties not known for any other sparse systems.

2
Main Results and Proof Overview

2.1
Background

Before stating our results in somewhat more formality, we cover some basic background on high dimensional expanders, random walks, and sampling. We refer the reader to
Section
4
for formal details and discussion.

Simplicial Complexes

A
d
-
uniform simplicial complex

X
consists of a
d
-uniform hypergraph
X{\left(d\right)}
together with its downward closure

X=X{\left(1\right)}\cup\ldots\cup X{\left(d\right)},

where
X{\left(i\right)}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{i}
, called the ‘
i
-faces’, are all
i
-size subsets that sit in some hyperedge in
X{\left(d\right)}
. Given a face
t\in X{\left(i\right)}
, the
link
of
t
is the sub-complex induced by localizing to faces that include
t
, that is
X_{t}\coloneqq\{s:s\cup t\in X\}
. We say
X
is
connected
if the base graph
(X_{t}{\left(1\right)},X_{t}{\left(2\right)})
of every link is connected. A
d
-uniform complex is
partite
if its vertices can be partitioned into
d
‘parts’ such that each top-level face has one part from each component. Finally, the
inclusion graph

(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is the bipartite graph between
k
-sets and
i
-sets of
X
where edges are given by inclusion.

We emphasize that we have changed convention here from dimension, where
X(d)
refers to sets of size
d+1
, to uniformity where
X{\left(d\right)}
consists of sets of size
d
. We will use the latter notation throughout the rest of the paper to simplify both the statements and proofs of our results.

High dimensional expanders

A simplicial complex
X
is a
\lambda
-one-sided HDX
(resp. two-sided) if for every
s\in X
of co-dimension
3
3

3

Here by co-dimension
j
we mean sets of size
d-j
.
at least
2

(X_{t}{\left(1\right)},X_{t}{\left(2\right)})
is a
\lambda
-one-sided spectral expander (resp. two sided).

The weakest form of high dimensional expanders are
\lambda
-
Trickling-Down (TD) Complexes
. We call
X

\lambda
-TD if it is connected and every co-dimension
2
link is a
\frac{\lambda}{d-1}
-one-sided expander.

Our results hold for a broad class of HDX we call
Nice Complexes
. We defer the formal definition to
Section
4.8
, and here give two basic examples of nice complexes for which our results hold:

–

X
is the
\sqrt{d}
-skeleton of a
\lambda
-TD complex

–

X
is a
2^{-d}
-two-sided HDX

We note our results also hold on
2^{-d}
-one-sided
partite
HDX, albeit with polynomially worse
\varepsilon
-dependence. We discuss this further in the main body (see
Theorem
5.1
and
Section
7
).

High Order Random Walks

Simplicial complexes admit a variety of ‘high order random walks’ generalizing the standard random walk on a graph. There are three natural walks critically important to our results. The first are the classical
down-up walks
of
[
KM17
]
, denoted
U_{k,d}D_{d,k}
, which walk between
s,s^{\prime}\in X{\left(d\right)}
through a shared
k
-face
t\subset s,s^{\prime}
. The second are the
swap walks
of
[
DD19
,
AJT19
]
, denoted
S_{i,j}
, which walk between
disjoint
faces
s\in X{\left(i\right)}
and
s^{\prime}\in X{\left(j\right)}
via
s\cup s^{\prime}\in X{\left(i+j\right)}
. The final walk is the
noise operator

T_{\rho}
, which walks between
s,s^{\prime}\in X{\left(d\right)}
via a random
i
-subface of
s
for
i\sim Bin(1-\rho,d)
.
4
4

4

We remark that while definition this may seem odd at outset, it is the natural extension of the noise operator to hypergraphs and recovers the standard notion on products. See
[
Baf+22a
]
or
Section
4
for further details.

Sampler Graphs

A bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
is an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-function additive sampler if
\forall f:R\to[0,1]

\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\E_{{u\in R,u\sim v}}\left[f(u)\right]-\E_{{u\in R}}\left[f(u)\right]\right\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\right]<\beta.

G
is called an
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-multiplicative sampler if for all
f:R\to[0,1]
of density at least
\E_{{u\in R}}\left[f(u)\right]\geqslant\alpha
:

\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\E_{{u\in R,u\sim v}}\left[f(u)\right]-\E_{{u\in R}}\left[f(u)\right]\right\rvert\geqslant\delta\E_{{u\in R}}\left[f(u)\right]\right]<\beta.

It is a well-known fact (c.f.
[
IKW12
]
) that one can move between additive and multiplicative samplers without much parameter loss, and moreover that one can ‘flip’
L
and
R
in the sense that if
G=(L,R,E)
is an
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-sampler, then
G^{op}=(R,L,E)
is roughly a
(\beta,\alpha,\delta)
-sampler up to slight decay in parameters. We refer the reader to
Section
4
for details.

2.2
Sampling and Concentration

We can now state and overview the proof of optimal sampling on HDX in somewhat more formality.

Theorem 2.1

(Sampling on HDX (Informal
Theorem
5.1
))
.

Let
X
be a nice,
k
-uniform HDX and
i\leqslant k
. The containment graph
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is a
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-function sampler for

\beta\leqslant\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right).

In other words, for any
f:X{\left(i\right)}\to[0,1]
of expectation
\mu
:

\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\left\lvert\E_{{t\subseteq s}}\left[f(t)\right]-\mu\right\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\right]<\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right).

Since
\sqrt{d}
-skeletons of
\lambda
-TD complexes are nice, we get the following immediate corollary.

Corollary 2.2

(Sampling for TD-Complexes (Informal
Claim
4.28
+
Lemma
4.15
))
.

Fix
\lambda<1
and let
X
be a
d
-uniform
\lambda
-TD complex. Then for any
i<k\leqslant\sqrt{d}
, the containment graph of
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is a
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler for

\beta\leqslant\exp\left(-\Omega\left((1-\lambda)^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right).

We remark that this (and all following) results also hold in some variation under the popular notion of
spectral independence
, which for our purposes act similar to TD complexes. We discuss in the main body.

En route to
Theorem
2.1
, we also show the
top level
of any
\lambda
-TD complex satisfies exponential concentration for Lipschitz functions, which may be of independent interest.

Theorem 2.3

(Exponential Concentration of
\lambda
-TD Complexes (Informal
Corollary
7.3
))
.

For any
\lambda<1
,
\nu>0
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
X
be a
k
-uniform
\lambda
-TD complex. For any
\nu
-Lipschitz function
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
:

\ProbOp_{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}[|f(s)-\mathbb{E}[f]|\geqslant t]\leqslant\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{c_{\lambda}\nu}}\right)\right)

where
c_{\lambda}\leqslant O(e^{\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}})
.

We refer the reader to
Section
4.6
for the definition of
\nu
-Lipschitz, and here just note that specialized to inclusion sampling this implies
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is roughly a
(\varepsilon,\exp(-\varepsilon\frac{\sqrt{k}}{i}))
-sampler. A similar statement holds under spectral independence, albeit for a more restricted class of functions (see
Corollary
7.4
).

Proof Overview

The proof of
Theorem
2.1
is broken into two main components: the ‘Chernoff’ (
i=1
) setting, and a bootstrapping argument lifting Chernoff to general
i
. Here we focus just on the simplest version of the proof, the case of
two-sided HDX
, and refer the reader to
Section
7
for the general case.

Part I: Chernoff-Hoeffding

Toward
Theorem
2.1
, we first prove a fairly general “
k
vs
1
” concentration theorem for any HDX
X
whose strength depends on
X
’s underlying quantitative expansion. Under strong assumptions (
2^{-k}
-HDX), we recover true Chernoff. Perhaps more surprisingly, even under very weak assumptions (
\frac{1}{k}
-HDX) we still recover strong concentration of the form
\exp(-\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{k})
. Taking a
\sqrt{k}
-skeleton of the latter recovers Chernoff (see
Section
4.6
). We give a simplified statement here:

Theorem 2.4

(Chernoff-Hoeffding for HDX (Informal
Theorem
5.3
))
.

Let
X
be a
k
-uniform
\frac{1}{k}
-two-sided HDX. Then for any
f:X{\left(1\right)}\to[0,1]
:

\underset{\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}\left[\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})-\mu\right|>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{k}))

Moreover, if
X
is a
2^{-k}
-two-sided HDX stronger concentration holds:

\underset{\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}\left[\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})-\mu\right|>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}k))

For simplicity let
f=1_{A}
be the indicator of some
A\subset X(1)
. The proof of
Theorem
2.4
follows the standard Chernoff-style method of bounding the moment generating function (MGF). Namely for any
r>0
:

\underset{\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k}1_{A}(v_{i})\geqslant k(\mu+\varepsilon)\right]=\ProbOp\left[\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}r1_{A}(v_{i})\right)\geqslant\exp(rk\left(\mu+\varepsilon\right))\right]\leqslant\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}e^{r1_{A}(v_{i})}\right]}{e^{rk(\mu+\varepsilon)}}.

In the classical proof of Chernoff, the variables
v_{i}
are independent, so one can bound the MGF by

\underset{\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}e^{r1_{A}(v_{i})}\right]=\underset{v\in X{\left(1\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[e^{r1_{A}(v)}\right]^{k}\lesssim e^{rk(\mu+r)}

(2.1)

and set
r=\Theta(\varepsilon)
to get the desired bound.

When
X
is an HDX, the variables
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
may be extremely correlated, so the above approach breaks down naively. Instead, we take inspiration from Healy’s proof
[
Hea08
]
of the Expander-Chernoff Theorem and recursively bound the MGF by ‘splitting’ it into components on lower levels of
X
using the swap walks. Toward this end, for any
\ell\leqslant k
, define the partial MGF
z_{\ell}:X{\left(\ell\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
on a face
t=\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{\ell}\}
as

z_{\ell}(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{\ell})=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}e^{r1_{A}(v_{i})}=e^{r|t\cap A|}.

Our goal is to bound
\mathbb{E}[z_{k}]\approx\mathbb{E}[z_{1}]^{k}
. To set up a recursion, we appeal to the elementary observation that a
k
-set
s\in X{\left(k\right)}
can be sampled by first drawing a
\frac{k}{2}
-set
t_{1}\in X{\left(k/2\right)}
, then
t_{2}\in X_{t_{1}}{\left(k/2\right)}
conditionally from its link. We set
s=t_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}t_{2}
. This allows us to ‘split’
z_{k}
into two correlated copies of
z_{k/2}
:

\displaystyle\underset{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[z_{k}(s)]

\displaystyle=\underset{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[e^{r|s\cap A|}]

\displaystyle=\underset{t_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}t_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[e^{r|t_{1}\cap A|}e^{r|t_{2}\cap A|}]

\displaystyle=\underset{t_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}t_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[z_{k/2}(t_{1})z_{k/2}(t_{2})].

The trick is now to observe that
(t_{1},t_{2})
is distributed exactly as an edge in the swap walk
S_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}
. Since swap walks on HDX have excellent spectral expansion
[
AJT19
,
DD19
,
GLL22
,
AP23
]
, we can ‘de-correlate’ the above and write the relation

\E[z_{k}]\leqslant(1-\gamma)\E[z_{k/2}]^{2}+\gamma\mathbb{E}[z_{k/2}^{2}]

(2.2)

where
\gamma
is the expansion of the swap walk.

We could now recurse if not for the error term
\gamma\mathbb{E}[z_{k/2}^{2}]
, which could very well be the dominating factor. The key is show
\E[z_{k/2}^{2}]
actually can’t be too much bigger than
\E[z_{k}]
itself:

\E[z_{k/2}^{2}]\lesssim e^{r^{2}k}\E[z_{k}].

(2.3)

Setting
r
such that
\gamma e^{r^{2}k}\ll 1
, (
2.2
) becomes

\E[z_{k}]\lesssim\frac{1-\gamma}{1-e^{r^{2}k}\gamma}\E[z_{k/2}]^{2}\approx\E[z_{k/2}]^{2},

allowing us to recurse to show an upper bound similar to (
2.1
). For ‘weak’ HDX, this strategy requires setting
r=\Theta(\varepsilon k^{-1/2})
, while for strong HDX we may set
r=\Theta(\varepsilon)
. This results in the gap in the stated bounds.

To prove (
2.3
) we in some sense ‘reverse’ the sampling process above, and draw a
\frac{k}{2}
-set
t
by first sampling a
k
-set
s\in X{\left(k\right)}
, then take
t\subset s
uniformly at random. This means we can write the error term as:

\underset{t\in X{\left(k/2\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[z_{k/2}(t)^{2}]=\E_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\E_{{t\subseteq s}}\left[z_{k/2}(t)^{2}\right]\right]=\E_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\E_{{t\subseteq s}}\left[e^{2r|t\cap A|}\right]\right].

Crucially, the inner expectation is now over the
\frac{k}{2}
-uniform
complete complex
(whose vertices are the
k
-set
s
), so standard concentration implies the exponent,
2r|t\cap A|
, is close to its expectation
r|s\cap A|
up to a factor of roughly
r^{2}k
with high probability. Thus:

\E_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\E_{{t\subseteq s}}\left[e^{2r|t\cap A|}\right]\right]\lesssim\E_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[e^{r|s\cap A|+r^{2}k}\right]=e^{r^{2}k}\underset{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[z_{k}].

Part II: Bootstrapping

We now argue one can lift Chernoff for HDX to optimal sampling for their inclusion graphs
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
. The idea, based on the method in
[
IKW12
]
for the complete complex, is to try to reduce to a
\frac{k}{i}
-uniform complex
Y
whose vertices are
i
-sets in
X{\left(i\right)}
, and whose
\frac{k}{i}
-faces correspond in some way to the
k
-sets in
X
. We could then hope to apply Chernoff to this system to bound the
k
vs.
i
sampling behavior in the original complex.

Somewhat more formally, we’ll consider the
\frac{k}{i}
-uniform complex generated by the following process: draw a random k-face
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X{\left(k\right)}
, and partition the face randomly into
\frac{k}{i}
subsets of size
i
denoted
\{I_{1},\ldots,I_{\frac{k}{i}}\}
. The resulting complex, called the
faces complex
of
X
, inherits local-spectral expansion from
X
’s swap walks so we can apply Chernoff for HDX to get concentration of the form
\exp(-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i})
.

Unfortunately, this does not prove concentration for quite the right object. In particular, given a function
f:X{\left(i\right)}\to[0,1]
, the above really states that a random partitioning of a
k
-face
s
into
\frac{k}{i}
sub
i
-faces satisfies

\ProbOp_{s\in X{\left(k\right)},\penalty\ \mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}_{j}I_{j}=s}\left[\left|\frac{i}{k}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k/i}f(I_{j})-\mu\right|>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right),

whereas we’d like to bound
\underset{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}\left[\left|\underset{t\subset s}{\mathbb{E}}[f(t)]-\mu\right|>\varepsilon\right]
. Here we are saved by the fact that each
I_{j}
is
marginally
distributed as a random
i
-set
t\subset s
. We prove in such cases it is possible to inherit sampling from the correlated bound up to a small loss in parameters, completing the proof.

2.3
Reverse Hypercontractivity

Leveraging
Theorem
2.1

locally
in
X
, we prove reverse hypercontractivity for high dimensional expanders.

Theorem 2.5

(Reverse Hypercontractivity (Informal
Theorem
6.1
))
.

Fix
\rho\in(0,1)
and let
X
be a
k
-uniform nice complex for
k
sufficiently large. Then there exist constants
C,q
(depending only on
\rho
) such that

\langle f,T_{\rho}g\rangle\geqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{q}\lVert g\rVert_{q}.

In other words
T_{\rho}
is “
(C,q)
-reverse hypercontractive.”

The core of
Theorem
2.5
is really a version of the result for the down-up walk which simply states any two subsets
A,B\subset X{\left(k\right)}
of non-trivial size must have correspondingly many edges between them.

Lemma 2.6

(Reverse Hypercontractivity of the Down-Up Walk (Informal
Theorem
6.4
))
.

Fix
\gamma\in(0,1)
and let
X
be a
k
-uniform nice complex for
k
sufficiently large. There exist constants
c,q
(depending only on
\gamma
) such that for any
A,B\subset X{\left(k\right)}
of measure at least
\exp(-ck)
:

\ProbOp_{s,s^{\prime}\sim U_{\gamma k,k}D_{k,\gamma k}}[s\in A,s^{\prime}\in B]\geqslant\ProbOp[A]^{q}\ProbOp[B]^{q}.

Note that the assumption on the set size in
Lemma
2.6
is essentially tight. Even if one walks from
s
to
s^{\prime}
while going down to a single vertex it is possible to find sets
A
and
B
of size roughly
\exp(-k)
which are totally disconnected, even on the complete complex.

Proof Overview

The proof of
Lemma
2.6
relies on the following simple observation: since the down-up walk samples an edge by first sampling
t\in X{\left(\gamma k\right)}
and then
independently
sampling
s,s^{\prime}\in X{\left(k\right)}
containing
t
, we can express the
\gamma
-correlated mass of
A
and
B
as:

\ProbOp_{{s,s^{\prime}\sim U_{\gamma k,k}D_{k,\gamma k}}}\left[s\in A,s^{\prime}\in B\right]=\E_{{t\in{X{\left(\gamma k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{s}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{As\supseteq t}\right.s\supseteq t\right]\ProbOp_{{s}}\left[B\left|\;\vphantom{Bs\supseteq t}\right.s\supseteq t\right]\right].

Now the connection with inclusion sampling becomes clear—if even a non-negligible fraction of
t\in X{\left(\gamma k\right)}
see a reasonable portion of
A
and
B
simultaneously, the righthand side should be large!

Naturally our first thought might be to appeal to
Theorem
2.1
which bounds this type of behavior, but when
i=\gamma k
the resulting bound is far too weak to be useful. In particular, after swapping from additive to multiplicative sampling and ‘flipping’ the graph,
Theorem
2.1
roughly states that
(X{\left(i\right)},X{\left(k\right)})
is an
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-sampler for
\alpha\approx\exp(-\delta^{2}\beta\frac{k}{i})
. When
i=\Theta(k)
, this only gives a guarantee for sets of constant size.

The key is to realize that because
X
expands
locally
, we don’t have to sample
t\in X{\left(\gamma k\right)}
all at once. Instead, we’ll sample
t
in ‘steps’ as
t=t_{1}\cup\ldots\cup t_{m}
where each
t_{j}
is sampled conditionally (i.e. from the link of) its predecessors, and apply
Theorem
2.1
to each
t_{j}
individually. Toward this end, assume
\ProbOp[A]\leqslant\ProbOp[B]
and fix the ‘step size’
\ell=|t_{j}|
roughly such that
\exp(-c^{\prime}\frac{k}{\ell})=\ProbOp[A]
for some sufficiently small
c^{\prime}>0
.

Drawing our first component
t_{1}
, observe for small enough
c^{\prime}
we have set our parameters such that
Theorem
2.1
promises
(X{\left(\ell\right)},X{\left(k\right)})
is an
(\alpha,\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2})
-sampler for
\alpha\leqslant\min\{\ProbOp[A],\ProbOp[B]\}
, so with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
the conditional measures of
A
and
B
in
t_{1}
are at least
\frac{1}{2}\ProbOp[A]
and
\frac{1}{2}\ProbOp[B]
respectively. Moreover, moving into the link of
t_{1}
we can apply exactly the same process to
t_{2}
(and so forth), so by the
i
-th step one can inductively show that with probability at least
2^{-i}
, the conditional mass of
A
and
B
are at least
2^{-i}\ProbOp[A]
and
2^{-i}\ProbOp[B]
respectively. On the other hand, we only take
m=\frac{\gamma k}{\ell}=O(\log(\ProbOp[A]^{-1}))
total steps by our choice of
\ell
, so at the end of the procedure we have that with probability at least
2^{-m}=\ProbOp[A]^{O(1)}
, the density of
A
and
B
in
t
is at least
\ProbOp[A]^{O(1)}
as well. Putting everything together, we have

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{s,s^{\prime}\sim U_{\gamma k,k}D_{k,\gamma k}}}\left[s\in A,s^{\prime}\in B\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{{t\in{X{\left(\gamma k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{s}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{As\supseteq t}\right.s\supseteq t\right]\ProbOp_{{s}}\left[B\left|\;\vphantom{Bs\supseteq t}\right.s\supseteq t\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant 2^{-m}\cdot(2^{-m}\ProbOp[A])\cdot(2^{-m}\ProbOp[B])

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp[A]^{O(1)}\ProbOp[B]

as desired.

Once one has
Lemma
2.6
,
Theorem
2.5
follows by passing to discrete approximations of
f
and
g
that carefully balance the quality of approximation (namely regarding the mass of
f
and
g
and pointwise closeness) and coarseness (number of discrete values in the approximation). Done correctly, one can then divide the functions into boolean level-sets and apply
Lemma
2.6
to achieve full reverse hypercontractivity at the cost of a slight decrease in the corresponding norm and an additional constant factor.

2.4
Optimality and the Trickling-Down Threshold

Oppenheim’s Trickling Down Theorem is among the most fundamental results in the theory of spectral high dimensional expansion
[
Opp18
]
. It states that any connected complex whose top links have expansion
strictly
better than
\frac{1}{d-1}
exhibits ‘local-to-global’ behavior: one can immediately give quantitative bounds on the spectral expansion of
all
links of
X
(including its ‘global’
1
-skeleton), as well as on the down-up walks
[
AL20
]
. Our results are in some sense a strengthening of the local-to global behavior of Oppenheim: essentially as soon as one passes this threshold, not only can one infer ‘Chernoff’-type concentration on skeletons of
X
,
X
itself actually satisfies exponential concentration for any Lipschitz function. This is in stark contrast to complexes
at
the TD-Barrier, which we observe may have no concentration properties whatsoever:

Theorem 2.7

(Lower Bounds at the TD-Barrier (
Proposition
11.7
))
.

For every
\beta<1
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a family of
k
-uniform
1
-TD complexes
\{X_{n}\}
such that
(X_{n}{\left(k\right)},X_{n}{\left(1\right)})
is not a
(\frac{1}{2},\beta)
-additive sampler.

Theorem
2.1
also gives essentially the best possible parameters for inclusion sampling on any complex.

Theorem 2.8

(Inclusion Sampling Lower Bounds (Informal
Theorem
11.1
))
.

Let
i<k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,0.1)
, and let
X
be a
k
-uniform complex, then one of the following holds:

1.

(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is not an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler for
\beta\leqslant\exp\left(-O\left(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right)
.

2.

(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is not an
(\frac{1}{10i},\frac{\varepsilon}{6i})
-sampler.

Classical lower bounds on samplers (e.g.
[
CEG95
]
) are based on
degree
and are far from tight in our setting where inclusion structure is the main barrier. Nevertheless, we are able to argue our bounds are optimal by reducing to
i=1
where the degree bound is tight. The idea is to show any set
A
which is a counter-example to sampling of
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(1\right)})
can be ‘lifted’ to a counter example to
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
by taking
A^{\prime}
to be the family of
i
-sets that hit
A
. This works when the measure of
A^{\prime}
is roughly
i\mu(A)
, and therefore when 1)
A
is small, and 2)
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is a sampler. We show the original technique of
[
CEG95
]
can be used to analyze sets of any density, thereby giving the desired counter-example.

2.5
Agreement Testing

Leveraging
Theorem
2.5
, we prove several new agreement theorems in both the
99\%
and
1\%
-regimes. In the former we consider a general setup of
[
DFH19
]
. Given a complex
X
and an ensemble of functions
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{s}:\binom{s}{i}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}\}_{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}
, we’d like to test whether
\mathcal{F}
‘comes from’ a global function
g:X{\left(i\right)}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
. To be concrete, consider
i=1
. Here we are given
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{s}:s\to\mathbb{F}_{2}\}
and can interpret each
f_{s}
as the indicator of some subset
r_{s}\subseteq s
. We want to test whether there is a ‘global’ subset
R\subseteq X{\left(1\right)}
that approximately determines most local subsets in the sense that
r_{s}\approx R\cap s
. For
i=2
, a similar interpretation holds but for
subgraphs
of
X
. Namely, think of
f_{s}:\binom{s}{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
now as a subgraph of
s
, i.e. corresponding to the indicator of an edge-set
e_{s}\subseteq\binom{s}{2}
. Now ‘coming from’ a global function means there is a global subgraph
E\subseteq X{\left(2\right)}
such that for most local graphs,
e_{s}\approx E\cap s
. For
i>2
one interprets this similarly as a question about local vs. global sub-complexes of
X
. Such ‘subcomplex’ tests have proved useful both in boolean analysis
[
DFH19
]
and as a tool for analyzing the more challenging
1\%
-regime
[
DD23a
,
DDL24
]
.

We study the classical
V
-test in this context, which draws a pair of
k
-faces
(s,s^{\prime})\sim U_{k/2,k}D_{k,k/2}
intersecting on
k/2
vertices and checks if
s
and
s^{\prime}
agree on most
i
-faces in the intersection. Let
Agree^{V}_{\eta}(\mathcal{F})
denote the probability
(s,s^{\prime})
agree on at least a
1-\eta
fraction of their shared
i
-faces. Similarly, for any global function
g:X{\left(i\right)}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
, write
f_{s}\overset{\eta}{\approx}g|_{s}
if
f_{s}
agrees with
g
on a
1-\eta
fraction of its
i
-faces. Then:

Theorem 2.9

(The Subcomplex V-Test,
99\%
-Regime (Informal
Theorem
8.1
))
.

Let
X
be a
k
-uniform nice complex. For any
\eta,\varepsilon>0
if
Agree^{V}_{\eta}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
:

\exists g:X{\left(1\right)}\to\mathbb{F}_{2},\;\;\ProbOp_{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}[f_{s}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\approx}g|_{s}]\geqslant 1-\varepsilon^{\prime}.

where
\eta^{\prime}\leqslant O(\eta+\varepsilon)
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leqslant\varepsilon^{O_{\eta}(1)}+e^{-\Omega_{\eta}(k)}
.

In the ‘
1\%
’-regime, our goal is to infer global structure of
\mathcal{F}
even when the test passes with small (but non-negligible) probability. We focus on the
i=1
setting and aim to construct a test
\mathcal{T}
that infers global structure whenever
Agree^{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant\exp(-\Omega(k))
, the best possible bound since a random function passes any such test with this probability. To this end, we consider a variant of
[
IKW12
]
’s
Z
-test on high dimensional expanders which samples a triple
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime})\in X{\left(k\right)}
roughly such that
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
is distributed as the V-test, and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
is drawn from the link of
\sigma^{\prime}\setminus\sigma
. The
Z
-test passes if
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
and
(\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime})
are consistent on their intersections. We prove that the Z-test is sound under the stronger assumption that the complex is “
\lambda
-global”, meaning the swap walk
S_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}
is
\lambda
-close to its stationary distribution in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm (see
Definition
8.6
).

Theorem 2.10

(The Z-Test,
1\%
-Regime (Informal
Theorem
8.7
))
.

\forall\lambda,\eta>0
and large enough
k
, let
X
be a
\lambda
-global
k
-uniform nice complex. Then for any
\delta\in(8\lambda+e^{-\Omega(\eta k)},\frac{1}{8})
if
Agree_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant\delta
:

\exists g:X{\left(1\right)}\to\mathbb{F}_{2},\;\;\ProbOp_{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}[f_{s}\overset{\eta}{\approx}g|_{s}]\geqslant\delta/8.

We will not give a true proof overview of our testing results (though it should be mentioned that the high level strategy closely follows
[
IKW12
,
DL17
]
), but wish to highlight that the core of
Theorem
8.7
is really a ‘weak’
local
agreement theorem for the V-test on nice HDX of independent interest. The statement is quite technical, so we give a very informal version here that captures it in spirit.

Theorem 2.11

(The Local Agreement Theorem (Informal
Theorem
8.12
))
.

Let
X
be a nice complex. Then for any
\delta\geqslant\exp(-\Omega(k))
if
Agree_{0}^{V}\geqslant\delta
, for an
\Omega(\delta)
-fraction of
t\in X{\left(k/2\right)}
there is a ‘smoothing’ of
\mathcal{F}
which maintains its structure and has
(1-\delta^{2})
-agreement on the link
X_{t}
.

In other words, any non-trivial agreement of the V-test
must
come from the fact that
\mathcal{F}
is locally consistent with a global function. It is possible
Theorem
2.11
could be propogated to a true Z-test under much weaker conditions than
\lambda
-globality, e.g. under the recent topological notions of
[
GK22
,
DD23a
,
BM23a
]
. We leave this as an open question for the
1\%
-regime.

2.6
Further Applications

Finally, we discuss our applications of
Theorem
2.1
and
Theorem
2.5
beyond agreement testing.

Geometric Overlap

A complex
X
has
(d,c)
-geometric overlap if for every embedding
X{\left(1\right)}
into
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, there is a point
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
that lies in the convex hulls of at least a
c
-fraction of
X
’s embedded
d
-faces.
[
Fox+12
]
proved every
d
-uniform bounded degree complex has geometric overlap at best
c_{d}
, where
c_{d}
is the overlap of the complete complex. We prove sufficiently strong high dimensional expanders match this bound.

Theorem 2.12

(Geometric Overlap (Informal
Theorem
9.13
))
.

For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\lambda>0
such that any
\lambda
-two-sided HDX with uniform vertex weights has
(d,c_{d}-\varepsilon)
-geometric overlap.

We remark that while geometric overlap is a classical problem in mathematics (dating back to
[
BF84
]
), we are not aware of any applications in computer science. Nevertheless other instances of overlap theorems have proven powerful (see e.g.
[
Cha+23
]
), so it seems plausible such results may be of future use.

Double Samplers

Double samplers are a powerful variant of sampler graphs used in
[
Din+18
]
to construct good list-decodable codes and in
[
DW22
]
for the heavy hitters problem. Roughly speaking, double samplers are ‘three-wise’ inclusion structures
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(j\right)},X{\left(1\right)})
such that

1.

(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(j\right)})
is a
(1/2,\beta)
-additive sampler

2.

For all
s\in X{\left(k\right)}
, the restriction of
(X{\left(j\right)},X{\left(1\right)})
to vertices in
s
is a
(1/2,\beta)
-additive sampler

The best known prior construction of double samplers
[
Din+18
]
had overhead
\frac{|X{\left(k\right)}|}{|X{\left(1\right)}|}=\exp(\poly(\beta^{-1}))
, leading to poor rate of their resulting list-decodable codes. One of the main open questions asked in
[
Din+18
]
was to determine the optimal overhead of a double sampler.

We nearly resolve this problem for ‘typical’ complexes
X
which are non-contracting (i.e.
|X{\left(j\right)}|>|X{\left(i\right)}|
whenever
j>i
), and satisfy a weak ‘hitting-set’ type guarantee. In particular,
Theorem
2.1
gives double samplers with only
quasi-polynomial overhead
, while for such typical complexes
Theorem
2.8
implies a corresponding near-matching lower bound.

Theorem 2.13

(Near-Optimal Double Samplers (Informal
Theorem
9.3
))
.

For every
\beta>0
, there exists an infinite family of double samplers with
\exp(O(\log^{6}(\beta^{-1})))
overhead. Moreover, any ‘typical’ double sampler has overhead at least
\exp(\Omega(\log^{2}(\beta^{-1})))
.

We refer the reader to
Section
9.1
for the exact restrictions on
X
for which the lower bound holds.

Locally Testable and List-Decodable Codes

While it is likely applying our double sampler machinery to the arguments of
[
Din+18
]
would give list-decodable codes with substantially improved rate, this was already achieved subsequent to
[
Din+18
]
’s work using walks on expanders
[
Ale+20
]
. Instead, using a variant of our machinery we give a closely related application to distance amplification for locally testable codes with near-optimal distance-alphabet trade-off. The resulting codes are list-decodable and preserve testability up to a log factor in the alphabet.

Theorem 2.14

(Large Distance List-Decodable LTCs (Informal
Theorem
10.21
))
.

For all large enough
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an explicit family of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-linear codes with

1.

Distance:
1-2^{-k}-\varepsilon

2.

Alphabet Size:
2^{k}

3.

Rate:
\varepsilon^{O(k)}

Moreover, the codes are efficiently list decodable up to distance
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
and locally testable in
O(1)
queries with soundness
\Omega(\frac{1}{k\log(1/\varepsilon)})
.

Prior distance amplification techniques for LTCs
[
Kop+17
]
have exponentially worse distance-alphabet trade-off, but improved rate. To our knowledge the above are the first large distance list-decodable LTCs. We also give a candidate technique for amplifying distance while maintaining
constant
soundness (removing the
k
factor in the denominator above) using HDX, and prove the method works on the complete complex. We refer the reader to
Section
10
for further details on these notions.

Degree Lower Bounds for HDX

One of the formational results in the study of expansion is the Alon-Boppana theorem
[
Nil91
]
, roughly stating any family of bounded-degree
\lambda
-expanders have degree at least
\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}
. Despite degree being a critical parameter in application (degree controls the ‘blow-up’ induced by using an HDX as a gadget), in high dimensions we understand very little about optimal degree. The best known constructions of
\lambda
-TD complexes have degree
\lambda^{\tilde{O}(d^{2})}
. Is this optimal? Could
\lambda^{o(d^{2})}
be achieved?

We take the first step toward answering this question. Combining our sampling results with degree lower bounds of
[
CEG95
]
, we show super-exponential lower bounds for certain special classes of HDX, including polynomial skeletons of any hyper-regular
\lambda
-TD complex.

Theorem 2.15

(Degree Lower Bounds (Informal
Theorem
9.8
))
.

Fix
\lambda<1
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and
k\leqslant\sqrt{d}
. Let
X
be the
k
-skeleton of a
d
-uniform hyper-regular
\lambda
-TD complex. Then

\text{deg}(X)\geqslant 2^{\Omega_{\lambda}(k^{2})}

Theorem
2.15
exhibits yet another threshold phenomenon at the TD-barrier, since there exist
1
-TD complexes
[
Gol21
]
which have exponential degree at any level. While the latter are not hyper-regular, they are still reasonably balanced and our techniques extend to this regime.
5
5

5

Namely, we only really require that the underlying graph of every link does not have a (roughly)
\exp(-\sqrt{d})
fraction of vertices making up
1/2
the measure. Unfortunately, constructions such as the Ramanujan complex have extremely unbalanced links, and actually do fail this property. See
Section
9.2
for further discussion.

If one could prove a true Chernoff bound for
\lambda
-TD complexes (rather than our concentration of
\exp(-\sqrt{k})
),
Theorem
2.15
could be improved to showing degree
2^{\Omega(d^{2})}
at the
top level
of
X
, albeit still under the assumption of hyper-regularity. While bounded-degree hyper-regular HDX do exist
[
FI20
]
, their degree is substantially worse and removing this constraint from our lower bound remains an important open problem.

Separating MLSI and Reverse Hypercontractivity

The Modified Log-Sobolev Inequality (MLSI) is a powerful analytic inequality used to bound the mixing time of Markov chains and previous weakest sufficient condition for reverse hypercontractivity
[
MOS13
]
. To the best of our knowledge, it was open until this work whether MLSI was
necessary
. We resolve this question for the setting allowing a leading constant
C>1
.

Corollary 2.16

(Separating RHC and MLSI (Informal
Corollary
9.17
))
.

There exist constants
C,q,q^{\prime}
such that for infinitely many
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
(C,q,q^{\prime})
-reverse hypercontractive operators on
N
vertices with vanishing MLSI constant:

\rho_{MLSI}\leqslant\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\log(N)}\right)

This separation is essentially the strongest possible for operators with constant expansion, since any such operator has LSI (and therefore MLSI) at least
\Omega(\frac{1}{\log(N)})

[
DS96
]
.

A Frankl–Rödl Theorem

The Frankl–Rödl Theorem
[
FR87
]
is a broadly applied result in extremal combinatorics bounding the independence number of the graph on
\{0,1\}^{n}
connecting strings of fixed intersection size.
Lemma
2.6
implies an analogous result for the
\gamma k
-step down-up walk on HDX.

Corollary 2.17

(Frankl–Rödl for HDX (Informal
Claim
9.19
))
.

Fix
\gamma>0
and let
X
be a
k
-uniform nice complex for
k
sufficiently large. The
\gamma k
-step down-up walk has independence number

\alpha\leqslant\exp(-\Omega_{\gamma}(k))\cdot|X{\left(k\right)}|

In comparison to Frankl–Rödl, the above bounds the size of sets avoiding intersections of size
at most
instead of
exactly

(1-\gamma)k
. A similar issue appears when using reverse hypercontractivity toward this end in the cube and is handled by a separate Fourier analytic argument in
[
Ben12
]
. It would be interesting to extend our results to the exact case to give a true analog of Frankl–Rödl.

‘It Ain’t Over Till It’s Over’

Reverse hypercontractivity was used to resolve Friedgut and Kalai’s ‘It Ain’t Over Till It’s Over’ Conjecture
[
MOO05
]
, which bounds the tail of random restrictions in the hypercube. The core of their proof was an analog for the closely related noise operator previously used in analysis of non-interactive correlation distillation protocols
[
Mos+06
]
. We generalize the latter theorem to HDX:

Theorem 2.18

(Noise Operator Tail Bounds (Informal
Theorem
9.18
))
.

Fix
\rho\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large. For any
k
-uniform nice complex
X
and
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to[0,1]
of density
\mu
:

\ProbOp\left[T_{\rho}f\notin[\delta,1-\delta]\right]\leqslant\delta^{O_{\mu,\rho}(1)}

3
Related Work

High Dimensional Expanders

Spectral high dimensional expansion was developed over a series of works
[
EK16
,
KM17
,
Opp18
,
DK17
]
building on prior notions of high dimensional expansion in topology
[
Gar73
,
LSV05a
,
LM06
,
Gro10
,
KL14
,
KKL14
]
. Basic higher order random walks were introduced by Kaufman and Mass
[
KM17
]
, with the swap operators later introduced independently in
[
DD19
,
AJT19
]
. A great deal of work has been done at the intersection of high dimensional expanders and analysis, including determining the optimal spectral gap of the down-up walks
[
KM17
,
KO20
,
Dik+18
,
AL20
,
KT21
]
, developing a general theory of Fourier analysis
[
KM20
,
Dik+18
,
Gai+22
,
Baf+22a
,
GLL22
,
GK23
]
, and applications thereof to agreement testing
[
DK17
,
DD19
,
KM20
,
GK22
]
, mixing of Markov Chains
[
Ana+19
,
ALG21
,
CLV21
,
Che+21
,
Liu21
,
Bla+22
,
Fen+22
,
Ana+23
]
(among many others), and algorithms
[
DW22
,
Baf+22
,
Bea+23
]
.

Two works
[
KS20
,
KKS21
]
in the HDX literature also study Chernoff-style bounds, albeit in very different regimes from our results. Kaufman and Sharakanski
[
KS20
]
study concentration for global functions over repeated random walks on
k
-faces, which is incomparable to our setting. Kaufman, Kyng, and Song
[
KKS21
]
prove scalar and matrix Chernoff bounds under
\ell_{\infty}
-independence
, a strengthening of spectral HDX that only holds for dense complexes. While in this work we are primarily interested in proving concentration for
bounded-degree
complexes, it is interesting to ask whether their stronger result (Chernoff for matrix-valued functions) extends to the sparse regime.

In
[
Baf+22a
,
GLL22
]
, the authors prove a notion called “global” hypercontractivity for high dimensional expanders. While it is known that reverse hypercontractivity follows from the standard hypercontractive inequality
[
MOS13
]
, we are not aware of a reduction from the weaker global variant. Our work also differs substantially from
[
Baf+22a
,
GLL22
]
in its tools and regime of application.

Samplers and Chernoff-Hoeffding

Samplers are among the most classical tools in theoretical computer science, see e.g.
[
Gol97
,
Sha04
,
Wig09
]
for surveys of their many constructions and applications. De-randomized Chernoff bounds are an important sub-family of sampler graphs that exhibit optimal tails in the degree of the graph. Sparse examples were constructed in
[
AKS87
,
Gil98
]
using walks on expanders, and there is a great deal of literature toward understanding what general families of hypergraphs admit such concentration, e.g. under limited independence
[
SS89
,
SS90
,
SSS95
,
Pem01
]
, negative correlation
[
DR96
]
, for edge colorings
[
PS97
]
, under Dobrushin uniqueness
[
Pau14
]
, for strongly Raleigh distributions
[
KS18
]
, and most recently under
\ell_{\infty}
-independence
[
KKS21
]
. Such bounds have many interesting applications beyond those discussed in this work.

Inclusion samplers were first introduced in Impagliazzo, Kabanets, and Wigderson’s
[
IKW12
]
work on agreement testers and PCPs with strong soundness. Prior to
Theorem
2.1
, there were only two known systems of inclusion samplers with optimal tails: the complete complex
[
IKW12
]
, and curves
[
Mos17
]
. Both examples were used toward the construction of PCPs with strong soundness, but failed to achieve optimal parameters due to their blow-up in size. Several sparse variants of inclusion samplers were known to admit weaker ‘Chebyshev’ type bounds, including high dimensional expanders
[
DK17
]
and the Grassmann
[
IKW12
]
. The latter also lead to PCPs with strong soundness
[
DM11
]
, but failed to achieve optimal parameters due to its polynomial tail.
Theorem
2.1
is the first to achieve the best of both worlds, though there remain substantial barriers toward its use for PCPs.

Reverse Hypercontractivity

Reverse hypercontractivity was first shown by Borell
[
Bor82
]
and first utilized in theoretical computer science by
[
Mos+06
]
who observed the inequality implies fine-control of mixing between sets (akin to
Lemma
2.6
). The result has since found great use in hardness of approximation
[
MOO05
,
FKO07
,
She09
,
Ben12
,
Kau+14
]
, social choice
[
MR12
,
Kel12
,
Mos12
]
, and extremal combinatorics
[
Ben12
,
Kau+14
]
. In 2013,
[
MOS13
]
extended known bounds on reverse hypercontractivity to general product spaces and settings with bounded modified Log-Sobolev Inequalities, a surprising result given the failure of standard hypercontractivity in these settings. Dinur and Livni-Navon
[
DL17
]
were the first to apply these results to agreement testing, where they resolved the conjecture of
[
IKW12
]
regarding exponential soundness for the Z-test and built the first ‘combinatorial’ PCPs with optimal soundness.

We note that MLSI bounds (and therefore RHC) are known for many ‘dense’ settings of HDX studied in the sampling literature (see e.g.
[
CLV21
]
), with the most general prior condition being the notions of entropic independence and fractional log-concavity
[
Ana+22
]
which are significant strengthenings of spectral HDX. These methods all necessarily rely on density and cannot capture our theory.

Agreement Tests

Agreement testing, also known as direct product testing, is a powerful tool in the construction of PCPs and locally testable codes
[
RS96
,
AS97
,
RS97
,
GS97
,
DR06
,
Din07
,
Dik+20
,
Din+22a
]
. Agreement tests in the
99\%
-regime were studied by
[
GS97
]
and
[
DS14
]
in the complete complex, and extended to high dimensional expanders in
[
DK17
,
DD19
,
KM20
]
.
[
DFH19
]
gave the first ‘subcomplex’ agreement theorem in the
99\%
-regime (the setting of
Theorem
2.9
) in the complete complex. Agreement testing in the
1\%
-regime was studied in the complete complex in
[
DG08
,
IKW12
,
DL17
]
, with the lattermost work giving optimal bounds.
[
IKW12
]
also gave a de-randomized test over the Grassmann complex, which is polynomial size, but suffers from exponentially worse soundness. A different
1\%
-regime test on the Grassmann was also used to resolve the
2
-
2
Games Conjecture
[
KMS17
,
Din+18a
,
BKS19
]
.

Finally,
[
GK22
,
BM23a
,
DD23a
,
BLM24
,
DDL24
]
studied the
1\%
(or closely related
list
agreement) regime on high dimensional expanders. These works identified key
topological
properties any complex must exhibit to be a good tester, and subsequently constructed complexes satisfying these notions, giving the first bounded degree
1\%
-agreement testers. The methods of
[
DD23a
,
DDL24
]
in particular rely on
Theorem
2.1
and
Theorem
2.9
. The soundness achieved by the above works, however, is inverse
logarithmic
in the dimension instead of inverse exponential (or even polynomial). Achieving inverse exponential soundness as in
Theorem
2.10
for families of bounded degree complexes remains an important open question.

Open questions

1.

While we are able to show optimal concentration for a fairly broad class of high dimensional expanders, in the weakest settings (namely under spectral independence and at the TD-Threshold), we are only able to prove exponential concentration at the top level. It is unclear whether this is a fundamental or purely technical barrier: do such complexes satisfy a true Chernoff bound? As discussed above, a resolution of this question in the positive leads to better degree lower bounds for HDX, and in particular a
2^{\Omega(d^{2})}
lower bound for the top level of hyper-regular
\lambda
-TD complexes.

2.

The best known constructions of high dimensional expanders are not hyper-regular. Is it possible to remove this constraint from our degree lower bound? While our technique holds even for ‘reasonably balanced’ complexes, it cannot handle objects like the Ramanujan complexes have extremely unbalanced links. It seems likely this is a technical rather than inherent barrier, and we conjecture some finer notion of concentration or application thereof may be able to remove this constraint.

3.

In
Section
7
we prove near-optimal concentration for partite HDX, but our bounds suffer polynomial loss in
\varepsilon
over the two-sided case. The reason boils down to understanding concentration of a simple hypergraph we call the
swap complex
, made up of
\frac{k}{i}
-tuples of disjoint
i
-sets
s\in\binom{[n]}{i}
. We prove a Chernoff bound for this hypergraph when
n\gtrsim\frac{k}{\varepsilon^{5}}
. Does such a bound hold in the regime where
n=\Theta(k)
? This would imply fully optimal concentration for partite HDX.

4.

We show that ‘standard’ simplicial complexes used in the construction of double samplers cannot achieve better than quasipolynomial overhead. Are there non-simplicial objects better served for this purpose?

5.

Our
1\%
-regime test only holds on dense complexes due to the assumption of
\ell_{\infty}
-expansion. However, the main argument only requires reverse hypercontractivity and spectral gap of the down-up walk. Can the argument be completed without the assumption of
\ell_{\infty}
-expansion to give new sparse agreement testers in the low acceptance regime?

Roadmap

In
Section
4
we give more detailed and formal preliminaries on high dimensional expanders, random walks, samplers, and reverse hypercontractivity. In
Section
5
we prove optimal inclusion sampling for two-sided HDX, and cover all other cases in
Section
7
.

In
Section
6
we prove reverse hypercontractivity. In
Section
8
and
Section
9
we give applications of reverse hypercontractivity to agreement testing, and analysis and combinatorics respectively.
In
Section
10
we give basic sampling lemmas for splitting trees and construct high distance list-decodable LTCs. Finally in
Section
11
we show optimality of our inclusion sampling results both in terms of spectral requirements on
X
and strength of sampling itself.

4
Preliminaries and Notation

4.1
Graphs and Spectral Expansion

A weighted un-directed graph
G=(V,E,\ProbOp)
consists of a finite vertex set
V
, a set of edges
E\subseteq\binom{V}{2}
, and a distribution on the edges
\ProbOp:E\to(0,1]
.
For a vertex
v
we denote by
\ProbOp\left[{v}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{uv\in E}\ProbOp\left[{uv}\right]
.
In this paper all graphs are assumed to be weighted (the weight is implicit in notation). We also assume that there are no isolated vertices.

Let
\ell_{2}(V)=\{f:V\to\mathbb{R}\}
. The graph
G
induces an inner product
\langle f,g\rangle=\E_{{v}}\left[f(v)g(v)\right]
on
\ell_{2}(V)
, as well as a normalized adjacency operator
A:\ell_{2}(V)\to\ell_{2}(V)
defined as

Af(v):=\E_{{u\sim v}}\left[f(u)\right]=\sum_{uv\in E}\frac{\ProbOp\left[{uv}\right]}{\sum\limits_{w\sim v}\ProbOp\left[{wv}\right]}f(u).

It is well known that
A
is diagonalizable and has eigenvalues

1=\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\ldots\geqslant\lambda_{|V|}\geqslant-1,

where the first ‘trivial’ eigenvalue corresponds to the space of constant functions. A graph is called a spectral expander if all non-trivial eigenvalues are small.

Definition 4.1

(Spectral expander)
.

A graph
G=(V,E)
is called a
\lambda
-one-sided spectral expander
if
\lambda_{2}\leqslant\lambda
. We say that
G
is a
\lambda
-two-sided spectral expander
if
\lambda_{2}\leqslant\lambda
and
\lambda_{|V|}\geqslant-\lambda
.

We record the following inequality for
\lambda
-two-sided spectral expanders, a variant of the classical
expander-mixing lemma
. For every
f,g\in\ell_{2}(V)
,

\left\lvert\langle f,Ag\rangle-\E[f]\E[g]\right\rvert\leqslant\lambda\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}.

(4.1)

4.1.1
Bipartite Graphs and Bipartite Expanders

A bipartite graph is a graph where the vertex set can be partitioned to two independent sets
V=L\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}R
, called sides. We sometimes denote such graphs by
G=(L,R,E)
.

The Bipartite Adjacency Operator

In a bipartite graph, we view each side as a separate probability space, where for any
v\in L
(resp.
R
),
\ProbOp\left[{v}\right]=\sum_{w\sim v}\ProbOp\left[{wv}\right]
. We can define the
bipartite adjacency operator
as the operator
B:\ell_{2}(L)\to\ell_{2}(R)
by

\forall f\in\ell_{2}(L),v\in R,\;Bf(v)=\E_{{w\sim v}}\left[f(u)\right]

where the expectation is taken with respect to the probability space
L
, conditioned on being adjacent to
v
.
There is a similar operator
B^{*}:\ell_{2}(R)\to\ell_{2}(L)
as the bipartite operator for the opposite side. As the notation suggests,
B^{*}
is adjoint to
B
with respect to the inner products of
\ell_{2}(L),\ell_{2}(R).

We denote by
\lambda(B)
the spectral norm of
B
when restricted to
\ell_{2}^{0}(L)=\{{\mathbf{1}}\}^{\bot}
, the orthogonal complement of the constant functions (according to the inner product the measure induces on
L
). Namely

\lambda(B)=\sup\left\{\langle Bf,g\rangle\left|\;\vphantom{\langle Bf,g\rangle\lVert g\rVert,\lVert f\rVert=1,f\bot{\mathbf{1}}_{L}}\right.\lVert g\rVert,\lVert f\rVert=1,f\bot{\mathbf{1}}_{L}\right\}.

Definition 4.2

(Bipartite Expander)
.

Let
G
be a bipartite graph, let
\lambda<1
. We say
G
is a
\lambda
-bipartite expander
, if

\lambda(B)\leqslant\lambda
.

It is easy to show that a bipartite graph is a
\lambda
-bipartite expander if and only if it is a
\lambda
-one-sided spectral expander. So we use these terms interchangeably on bipartite graphs.

We record the following inequality for
\lambda
-bipartite expanders similar to (
4.1
). For every
f\in\ell_{2}(L),g\in\ell_{2}(R)
,

\left\lvert\langle f,Ag\rangle-\E[f]\E[g]\right\rvert\leqslant\lambda\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}.

(4.2)

4.2
Reverse Hypercontractivity

Hypercontractivity and reverse hypercontractivity are powerful analytic inequalities from boolean function analysis that bound the contraction behavior of operators between normed spaces. Recall that for
p\neq 0
and a function
f
on a probability space, we denote by
\lVert f\rVert_{p}=\E[|f|^{p}]^{1/p}
(for
p\leqslant 0
this is only defined for
f
that are non-zero almost everywhere). We note that
p\mapsto\lVert f\rVert_{p}
is monotone increasing.

Let
V
be a finite probability space and let
\ell_{2}(V)=\{f:V\to\mathbb{R}\}
. A monotone operator is an operator such that for any non-negative function
f:V\to[0,\infty)
,
Af
is also non-negative (i.e. for any
x\in X
,
Af(x)\geqslant 0
). For example, adjacency operators of graphs are always monotone.

Let
A
be a monotone operator. Let
1\leqslant p<q
and let
C>0
.
A
is typically called
(p,q,C)
-hypercontractive
if for every
f:V\to\mathbb{R}
,

\lVert Af\rVert_{q}\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{p}.

It is well know that this is equivalent to ‘two-function hypercontractivity’, that for every
f,g:V\to\mathbb{R}

\langle Af,g\rangle\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{p}\lVert g\rVert_{q^{\prime}}

where
q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{q-1}
is
q
’s Hölder conjugate
[
ODo14
]
.
Reverse hypercontractivity
‘flips’ this inequality in multiple ways: both in the direction of the inequality, and in the relation of
p
and
q
.

Definition 4.3

(Reverse hypercontractivity)
.

Let
q<p<1
such that
p,q\neq 0
. Let
C>0
. Let
A
be a monotone operator. We say that
A
is
(p,q,C)
-reverse hypercontractive if for every
f:V\to\mathbb{R}^{\geqslant 0}
,

\lVert Af\rVert_{q}\geqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{p}

or equivalently if for every
f,g:V\to\mathbb{R}^{\geqslant 0}
it holds that

\left\lvert\langle Af,g\rangle\right\rvert\geqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{p}\lVert g\rVert_{q^{\prime}}

where
q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{q-1}
is
q
’s Hölder conjugate (the equivalence is similar to standard hypercontractivity).

It is sometimes convenient to substitute
f^{\prime}:=f^{p},g^{\prime}=g^{q^{\prime}}
and write the two-function reverse hypercontractivity inequality as

\left\lvert\langle Af^{\prime 1/p},g^{\prime 1/q^{\prime}}\rangle\right\rvert\geqslant\E[f^{\prime}]^{1/p}\E[g^{\prime}]^{1/q^{\prime}}.

Probably the simplest (and most useful) interpretation of this inequality is when
f
and
g
are indicators of sets
M,N\subseteq V
respectively, and that
A
is an adjacency operator of a graph (whose vertices are the points in
V
). Then this inequality has a combinatorial interpretation as a type of ‘mixing’ lemma. Namely that the probability a random edge hits
M
and
N
:

\ProbOp_{{uv\in E}}\left[u\in M,v\in N\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{M}\right]^{1/p}\ProbOp\left[{N}\right]^{1/q^{\prime}}.

This becomes a powerful tool in the regime where the sets are smaller than can be controlled by the expander-mixing lemma.

4.3
Simplicial Complexes

Simplicial complexes

A pure
d
-uniform simplicial complex
X
is a finite set system (hypergraph) consisting of an arbitrary collection of sets of size
d
together with all their subsets (individually called ‘faces’). The sets of size
i
in
X
are denoted by
X{\left(i\right)}
, and in particular, the vertices of
X
are denoted by
X{\left(1\right)}
(we do not distinguish between vertices and the singletons of vertices). We write
X^{\leqslant j}
to denote faces of
X
up to size
j
.

We note that this notation departs somewhat from the typical convention in the literature which denotes faces of size
i+1
by
X(i)
, and what we have called a
d
-uniform simplicial complex is more typically referred to as a
(d-1)
-dimensional
simplicial complex. We have chosen to adopt the former notation since most theorems in this work require a substantial amount of arithmetic on set sizes, and the latter convention quickly becomes overly cumbersome.

Probability over simplicial complexes

Let
X
be a simplicial complex and
\ProbOp_{d}:X{\left(d\right)}\to(0,1]
a density function on
X{\left(d\right)}
.
This density function induces densities on lower level faces
\ProbOp_{k}:X{\left(k\right)}\to(0,1]
by
\ProbOp_{k}(t)=\frac{1}{\binom{d}{k}}\sum_{s\in X{\left(d\right)},s\supset t}\ProbOp_{d}(s)
. Equivalently
\ProbOp_{k}
is the density induced by drawing a
d
-face from
s\sim\ProbOp_{d}
, and a uniformly random
k
-face
t\subset s
.

When clear from the context, we omit the level of faces and just write
\ProbOp[T]
or
\ProbOp_{{t\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[T\right]
for an event
T\subseteq X{\left(k\right)}
.

Links of faces

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex. Let
k<d
and
s\in X{\left(k\right)}
. The link of
s
(called a
k
-link) is a
(d-k)
-uniform simplicial complex defined by

X_{s}=\left\{t\setminus s\left|\;\vphantom{t\setminus st\in X,t\supseteq s}\right.t\in X,t\supseteq s\right\}
. Note the link of the emptyset is
X
itself
X_{\emptyset}=X
.

Let
s\in X{\left(k\right)}
for some
k\leqslant d
. The density function
\ProbOp_{d}
on
X
induces a density function
\ProbOp^{s}_{d-k}:X{\left(d-k\right)}\to(0,1]
on the link where

{\ProbOp}_{d-k}^{s}[{t}]=\frac{\ProbOp\left[{t\cup s}\right]}{\ProbOp\left[{s}\right]\binom{d}{k}}
.
We usually omit
s
in the probability, and for
T\subseteq X_{s}{\left(k\right)}
write
\ProbOp_{{t\in{X_{s}{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[T\right]
.

Connected Complexes

We call a complex connected if the graph underlying every link is connected.

Partite complexes

A
d
-partite simplicial complex is a
d
-uniform complex whose vertices can be partitioned into
d
disjoint sets (typically called “parts” or “colors”)

X{\left(0\right)}=X[1]\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}X[2]\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\ldots\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}X[d]

such that every
s\in X{\left(d\right)}
has exactly one vertex from each part.

Let
X
be a
d
-partite simplicial complex. The
color
of a face
t\in X{\left(k\right)}
is
col(t)=\left\{i\in[d]\left|\;\vphantom{i\in[d]t\cap X[i]\neq\emptyset}\right.t\cap X[i]\neq\emptyset\right\}
. Let
F\subseteq[d]
. We denote by
X[F]=\left\{s\in X\left|\;\vphantom{s\in Xcol(s)=F}\right.col(s)=F\right\}
the faces of
X
with color
F
. The projection of the
complex

X
onto
F
is the sub-simplicial complex
X^{F}=\left\{s\in X\left|\;\vphantom{s\in Xcol(s)\subseteq F}\right.col(s)\subseteq F\right\}
. When
X
is endowed with a density
\ProbOp_{d}
,
X^{F}
has a naturally induced density
\ProbOp^{F}
by sampling
\sigma\sim\ProbOp_{d}
, and outputting the projection
\sigma_{F}
. Quantitatively, the induced distribution can be written as

\mathbb{P}^{F}(s)=\sum_{t\in X{\left(d\right)},s\subseteq t}\ProbOp[t].

Finally, when
F=\{i\}
is a singleton we just write
X[i]
,
X^{i}
, and
x_{i}
for brevity.

Given a generic complex
X
, it will often be useful to consider
X
’s ‘partitification’
P=P(X)
, which simply includes every possible ordering of the faces of
X
as tuples. Formally, endow the faces of
X
with an arbitrary order and define

P{\left(k\right)}\coloneqq\left\{(s,\pi)\coloneqq\left\{(s_{\pi(1)},1),(s_{\pi(2)},2),\ldots,(s_{\pi(k+1)},k)\right\}\left|\;\vphantom{(s,\pi)\coloneqq\left\{(s_{\pi(1)},1),(s_{\pi(2)},2),\ldots,(s_{\pi(k+1)},k)\right\}s\in X{\left(k\right)},\pi\in S_{k}}\right.s\in X{\left(k\right)},\pi\in S_{k}\right\},

where
S_{k}
is the group of permutations on
k
-letters and the measure of a face
(s,\pi)
is inherited naturally as
\frac{1}{k!}\ProbOp_{{X}}\left[s\right]
. Note that
P(X)
does not depend on the choice of ordering, which is simply a notational convenience.

Degree of Simplicial Complexes

Degree is a critical parameter of simplicial complexes capturing the (local) ‘blow-up’ incurred by moving to higher uniform faces. We define the (max) degree of a complex with respect to
i
-faces as:

\deg^{i}(X)=\max_{v\in X{\left(1\right)}}|\{s\in X{\left(i\right)}:v\in s\}|

We write just
\deg(X)\coloneqq\deg^{d}(X)
to denote degree with respect to top level faces of
X
, and
\deg^{(i)}(v)
for the degree of a specific vertex. An infinite family of
d
-uniform simplicial complexes
\{X_{n}\}
is called
bounded-degree
if there exists a (dimension-dependent) constant
C
such that
\deg^{i}(X)\leqslant C
for all
i\leqslant d
.

Hitting Set

It will occasionally be useful for us to use a common variant of sampling on simplicial complexes called
hitting set
.
We call a complex
(\gamma,i)
-hitting if for any
A\subset X{\left(1\right)}
:

\ProbOp_{\sigma\in X{\left(i\right)}}[\sigma\subset A]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{i}+\gamma

In other words, if the probability
\sigma\in X{\left(i\right)}

hits

X{\left(1\right)}\setminus A
is at least
1-\mu(A)^{i}-\gamma
. If a complex is
(\gamma,i)
-hitting for all sizes
i
, we just call it
\gamma
-hitting.

4.4
Higher Order Random Walks

Simplicial complexes come equipped with several natural families of random walks generalizing the standard random walk on a graph. Toward this end, let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex and
\ell<k\leqslant d
we define the standard ‘averaging’ or ‘random walk’ operators that move up and down the complex:

Down and up operators

The
down operator

D_{k,\ell}:\ell_{2}(X{\left(k\right)})\to\ell_{2}(X{\left(\ell\right)})
is the bipartite operator of the containment graph of
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(\ell\right)})
, that is:

D_{k,\ell}f(s)=\E_{{t\supseteq s}}\left[f(t)\right].

The adjoint of
D_{k,\ell}
is the
up operator
,
U_{\ell,k}:\ell_{2}(X{\left(\ell\right)})\to\ell_{2}(X{\left(k\right)})
, given by:

U_{\ell,k}g(t)=\E_{{s\subseteq t}}\left[g(s)\right].

We also write
U_{k}\coloneqq U_{k-1,k}
and
D_{k}\coloneqq D_{k,k-1}
as shorthand throughout.

The composition of the up and down operators
U_{\ell,k}D_{k,\ell}
, called the “down-up” walk, is the normalized adjacency matrix of the graph whose vertices are
X{\left(k\right)}
and whose edge distribution is defined by sampling
t\sim\ProbOp_{\ell}
, then
s,s^{\prime}\sim\ProbOp_{k}
conditioned on containing
t
. We denote the corresponding (bipartite) graph by
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(\ell\right)})
. For brevity, we sometimes just write
UD_{k,\ell}
instead of
U_{\ell,k}D_{k,\ell}
.

Noise operator

The noise operator is one the most classical objects of study in Boolean function analysis. Given
\rho\in[0,1]
, the standard noise operator
T_{k,\rho}
operates on the hypercube
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
by re-sampling each bit uniformly with probability
1-\rho
.

The noise operator has a natural extension to simplicial complexes
[
Baf+22a
,
GLL22
]
. It is convenient to define
T_{k,\rho}:\ell_{2}(X{\left(k\right)})\to\ell_{2}(X{\left(k\right)})
by its action on a face
\sigma\in X{\left(k\right)}
by the following process:

1.

Sample
\ell\sim Bin(k,\rho)

2.

Sub-sample
\tau\subset\sigma
of size
\ell

3.

sample
\sigma^{\prime}\in X{\left(k\right)}
conditioned on containing
\tau
.

It is easy to check that when
X
is a product space,
6
6

6

One can always express a product space
\otimes_{i}\Omega_{i}
as a partite simplicial complex by defining each coordinate as a part, see
[
Baf+22a
]
for further details.
this exactly recovers the standard noise operator. We note it is also possible to write the noise operator as a convex combination of down-up walks:

T_{k,\rho}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}\rho^{\ell}(1-\rho)^{k-\ell}UD_{k,\ell}.

Swap walks

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex. Let
i,j
be so that
i+j\leqslant d
. The swap walk
S_{i,j}=S_{i,j}(X)
is the bipartite adjacency operator of the graph
(X{\left(i\right)},X{\left(j\right)},E)
. An edge
\{s_{i},s_{j}\}
is chosen in this graph by first selecting a face
t\in X{\left(i+j\right)}
, and then partitioning it to
t=s_{i}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s_{j}
where
s_{i}\in X{\left(i\right)}
and
s_{j}\in X{\left(j\right)}
uniformly at random. This walk was defined and studied independently by
[
AJT19
]
and by
[
DD19
]
.

Colored swap walks

The standard swap walks are not well behaved on partite complexes, but there is a useful analog for this setting defined in
[
DD19
]
. Let
X
be a
d
-uniform partite simplicial complex, and
F_{1},F_{2}\subseteq[d]
be two disjoint subsets. The colored swap walk
S_{F_{1},F_{2}}=S_{F_{1},F_{2}}(X)
is the bipartite adjacency operator of the graph
(X[F_{1}],X[F_{2}],E)
. An edge
\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
is chosen in this graph by first selecting a face
t\in X[F_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}F_{2}]
, and then partitioning it to
t=s_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s_{2}
according to its colors.

A useful observation is that the spectral expansion of swap walks (and color swap walks) is monotone in the following sense.

Observation 4.4
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex. Then for every
i
and
j^{\prime}<j
,
\lambda(S_{i,j^{\prime}})\leqslant\lambda(S_{i,j})
. The same holds for partite complexes. For every disjoint
F_{1},F_{2}
and
F_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq F_{2}
,
\lambda(S_{F_{1},F_{2}^{\prime}})\leqslant\lambda(S_{F_{1},F_{2}})
.

Proof.

The proof follows from the fact that one can factor

S_{i,j^{\prime}}(X)=D_{j,j^{\prime}}S_{i,j}(X)

and the observation that
D_{j,j^{\prime}}
contracts
2
-norms. A similar argument is true for colored swap walks in the partite case, replacing
D_{j,j^{\prime}}
with the corresponding bipartite graph between the
F_{2}
-colored faces and the
F_{2}^{\prime}
-colored faces they contain.
∎

4.5
Sampler Graphs

Sampler graphs
are bipartite graphs
G=(L,R,E)
where a random vertex
v\in L
“sees” any large enough set in
R
with approximately the correct probability. Here we discuss a few classical variants of samplers and their relations, and refer the reader to
[
Gol97
]
for further discussion.

Definition 4.5

(Multiplicative sampler)
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a bipartite graph and
\alpha,\beta,\delta>0
.
G
is an
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-multiplicative sampler
if for every set
A\subseteq R
of size
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant\alpha
it holds that

\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\sim v}}\left[u\in A\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>\delta\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right]\leqslant\beta.

Note that the definition of a sampler is not a priori symmetric,
L
and
R
have different roles. We will also study a related
additive
notion of samplers.

Definition 4.6

(Additive sampler)
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a bipartite graph and
\beta,\varepsilon>0
.
G
is an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-additive sampler
if for every set
A\subseteq R
it holds that

\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\sim v}}\left[u\in A\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\beta.

Finally, sometimes it is also useful to sample
functions
instead of sets. toward this we introduce the following definition in additive notation.
