Definition 4.7
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a bipartite graph and
\varepsilon,\beta>0
.
G
is a
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-
function additive sampler
if for every
f:R\to[0,1]
with expectation
\E_{{v\in R}}\left[f(v)\right]=\mu
, it holds that

\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\E_{{u\sim v}}\left[f(v)\right]-\mu\right\rvert>\varepsilon\right]<\beta,

and similarly for a
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-
function multiplicative sampler
.

4.5.1
Basic Sampler Properties

While the definition of samplers are not symmetric with respect to
L
and
R
, a near-tight correspondence
(L,R,E)
and
(R,L,E)
is given in
[
IKW12
, Lemma 2.5]
. We repeat their proof in
Appendix
C
in a more general setup.

Claim 4.8
.

Let
\beta,\delta>0
, let
\delta^{\prime}>\delta
and
\alpha<\frac{\min\{\delta,0.5\}}{1+\delta}
. Then for every
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-sampler
G=(L,R,E)
, it holds that
G_{op}\coloneqq(R,L,E)
is a
(\frac{1-\alpha(1+\delta)}{\alpha(\delta^{\prime}-\delta)}\beta,2\alpha,\delta^{\prime})
-sampler.

We also note that additive samplers and multiplicative are equivalent, at least in the weak sense as below. We prove this claim in
Appendix
C
.

Claim 4.9
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a bipartite graph.

1.

If
G
is a
(\beta,\delta)
-additive sampler then
G
is a
(C\delta,\beta,\frac{1}{C})
-multiplicative sampler for any
C>1
.

2.

If
G
is a
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-multiplicative sampler for
\alpha\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Then
G
is a
(\beta,\delta)
-additive sampler, where
\delta=\max\{\delta,(1+\delta)(\alpha+p)\}
and
p=\max_{v\in R}\ProbOp\left[{v}\right]
.

Under a slightly stronger assumption one can also remove the dependence on
\delta
in the second item.

Claim 4.10
.

Let
\beta,\alpha_{0}>0
. If for every
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
it holds that
G
is an
(\alpha,\beta,\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\sqrt{\alpha}})
-multiplicative sampler, then
G
is a
(\beta,2(\alpha+p))
-additive sampler where
p=\max_{v\in R}\ProbOp\left[{v}\right]
.

Below we show that every additive sampler is also a function additive sampler (albeit with worse parameters). We did not try to optimize parameters and it could be the case that a better reduction exists.

Claim 4.11
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-additive sampler such that every
r\in R
has degree at least
k
and every
v\in L
has probability at most
\frac{1}{k}
. Assume that
\exp(-0.01\varepsilon^{2}k))<\frac{1}{4}
. Then
G
is also a
(4\beta,2\varepsilon)
-function additive sampler.

We prove the claim in
Appendix
C
.
We note that
[
Gol97
]
also presents a reduction from an additive sampler to a function additive sampler. The reduction there changes the underlying graph, which is why we prove a reduction more suitable to our needs.

4.6
Concentration of Measure

Sampler graphs are a special case of the powerful concept of concentration of measure, a viewpoint we will also take throughout this work. We follow the standard notation of
[
BLM13
]
adapted in the natural way from product measures to simplicial complexes. Given a simplicial complex
X
and a function
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
, let
Z=f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
denote the random variable distributed as
f(x)
where
x\in X{\left(k\right)}
. We are interested in understanding the concentration of
Z
around its mean.
Z
is said to satisfy
subexponential
concentration if there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\ProbOp[|Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]|>t]\leqslant\exp(-ct),

and
subgaussian
concentration if

\ProbOp[|Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]|>t]\leqslant\exp(-ct^{2}).

Classical concentration bounds (e.g. on the cube, products) typically hold for functions
f
satisfying certain Lipschitz-type conditions. Toward this end, define the
Z
-dependent variable
Z^{\prime}_{(i)}=f(x_{1},\ldots z_{i},\ldots,x_{k})
where
z_{i}
is sampled conditional on
x_{-i}
.
7
7

7

Recall that even in the non-partite setting, we assign each
k
-face an arbitrary order, making
Z^{\prime}_{(i)}
well defined.
Abusing notation slightly, we will call a function
\nu
-Lipschitz if its squared difference with respect to the down-up walk is bounded:

Definition 4.12

(
\nu
-Lipschitz function)
.

Let
X
be a simplicial complex and
\nu>0
. We call a function
f:X{\left(d\right)}\to\mathbb{R}

\nu
-Lipschitz if with probability
1
:

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}(Z-Z^{\prime}_{(i)})_{+}^{2}\leqslant\nu,

where
(z)_{+}=\max\{z,0\}
.

We will occasionally rely on a stricter variant that requires the difference of
f
on any two neighboring
k
-faces be bounded.

Definition 4.13

(
\nu
-bounded difference)
.

We say a function
f:X{\left(d\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
has
\nu
-bounded difference if for every
s\in X{\left(d\right)}
and neighboring
s^{\prime}
of the down-up walk:

(f(s)-f(s^{\prime}))^{2}\leqslant\frac{\nu}{d}.

Most of our work focuses on a special case of functions with
\nu
-bounded difference we call
lifted
functions.

Definition 4.14

(Lifted functions)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex and
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
any function. For any
k<k^{\prime}\leqslant d
, the
k^{\prime}
-lift of
f
is the function:

U_{k,k^{\prime}}f(s)=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\subset s}[f(s^{\prime})]

We remark that lifted functions (sometimes called ‘degree’ or ‘level’-
k
functions) are fundamental objects in boolean analysis and heavily studied in the HDX literature
[
Dik+18
,
Baf+22
,
Gai+22
]
. As discussed in the introduction, inclusion sampling is in one-to-one correspondence with concentration bounds for lifted functions. More generally, bipartite sampling guarantees on
G=(L,R,E)
correspond to concentration for functions of the form
A_{G}f
, where
f:R\to[0,1]
and
A_{G}:\mathbb{R}^{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{L}
is the normalized bipartite adjacency matrix of
G
.

On simplicial complexes, we will typically be interested in understanding concentration at various levels of the complex. One frequently useful fact is that once concentration has been established for
k
-faces, it can typically be ‘raised’ or ‘lowered’ to functions on other levels via simple reductions to concentration of the complete complex. In cases, this can even be used to
improve
bounds on lower levels by bootstrapping from stronger bounds from larger set sizes. We record the following basic results to this effect here and use them throughout. Their proofs are given in
Appendix
C
.

Given integers
k\leqslant d
, a
d
-uniform simplicial complex
X
, and a function
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
, denote

\pi_{up}^{d,k,f}(t)=\max_{s\in X{\left(d\right)}}\left\{\underset{r\subset s}{\ProbOp}\left[f(r)-\underset{r\subset s}{\mathbb{E}}[f]>t\right]\right\}

and similarly

\pi_{low}^{d,k,f}(t)=\max_{s\in X{\left(d\right)}}\left\{\underset{r\subset s}{\ProbOp}\left[f(r)-\underset{r\subset s}{\mathbb{E}}[f]<-t\right]\right\}

to be the worst-case concentration of
f
restricted to the induced complete complex on
d
-faces of
X
.

Lemma 4.15

(Raising Concentration)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex,
k\leqslant d
, and
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to[0,1]
a function satisfying

1.

Upper Tail
:
\underset{X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}[f-\mathbb{E}[f]>t]\leqslant up(t)

2.

Lower Tail
:
\underset{X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}[f-\mathbb{E}[f]<-t]\leqslant low(t)
.

for some functions
up,low:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to[0,1]
. Then the
d
-lift
U_{k,d}f:X{\left(d\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies:

1.

Upper Tail
:
\underset{X{\left(d\right)}}{\ProbOp}[U_{k,d}f-\mathbb{E}[f]>t]\leqslant up(\frac{t}{2})(1-\pi_{low}^{d,k,f}(\frac{t}{2}))^{-1}

2.

Lower Tail
:
\underset{X{\left(d\right)}}{\ProbOp}[U_{k,d}f-\mathbb{E}[f]<-t]\leqslant low(\frac{t}{2})(1-\pi_{up}^{d,k,f}(\frac{t}{2}))^{-1}
.

We remark that since concentration in the complete complex is typically quite good, for most function classes of interest the latter term is close to
1
and very little is lost lifting the concentration to level
d
. We will also use a variant of this result for ‘lowering’ concentration bounds specified to Lipschitz functions.

Lemma 4.16

(Lowering Concentration)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex and
k\leqslant d
. Assume there exist functions
up(t,\nu)
and
low(t,\nu)
such that any
\nu
-Lipschitz
f:X{\left(d\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies:

1.

Upper Tail
:
\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]>t]\leqslant up(t,\nu)

2.

Lower Tail
:
\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]<-t]\leqslant low(t,\nu)
.

Then any function
f^{\prime}:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\nu
-bounded difference satisfies:

1.

Upper Tail
:
\ProbOp[f^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}]>t]\leqslant up(\frac{t}{2},\frac{k}{d}\nu)+e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{4\nu}}

2.

Lower Tail
:
\ProbOp[f^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}]<-t]\leqslant low(\frac{t}{2},\frac{k}{d}\nu)+e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{4\nu}}
.

4.7
High Dimensional Expanders

In this section we define the notions of high dimensional expansion used throughout this work and dicsuss their relation with high order random walks.

Local Spectral Expanders

The most standard notion of spectral high dimensional expansion is ‘local-spectral expansion’, due to
[
DK17
,
Opp18
]
.

Definition 4.17

(Local-spectral expander)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex. We say that
X
is a
\lambda
-one-sided (two sided) high dimensional expander if for every
i\leqslant d-2
and
s\in X{\left(i\right)}
, the graph underlying
X_{s}
is a
\lambda
-one-sided (two sided) spectral expander.

A key property of local-spectral expanders is that they imply the expansion of associated high order random walks
[
KM17
,
DK17
,
KO20
,
Dik+18
,
AL20
]
. We first state such a bound for the single and multi-step down-up walks (or more accurately their corresponding bipartite operator).

Theorem 4.18

(
[
AL20
]
)
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-one-sided
d
-uniform high dimensional expander. Then for every
\ell<k\leqslant d
it holds that
\lambda(D_{k,\ell})\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{k}}\cdot(1+\lambda)^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}.

In the special case of the single step operator, we will occasionally use the following refined bound.

Theorem 4.19

(
[
AL20
]
)
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda_{i}
-one-sided
d
-uniform high dimensional expander. Then for every
\ell<k\leqslant d
it holds that
\lambda(D_{k})\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{1}{k}\prod\limits_{i=0}^{k-2}(1-\lambda_{i})}

A critical observation of
[
AJT19
,
DD19
]
is that by removing the laziness inherent in the down-up walk, one can substantially improve expansion. We state an improvement of their bounds for these swap walks by Gur, Lifshitz, and Liu
[
GLL22
]
:
8
8

8

Formally this result is stated in
[
GLL22
]
for
\lambda
-products (defined below), but follows for
\lambda
-two-sided HDX since their partifications are
\lambda
-products.

Theorem 4.20

(
[
GLL22
]
)
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda
-two-sided high dimensional expander. Then

\lambda(S_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}})\leqslant\sqrt{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\lambda

Recently,
[
AP23
]
proved tighter quantitative bounds that are non-trivial for any underlying local-spectral expansion. We will not need such fine-grain control in this work.

In the partite case, the colored swap walks also have known expansion bounds assuming partite local-spectral expansion
[
DD19
,
GLL22
]
.

Theorem 4.21

(
[
DD19
]
)
.

Let
X
be a partite
\lambda
-one-sided high dimensional expander. Then

\lambda(S_{F_{1},F_{2}})\leqslant\left\lvert F_{1}\right\rvert\left\lvert F_{2}\right\rvert\lambda.

We will also need a bound on the spectrum of the averaging operators based on local-spectral expansion. This has been the subject of intense study
[
KM17
,
DK17
,
Dik+18
,
KO20
,
AL20
]
, with the best known bounds given by Alev and Lau
[
AL20
]
.

\lambda
-Products

Another useful notion of partite high dimensional expansion is to look directly at the expansion of the bipartite graphs between different components. We say that
X
is a
\lambda
-product (a term coined in
[
GLL22
]
), if for every face
s\in X
and every two sides
X_{s}[i],X_{s}[j]
, the bipartite graph between the two sides is a
\lambda
-one-sided spectral expander. Dikstein and Dinur showed any partite local-spectral expander is a
\lambda
-product.

Claim 4.22

(
[
DD19
]
)
.

Let
X
be a
k
-partite
\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda}
-one-sided local-spectral expander. Then
X
is also a
\lambda
-product.

\lambda
-Trickling-Down Complexes

Oppenheim’s trickling-down theorem
[
Opp18
]
shows that in any connected complex, expansion in the top links ‘trickles down’ to expansion at lower levels.

Theorem 4.23

(The Trickling-Down Theorem
[
Opp18
]
)
.

Let
X
be a connected
d
-uniform simplicial complex. Assume that for every
s\in X{\left(d-2\right)}
, it holds that all non-trivial eigenvalues of
X_{s}
are in
[-\tau,\lambda]
for some
\tau,\lambda\geqslant 0
. Then all non-trivial eigenvalues of
X
are in

\left[-\frac{\tau}{1+(d-2)\tau},\frac{\lambda}{1-(d-2)\lambda}\right].

As discussed in the introduction, the Trickling-Down Theorem introduces a ‘phase transition’ in the local-to-global behavior of HDX when co-dimension
2
links have expansion
\frac{1}{d-1}
. Past this point, expansion of the
1
-skeleton of
X
can be inferred from expansion of the links. Before it, no guarantee is possible. This threshold-type behavior naturally suggests studying following definition of complexes ‘approaching’ the TD-barrier.

Definition 4.24

(
\lambda
-Trickling-Down Complex)
.

We call a
d
-uniform simplicial complex
\lambda
-Trickling-Down (
\lambda
-TD) if it is connected, and all co-dimension 2 links have (one-sided) expansion
\frac{\lambda}{d-1}
.

Spectral Independence

Spectral independence is a closely related notion to local-spectral expansion introduced in the sampling literature
[
ALG21
]
. Here we give an equivalent version of the definition in terms of link expansion, and refer the reader to
[
ALG21
]
for the original definition and their equivalence.

Definition 4.25

(Spectral independence)
.

For
\eta>0
, a
d
-uniform complex
X
is called
\eta
-spectrally independent (
\eta
-SI) if the graph underlying every co-dimension
j
link is a (one-sided)
\frac{\eta}{j}
-expander.

4.8
Nice Complexes

In the following sections of the paper we prove results for various standard notions of high dimensional expansion including two sided high dimensional expanders, partite high dimensional expanders and
\sqrt{d}
-skeletons of one-sided high dimensional expanders. To compactify our statements, we bundle these notions into one definition henceforth dubbed ‘nice complexes’.

Definition 4.26

(Nice complex)
.

Let
c>0
. A
k
-uniform complex
X
is
c
-
nice
if it meets one of the following conditions:

1.

X
is a
2^{-ck}
-two-sided HDX.

2.

X
is a
2^{-ck}
-one-sided
k
-partite HDX (or a skeleton thereof)
9
9

9

A skeleton of a
2^{-ck}
-two-sided HDX is still a
2^{-ck}
-two-sided HDX, so this distinction is not meaningful in the previous condition.
.

3.

X
is the
k
-skeleton of a
d
-uniform complex
Y
with
\lambda(U_{d-1}D_{d})=1-\frac{c}{d}
and
d\geqslant 2k^{2}
.

We call
X

c
-locally nice
if every link of the complex is also
c
-nice.

Remark 4.27
.

We note that the two-sided condition of
Definition
4.26
could be relaxed to instead assuming that for some
\alpha\in[0,1]
,
X
is the
k
-skeleton of a
\frac{1}{d}2^{-cd^{\alpha}}
-two-sided
d
-uniform HDX for
d\geqslant k^{\frac{2}{1+\alpha}}
. We prove a Chernoff bound in this regime which we believe is of independent interest, but for simplicity focus just on the
\alpha=1
case for the rest of the paper.

Note that skeletons of nice complexes are nice (see
[
Lee23
, Theorem 3.5]
for the third case), and that if
X
is
c
-nice for one of the first two items, it is also
c
-locally nice. It is simple computation to verify that skeletons of
\lambda
-TD and
\eta
-SI complexes are locally nice.

Claim 4.28
.

Let
\lambda<1
and
\eta>0
. For any
d
-uniform complex
Y
:

1.

If
Y
is
\lambda
-TD, then its
\sqrt{(1-\lambda)d}
-skeleton is
e^{-1}
-locally nice.

2.

If
Y
is
\eta
-SI, then its
\sqrt{\frac{d}{\max\{2,\eta\}}}
-skeleton is
e^{-2}
-locally nice.

Proof.

We first prove niceness. The first item follows from observing by
Theorem
4.23
and
Theorem
4.19
that the down-up walk on the
k=(1-\lambda)d
skeleton of a
\lambda
-TD complex has
\lambda(U_{k-1}D_{k})=1-\frac{e^{-1}}{(1-\lambda)d}
.

For the second item, let
k^{\prime}=\frac{d}{\max\{2,\eta\}}
. By definition one can check the
k^{\prime}
-skeleton of
Y
is a
\min\{\frac{3}{d},\frac{\eta}{(1-1/\eta)d}\}
-one-sided local-spectral expander and
Theorem
4.19
implies
\lambda(U_{k^{\prime}-1}D_{k^{\prime}})\leqslant 1-\frac{e^{-2}}{k^{\prime}}
.

For local niceness, note that
\eta
-SI and
\lambda
-TD complexes have
\eta
-SI and
\lambda
-TD links respectively. Further, one can check directly that if
X
is a
k
-skeleton of
d
-uniform
Y
, then
X_{s}
is a
(k-|s|)
-skeleton of
Y_{s}
, which is
(d-|s|)
-uniform. The rate
\frac{d-|s|}{k-|s|}\geqslant\frac{d}{k}
thus we can apply the same proof of niceness to every link as well.
∎

5
Concentration on HDX

With ‘nice’ complexes so defined, we may finally state our main concentration bound in full formality.

Theorem 5.1

(Sampling on HDX)
.

Fix
c>0
. There are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
i<k\leqslant d
, and
d
-uniform
c
-nice complex
X
, the inclusion graph
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-function sampler for

1.

\beta=\frac{c_{1}}{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-c_{2}\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right)
in the non-partite cases

2.

\beta=\frac{c_{1}}{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-c_{2}\varepsilon^{8}\frac{k}{i}\right)
in the partite case

Moreover, if
X
is a
k
-skeleton of a
d
-uniform complex
Y
, then for every
k<k^{\prime}\leqslant d
, the graph
(Y{\left(k^{\prime}\right)},Y{\left(i\right)})
is also an
(\varepsilon,O(\beta))
-function sampler.

A few remarks are in order. First, in the
\lambda
-TD setting, it is worth noting the constant in the exponent can be taken to be
c_{2}=\Theta((1-\lambda)^{1/2})
. Second, we note that in the partite case, the optimal
\varepsilon^{2}
-dependence in the exponent can be recovered whenever
k\leqslant O(\varepsilon^{6}d)
(see
Section
7
for details).

Finally, by applying
Claim
4.8
and
Claim
4.9
, we also get the following useful corollary for multiplicative sampling of the reversed inclusion graph
(X{\left(i\right)},X{\left(k\right)})
.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
X
be a
k
-uniform
c
-
nice
complex.
Then for every
\delta,\beta\in(0,1)
,
(X{\left(i\right)},X{\left(k\right)})
is a
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-sampler for

1.

\alpha=\frac{c^{\prime}}{\beta\delta}e^{-\Omega_{c}(\beta^{2}\delta^{2}\frac{k}{i})}
in the non-partite cases

2.

\alpha=\frac{c^{\prime}}{\beta\delta}e^{-\Omega_{c}(\beta^{8}\delta^{8}\frac{k}{i})}
in the partite case.

We note that in application, we typically take
\beta
and
\delta
in the above to be constant, in which case the bounds are tight in both regimes.

In this section, we prove
Theorem
5.1
only for the two-sided case, and defer the full proof to
Section
7
. The proof (and section) are split into two main components: a new ‘Chernoff-Hoeffding’-style bound for simplicial complexes based on expansion of swap walks, and a bootstrapping technique that lifts this bound to full inclusion sampling on two-sided HDX.

5.1
Chernoff-Hoeffding

In this section we introduce a new technique to prove ‘
k
vs.
1
’ concentration on simplicial complexes
X
whose underlying strength depends on the expansion of the
swap walks
of
X
. The general form is fairly cumbersome (we refer the reader to
Proposition
5.5
, which bounds the moment generating function in terms of swap walk expansion), and for readability we only state the result here for
\frac{1}{k}2^{-k^{\alpha}}
-two-sided HDX, interpolating between
\frac{1}{2k}
-HDX at
\alpha=0
, and
2^{-\Omega(k)}
-HDX at
\alpha=1
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
X
be a
k
-uniform
\frac{1}{k}2^{-k^{\alpha}}
-two-sided local-spectral expander. Then for any
f:X{\left(1\right)}\to[0,1]
and
\varepsilon>0
:

1.

Upper tail:

\underset{\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}\left[\frac{1}{k}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})-\mu>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant 2\min\left\{k,c_{\alpha}\right\}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{12}\varepsilon^{2}k^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}\right)

2.

Lower tail:

\underset{\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}\left[\frac{1}{k}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{k}f(v_{i})-\mu<-\varepsilon\right]\leqslant 2\min\left\{k,c_{\alpha}\right\}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{12}\varepsilon^{2}k^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}\right)

where
c_{\alpha}\leqslant e^{\frac{(\frac{3}{\alpha})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}{e^{\alpha}}}
.

We remark it is possible to use this approach to prove similar concentration for any
\lambda
-TD complex, but in
Section
7.1
we will see a stronger bound for this specific regime based on a variant of the Herbst argument. As such we allow ourselves the additional constant factor here in expansion for convenience. Second, note that by setting
\alpha
appropriately,
Theorem
5.3
shows for any
c>0
that the containment graph
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(1\right)})
of any
2^{-ck}
-two-sided local spectral expanders is a
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler for
\beta=\exp(-\Omega_{c}(-\varepsilon^{2}k))
.

The core of
Theorem
5.3
centers around the notion of
splittability

[
AJT19
]
, which allows us to de-correlate the variables in our complex and bound the moment generating function of
U_{1,k}f
. We introduce a slight variant of the standard definition convenient for our purposes.

Definition 5.4

(Balanced Splittability)
.

We call a
d
-uniform complex
X

\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in[\lfloor\log(d)\rfloor-1]}
-balanced-splittable if for every
i
the
2^{i}
-th swap walk expands:

\lambda(S_{2^{i},2^{i}})\leqslant\gamma_{i}

All applications of splittability in the literature lose
additive
factors in the
\gamma_{i}
. We introduce a new approach combining splittability with an ‘internal’ reduction to the complete complex that allows finer control over this error and instead incurs more manageable
multiplicative
loss.

Proposition 5.5
.

For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\in[0,1]
, let
k=2^{j}
, and
X
be a
k
-uniform
\{\gamma_{i}\}
-balanced-splittable complex with
e^{\frac{5}{4}2^{-i}r^{2}k}\gamma_{j-1-i}<1
. Then for any
f:X{\left(1\right)}\to[0,1]
:

\underset{\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=0}^{k}e^{rf(v_{i})}\right]\leqslant c_{r,\gamma}\underset{X{\left(1\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[e^{rf}]^{k}

where
c_{r,\gamma}\leqslant\left(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}\left(\frac{1-\gamma_{j-1-i}}{1-e^{\frac{5}{4}2^{-i}r^{2}k}\gamma_{j-1-i}}\right)^{2^{i}}\right)
.

While the definition of
c_{r,\gamma}
may be somewhat hard to parse, note at least that for any fixed
r
,
c_{r,\gamma}
goes to
1
as
\gamma
tends to
0
.

Proof.

For any even
\ell
, denote by
z_{\ell}:X{\left(\ell\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
the ‘partial’ moment generating function:

z_{\ell}(\{v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}\})=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}e^{rf(v_{i})}.

Our goal is to bound
\E[z_{k}]=\mathbb{E}[\exp(r\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i}))]
by induction on
z_{\ell}
. Denote by
z_{\ell}^{0}=\E[z_{\ell}]{\mathbf{1}}
the projection of
z_{\ell}
to the space of constant functions and by
z_{\ell}^{\perp}=z_{\ell}-z_{\ell}^{0}
its orthogonal part. The first key observation is that we may split
z_{\ell}
into two ‘copies’ of
z_{\ell/2}
using the swap operator:

\E_{{X{\left(\ell\right)}}}\left[z_{\ell}\right]=\E_{{X{\left(\ell/2\right)}}}\left[z_{\ell/2}S_{\ell/2}z_{\ell/2}\right].

Now splitting
z_{\ell/2}
itself into parallel and perpendicular components, we can bound this as

\displaystyle\E_{{X{(\ell/2)}}}\left[z_{\ell/2}S_{\ell/2}z_{\ell/2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[{z_{\ell/2}S_{\ell/2}(z_{\ell/2}^{0}+z_{\ell/2}^{\perp})}]

\displaystyle=\E[z_{\ell/2}]^{2}+\langle z_{\ell/2}^{\perp},S_{\ell/2}z_{\ell/2}^{\perp}\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\E[z_{\ell/2}]^{2}+\gamma_{\log(\ell)-1}\lVert z_{\ell/2}^{\perp}\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma_{\log(\ell)-1})\E[z_{\ell/2}]^{2}+\gamma_{\log(\ell)-1}\lVert z_{\ell/2}\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(5.1)

The first of these two terms can clearly be bounded by induction. The difficulty lies in the error term, as the 2-norm of the partial MGF may be much larger than its expectation. To handle this, we argue via reduction to the complete complex the second moment of
z_{\ell/2}
is actually quite close to the original expectation of
z_{\ell}
.

Claim 5.6
.

For any even
\ell
and
r\in[0,1]
:

\lVert z_{\ell/2}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant e^{\frac{5}{4}r^{2}\ell}\mathbb{E}[z_{\ell}]

Let’s first complete the proof given this fact. Plugging the claim back into
(
5.1
)
and applying induction we get the following bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[z_{k}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1-\gamma_{j-1}}{1-e^{\frac{5}{4}r^{2}\ell}\gamma_{j-1}}\mathbb{E}[z_{k/2}]^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1-\gamma_{j-1}}{1-e^{\frac{5}{4}r^{2}k}\gamma_{j-1}}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{j-2}\left(\frac{1-\gamma_{j-2-i}}{1-e^{2^{-i}\frac{5}{4}r^{2}\frac{k}{2}}\gamma_{j-2-i}}\right)^{2^{i+1}}\right)\mathbb{E}[z_{1}]^{k}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}\left(\frac{1-\gamma_{j-1-i}}{1-e^{2^{-i}\frac{5}{4}r^{2}(k+1)}\gamma_{j-1-i}}\right)^{2^{i}}\right)\mathbb{E}[e^{rf}]^{k}

as desired.
∎

It is left to prove the claim relating
\lVert z_{\ell/2}\rVert_{2}^{2}
to
\mathbb{E}[z_{\ell}]
.

Proof of
Claim
5.6
.

Recall our goal is to show

\mathbb{E}[z_{\ell/2}^{2}]=\underset{t\in X{\left(\ell/2\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{v\in t}e^{2rf(v)}\right]\leqslant e^{\frac{5}{4}r^{2}\ell}\mathbb{E}[z_{\ell}]

The key is to observe that we can draw
t\in X{\left(\ell/2\right)}
by first drawing
s\in X{\left(\ell\right)}
, and then drawing
t\subset s
uniformly at random:

\displaystyle\underset{t\in X{\left(\ell/2\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{v\in t}e^{2rf(v)}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{s\in X{\left(\ell\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{t\subset s}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{v\in t}e^{2rf(v)}\right]\right]

The inner expectation is now within the complete complex which is negatively correlated, so may therefore ‘pull out’ the product (e.g. by
[
Hoe94
, Theorem 4]
) and write:

\underset{t\subset s}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{v\in t}e^{2rf(v)}\right]\leqslant\underset{v\in s}{\mathbb{E}}[e^{2rf(v)}]^{\ell/2}.

Let
\mu_{s}=\mathbb{E}_{v\in s}[f(v)]
and observe
\ell\mu_{s}=\sum_{v\in s}f(v)
. Combining the above, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[z_{\ell/2}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\underset{s\in X{\left(\ell\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{{v\in s}}{\mathbb{E}}[e^{2rf(v)}]^{\ell/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\underset{s\in X{\left(\ell\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{{v\in s}}{\mathbb{E}}[1+2rf(v)+\frac{5}{2}r^{2}f(v)]^{\ell/2}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{s\in X{\left(\ell\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(1+2r\mu_{s}+\frac{5}{2}r^{2}\mu_{s})^{\ell/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\underset{s\in X{\left(\ell\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[e^{(r+\frac{5}{4}r^{2})\ell\mu_{s}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{s\in X{\left(\ell\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod\limits_{v\in s}e^{(r+\frac{5}{4}r^{2})f(v)}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\frac{5}{4}r^{2}\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[z_{\ell}\right]

where in the second inequality we’ve used that
e^{x}<1+x+\frac{5}{4}x^{2}
for
x\in[0,2]
and that
f^{2}(v)\leqslant f(v)
.
∎

The proof of
Theorem
5.3
is now essentially immediate from Chernoff’s method.

Proof of
Theorem
5.3
.

We prove the upper tail. The lower tail follows from applying the upper tail to
1-f
. Let
k^{\prime}
be the largest power of
2
such that
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
. Fix
r=\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon k^{\prime\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}
, and recall by
Theorem
4.20
that
\gamma_{i}\leqslant\frac{2^{i}}{k^{\prime}}2^{-(k^{\prime})^{\alpha}}
. By
Proposition
5.5
, we therefore have:

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k^{\prime}}\}\in{X{\left(k^{\prime}\right)}}}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}f(v_{i})\geqslant\mu k^{\prime}+\varepsilon k^{\prime}\right]=

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k^{\prime}}\}\in{X{\left(k^{\prime}\right)}}}}\left[\exp\left(r\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})\right)\geqslant\exp\left(rk^{\prime}\mu+rk^{\prime}\varepsilon\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{Markov}{\leqslant}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}}e^{rf(v_{i})}\right]}{\exp(rk^{\prime}\mu+rk^{\prime}\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}\left(\frac{1}{1-2^{-i-1}e^{-\frac{1}{3}(k^{\prime})^{\alpha}}}\right)^{2^{i}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}(k^{\prime})^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}\right)

where in the final inequality we have applied
Proposition
5.5
and bounded
\mathbb{E}[e^{rf}]^{k^{\prime}}
by standard manipulations. To bound the leading coefficient first note that for any
\alpha\geqslant 0
we have

\prod\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}\left(\frac{1}{1-2^{-i-1}e^{-\frac{1}{3}(k^{\prime})^{\alpha}}}\right)^{2^{i}}=\prod\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}\left(\frac{1}{1-2^{-i-1}}\right)^{2^{i}}\leqslant k^{\prime}

since each individual term in the product is at most
\frac{1}{2}
and
j=\log_{2}(k^{\prime})
. For
\alpha>0
we may also write

\displaystyle\prod\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}\left(\frac{1}{1-2^{-i-1}e^{-\frac{1}{3}(k^{\prime})^{\alpha}}}\right)^{2^{i}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\prod\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{3}(k^{\prime})^{\alpha}}}{2}}\right)^{2^{i}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(1+e^{-\frac{1}{3}(k^{\prime}+1)^{\alpha}}\right)^{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\frac{k^{\prime}}{e^{\frac{1}{3}(k^{\prime})^{\alpha}}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\frac{(\frac{3}{\alpha})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}{e^{\alpha}}}

where the last inequality follows from observing
e^{\frac{k^{\prime}}{e^{\frac{1}{3}(k^{\prime})^{\alpha}}}}
is maximized at
k^{\prime}=(\frac{3}{\alpha})^{-\alpha}
.

Finally we apply
Lemma
4.15
to lift this guarantee to
X{\left(k\right)}
:

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{X{\left(k\right)}}[U_{1,k}f-\mu>\varepsilon]

\displaystyle\leqslant\min\{k^{\prime},c_{\alpha}\}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{12}(k^{\prime}+1)^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}\right)\left(1-\pi^{k,k^{\prime},U^{k^{\prime}}_{0}f}_{low}(\varepsilon/2)\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\min\{k,c_{\alpha}\}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{12}(k+1)^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}\right)\left(1-e^{-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}(k+1)}{6}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\min\{k,c_{\alpha}\}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{12}k^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}\right)

where in the final inequality we have assumed
\varepsilon^{2}\geqslant\frac{6}{k}
(else the stated bound is trivial).
∎

5.2
From Chernoff to Inclusion Sampling

We now show how to bootstrap Chernoff-type concentration to optimal sampling for the general inclusion graph
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
. We focus on the case of two-sided HDX, and give the more involved argument for the full theorem in
Section
7
. We restate the theorem in the two-sided case here for convenience.

Theorem 5.7

(Sampling on two-sided HDX)
.

For any
c>0
there exists
c^{\prime}>0
such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
i<k
, and
k
-uniform
2^{-ck}
-two-sided local spectral expander
X
,
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
sampler for

\beta=\frac{c^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\Omega_{c}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right).

Moreover, if
X
is a
k
-skeleton of a
d
-uniform complex
Y
, then for every
k<k^{\prime}\leqslant d
, the graph
(Y{\left(k^{\prime}\right)},Y{\left(i\right)})
is also an
(\varepsilon,O(\beta))
-function sampler.

The proof of
Theorem
5.7
is based on a refinement of an idea of
[
IKW12
]
, who observed on the complete complex one can prove an analogous bound by partitioning
k
-sets into
i
disjoint
\frac{k}{i}
-sets, and treating these as independent variables. In the world of general simplicial complexes, this type of subdivision is called the
faces complex
of
X

[
DD23a
]
.

Definition 5.8

(Faces Complex
[
DD23a
]
)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex. For any
i\leqslant d
, the faces complex
F_{X}^{(i)}
is the
\lfloor\frac{d}{i}\rfloor
-uniform complex with vertices
X{\left(i\right)}
and top level faces:

F^{(i)}_{X}{\left(\left\lfloor\frac{d}{i}\right\rfloor\right)}=\left\{\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{\lfloor\frac{d}{i}\rfloor}\}\penalty\ \Bigg|\penalty\ \bigcup_{j=1}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{i}\rfloor}s_{j}\in X,\penalty\ \bigcup_{j\neq\ell}s_{j}\cap s_{\ell}=\emptyset\right\}

The weight of a face is proportional to the weight of its union in
X
. We drop
X
from the notation when clear from context.

In our setting, subdivided faces have extreme dependencies so
[
IKW12
]
’s analysis for the complete complex fails. Nevertheless, it is in fact possible to generalize this approach to any simplicial complex through somewhat more careful analysis.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform complex and
i\leqslant k\leqslant d
be such that
\left(F^{(i)}{\left(\lfloor\frac{k}{i}\rfloor\right)},F^{(i)}{\left(1\right)}\right)
is an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-function additive sampler. Then
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is a
(2\varepsilon,\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\beta)
-function additive sampler.

The proof of
Proposition
5.9
itself centers around a lemma for general bipartite samplers stating that sampling over correlated sub-divisions is sufficient to imply sampling for the general graph as long as the components are appropriately marginally distributed.

Lemma 5.10
.

Given a graph
G=(L,R,E)
, parameters
\varepsilon,\beta>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
f:L\to[0,1]
, suppose there is a distribution
(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k},u)\sim D
over
L^{k}\times R
satisfying

1.

For every
i\in[k]
, the marginal
(v_{i},u)
is distributed according to the edges of
G
.

2.

\ProbOp_{{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k},u)\sim D}}\left[\frac{1}{k}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})>\mathbb{E}[f]+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\beta
.

Then

\ProbOp_{{u\sim R}}\left[\E_{{v\in L,v\sim u}}\left[f(v)\right]>\mathbb{E}[f]+2\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\frac{\beta}{\varepsilon}.

(5.2)

The same statement applies replacing
>
with
<
and
\varepsilon
with
-\varepsilon
inside the probabilities.

We defer the proof to the end of the subsection and first prove
Proposition
5.9
.

Proof of
Proposition
5.9
.

Let
m=\lfloor\frac{k}{i}\rfloor
and fix a function
f:X{\left(i\right)}\to[0,1]
with expectation
\mu=\E_{{s\in{X{\left(i\right)}}}}\left[f(v)\right]
. Consider the distribution
D
which samples
(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m},t)\subset X{\left(i\right)}^{m}\times X{\left(k\right)}
by drawing
\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
from the faces complex, and draws
t\supset\bigcup s_{i}
from
X{\left(k\right)}
. We claim
D
satisfies the requirements of
Lemma
5.10
, namely:

1.

\forall i\in[m]
,
(s_{i},t)
is marginally distributed according to the inclusion graph edges
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})

2.

\ProbOp_{\{s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m},t\}\sim D}\left[\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}f(s_{i})-\mu\right|>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\beta
.

The first fact follows from the definition of the faces complex, namely that marginally each
s_{i}
is distributed as
X{\left(i\right)}
, and
t
is drawn from
X{\left(k\right)}
conditional on containing
s_{i}
. The second fact is immediate from our assumption that the faces complex is an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler.

∎

We are now ready to prove the two-sided case of
Theorem
5.1
.

Proof of
Theorem
5.7
(
Theorem
5.1
two sided case).

By
Theorem
5.3
if the faces complex of
X
is a
2^{-ck}
-two-sided HDX, then it is an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler for
\beta\leqslant c_{1}e^{\Omega_{c}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\lfloor\frac{k}{i}\rfloor\right)}
and
c_{1}>0
some constant dependent only on
c
. Then by
Proposition
5.9
,
X
would be a
(2\varepsilon,\frac{\beta}{\varepsilon})
-sampler. The proof is completed by observing
2^{-\Omega(k)}
-local-spectral expansion of the faces complex is immediate from
Theorem
4.20
, since the graph underlying any link of the faces complex is a swap walk within (a link of)
X
. The ‘moreover’ statement follows directly from
Lemma
4.15
.
∎

It remains to prove
Lemma
5.10
.

Proof of
Lemma
5.10
.

Let
Z:R\to[0,1]
record the probability
u_{0}\in R

\varepsilon
-mis-samples
f
under
D
:

Z(u_{0})\coloneqq\ProbOp_{{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k},u)\sim D}}\left[\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})>\mu+\varepsilon\left|\;\vphantom{\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})>\mu+\varepsilon u=u_{0}}\right.u=u_{0}\right].

By assumption, the expected mis-sampling probability is small

\E_{{u_{0}}}\left[Z(u_{0})\right]=\ProbOp_{{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k},u)\sim D}}\left[\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})>\mu+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\beta,

so Markov’s inequality implies the following tail bound on
Z(u_{0})
:

\ProbOp_{{u_{0}\in R}}\left[Z(u_{0})>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\frac{\beta}{\varepsilon}.

We argue if
u_{0}
is such that
\E_{{v\in L,v\sim u_{0}}}\left[f(v_{i})\right]>\mu+2\varepsilon
then
Z(u_{0})>\varepsilon
, thus concluding that (
5.2
) holds.

The key is the simple observation that the (conditional) expectation
A
under
D
and
G
are equivalent:

\displaystyle\E_{{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k},u)\sim D}}\left[f(v_{i})\left|\;\vphantom{f(v_{i})u=u_{0}}\right.u=u_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\E_{{(v_{i},u)}}\left[f(v_{i})|u=u_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{{v\in L}}\left[f(v)\left|\;\vphantom{f(v)v\sim u_{0}}\right.v\sim u_{0}\right]

(5.3)

where the first equality is by linearity of expectation, and the second is by our assumption that
(v_{i},u)
is distributed as
G
. The result now follows roughly from arguing the random variable
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})
restricted to
u_{0}
, is non-trivially concentrated around its expectation
\E_{{v\in L}}\left[f(v)\left|\;\vphantom{f(v)v\sim u_{0}}\right.v\sim u_{0}\right]
, which is at least
2\varepsilon
-far from
\mu
.

In particular, fix
u_{0}
such that
\E_{{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k},u)\sim D}}\left[f(v_{i})\left|\;\vphantom{f(v_{i})u=u_{0}}\right.u=u_{0}\right]=\mu_{u_{0}}>\mu+2\varepsilon
. Then, we bound
Z(u_{0})
by its upper tail:

\displaystyle Z(u_{0})

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp_{{(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k},u)\sim D}}\left[\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})>\mu_{u_{0}}-\varepsilon\left|\;\vphantom{\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(v_{i})>\mu_{u_{0}}-\varepsilon u=u_{0}}\right.u=u_{0}\right].

Denote the right-hand side by
p
, and as
f(v)\in[0,1]
we have

\mu_{u_{0}}\leqslant p+(1-p)(\mu_{u_{0}}-\varepsilon).

Re-arranging gives

Z(u_{0})\geqslant p\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\mu_{u_{0}}-\varepsilon}\geqslant\varepsilon.

The last inequality follows from the the denominator being in
(0,1)
(
0<\mu_{u_{0}}-\varepsilon<1
because
\mu_{u_{0}}-\varepsilon\geqslant\mu+\varepsilon>0
and
\mu_{u_{0}}-\varepsilon<\mu_{u_{0}}\leqslant 1
).

Finally, we note the usual trick (changing
f
in the proof to
1-f
) gives the bounds for

\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[\E_{{v\sim u,v\in L}}\left[f(v)\right]<\mu-2\varepsilon\right].

∎

6
Reverse Hypercontractivity

In this section we recursively leverage optimal inclusion sampling to prove reverse hypercontractivity for high dimensional expanders.

Theorem 6.1

(Reverse Hypercontractivity)
.

Fix
c>0
,
\rho\in(0,1)
, and let
X
be a
k
-uniform
c
-locally nice complex for
k
sufficiently large. Then there exist constants
C,q
(dependent only on
c,\rho
) such that

\langle f,T_{\rho}g\rangle\geqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{q}\lVert g\rVert_{q}.

At its core, the proof of
Theorem
6.1
really only relies on
X
and its links satisfying optimal
i
vs
k
sampling. With this in mind, we will instead work with the following notion of a
Link up-sampler
that captures this core property:

Definition 6.2

(Link up-sampler (LUS))
.

Let
\tau\in(0,1)
and let
X
be a
k
-uniform simplicial complex. We say that
X
is a
\tau
-LUS if for every
i\leqslant k-2
, every
j\leqslant k-i
, and every
s\in X{\left(i\right)}
, it holds that
(X_{s}{\left(j\right)},X_{s}{\left(k-i\right)})
is a
(\frac{1}{\tau}\exp(-\tau\frac{k-i}{j}),0.1,0.2)
-multiplicative sampler.

We note the constants
0.1,0.2
are essentially arbitrary and fixed for convenience. The notion can also be further relaxed in a number of ways such as asking for sampling for
j
only up to a linear number of levels or only within links up to a certain level and the main results of this section would still hold.

We prove link-up samplers are reverse hypercontractive.
Theorem
6.1
is an immediate corollary.

Theorem 6.3
.

For every
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\tau>0
, there is some
\ell>1
and
C>0
such that the following holds for all sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
X
be
k
-uniform and
\tau
-LUS. Then
T_{\rho}
is
(\frac{1}{\ell},\frac{\ell}{1-\ell},C)
-reverse hypercontractive.

We split the proof of
Theorem
6.3
below into two parts, roughly corresponding to reverse hypercontractivity for boolean functions, and a reduction from the general to boolean case.

6.1
The Boolean Case

The main workhorse behind
Theorem
6.3
is to show that any linear-step
down-up walk
on an LUS is reverse hypercontractive for sets of size up to
\exp(-\Omega(k))
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
\tau>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
. There exist constants
c,q>0
such that the following holds for any sufficiently large
k
. Let
X
be a
k
-uniform
\tau
-LUS. Then for any sets
A,B\subseteq X{\left(k\right)}
of relative size at least
\exp\left(-ck\right)
and
\gamma^{\prime}\leqslant\gamma
it holds that

\ProbOp_{{t,t^{\prime}\sim UD_{k,\lfloor\gamma^{\prime}k\rfloor}}}\left[t\in A,t^{\prime}\in B\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{q}.

It will be convenient for us to assume at least one of the sets
A
and
B
is not too large. To this end, we first handle the case that both are large separately.

Claim 6.5
.

For every
p>0.5
there exists
q\geqslant 1
so that the following holds. Let
X
be a simplicial complex and
A,B\subseteq X{\left(k\right)}
subsets such that
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right],\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\geqslant p
. Then for any
\gamma^{\prime}\leqslant\gamma
:

\ProbOp_{{t,t^{\prime}\sim UD_{k,\lfloor\gamma^{\prime}k\rfloor}}}\left[t\in A,t^{\prime}\in B\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{q}.

The proof is largely un-enlightening computation, so we postpone it to the end of the subsection. Henceforth, assume without loss of generality that
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]
and has
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\leqslant 0.8
.

With this out of the way, we overview the main proof strategy. Assume for the moment that
\gamma^{\prime}k
is integral. As discussed in the introduction,
Theorem
6.4
relies on the following elementary observation: since the down-up walk samples an edge by first sampling
s\in X{\left(\gamma k\right)}
and then
independently
sampling
t,t^{\prime}\in X{\left(k\right)}
containing
s
, we can express the
\gamma^{\prime}
-correlated mass of
A
and
B
as:

\ProbOp_{{t,t^{\prime}\sim UD_{k,\gamma^{\prime}k}}}\left[t\in A,t^{\prime}\in B\right]=\E_{{s\in{X{\left(\gamma^{\prime}k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{t}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{At\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\cdot\ProbOp_{{t}}\left[B\left|\;\vphantom{Bt\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\right].

This motivates us to consider the set of
s\in X{\left(\gamma k\right)}
that
simultaneously
see a large enough fraction of
A,B
. Toward this end, for any integer
\ell_{0}\leqslant k
and real number
\delta>0
, let

G^{X}(\ell_{0},\delta)=\left\{s\in{X{\left(\ell_{0}\right)}}\left|\;\vphantom{s\in{X{\left(\ell_{0}\right)}}\ProbOp_{{t}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{At\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\geqslant\delta\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\;\hbox{and}\;\ProbOp_{{t}}\left[B\left|\;\vphantom{Bt\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\geqslant\delta\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]}\right.\ProbOp_{{t}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{At\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\geqslant\delta\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\;\hbox{and}\;\ProbOp_{{t}}\left[B\left|\;\vphantom{Bt\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\geqslant\delta\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\right\}

denote the set of elements in
X{\left(\ell_{0}\right)}
that see a noticeable fraction of both
A
and
B
.

Recalling
\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]
, to prove
Theorem
6.4
it suffices to show for any
\gamma^{\prime}\leqslant\gamma

\ProbOp\left[{G^{X}(\gamma^{\prime}k,\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{O(1)})}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{O(1)},

(6.1)

since (writing
G\coloneqq G^{X}(\gamma^{\prime}k,\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{O(1)})
) we then have

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{t,t^{\prime}\sim UD_{k,\gamma^{\prime}k}}}\left[t\in A,t^{\prime}\in B\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(\gamma^{\prime}k\right)}}}}\left[G\right]\cdot\ProbOp_{s\in X{\left(\gamma^{\prime}k\right)}}\left[\ProbOp_{t}[t\in A|s\in G]\ProbOp_{t}[t\in B|s\in G]\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp[A]^{O(1)}\ProbOp[B]

(6.2)

As such, our main goal is to prove (
6.1
). A key observation toward this end is that
G
satisfies certain useful ‘composition’ behavior: we can bound
G(\gamma^{\prime}k,\cdot)
by iteratively bounding
G(i,\cdot)
for smaller
i
within repeatedly deeper links of the complex. In particular, for sets
A
and
B
and any
r\in X
, define the restrictions of
A
and
B
to the link of
r
as

A_{r}\coloneqq\{t\in X_{r}:r\cup t\in A\}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad B_{r}\coloneqq\{t\in X_{r}:r\cup t\in B\}

and define the
local
subset
G^{X_{r}}(\ell_{0},\delta)
as the set of
s\in X_{r}{\left(\ell_{0}\right)}
that see a sufficiently fraction of
A_{r}
and
B_{r}
:

G^{X_{r}}(\ell_{0},\delta)=\left\{s\in{X_{r}{\left(\ell_{0}\right)}}\left|\;\vphantom{s\in{X_{r}{\left(\ell_{0}\right)}}\ProbOp_{{t\in X_{r}{\left(k-r\right)}}}\left[A_{r}\left|\;\vphantom{A_{r}t\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\geqslant\delta\ProbOp\left[{A_{r}}\right]\;\hbox{and}\;\ProbOp_{{t\in X_{r}{\left(k-r\right)}}}\left[B_{r}\left|\;\vphantom{B_{r}t\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\geqslant\delta\ProbOp\left[{B_{r}}\right]}\right.\ProbOp_{{t\in X_{r}{\left(k-r\right)}}}\left[A_{r}\left|\;\vphantom{A_{r}t\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\geqslant\delta\ProbOp\left[{A_{r}}\right]\;\hbox{and}\;\ProbOp_{{t\in X_{r}{\left(k-r\right)}}}\left[B_{r}\left|\;\vphantom{B_{r}t\supseteq s}\right.t\supseteq s\right]\geqslant\delta\ProbOp\left[{B_{r}}\right]\right\}.

G
and
G^{X_{r}}
compose in the following fashion:

Observation 6.6

(Composition of
G^{X}
)
.

Let
A,B\subset X{\left(k\right)}
,
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}>0
. If

1.

\ProbOp\left[{G^{X}(\ell_{1},\delta_{1})}\right]\geqslant\eta_{1}
.

2.

\forall r\in G^{X}(\ell_{1},\delta_{1})
:
\ProbOp_{{X_{r}{\left(\ell_{2}\right)}}}\left[G^{X_{r}}(\ell_{2},\delta_{2})\right]\geqslant\eta_{2}

Then

\ProbOp\left[{G^{X}(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},\delta_{1}\delta_{2})}\right]\geqslant\eta_{1}\eta_{2}.

Proof.

Sample
s\in X{\left(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\right)}
by sampling
r_{1}\in X{\left(\ell_{1}\right)}
and then
r_{2}\in X_{r}{\left(\ell_{0}\right)}
setting
s=r_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}r_{2}
. Fix
r_{1}\in G^{X}(\ell_{1},\delta_{1})
. Observe that if
r_{2}\in G^{X_{r_{1}}}(\ell_{2},\delta_{2})
then

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{t}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Ar_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}r_{2}\subseteq t}\right.r_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}r_{2}\subseteq t\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\delta_{2}\ProbOp_{{t}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Ar_{1}\subseteq t}\right.r_{1}\subseteq t\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right],

and similarly for
B
. Therefore in this case
s\in G^{X}(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},\delta_{1}\delta_{2})
. Hence

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{s}}\left[G^{X}(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},\delta_{1}\delta_{2})\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp_{{r_{1}}}\left[G^{X}(\ell_{1},\delta_{1})\right]\cdot\ProbOp_{{s=r_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}r_{2}}}\left[r_{2}\in G^{X_{r_{1}}}(\ell_{2},\delta_{2})\left|\;\vphantom{r_{2}\in G^{X_{r_{1}}}(\ell_{2},\delta_{2})r_{1}\in G^{X}(\ell_{1},\delta_{1})}\right.r_{1}\in G^{X}(\ell_{1},\delta_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\eta_{1}\eta_{2}.

∎

The proof strategy (modulo details), is then simple. To bound
G(\gamma^{\prime}k,\ProbOp[A]^{q_{0}})
, we divide
t\in X{\left(k\right)}
into pieces as
r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup r_{3}\ldots
, where each
r_{i}
is viewed as being drawn from the link of
r_{1}\cup\ldots\cup r_{i-1}
. The local
G
-set for each
r_{i}
can be lower bounded by the sampling properties of
X_{\cup_{j<i}r_{j}}
, and by carefully choosing the size of the
r_{i}
, we can ensure that composition gives the desired bound on
G
.

In practice, this approach hits some complications due to the fact that the localized restrictions of
A
and
B
shrink throughout the process. To handle this, we’ll instead use a ‘two-phase’ version of the above. First, we show that there exists some small absolute constant
\gamma_{0}
, potentially much smaller than the desired
\gamma
, for which the above approach does work. We call complexes satisfying such a bound lovely:

Definition 6.7

(Lovely complex)
.

We say that
X
is
(c,1-\gamma_{0},k,q)
-
lovely
, if for all
A,B\subset X{\left(k\right)}
with
\mathbb{E}[B]\geqslant\mathbb{E}[A]\geqslant\exp(-ck)

\ProbOp\left[{G^{X}(\gamma_{0}k,\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q})}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q}.

We first argue any LUS complex is
\gamma^{\prime}
-lovely for some
\gamma^{\prime}
depending only on
\tau
and
\gamma
.

Lemma 6.8
.

For every
\tau>0
and
\gamma<1
there exist
c,q
and
\gamma_{0}>0
so that following holds for large enough
k
. Let
X
be
\tau
-LUS, then for every
t\in X^{\leqslant\gamma k}
,
X_{t}
is
(c,1-\gamma^{\prime},k-|t|,q)
-lovely for any
\gamma^{\prime}\leqslant\gamma_{0}
.

Second, we give a similar argument showing that loveliness itself can be bootstrapped to higher levels.

Claim 6.9
.

Let
X
be a
k
-uniform simplicial complex. Let
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\geqslant 1
, and
c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\geqslant 0
. Assume that:

1.

X
is
(c^{\prime},1-\gamma_{1},k,q^{\prime})
-lovely.

2.

For every
r\in X(\gamma_{1}k)
,
X_{r}
is
(c^{\prime\prime},1-\gamma_{2},(1-\gamma_{1})k,q^{\prime\prime})
-lovely.

Then
X
is
(c,(1-\gamma_{1})(1-\gamma_{2}),k,q)
-lovely for
q=q^{\prime\prime}q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}+q^{\prime}
and
c=\min\{\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{(q^{\prime}+1)(1-\gamma_{1})},c^{\prime}\}
.

Combined with some definition chasing,
Lemma
6.8
and
Claim
6.9
immediately imply
Theorem
6.4
.

Proof of
Theorem
6.4
.

Recall that by (
6.2
) it suffices to show
X
is
(c,1-\gamma,k,q)
-lovely for some constants
c,q
depending only on
\gamma
and
\tau
. We first prove a slightly different statement by induction:

\forall\ell\in\left[\left\lceil\frac{\log(1-\gamma)}{\log(1-\gamma_{0})}\right\rceil\right]:\quad\text{$X$ is $(c_{\ell},(1-\gamma_{0})^{\ell},k,q_{\ell})$-lovely}.

for constants
c_{\ell},q_{\ell}
dependent only on
\tau,\gamma
. Note this implicitly assumes
(1-\gamma_{0})^{\ell}
is integer. This is unecessary and we discuss removing this assumption at the end of the subsection. Finally, observe that for
\ell=\left\lceil\frac{\log(1-\gamma)}{\log(1-\gamma_{0})}\right\rceil
we have
(1-\gamma_{0})^{\ell}\leqslant 1-\gamma
. We will argue a slight modification gives exactly
1-\gamma^{\prime\prime}
for any
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\gamma
after showing the above.

The base case
\ell=1
is immediate from
Lemma
6.8
. Assume the statement is true for some
\ell<\left\lceil\frac{\log(1-\gamma)}{\log(1-\gamma_{0})}\right\rceil
, then
X
is
(c(\ell),(1-\gamma_{0})^{\ell},k,q(\ell))
-lovely. Moreover, for
j=1-(1-\gamma_{0})^{\ell}\leqslant\gamma k
,
Lemma
6.8
implies every
j
-link
X_{t}
is
(c^{\prime\prime},1-\gamma^{\prime},k-j,q^{\prime\prime})
-lovely for any
\gamma^{\prime}\leqslant\gamma_{0}
.
Thus by
Claim
6.9
, we have that
X
is
(c_{\ell+1},1-(1-\gamma_{0})^{\ell+1},k-|t|,q_{\ell+1})
-lovely where
c_{\ell+1}
and
q_{\ell+1}
are functions of
\gamma_{0}
,
c(\ell)
,
q(\ell)
,
c^{\prime\prime}
, and
q^{\prime\prime}
as described in
Claim
6.9
. Since
\gamma_{0}
,
c^{\prime\prime}
, and
q^{\prime\prime}
are themselves functions only of
\tau
and
\gamma
, this gives the desired bound.

In order to prove
X
is
(c,1-\gamma,k,q)
-lovely, simply observe that in the final step of this induction we may choose
\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant\gamma_{0}
such that
(1-\gamma_{0})^{\ell}(1-\gamma_{0}^{\prime})=1-\gamma
. Exactly the same argument as above then gives
X
is at least
(c_{\ell},1-\gamma,k,q_{\ell})
-lovely for
\ell=\left\lceil\frac{\log(1-\gamma)}{\log(1-\gamma_{0})}\right\rceil
. The same strategy can be used to achieve the same (or better) constants for any
\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
as well.
∎

It remains to prove
Lemma
6.8
and
Claim
6.9
. While
Lemma
6.8
is the central component, it is instructive to first prove the simpler
Claim
6.9
which is in some sense a ‘one-step’ variant of the former.

Proof of
Claim
6.9
.

Let
A,B
be sets of size at least
\exp(-ck)
. Our goal can be rephrased as showing

G^{X}((\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}(1-\gamma_{1}))k,\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q^{\prime\prime}q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}+q^{\prime}})\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q^{\prime\prime}q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}+q^{\prime}}

since
1-(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}(1-\gamma_{1}))=(1-\gamma_{1})(1-\gamma_{2})
. We prove this via
Observation
6.6
. In particular since
c^{\prime}<c
,
(c^{\prime},1-\gamma_{1},k,q^{\prime})
-loveliness of
X
implies

G^{X}(\gamma_{1}k,\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q^{\prime}})\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q^{\prime}}.

Moreover, for every
r\in G^{X}(\gamma_{1}k,\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q^{\prime}})
we have that
\ProbOp_{X_{r}}[A],\ProbOp_{X_{r}}[B]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q^{\prime}+1}\geqslant\exp(-c^{\prime\prime}(1-\gamma_{1})k)
. Therefore by
(c^{\prime\prime},1-\gamma_{2},(1-\gamma_{1})k,q^{\prime\prime})
-loveliness of
X_{r}
it holds that

G^{X_{t_{1}}}(\gamma_{2}(1-\gamma_{1})k,\ProbOp_{{X_{r}}}\left[A\right]^{q^{\prime\prime}})\geqslant\ProbOp_{{X_{r}}}\left[A\right]^{q^{\prime\prime}}\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{(q^{\prime}+1)q^{\prime\prime}}.

Combining the two bounds and applying
Observation
6.6
we then have

G^{X}(\gamma_{1}k+\gamma_{2}(1-\gamma_{1})k,\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q^{\prime\prime}q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}+q^{\prime}})\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q^{\prime\prime}q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}+q^{\prime}}

as desired.
∎

Finally, we end with the proof of
Lemma
6.8
. Here, instead of decomposing
s=r\cup r^{\prime}
, we will need to more carefully decompose
s=r_{1}\cup r_{2}\cup r_{3}\dots
and control in every step how much
A
and
B
shrink using the link-up-sampling property.

Proof of
Lemma
6.8
.

We start with a few simplifying assumptions. First, recall that by
Claim
6.5
we may assume that
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\leqslant 0.8
. Second, note it is sufficient to prove the theorem just for
X
. The result then follows for all
\gamma k
-links
X_{t}
since the link of a
\tau
-LUS complex is itself
\tau
-LUS, and the ambient dimension of each link is at least
(1-\gamma)k
which we take to be still sufficiently large. Finally we introduce one notational simplification. Recall in the definition of a
\tau
-LUS that for any
i
-face
s
the graph
(X_{s}{\left(j\right)},X_{s}{\left(k-i\right)})
is a
(\frac{1}{\tau}\exp(-\tau\frac{k-i}{j}),0.1,0.2)
-multiplicative sampler. In the proof of this lemma we will only consider links of faces
s
with size
i\leqslant\gamma_{0}k
for some sufficiently small
\gamma_{0}
, so these are always
(\frac{1}{\tau}\exp(-\tau(1-\gamma_{0})\frac{k}{j}),0.1,0.2)
-multiplicative samplers. Moreover, we will also always ensure
j\leqslant\gamma_{0}k
, allowing us to subsume the constant
\frac{1}{\tau}
into the exponent. In particular, as long as
\gamma_{0}\leqslant\frac{\tau}{3\ln\frac{1}{\tau}}
, we may assume all (examined) links in the proof are
(\exp(-\tau^{\prime}\frac{k}{j}),0.1,0.2)
-multiplicative samplers where
\tau^{\prime}=\frac{\tau}{3}
.

Fix
\gamma_{0}=\min\{\frac{\tau}{3\ln\frac{1}{\tau}},\gamma\}
, let
c=\gamma_{0}\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{3}
, and assume
\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant\exp(-ck)
. Fix
m=\frac{-\log\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]}{c}
, and define the ‘step size’
\ell=\frac{k}{m}
. Observe that
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant\gamma_{0}k
. Assume for the moment that
\ell
is also integer (see
Remark
6.11
). We record the following immediate observations:

Observation 6.10
.

1.

\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\exp(-cm)
.

2.

0.8^{m}=\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q_{0}}
where
q_{0}=-c^{-1}\log 0.8
is a constant.

3.

For every
i\leqslant\gamma_{0}m
:

0.8^{i}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant\exp(-\tau^{\prime}m)=\exp(-\tau^{\prime}\frac{k}{\ell}).

The first two items are by definition. The third is satisfied for any
\gamma_{0},c
such that
c(1+\gamma_{0}q_{0})\leqslant\tau^{\prime}
. One can check that taking
\gamma_{0}\leqslant\min\{\gamma,\frac{\tau}{3\ln\frac{1}{\tau}}\}
suffices. Finally, assume for simplicity
k
is such that
\gamma_{0}k\in\mathbb{N}
.

We now move to the main argument. Let
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,j_{fin}=\lceil\gamma_{0}m\rceil\}
and for every
j\leqslant\lfloor\gamma_{0}m\rfloor
define

G_{j}\coloneqq G^{X}(j\ell,(0.8)^{j})

as above. If
j_{fin}>\gamma_{0}m
, additionally define

G_{j_{fin}}\coloneqq G^{X}(\gamma_{0}k,0.8^{j_{fin}})\subseteq G^{X}(\gamma_{0}k,\ProbOp\left[A\right]^{\gamma_{0}q_{0}+1}).

where in the last step we’ve used the assumption that
\ProbOp[A]\leqslant 0.8
. We will show

\ProbOp\left[{G_{j_{fin}}}\right]\geqslant\left(0.8\right)^{j_{fin}}\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{\gamma_{0}q_{0}+1}.

(6.3)

Setting
q=\gamma_{0}q_{0}+1
proves the lemma.
We prove (
6.3
) by inductively showing that for every
j=1,2,\ldots,j_{fin}
,

\ProbOp\left[{G_{j}}\right]\geqslant\left(0.8\right)^{j}.

We start with the base case
j=1
, which already contains the main idea. Given
S\subset X{\left(k\right)}
, define

T(S)\coloneqq\left\{r\in X{\left(\ell\right)}:\ProbOp\left[S\left|\;\vphantom{Sr}\right.r\right]<0.8\ProbOp\left[{S}\right]\right\}

to be the set of ‘terrible’
\ell
-sets which see too little of
S
. Observe that by construction

G_{1}=X{\left(\ell\right)}\setminus(T(A)\cup T(B)).

Since
\ell\leqslant\gamma_{0}k
, we have (
X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(\ell\right)})
is a
(\exp(-\tau^{\prime}\frac{k}{\ell}),0.1,0.2)
-sampler by our earlier discussion. On the other hand, by
Observation
6.10
, we can bound the sizes of
A
and
B
as:

\ProbOp[B]\geqslant\ProbOp[A]=\exp(-cm)\geqslant\exp(-\tau^{\prime}\frac{k}{\ell}).

Thus applying sampling we can bound
\ProbOp\left[{G_{1}}\right]
by

\ProbOp\left[{G_{1}}\right]\geqslant 1-\ProbOp\left[{T(A)}\right]-\ProbOp\left[{T(B)}\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot 0.1=0.8

as desired.

The inductive step is similar to the base case, only performed
locally
within the links of
X
together with
Observation
6.6
.
By induction hypothesis
\ProbOp\left[{G_{i}}\right]\geqslant 0.8^{i}
. For every
s\in G_{i}
, by
Observation
6.10

\ProbOp_{{X_{s}}}\left[B\right],\ProbOp_{{X_{s}}}\left[A\right]\geqslant(0.8)^{i}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant\exp(-\tau^{\prime}m)\geqslant\exp(-\tau^{\prime}\frac{k}{\ell}).

Thus by the same argument above for
X_{s}
and
A_{s}=\left\{t\setminus s\left|\;\vphantom{t\setminus ss\subseteq t\in A}\right.s\subseteq t\in A\right\},B_{s}=\left\{t\setminus s\left|\;\vphantom{t\setminus ss\subseteq t\in B}\right.s\subseteq t\in B\right\}
, we have

\ProbOp\left[{G^{X_{s}}(\ell,0.8)\penalty\ \Bigg|\penalty\ s\in G_{i}}\right]\geqslant 1-\ProbOp\left[{T(A)}\right]-\ProbOp\left[{T(B)}\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot 0.1=0.8.

The inequality
\ProbOp\left[{G_{i+1}}\right]\geqslant 0.8^{i+1}
then follows by
Observation
6.6
.

Finally, we note that for the last step, going between
j_{fin}-1
to
j_{fin}
(in the case that
j_{fin}>\gamma m
), we follow the same procedure except that we may need to sample less than
\ell
new vertices. This only improves the sampling so the same analysis as above holds.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

Throughout the proof we have ‘cheated’ in the typical way assuming a priori that various parameters (
\ell
,
\gamma k
,
\gamma_{0}k
, and
(1-\gamma_{0})^{i}(1-\gamma^{\prime})k
) are integer. Note that we did not assume above
\gamma_{0}m
was integer because of the careful interplay between
m
and
\ProbOp[A]
. In all other cases, however, these assumptions are benign and taking the appropriate integer floor does not substantially change the proof. The only modification is that every ‘step’ in the process (e.g., drawing an
\lfloor\ell\rfloor
-size set) potentially moves one fewer level up the complex. In the worst case, this results in taking a constant multiplicative factor more steps than under integer assumptions, resulting in additional constant factors in
c
and
q_{0}
. We omit the details.

Proof of
Claim
6.5
.

Denote by
E(X,Y)
the set of directed edges
(t,t^{\prime})
such that
t\in X,t^{\prime}\in Y
. It is easy to see that
\ProbOp\left[{E(A,B)}\right]+\ProbOp\left[{E(A,B^{c})}\right]=\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]
. Similarly we have that
\ProbOp\left[{E(A,B^{c})}\right]+\ProbOp\left[{E(A^{c},B^{c})}\right]=\ProbOp\left[{B^{c}}\right]
so
\ProbOp\left[{B^{c}}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{E(A^{c},B^{c})}\right]
. combining these together gives that
\ProbOp\left[{E(A,B)}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]-\ProbOp\left[{B^{c}}\right]
. Assume that
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]
, this implies that
\ProbOp\left[{E(A,B)}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A^{c}}\right]=2\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]-1
.
Hence it is sufficient to show that given
A
such that
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]>p
, there exists a constant
q>0
such that
2\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]-1\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q}
(which in turn is at least
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{q}
). Indeed let us consider
f(x)=2x-1-x^{q}
and show this function is non-negative in the range
[p,1]
for large enough
q
.

Assume without loss of generality that
q
is an integer. In this case
f(x)=(1-x)(\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}x^{j}-1)
. This function is greater than
0
in
[p,1]
if and only if
\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}x^{j}\geqslant 1
in this range. In the range
[p,0.9]
the series
\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}x^{j}
converges uniformly to
\frac{x}{1-x}
which is always greater or equal
\frac{p}{1-p}>1
. In the range
x\in[0.9,1]
we can take any
q\geqslant 3
and one can verify that
\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}x^{j}>0.9+0.9^{2}>1
.
∎

6.2
The General Case

We now prove
Theorem
6.3
using our restricted reverse hypercontractive inequality for indicators. This is done via the following reduction from reverse hypercontractivity for sets to the general case.

Theorem 6.12
.

For every
\ell\geqslant 1
and
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\kappa^{\prime}\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{5}\right)^{2\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{18+\frac{12}{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2(\ell-1)}
such that the following holds. Let
V
be a finite probability space and
D
a monotone linear operator such that for every
A,B\subseteq V
,

\langle 1_{A},D1_{B}\rangle\geqslant\kappa\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{\ell}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{\ell}.

(6.4)

Then for every two
arbitrary
functions
f_{1},f_{2}:V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
it holds that

\langle f_{1},Df_{2}\rangle\geqslant\kappa\kappa^{\prime}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{\frac{1}{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}}\lVert f_{2}\rVert_{\frac{1}{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}}.

(6.5)

That is,
D
is
(\frac{1}{\ell(1+\varepsilon)},\frac{1}{1-\ell(1+\varepsilon)},\kappa\kappa^{\prime})
-reverse hypercontractive.

The proof involves careful discretization and thresholding of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
into level sets in a way that largely maintains the functions’ moments. We defer the details to
Appendix
E
and prove the main Theorem assuming this result.

Proof of
Theorem
6.3
.

By
Theorem
6.12
it is enough that we show that there exist
\ell>1,\kappa>0
such that for every
A,B\subseteq X{\left(k\right)}

\langle 1_{A},T_{\rho}1_{B}\rangle\geqslant\kappa\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{\ell}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{\ell}.

Since
\gamma>\rho
, observe we can always take
k
sufficiently large so that
\ProbOp_{{Y\sim Bin(\rho,k)}}\left[Y\leqslant\lfloor\gamma k\rfloor\right]\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Let
q,c
be the constants promised by
Theorem
6.4
with respect to the
UD_{\lfloor\gamma k\rfloor,k}
. By monotonicity of
q
and
c
, these apply to all walks of intersection at most
\lfloor\gamma k\rfloor
). Thus if both
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right],\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\geqslant\exp(-ck)
then by
Theorem
6.4
:

\ProbOp_{{t_{1},t_{2}\sim T_{\rho}}}\left[t_{1}\in A,t_{2}\in B\right]\geqslant\ProbOp_{{Y\sim Bin(\rho,k)}}\left[Y\leqslant\gamma k\right]\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{q}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{q}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{q}.

Otherwise, it holds that
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\leqslant\exp(-ck)
. On the other hand, the probability of resampling
all vertices
in the noise operation is
(1-\rho)^{k}=\exp(-ck)^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}
for some constant
\ell^{\prime\prime}>1
that depends on
c
and on
\rho
. In particular,

\ProbOp_{{t_{1},t_{2}\sim T_{\rho}}}\left[t_{1}\in A,t_{2}\in B\right]\geqslant(1-\rho)^{k}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{\ell^{\prime\prime}+1}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{\ell^{\prime\prime}+1}.

Taking
\ell=\max\{q,\ell^{\prime\prime}+1\}
completes the proof.
∎

6.3
Proof of
Theorem
6.1

Finally, we briefly give the formal proof for reverse hypercontractivity on high dimensional expanders. This follows from the fact that any
c
-locally nice complex is
\tau(c)
-LUS for some appropriate setting of constants.

Corollary 6.13
.

For every
c<1
there exists a constant
\tau>0
such that any
c
-locally nice complex is
\tau
-LUS.

Proof.

By definition every
i
-link
X_{s}
is a
c
-nice complex, so by
Corollary
5.2
every
(X_{s}{\left(j\right)},X_{s}{\left(k-i\right)})
is a
(\frac{1}{c_{1}}\exp(-c_{2}\frac{k-i}{j})),0.1,0.2)
-sampler for some constants
c_{1},c_{2}
dependent only on
c
. Taking
\tau=\min\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
then suffices.
∎

Proof of
Theorem
6.1
.

The proof is immediate from
Corollary
6.13
and
Theorem
6.3
.
∎

7
Concentration for All Nice HDX

In this section we study concentration of measure for partite complexes and complexes whose down-up walk have an
\Omega(\frac{1}{d})
-spectral gap, completing the proof of
Theorem
5.1
. Along the way, we’ll show that complexes near the TD barrier satisfy exponential concentration for Lipschitz functions.

7.1
Concentration from Spectral Gap

In this section, we prove concentration inequalities for simplicial complexes under the (typically) weaker quantitative assumption that the down-up walk has a good spectral gap. The bounds so derived are incomparable to the techniques in
Section
5
for two-sided HDX: they hold for a broader family of complexes (and functions), but imply at best exponential (rather than subgaussian) concentration at the top level. We derive subgaussian bounds in this setting by moving to an appropriate skeleton of
X
.

Before stating our results, we briefly recall some notation from
Section
4.6
. Given a function
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
, let
Z=f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
and
Z^{\prime}_{(i)}=f(x_{1},\ldots z_{i},\ldots,x_{k})
where
z_{i}
is sampled conditional on
x_{-i}
. We call
f

\nu
-Lipschitz if with probability
1
over
Z,Z^{\prime}

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}(Z-Z^{\prime}_{(i)})_{+}^{2}\leqslant\nu

and say it has
\nu
-bounded difference if for all adjacent
(s,s^{\prime})
in the down-up walk:

(f(s)-f(s^{\prime}))^{2}\leqslant\frac{\nu}{k}

We prove that
\nu
-Lipschitz functions on HDX satisfy exponential concentration of measure.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex such that
\lambda_{2}(U_{d-1}D_{d})\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{Cd}
for some
C>0
. Then for any
0\leqslant k\leqslant d
and
\nu
-Lipschitz function
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
:

1.

Upper Tail:
\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]\geqslant t]\leqslant 2e^{-t/\sqrt{(C+1)\nu}}

2.

Lower Tail:
\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]\leqslant-t]\leqslant 2e^{-t/\sqrt{(C+1)\nu}}

Note that the spectral gap case of
Theorem
5.1
follows as an almost immediate corollary.

Proof of
Theorem
5.1
, spectral gap case.

Note the ‘moreover’ part follows from
Lemma
4.15
. Let
Y
be the promised
d
-uniform complex such that
X
is an (at most)
\sqrt{\frac{d}{2}}
-uniform skeleton of
Y
such that
Y
’s down-up walk has spectral gap
\lambda(U_{d-1}D_{d})\leqslant 1-\frac{c}{d}
. For any
g:X{\left(i\right)}\to[0,1]

Theorem
7.1
implies
U_{i,d}g
has the following exponential tail:

\ProbOp[U_{i,d}g(s)-\mathbb{E}[g]\geqslant\varepsilon]\leqslant 2e^{-\Omega_{c}(\varepsilon\frac{\sqrt{d}}{i})}\leqslant 2e^{-\Omega_{c}(\varepsilon\frac{k}{i})}

since
U_{i,d}g
has
\frac{i^{2}}{d}
-bounded difference. By
Lemma
4.16
,
U_{i,k}g
therefore has tail:

\ProbOp[U_{i,k}g(s)-\mathbb{E}[g]\geqslant\varepsilon]\leqslant 2e^{-\Omega_{c}(\varepsilon\frac{k}{i})}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon}e^{-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i})},

where the latter term is from the following concentration bound on the complete complex:

Claim 7.2
.

For any
i\leqslant k\leqslant n
and
g:\Delta_{n}{\left(i\right)}\to[0,1]
:

\ProbOp_{\Delta_{n}(k)}[U_{i,k}g-\mathbb{E}[g]>\varepsilon]\leqslant\frac{2}{\varepsilon}e^{-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i})}

and likewise for the lower tail.

We prove this claim in
Appendix
A
via a simple application of
Lemma
5.10
. The lower tail follows similarly.
∎

We also get the following corollaries for TD and SI complexes potentially of independent interest.

Corollary 7.3

(Concentration up to the TD Barrier)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform
\lambda
-TD complex for
\lambda<1
. Then for any
0\leqslant k\leqslant d
and
\nu
-Lipschitz function
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}

1.

Upper tail:

\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]\geqslant t]\leqslant 2e^{-\frac{t}{\sqrt{c_{\lambda}\nu}}}

2.

Lower tail:

\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]\leqslant-t]\leqslant 2e^{-\frac{t}{\sqrt{c_{\lambda}\nu}}}

where
c_{\lambda}\leqslant 1+e^{\frac{\lambda}{(1-\lambda)}}
.

Corollary 7.4

(Concentration under Spectral Independence)
.

Fix
\eta>0
and let
X
be a
d
-uniform
\eta
-spectrally independent complex. Then for any
0\leqslant k\leqslant d
,
g:X{\left(1\right)}\to[0,1]
, and
f=U_{1,k}g
:

1.

Upper tail:

\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]\geqslant\varepsilon]\leqslant 4e^{-c_{\eta}\varepsilon\sqrt{k}}

2.

Lower tail:

\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]\leqslant-\varepsilon]\leqslant 4e^{c_{\eta}\varepsilon\sqrt{k}}

where
c_{\eta}\leqslant\frac{1}{12\max\{1,\sqrt{\eta}\}}
.

We note it is possible to extend the latter to lifted Lipschitz functions from an appropriate skeleton.

7.1.1
The Herbst Argument

The proof of
Theorem
7.1
is via a variant of the
Herbst Argument
, a classical strategy for proving concentration of random variables based on applying functional inequalities like MLSI and LSI to the moment generating function. While sparse simplicial complexes cannot have bounded (modified) log-sobolev constants, a somewhat lesser known variant of the Herbst argument using spectral gap was developed by Aida and Stroock
[
AS94
]
, Bobkov and Ledoux
[
BL97
]
, and Boucheron, Lugosi, and Massart
[
BLM13
]
. We will give an elementary adaption of their method to HDX achieving the above bounds.

The key to
Theorem
7.1
is really the following approximate variant of the Efron-Stein inequality, sometimes called ‘approximate tensorization of variance’. We remark that similar inequalities for local-spectral expanders appear in the literature
[
KM20
,
Che+21
]
.

Lemma 7.5

(Approximate Efron-Stein Inequality)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex such that
\lambda_{2}(U_{d-1}D_{d})\leqslant 1-\frac{1}{Cd}
for some
C>0
. Then for any
0\leqslant k\leqslant d
,
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
Z=f(X)
we have:

Var(f)\leqslant(C+1)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[(Z-Z^{\prime}_{(i)})_{+}^{2}\right]

Proof.

It is sufficient to instead prove that any
k
-uniform complex whose down-up walk
U_{k-1}D_{k}
has gap
\frac{1}{C^{\prime}k}
satisfies

Var(f)\leqslant C^{\prime}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[(Z-Z^{\prime}_{(i)})_{+}^{2}\right].

The result then follows from
[
Lee23
, Theorem 3.5]
, who shows that
\lambda_{2}(U_{d-1}D_{d})\leqslant 1-\frac{1}{Cd}
implies
\lambda_{2}(U_{k-1}D_{k})\leqslant 1-\frac{1}{(C+1)k}
for any
0\leqslant k\leqslant d
.

With this in mind, let
E_{i}
be the
i
th averaging operator:

E_{i}f(s)=\underset{s_{i}\sim X_{s_{-i}}}{\mathbb{E}}[f(s_{-i}\cup s_{i})],

where we recall
s_{-i}
is
s
without the
i
th vertex in its ordering. Define the
i
th Laplacian
L_{i}=I-E_{i}
, and the total laplacian operator as
L=I-UD=\frac{1}{k+1}\sum\limits L_{i}
. By assumption on the spectral gap of
UD
, we have:

Var(f)\leqslant Ck\langle f,Lf\rangle

On the other hand expanding out the Laplacian gives:

\displaystyle Ck\langle f,Lf\rangle

\displaystyle=C\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\langle f,L_{i}f\rangle

\displaystyle=C\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\langle L_{i}f,L_{i}f\rangle

\displaystyle=C\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\underset{{s\sim X{\left(k\right)}}}{\mathbb{E}}[Var^{(i)}_{s}(f)]

where
Var^{(i)}_{s}(f)
is the variance of
f
over the link
X_{s_{-i}}
. Finally examining this expected variance, we have:

\displaystyle\underset{{s\sim X{\left(k\right)}}}{\mathbb{E}}[Var^{(i)}_{s}(f)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(Z-Z^{\prime}_{(i)})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[(Z-Z^{\prime}_{(i)})_{+}^{2}]

where both equalities follow from the fact that
Z
and
Z^{\prime}_{(i)}
are i.i.d conditioned on
s_{-i}
. Combining with the above completes the proof.
∎

We can now prove
Theorem
7.1
closely following the approach of
[
BLM13
]
.

Proof of
Theorem
7.1
.

We prove only the upper tail. The lower tail follows similarly. It is sufficient to prove the following exponential integrability of the moment generating function:

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{C\nu}}(f-\mathbb{E}[f])}\right]\leqslant 2,

(7.1)

since we then have:

\displaystyle\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]>t]

\displaystyle=\ProbOp\left[\exp\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{C\nu}}(f-\mathbb{E}[f])\right)>\exp\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{C\nu}}t\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{C\nu}}t\right)

by Markov.

For notational simplicity, let
g=f-\mathbb{E}[f]
and let
Z=g(X)
. Following
[
BLM13
]
, we use Efron-Stein to set up a recurrence relating
e^{\lambda Z}
to
e^{\lambda Z/2}
for small enough
\lambda>0
. Applying Efron-Stein to the latter gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda Z}]-\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda Z/2}]^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[(e^{\lambda Z/2}-e^{\lambda Z^{\prime}_{(i)}/2})_{+}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant C\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\lambda Z}(1-e^{\lambda(Z^{\prime}_{(i)}-Z)/2})_{+}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C\lambda^{2}}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\lambda Z}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}(Z-Z_{(i)}^{\prime})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C\lambda^{2}\nu}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\lambda Z}\right]

since
f
(and therefore
g
) are
\nu
-bounded. Re-arranging we have the recurrence:

\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda Z}]\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{\lambda^{2}C\nu}{4}}\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda Z/2}]^{2}.

It is then an elementary exercise to solve when
\lambda\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{C\nu}}
and derive
(
7.1
)
(see
[
BLM13
, Section 3.6]
for the full derivation).
∎

We now prove the corollaries for SI/TD-complexes

Proof of
Corollary
7.3
.

By
Theorem
4.23
,
X
is a
\frac{\lambda}{(d-1)(1-\lambda)}
-one-sided local-spectral expander, so
Theorem
4.19
gives
\lambda_{2}(U_{d-1}D_{d})\leqslant 1-\frac{1}{d}e^{\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}}
. Plugging this into
Theorem
7.1
gives the result.
∎

In the spectral independence regime, one must be slightly more careful since an
\eta
-spectrally independent system has
C\approx k^{\eta}
. We recover concentration by looking only at lifted functions and analyzing concentration on some lower skeleton.

Proof of
Corollary
7.4
.

We prove the upper tail. The lower tail follows similarly. We break the proof into two cases. First, assume that
\eta\leqslant 2
and let
k^{\prime}=\lfloor\frac{d}{3}\rfloor
. The
k^{\prime}
-skeleton of
X
is a one-sided
\frac{1}{k^{\prime}}
-local-spectral expander. Thus by
Theorem
4.19

\lambda(U_{k^{\prime}-1}D_{k^{\prime}})\leqslant 1-\frac{1}{ek^{\prime}}
and
Theorem
7.1
implies every
k\leqslant k^{\prime}
has concentration:

\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]>\varepsilon]\leqslant 2e^{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{e+1}}\varepsilon\sqrt{k}}.

Further, by
Lemma
4.15
every
k>k^{\prime}
has concentration:

\displaystyle\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]>\varepsilon]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2e^{-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{e+1}}\varepsilon\sqrt{k^{\prime}}}(1-e^{-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{12}k})

\displaystyle\leqslant 4e^{-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6(e+1)}}\varepsilon\sqrt{d}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4e^{-\frac{1}{12}\varepsilon\sqrt{d}}

assuming
k\geqslant 6
and
\varepsilon^{2}\geqslant\frac{12}{k}
(else the bound is trivial).

Otherwise, assume
\eta>2
and let
k^{\prime}=\lfloor\frac{d}{\eta}\rfloor
. The
k^{\prime}
-skeleton of
X
is a
\frac{\eta}{(1-\frac{1}{\eta})d}
-one-sided local-spectral expander. Then by
Theorem
4.19

\lambda(U_{k^{\prime}-1}D_{k^{\prime}})\leqslant 1-\frac{1}{e^{2}k^{\prime}}
and
Theorem
7.1
implies for every
k\leqslant k^{\prime}
:

\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]>\varepsilon]\leqslant 2e^{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{e^{2}+1}}\varepsilon\sqrt{k}}.

Further, by
Lemma
4.15
every
k>k^{\prime}
has concentration:

\displaystyle\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]>\varepsilon]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2e^{-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{e^{2}+1}}\varepsilon\sqrt{k^{\prime}}}(1-e^{-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{12}k})

\displaystyle\leqslant 4e^{-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{4\eta(e^{2}+1)}}\varepsilon\sqrt{d}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4e^{-\frac{1}{12\sqrt{\eta}}\varepsilon\sqrt{d}}

assuming
\eta\leqslant\frac{d}{2}
and
\varepsilon^{2}\geqslant\frac{12}{k+1}
(again, otherwise the stated bound is trivial).

∎

Finally before moving to reverse hypercontractivity, we briefly remark one a additional corollary regarding subgaussian concentration for more general function classes:

Corollary 7.6

(Subgaussian concentration for skeletons)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex with
\lambda_{2}(U_{d-1}D_{d})\leqslant 1-\frac{1}{Cd}
. Then for any
k\leqslant d
,
\nu>0
, and function
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\nu
-bounded difference:

1.

Upper Tail
:
\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]\geqslant t]\leqslant 2e^{-\frac{t}{\sqrt{4(C+1)\nu}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{d}{k}}}+e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{4\nu}}

2.

Lower Tail:

\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]\leqslant-t]\leqslant 2e^{-\frac{t}{\sqrt{4(C+1)\nu}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{d}{k}}}+e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{4\nu}}

Proof.

The proof is immediate from
Theorem
7.1
and
Lemma
4.16
, setting
f_{up}(t,\nu)=f_{low}(t,\nu)=2e^{\frac{-t}{\sqrt{(C+1)\nu}}}
.
∎

We remark it is likely possible to generalize the above strategy from bounded difference to Lipschitz functions on sufficiently strong local-spectral expanders by more carefully analyzing the eigenspaces of the Laplacian and higher order random walks associated with the
d
-lift (e.g. as in
[
KO20
,
Dik+18
,
Gai+22
]
).

7.2
Concentration for Partite HDX

The final component of
Theorem
5.1
is the partite case. We re-state the partite version of the theorem here for convenience.

Theorem 7.7

(Sampling on HDX)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform partite
2^{-cd}
-HDX. Then for any
i<k\leqslant d
the containment graph
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is a
(\varepsilon,\beta)
sampler for

\beta=\frac{64}{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\Omega_{c}\left(\varepsilon^{8}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right).

Note that in the special case
i=1
(i.e. the Chernoff-Hoeffding setting), the
\varepsilon
-dependence can be improved to
\varepsilon^{-2}
in the exponent and removed entirely from the leading coefficient. Similarly, the exponent can be improved to
\varepsilon^{-2}
dependence by
Lemma
4.16
whenever
k\leqslant\varepsilon^{6}d
.

Similar to our approach in the two-sided case, we will split our analysis into a Chernoff-Hoeffding bound, and a bootstrapping method to lift Chernoff to full inclusion sampling. Both steps require significant modification from the two-sided case to handle inherent dependencies between coordinates only occuring in the partite case.

7.2.1
Chernoff-Hoeffding

Unfortunately, partite HDX are not ‘splittable’ in the sense of two-sided HDX. Thus to prove a Chernoff-Hoeffding bound, we will instead rely on a more classical
localization
