approach, leveraging the local sampling properties between bipartite components of the complex. The resulting quantitative bounds via this technique are again incomparable with our prior methods. While they are weaker for large
\lambda
, they approach the error of true independent sampling for small enough
\lambda
and remove the restrictive condition of two-sided expansion. The latter in particular actually leads to sparser complexes with subgaussian concentration due to better known degree bounds for partite constructions.

Before stating the result formally, we briefly introduce some relevant notation. Given a complex
X
, let
X^{ind}
denote the complete complex whose vertices are
X{\left(1\right)}
, that is:

X^{ind}(k)=\{\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\}:v_{i}\in X{\left(1\right)}\}

where each
v_{i}
is drawn independently from
X{\left(1\right)}

with repetition
.
10
10

10

We remark we have abused notation somewhat as this is not formally a simplicial complex and the faces are
multi
-sets, but this is irrelevant for our purposes.
Similarly for
X
partite, let
X^{ind,p}
be the complete
k
-partite complex whose sides are
X[1],X[2],\dots,X[k]
:

X^{ind,p}(k)=\{\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\}:v_{1}\in X[1],v_{2}\in X[2],\dots,v_{k}\in X[k]\}

where each
v_{i}
is drawn independently from the marginal over
X[i]
.

Finally, let
\sigma(X,k,\varepsilon)
denote the worst case ‘
k
vs
1
’
\varepsilon
-sampling error of
X
.

\sigma(X,k,\varepsilon)=\max\left\{\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{v\in X{\left(1\right)}}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Av\in s}\right.v\in s\right]-\ProbOp_{{v\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[A\right]\right\rvert>\varepsilon\right]\left|\;\vphantom{\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{v\in X{\left(1\right)}}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Av\in s}\right.v\in s\right]-\ProbOp_{{v\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[A\right]\right\rvert>\varepsilon\right]A\subseteq X{\left(1\right)}}\right.A\subseteq X{\left(1\right)}\right\}

We prove that sampling in two-sided and one-sided partite HDX match the bounds in their respective independent complex up to
\poly(k)\lambda
-error.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
\eta>0,\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, and
\lambda\leqslant\frac{\eta^{2.5}}{16k^{4}}
. Then

1.

If
X
is
k
-uniform
\lambda
-two-sided local-spectral expander:

\sigma(X,k,\varepsilon)\leqslant\sigma(X^{ind},k,\varepsilon)+\eta

2.

If
X
is a
k
-partite
\lambda
-one-sided local-spectral expander:

\sigma(X,k,\varepsilon)\leqslant\sigma(X^{ind,p},k,\varepsilon)+\eta

In particular, in both cases
X
is then a
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler for
\beta=\eta+\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}k))
by Chernoff-Hoeffding.

In the two-sided case, note that
Theorem
5.3
is tighter when
\lambda>\exp(-\Omega(k))
, while for small enough
\lambda

Theorem
5.3
wins out achieving parameters arbitrarily close to independent sampling. We note that here we only give a bound on sampling sets, but this can be converted to concentration for lifts of bounded functions with essentially no loss by
Claim
4.11
.

The core of
Theorem
7.8
is a lower bound on the
k
-wise correlation across coordinates of a
\lambda
-product.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
X
be a
k
-uniform
\lambda
-product. For every
\eta<1
and functions
f_{i}:X[i]\to[0,1]
:

\E_{{\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(v_{i})\right]\geqslant(1-\eta)^{k}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}[f_{i}]

whenever
\lambda\leqslant\frac{\eta^{1.5}\min_{i}\{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}]^{2}\}}{\sqrt{8k^{3}}}
.

Proof.

The proof relies on the following adaptation of an elementary connection between bipartite expansion and sampling observed in
[
DK17
]
:

Claim 7.10
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a
\lambda
-bipartite expander and let
A
be its bipartite adjacency operator. Let
f:L\to[0,1]
be a function with
\E[f]\geqslant\mu
and let
0<\varepsilon<\mu
. Then

\ProbOp_{u\in R}[Af(u)<\mu-\varepsilon]<\frac{\lambda^{2}\mu}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

We prove this in
Appendix
C
for completeness. Using this fact, we prove the following more general claim. Write
\mu_{i}=\mathbb{E}[f_{i}]
and fix
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\dots,\varepsilon_{k}>0
such that
\mu_{i}-(i-1)\varepsilon_{i}>0
. For
\varepsilon=\min_{i}\{\varepsilon_{i}\}
we show

\displaystyle\E_{{\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(v_{i})\right]\geqslant\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\mu_{i}-(i-1)\varepsilon_{i}-\lambda^{2}\frac{k-i}{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\underset{j>i}{\min}(\mu_{j}-(i-1)\varepsilon_{j})}\right).

(7.2)

Setting
\varepsilon_{i}=\frac{\mu_{i}\eta}{2i}
and applying our assumed bound on
\lambda
then gives the desired result.

We prove (
7.2
) by induction on
k
, the uniformity of the complex.
For
k=1
the bound is trivial since
\E[f_{1}]=\mu_{1}
. Assume the statement holds for any
(k-1)
-uniform
\lambda
-product, and for each
i=2,\dots,k
let
T_{i}
denote the vertices in
X[1]
that under-sample
f_{i}
:

T_{i}=\left\{v_{1}\in X[1]\left|\;\vphantom{v_{1}\in X[1]\E_{{v_{i}\in X_{v_{1}}[i]}}\left[f(v_{i})\right]<\mu_{i}-\varepsilon_{i}}\right.\E_{{v_{i}\in X_{v_{1}}[i]}}\left[f(v_{i})\right]<\mu_{i}-\varepsilon_{i}\right\}.

Since each
(X[1],X[i])
is a
\lambda
-bipartite expander,
Claim
7.10
implies

\ProbOp\left[{\bigcup_{i=2}^{k}T_{i}}\right]\leqslant\sum_{i=2}^{k}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\mu_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}}\leqslant\lambda^{2}\frac{k-1}{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\min_{j>1}\{\mu_{j}\}}.

We de-correlate our expected product along the first coordinate by conditioning on
G=X[1]\setminus\{\bigcup_{i=2}^{k}T_{i}\}
:

\displaystyle\E\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(v_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\E\left[\mathbf{1}_{G}(v_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(v_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\E[\mathbf{1}_{G}(v_{0})f_{0}(v_{0})]\cdot\min_{v_{1}\in G}\left(\E_{{\{v_{2},v_{3},\dots,v_{k}\}\in X_{v_{1}}}}\left[\prod_{i=2}^{k}f_{i}(v_{i})\right]\right).

We bound the two terms separately. For the first:

\displaystyle\E[\mathbf{1}_{G}(v_{0})f_{0}(v_{0})]

\displaystyle\geqslant\E[f_{0}(v_{0})]-\ProbOp\left[{\bigcup_{i=2}^{k}T_{i}}\right]

(7.3)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mu_{0}-\lambda^{2}\frac{k-1}{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\min_{j>1}\{\mu_{j}\}}.

(7.4)

To bound the minimum term, we apply the inductive hypothesis on every link
X_{v_{1}}
. Toward this end, for notational simplicity re-index the sides of
X_{v_{1}}
to be from
1
to
k-1
and set:

1.

f^{\prime}_{i}=f_{i+1}
,

2.

\varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{i+1}
,

3.

\mu_{i}^{\prime}=\mu_{i+1}-\varepsilon_{i+1}
and
\mu^{\prime}=\min_{j>1}\mu_{i}^{\prime}
.

Since
\E_{{X_{v_{1}}[i]}}\left[f^{\prime}_{i}\right]\geqslant\mu_{i}^{\prime}
by assumption on the links, applying the inductive hypothesis to
X_{v_{1}}
and
\{f^{\prime}\}
bounds the minimum by

\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(\mu_{i}^{\prime}-(i-1)\varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda^{2}\frac{k-i}{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\underset{j>i}{\min}(\mu_{j}^{\prime}-(i-1)\varepsilon_{j}^{\prime})}\right)

Combining this with the prior term (corresponding to
i=0
) gives the desired bound

\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\mu_{i}-(i-1)\varepsilon_{i}-\lambda^{2}\frac{k-i}{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\underset{j>i}{\min}(\mu_{j}-(i-1)\varepsilon_{j})}\right).

∎

We note a more involved variant of the above applying different bounds in each level of induction implies the following finer-grained bound:

Claim 7.11
.

Let
X
be a
k
-partite
\lambda
-product. Let
f_{i}:X[i]\to[0,1]
be such that
\E_{{v_{i}\in X{[i]}}}\left[f(v_{i})\right]\geqslant\mu_{i}
. Let
\{\varepsilon_{i,\ell}>0\}_{1\leqslant i<\ell\leqslant k}
and let
s_{i,\ell}^{(m)}=\sum_{j=\ell}^{m}\varepsilon_{j,i}
(and for brevity
s_{m+1,\ell}^{(m)}=0
). Then

\E_{{\{v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(v_{i})\right]\geqslant\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\mu_{i}-s_{i,i}^{(k-1)}-\lambda^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{\mu_{j}-s_{i,j}^{(k-1)}}{\varepsilon_{i,j}^{2}}\right).

(7.5)

We omit the proof which is an unenlightening technical extension of the above.

Using our lower bound on negative correlation, we show that for any function tuple
f=(f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{k})
, the random variables
f(X)
and
f(X^{ind})
are actually
distributionally
close. This will allow us to immediately recover any concentration bound on
X^{ind}
up to the distributional error. More formally, given a
k
-partite simplicial complex
X
, let
D_{ind}
denote the distribution supported by of
X^{ind,p}
, i.e.
\ProbOp_{{D_{ind}}}\left[\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}\right]=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\ProbOp_{{X{[i]}}}\left[v_{i}\right]
. Moreover, for any distribution
D
over
\Omega=X[1]\times X[2]\times\dots\times X[k]
, and a function
\bar{f}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
define the
push forward
distribution
\bar{f}_{*}D:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to[0,1]
as

\bar{f}_{*}D(\bar{x})=\ProbOp_{{\omega\sim D}}\left[\bar{f}(\omega)=\bar{x}\right].

Let
D_{X}=\pi_{k}
denote the distribution over
k
-sets of
X
. We show the pushforward distributions
\bar{f}_{*}D_{X}
and
\bar{f}_{*}D_{ind}
are close in TV-distance.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
\varepsilon,\eta>0
and let
X
be a
k
-partite
\lambda
-product for
\lambda\leqslant\frac{\eta^{2.5}}{8k^{4}}
. Then for any
f_{i}:X[i]\to\{0,1\}
and
\bar{f}=(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k})
:

d_{TV}(\bar{f}_{*}D_{X},\bar{f}_{*}D_{ind})\leqslant\eta.

Proof.

Note that both distributions are supported in
\{0,1\}^{k}
by our assumption on the domain of
\bar{f}
. Thus we need to show for every
B\subseteq\{0,1\}^{k}
:

\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{X}}}\left[B\right]-\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{ind}}}\left[B\right]\right\rvert\leqslant\eta.

Indeed since
\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{X}}}\left[B\right]-\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{ind}}}\left[B\right]=\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{ind}}}\left[\bar{B}\right]-\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{X}}}\left[\bar{B}\right]
for
\bar{B}=\{0,1\}^{k}\setminus B
, it is enough to just show the one-sided bound

\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{X}}}\left[B\right]\geqslant\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{ind}}}\left[B\right]-\eta.

Fix any event
B\subseteq\{0,1\}^{k}
. We’ll decompose
B
into two parts. First, we’ll look at the subset
B_{1}
on which
f
takes many ‘unlikely’ values, and argue the difference is small just because
D_{X}
and
D_{ind}
have the same marginals over each coordinate of
\{0,1\}^{k}
. In particular, let
I=\left\{i\in[k]\left|\;\vphantom{i\in[k]\E[f_{i}]\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2k}}\right.\E[f_{i}]\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2k}\right\}
and
J=\left\{i\in[k]\left|\;\vphantom{i\in[k]\E[f_{i}]\geqslant 1-\frac{\eta}{2k}}\right.\E[f_{i}]\geqslant 1-\frac{\eta}{2k}\right\}
be the sets of coordinates with extreme expectations and let

B_{1}=B\cap\left\{x\penalty\ \Bigg|\left(\bigvee_{i\in I}\{f_{i}(x)=1\}\right)\lor\left(\bigvee_{j\in J}\{f_{j}(x)=0\}\right)\right\}.

Set
B_{2}=B\setminus B_{1}
.

We first show that
\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{X}}}\left[B_{1}\right]-\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{ind}}}\left[B_{1}\right]\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2}
. Since both distributions have the same marginals, we can write for either
D_{X}
or
D_{ind}
by a union bound:

\ProbOp\left[{B_{1}}\right]\leqslant\sum_{i\in I}\ProbOp\left[{f_{i}=1}\right]+\sum_{j\in J}\ProbOp\left[{f_{j}=0}\right]\leqslant(|I|+|J|)\frac{\eta}{2k}\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2}.

In particular the difference is no more than
\frac{\eta}{2}
.

Moving on to
B_{2}
, we decompose the probability as a sum over
k
-wise products:

\ProbOp_{{\bar{f}_{*}D_{X}}}\left[B_{2}\right]=\sum_{\bar{b}\in B_{2}}\ProbOp_{{D_{X}}}\left[\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}=b_{i}\right]=\sum_{\bar{b}\in B_{2}}\E_{{\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}\sim D_{X}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert b_{i}-f_{i}(v_{i})\right\rvert\right].

We note that the functions
\left\lvert b_{i}-f_{i}(v_{i})\right\rvert
have values in
[0,1]
and by assumption have expectation
\geqslant\frac{\eta}{2}
(since otherwise
\bar{b}\in B_{1}
). Then by
Proposition
7.9
setting
\varepsilon=\frac{\eta}{2}
we have

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{D_{X}}[B_{2}]

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-\frac{\eta}{2k})^{k}\sum_{\bar{b}\in B_{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\E_{{v_{i}\in X{[i]}}}\left[\left\lvert b_{i}-f_{i}(v_{i})\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle=(1-\frac{\eta}{2k})^{k}\sum_{\bar{b}\in B_{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\ProbOp_{{D_{ind}}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{f_{i}=b_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle=(1-\frac{\eta}{2k})^{k}\sum_{\bar{b}\in B_{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\ProbOp_{{D_{ind}}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{f_{i}=b_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp_{{D_{ind}}}\left[B_{2}\right]-\frac{\eta}{2}

where we’ve used the marginal equivalence of
D_{X}
and
D_{ind}
and independence of
D_{ind}
in the second and third steps respectively. Combining our bounds on
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
gives the result.
∎

We can now easily prove
Theorem
7.8
.

Proof of
Theorem
7.8
.

Let
f:X{\left(1\right)}\to[0,1]
. For the partite case, set
f_{i}=f|_{{X[i]}}
. Consider the set of ‘bad’
k
-tuples that mis-sample
f
:

B=\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{k}f(v_{j})-\mu\right\rvert>\varepsilon\right\}.

By
Lemma
7.12
, the TV-distance between the distribution over values of
\bar{f}(x)=f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
over
X
and
X^{ind,p}
is at most
\eta
so we have:

\displaystyle\sigma(X,k,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\max_{\bar{f}}\ProbOp_{\bar{f}_{*}D_{X}}[B]

\displaystyle\leqslant\max_{\bar{f}}\ProbOp_{\bar{f}_{*}D_{ind}}[B]+\eta

\displaystyle\leqslant S(X^{ind,p},k,\varepsilon)+\eta

as desired. The two-sided case follows the same argument after taking the partitification
P(X)
of
X
and setting
f_{i}(v,i)=f(v)
.
∎

7.2.2
Partite Inclusion Sampling

Broadly speaking, the proof of
Theorem
7.7
(that is the partite case of
Theorem
5.1
), follows the same strategy as the two-sided case. We’d like to lift Chernoff-Hoeffding to sampling of the inclusion graph via a Chernoff-Hoeffding style bound for the faces complex. Unfortunately, unlike the two-sided case, the faces complex of a partite HDX is not necessarily an HDX (indeed its links may not even be connected). Nevertheless, lacking expansion does not necessarily mean the faces complex is a poor sampler. Indeed we will argue
F_{X}
is a near-optimal sampler by splitting its concentration into two components 1) the probability of sampling a good partition of
colors
2) sampling a good subset conditioned on this partition. We show the first step may be reduced to concentration on the faces complex of the complete complex (which we’ll call the
swap complex
), while the second reduces to Chernoff for partite HDX.

With this in mind, before jumping into the proof we will first cover some necessary background on the swap complex, which is simply the uniform distribution over (families of) disjoint
\ell
-sets of
[n]
.

Definition 7.13

(The Swap Complex)
.

Let
n\geqslant k\geqslant i\in\mathbb{N}
. The
(i,k,n)
-swap complex is the
\lfloor\frac{k}{i}\rfloor
-uniform simplicial complex
C=C_{i,k,n}
whose vertices are all size-
\ell
subsets
[n]
:

C{\left(1\right)}=\binom{[n]}{i},

and whose top-level faces are all possible pair-wise disjoint
\ell
-sets:

C{\left(\left\lfloor\frac{k}{i}\right\rfloor\right)}=\left\{\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{\lfloor\frac{k}{i}\rfloor}\}:\bigcup_{i\neq j}s_{i}\cap s_{j}=\emptyset\right\}

endowed with the uniform distribution.

In other words,
C_{\ell,k,n}
is exactly
F^{i}_{\Delta_{n}(k)}
, the faces complex of the
k
-uniform complete complex on
n
vertices, and is simply the complete complex itself when
i=1
.

Thus, like the complete complex, one might reasonably hope that the disjointness requirement on the swap complex does not substantially hinder concentration. Indeed this is relatively easy to show when
n\gg k
(e.g. from
\ell_{\infty}
-independence
[
KKS21
]
, or even just negative correlation for large enough
n
), but becomes challenging in the regime where
n=\Theta(k)
where disjointness introduces non-trivial positive correlations between the variables. Nevertheless, in
Appendix
B
, we show these correlations can be handled whenever
n=\Theta_{\varepsilon}(k)
:

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
i\leqslant k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
C=C_{i,k,n}
for
n\geqslant\frac{1153k\log\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon^{5}}
. For any
f:C(1)\to[0,1]
:

1.

Upper tail
:
\ProbOp[U_{1,\frac{k}{i}}f-\mathbb{E}[f]>\varepsilon]\leqslant\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}))

2.

Lower tail
:
\ProbOp[U_{1,\frac{k}{i}}f-\mathbb{E}[f]<-\varepsilon]\leqslant\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}))

With this in hand, we are finally ready to handle the partite case of
Theorem
5.1
.

Proof of
Theorem
7.7
(
Theorem
5.1
, partite case).

Let
X
be a
k
-uniform skeleton of a
d
-partite
2^{-\Omega(d)}
-HDX. We will actually work with
k^{\prime}
-skeleton of
X
where
k^{\prime}=\min\left\{k,\frac{\varepsilon^{5}}{1153\log\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)}d\right\}
. Note that if
i\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{2}k^{\prime}}{100\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}
, the stated bound is trivial. Otherwise, by
Lemma
4.15
, it is enough to show
(X(k^{\prime}),X(i))
is an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler for

\beta\leqslant\frac{16}{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k^{\prime}}{i}\right)\right).

Let
m=\lfloor\frac{k^{\prime}}{i}\rfloor
. By
Proposition
5.9
, it is enough to prove the faces complex
F_{X}^{(i)}(m)
is an
(\varepsilon,8\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}m)))
sampler for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Toward this end, fix
f:X{\left(i\right)}\to[0,1]
of expectation
\mu
viewed as a function on the vertices of the faces complex. Consider the random variable
\mathcal{J}\coloneqq(col(s_{1}),col(s_{2}),\dots,col(s_{m}))
corresponding to the partition generated by drawing
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})
from the faces complex of
X
. For a fixed
\mathcal{J}=\{I^{1},I^{2},\dots,I^{m}\}
, denote the conditional expectation of
f
under
\mathcal{J}
as

\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\coloneqq\underset{s\sim X{\left(i\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[f|\penalty\ col(s)\in\mathcal{J}].

Further let
C_{\mathcal{J}}
denote the complex generated by restricting to top-level faces of
C
with partition
\mathcal{J}
. We define two ‘bad’ events based on
\mathcal{J}
outside which our sampling is
\varepsilon
-accurate:

1.

(Bad Colors):

E_{1}
the event that
\mu_{\mathcal{J}}
deviates significantly from
\mu

\left\lvert\mu_{\mathcal{J}}-\mu\right\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon/2.

2.

(Bad Sampling):

E_{2}
the event that
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})\sim C_{\mathcal{J}}
samples
\mu_{\mathcal{J}}
poorly

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{i\in[m]}f(s_{i})-\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\right\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon/2.

It is then sufficient to prove the following three claims:

1.

\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m})\sim C}}\left[\left\lvert\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{i\in[m]}f(s_{i})-\mu\right\rvert>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{E_{1}}\right]+\ProbOp\left[{E_{2}\land\neg E_{1}}\right].

2.

\ProbOp\left[{E_{1}}\right]\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}m)\right)
.

3.

\ProbOp\left[{E_{2}\land\neg E_{1}}\right]\leqslant 4\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}m))
.

The first claim is immediate from definition and the triangle inequality, as the event
|\sum f-\mu|>\varepsilon
only occurs if
E_{1}\lor E_{2}
holds, the probability of which is bounded by
\ProbOp[E_{1}]+\ProbOp[E_{2}\land\lnot E_{1}]
as desired.

Bounding
\ProbOp\left[{E_{1}}\right]

Observe
\mathcal{J}
is equidistributed with the complex
C_{i,im,d}
and define
g:C{\left(1\right)}\to[0,1]
by
g(I^{j})=\mathbb{E}_{C}[f\penalty\ |\penalty\ I^{j}\in col(s)]
. For
\mathcal{J}=\{I^{1},\ldots,I^{m}\}
we then have:

1.

\mathbb{E}[g]=\underset{I\sim C{\left(1\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbb{E}[f\penalty\ |\penalty\ col(s)\in I]\right]=\mu

2.

\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}g(I^{j})=\mu_{\mathcal{J}}
.

Combining these observations with
Theorem
7.14
we can write

\displaystyle\ProbOp\left[{E_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=\ProbOp_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\left|\mu_{\mathcal{J}}-\mu\right|>\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\ProbOp_{\{I^{1},\ldots,I^{m}\}\sim C{\left(m\right)}}\left[\left|\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}g(I^{j})-\mathbb{E}[g]\right|>\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}m)\right)

as desired.

Bounding
\ProbOp\left[{E_{2}\land\neg E_{1}}\right]

It is left to argue that for any fixed
\mathcal{J}=\{I^{1},I^{2},\ldots,I^{m}\}
,
C_{\mathcal{J}}
is a partite
2^{-\Omega(k^{\prime})}
-HDX. The result is then immediate from applying
Theorem
7.8
with
\eta=\exp(-\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon^{2}m)
:

\displaystyle\ProbOp[E_{2}\land\lnot E_{1}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp_{\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{m}\}\sim D}[E_{2}|\penalty\ \mathcal{J}\in\lnot E_{1}]

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathcal{J}\in\lnot E_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ProbOp_{s\sim C_{\mathcal{J}}}\left[\left\lvert\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{i\in[m]}f(s_{i})-\mu_{\mathcal{J}}\right\rvert\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon^{2}m\right).

By construction it is easily checked
C_{\mathcal{J}}
is a partite complex whose links are isomorphic to swap walks
S_{I_{i},I_{j}}
within a link of the original complex
X
. By
Theorem
4.21
,
C_{\mathcal{J}}
is therefore a partite
\lambda
-one-sided HDX for
\lambda\leqslant 2^{-\Omega(d)}
as well.
∎

8
Agreement Testing

In this section we use our reverse hypercontractive inequality to prove several new agreement testing theorems on HDX. In the first subsection, we give new agreement tests in the
99\%
-regime between any two levels of an HDX. In the second subsection, we explore the role of reverse hypercontractivity in the
1\%
-regime, and prove that HDX admit agreemment tests with optimal
local
soundness (a weaker notion of soundness only requiring globalness on links), and show under the stronger assumption of
\ell_{\infty}
-expansion that this guarantee can be propagated to a true tester with optimal soundness.

8.1
Background

Suppose
U
is some finite set of vertices and
S\subseteq\mathbb{P}(U)
are subsets of
U
. An
agreement test
is a procedure that inputs a family of assignments to the subsets
\{f_{s}:s\to\{0,1\}\}_{s\in S}
, and aims to check whether the assignments are consistent with a
global
assignment to the ground set
G:U\to\{0,1\}
. This is a classical setup in hardness of approximation (in particular in the construction of PCPs), where one repeats a problem in parallel and needs to check that answers across repetitions are consistent.

Let us give the simplest setup first, slightly generalizing it later on. Let
(U,S,\Sigma,D)
be such that

1.

U
is a finite set (“Universe”).

2.

S\subseteq\mathbb{P}(U)
(“Sets”).

3.

\Sigma
is another finite set (“Alphabet”).

4.

A distribution
s_{1},s_{2}\sim D
such that
s_{1},s_{2}\in S
(“Distribution”).

An
ensemble of functions
in this context is a family
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{s}:s\to\Sigma\}_{s\in S}
. An ensemble is called
global
if there exists a function
G:U\to\Sigma
such that for every
s\in S
it holds that
f_{s}=G|_{s}
.

We wish to relate the following two quantities. The first quantity is the “agreement” of an ensemble. For
\eta\in[0,1)
we denote by

Agree_{\eta}(\mathcal{F}):=\ProbOp_{{s_{1},s_{2},t\sim D}}\left[f_{s_{1}}|_{s_{1}\cap s_{2}}\overset{\eta}{\approx}f_{s_{2}}|_{s_{1}\cap s_{2}}\right],

(8.1)

where the notation inside the probability means that there is a set
t^{\prime}\subseteq s_{1}\cap s_{2}
of size
|t^{\prime}|\geqslant(1-\eta)|s_{1}\cap s_{2}|
such that
f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime}}=f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime}}
. We will also use
\overset{\eta}{\not\approx}
to denote the negation of this.

The second quantity is the distance from global functions. For
\eta,\varepsilon>0
we say that
\mathcal{F}
is
(\eta,\varepsilon)
-close to a global function if the exists some
G:U\to\Sigma
such that

\ProbOp_{{s}}\left[f_{s}\overset{\eta}{\approx}G|_{s}\right]\geqslant 1-\varepsilon.

The probability distribution in which we take
s\in S
above is the marginal distribution of
D
.
11
11

11

Formally, draw
(s,s^{\prime})\sim D
, then
s
or
s^{\prime}
with probability
1/2
independently.

We denote by

\dist_{\eta}(\mathcal{F},Glob)=\min\left\{\varepsilon\geqslant 0\left|\;\vphantom{\varepsilon\geqslant 0\text{there exists $G:U\to\Sigma$ such that $\mathcal{F}$ is $(\eta,\varepsilon)$-close to $G$}}\right.\text{there exists $G:U\to\Sigma$ such that $\mathcal{F}$ is $(\eta,\varepsilon)$-close to $G$}\right\}.

A
high acceptance agreement theorem
(also known as a “99% agreement theorem”) is a theorem that states that if a set of local functions pass the test with almost perfect probability (“99%”), then the functions are close to being global. In other words, this is a theorem that states for a specific
(U,S,\Sigma,D)
that for every ensemble
\mathcal{F}
,

Agree_{\eta}(\mathcal{F})=1-\varepsilon\quad\implies\quad\dist_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F},Glob)\leqslant\varepsilon^{\prime}.

where
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}>0
. Ideally, one wants such a statement for every
\varepsilon,\eta
small enough, with
\varepsilon^{\prime}=O(\varepsilon)
and
\eta^{\prime}=O(\eta)
. But other parameter regimes are interesting in applications as well.

Agreement tests originated in low-degree testing results such as
[
RS96
]
and were later abstracted by
[
GS97
]
to roughly the setup above. They are an important component of Dinur’s proof of the PCP Theorem
[
Din07
]
. An optimal high acceptance agreement theorem was proven by
[
DS14
]
on the complete complex. This was extended by
[
DFH19
]
to another setup related to the complete complex we discuss below. Agreement theorems on sparse complexes such as high dimensional expanders are also known, pioneered by
[
DK17
]
.

A
low acceptance agreement theorem
(also known as a “1% agreement theorem”) deals with the regime where it is only assumed that the functions pass the agreement test with small (sometimes even sub-constant) probability (“1%”). This is a theorem of the form

Agree_{\eta}(\mathcal{F})=\delta\geqslant\varepsilon\quad\implies\quad\dist_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F},Glob)\leqslant 1-\poly(\delta).

for some
(\varepsilon,\eta,\eta^{\prime})
(and some explicit polynomial in
\delta
). We note here that in this test even when
\eta=0
, an
\eta^{\prime}>0
is sometimes unavoidable (see discussions in
[
DG08a
,
DL17
]
).

Agreement theorems in the low acceptance regime are typically harder to prove than in the high acceptance regime, since the promised structure on the family of functions is mild. However, in applications such that a parallel repetition theorem
[
IKW12
]
and low-degree testing
[
AS97
]
, they are often crucial. Dinur and Goldenberg
[
DG08a
]
proved a low acceptance agreement theorem on the complete complex. Their proof was simplified by
[
IKW12
]
, who also derived a more sophisticated Z-test (see below) and gave the first sparse structure supporting such agreement tests using subspaces. Work by
[
DL17
]
gave an improved analysis of
[
IKW12
]
, asymptotically matching the lower bound of a random set of local functions. Their work heavily relied on reverse hypercontractivity, a strategy we follow below. Recently,
[
DD23a
]
and
[
BM23a
]
gave characterizations of high dimensional expanders that support agreement theorems, and
[
DDL24
,
BLM24
]
constructed such complexes.

There are also other kinds of theorems about agreement tests that don’t strictly fall into any of these categories. One example such as
[
GK22
]
list agreement theorem, that inspired the former line
[
DD23a
,
BM23a
,
DDL24
,
BLM24
]
.

8.1.1
Varying the Test

We present two simple extensions to the agreement test above. The first comes from sampling more than two sets. For example, in the Z-test by
[
IKW12
]
, three sets
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}
are sampled, and the test passes if
f_{s_{1}}=f_{s_{2}}

and

f_{s_{2}}=f_{s_{3}}
(equality is with respect to the respective intersections). See the formal definition of this test below.

Another extension to the definition of a test, mainly for technical reasons, is a test that checks
f_{s_{1}}|_{t}=f_{s_{2}}|_{t}
only on some
t\subset s_{1}\cap s_{2}
which is also sampled by the tester. This is sometimes more convenient to analyze than the test checking agreement on the whole intersection. To summarize this discussion more formally, the distribution
D
in our setup samples
(\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k},\{t_{i,j}\}_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k})
such that
\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}\subseteq S
are sets, and for every
i<j
,
t_{i,j}\subseteq s_{i}\cap s_{j}
. The agreement test passes if for all
i<j
,
f_{s_{i}}|_{t_{i,j}}\overset{\eta}{\approx}f_{s_{j}}|_{t_{i,j}}
.

8.2
Agreement Testing for Subsets

We now move to the first setting of interest, agreement tests on simplicial complexes in the 99% regime. In particular we study the following setup due to
[
DFH19
]
. Let
j<k<d
and let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex. Let us denote by
SS(X,\Sigma,j,k,d)\coloneqq(U,S,\Sigma,D)
where:

1.

U=X{\left(j\right)}
.

2.

S=\left\{\binom{s}{j}\left|\;\vphantom{\binom{s}{j}s\in X{\left(d\right)}}\right.s\in X{\left(d\right)}\right\}
.

3.

The distribution
D=D_{d,k}
samples
s_{1},s_{2}
according to the down-up walk. The ‘test’ set
t\subseteq s_{1}\cap s_{2}
is a randomly chosen
k
-face.

For simplicity of notation we sometimes refer to the sets as
s
instead of
\binom{s}{j}
, and also index functions by
f_{s}
for
s\in X{\left(d\right)}
(and not
f_{\binom{s}{j}}
). However, in this context when writing
v\in s
, we mean a
j
-set instead of a vertex.

In other words, we study the following variant of the classical ‘V-test’ in this setting:

The V-test on
X{\left(k\right)}
:

1.

Draw
t\in X{\left(k\right)}
, and
s_{1},s_{2}\in X_{t}{\left(d\right)}
independently

2.

Accept if:

f_{t\cup s_{1}}|_{t}\overset{\eta}{\approx}f_{t\cup s_{2}}|_{t}

When
j=1
, this reduces to the heavily studied setting of (de-randomized) ‘direct product testing’.

We are now ready state the main result of the subsection, our generalized 99%-tester:

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
j,d
be integers, let
\gamma,\eta,c,\nu<1
. Denote by
k=\gamma d
. Suppose that
j<k
,
\eta\lfloor\frac{k}{j}\rfloor\geqslant 192\log\frac{16}{\eta}
and
\nu<1
. Let
X
be a
d
-uniform
c
-locally nice complex such that for every link of a set
v\in X{\left(j\right)}
,
\lambda(UD_{d-j,k-j}(X_{v}))\leqslant\nu
.
Then the following holds for the set system
SS(X,\Sigma,j,k,d)
; for every alphabet
\Sigma
, family
\mathcal{F}
, and
\varepsilon>0

Agree_{\eta}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon\quad\implies\quad\dist_{8\frac{\eta+\varepsilon}{1-\nu}}(\mathcal{F},g_{\text{maj}})\leqslant\varepsilon^{O_{\gamma,c}(1)}+2^{-\Omega_{\gamma,c}(d)}

(8.2)

where
g_{\text{maj}}\coloneqq\underset{s\in X{\left(d\right)}:s\supset v}{\text{plurality}}\{f_{s}(v)\}
is the plurality decoding.
12
12

12

Note that ties may be broken arbitrarily and the weighting is according to the (normalized) complex weights
\pi_{d}
.

We note that every
c
-nice complex has
\lambda(UD_{d-j,k-j})\leqslant\frac{k-j}{d-j}(1+\frac{1}{c^{\prime}(d-j)})^{(1-\gamma)(d-j)}
, so this is not an extra requirement in most cases (see
[
Lee23
, Theorem 3.5]
).

We remark that for the setup of
j=1
there exist agreement theorems that achieve (exact) closeness
1-O(\varepsilon)
for high dimensional expanders
[
DK17
,
DD19
,
KM20
]
.
Theorem
8.1
is incomparable to these results. On the one hand, in
Theorem
8.1
the resulting ‘globalness’ is weaker than
[
DK17
,
DD19
,
KM20
]
where one gets a guarantee of
\dist_{0}(\mathcal{F},g_{maj})\leqslant O(\varepsilon)
. On the other hand, the initial assumption on the sets of local functions in the aforementioned results is
Agree_{0}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
, wheras this result assumes
Agree_{\eta}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
for
\eta>0
. This relaxed assumption is crucial in the low-soundness regime theorem we give in the next section.

For
j>1
, to our knowledge
Theorem
8.1
is the first agreement theorem beyond the complete complex, where Dinur, Filmus, and Harsha
[
DFH19
]
showed

Agree_{0}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon\Rightarrow\dist_{0}(\mathcal{F},Glob)\geqslant 1-O_{j}(\varepsilon).

(8.3)

It is plausible that, with more effort,
(
8.3
)
could be proven directly on HDX. The advantage of our method in this sense lies in its generality and (relative) simplicity.

Proof of
Theorem
8.1
.

Given a family
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{s}\}
with high agreement, we show it can be (approximately) decoded to the majority function
G=g_{\text{maj}}
. We argue this can be inferred directly from reverse hypercontractivity and the following claim bounding the
average
probability of disagreement with
G
for a random pair
v\subseteq s
drawn from
(X{\left(j\right)},X{\left(d\right)})
:

Claim 8.2
.

\ProbOp_{{v\subset s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon+\eta}{1-\nu}.

We first show this implies the result. Define the set of ‘bad’
d
-faces which noticeably disagree with
G
as

B\coloneqq\left\{s\in S\left|\;\vphantom{s\in S\ProbOp_{{v\in s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]>\frac{8(\varepsilon+\eta)}{1-\nu}}\right.\ProbOp_{{v\in s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]>\frac{8(\varepsilon+\eta)}{1-\nu}\right\}.

It is enough to show

\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\leqslant\max\{\varepsilon^{O_{\gamma,c}(1)},\exp(-\Omega_{\gamma,c}(d))\}\}
. Toward bounding
B
, define an intermediary set
A
of ‘good’ faces:

A\coloneqq\left\{s\in S\left|\;\vphantom{s\in S\ProbOp_{{v\in s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]\leqslant\frac{4(\varepsilon+\eta)}{1-\nu}}\right.\ProbOp_{{v\in s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]\leqslant\frac{4(\varepsilon+\eta)}{1-\nu}\right\}

and note that
\ProbOp[A]\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
by
Claim
8.2
and Markov’s Inequality.
The idea is to argue that, on the one hand, reverse hypercontractivity implies the number of edges
(s_{1},s_{2})
between
A
and
B
is at least some power of
\ProbOp[B]
, while, on the other hand, agreement implies the number of such edges is at most
O(\varepsilon)
because they (typically) contain many
v\in s_{1}\cap s_{2}
that disagree and therefore fail the test.

More formally, denote the event that
(s_{1},s_{2})\in(A\times B)
as
E_{A,B}
. Assuming
\ProbOp[B]\geqslant\exp(-c^{\prime}d)
(for
c^{\prime}
depending on
c,\gamma
as in
Theorem
6.4
), reverse hypercontractivity lower bounds the measure of this event by

\ProbOp[E_{A,B}]\geqslant\left(\frac{\ProbOp[B]}{2}\right)^{q}

for some constant
q
depending only on
c,\gamma
. On the other hand, since the test rejects with probability
\varepsilon
, we can upper bound the measure of
E_{A,B}
by

O(\ProbOp\left[{\text{Test rejects}}\right])=O(\varepsilon)
we will obtain a bound of the form
\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\leqslant O(\varepsilon^{1/q})
. To this end, note

\ProbOp[E_{A,B}]\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{\ProbOp[\text{Test rejects}|\penalty\ E_{A,B}]}.

It is therefore enough to argue the conditional rejection probability is at least some constant (say
\frac{1}{2}
). Toward this end, for a face
s\in X{\left(d\right)}
, define

T_{s}\coloneqq\{v\in s:f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\}

to be the
j
-faces on which
f_{s}
disagrees with majority, and recall that an edge in our test is sampled by first picking
s_{1}\in X{\left(d\right)}
, then
t\subset s_{1}
in
X{\left(\gamma d\right)}
, then
s_{2}\supset t
conditionally. Given
t
, denote by
t(j)=\binom{t}{j}
the set of sub
j
-faces in
t
, for shortness. We define two bad events outside of which the test rejects and prove they occur with vanishing probability.

1.

Mis-sampling
\mathbf{A}
:

E_{1}
the event that
t
sees ‘too many’
v\subset s_{1}
that disagree with majority:

E_{1}\coloneqq\left\{\frac{|t(j)\cap T_{s_{1}}|}{\binom{k}{j}}\geqslant 5\frac{\varepsilon+\eta}{1+\gamma}\right\}

2.

Mis-sampling
\mathbf{B}
:

E_{2}
the event that
t
sees ‘too few’
v\subset s_{2}
that disagree with majority:

E_{2}\coloneqq\left\{\frac{|t(j)\cap T_{s_{2}}|}{\binom{k}{j}}\leqslant 7\frac{\varepsilon+\eta}{1+\gamma}\right\}

Conditioning on
\lnot E_{1}
and
\lnot E_{2}
the test rejects since
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
disagree on a
2\frac{\varepsilon+\eta}{1-\nu}
-fraction of
t
. Thus by a union bound:

\ProbOp[\text{Test Rejects}|\penalty\ E_{A,B}]\geqslant 1-\ProbOp[E_{1}|\penalty\ E_{A,B}]-\ProbOp[E_{2}|\penalty\ E_{A,B}]

and it is enough to show that each
\ProbOp[E_{i}|\penalty\ E_{A,B}]\leqslant\frac{1}{4}

Bounding
\mathbf{E_{1}}
and
\mathbf{E_{2}}

Events
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
occur with vanishing probability due to the sampling properties of the complete complex
\Delta
(
Claim
A.1
), namely that for any
\alpha,\delta>0
and
j\leqslant k
, the inclusion graph
(\Delta(k),\Delta(j))
is an
(\alpha,\frac{4}{\alpha\delta}\exp(\frac{\delta^{2}}{12}\alpha\left\lfloor\frac{k}{j}\right\rfloor),\delta)
-sampler.

With this in mind, recall
t\in X{\left(\gamma d\right)}
is drawn uniformly at random from
s_{1}
, so we may equivalently view
t
as being drawn from a
\gamma d
-uniform complete complex
\Delta
on the
d
vertices of
s_{1}
. By assumption,
s_{1}\in A
, so
T_{s_{1}}\subset\Delta(j)
is of measure at most
4\frac{\varepsilon+\eta}{\gamma}
, and sampling then implies

\ProbOp\left[\frac{|t(j)\cap T_{s_{1}}|}{\binom{k}{j}}>5\frac{\varepsilon+\eta}{1+\gamma}\right]\leqslant\frac{4(1+\gamma)}{\varepsilon+\eta}\exp\left(-\frac{\eta}{96}\left\lfloor\frac{k}{j}\right\rfloor\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

Since we can equivalently sample an edge
(s_{1},s_{2})
by first picking
s_{2}
, then
t\subset s_{2}
, then
s_{1}\supset t
,
E_{2}
can be bounded using the lower tail of
\Delta
in exactly the same fashion to get

\ProbOp\left[\frac{|t(j)\cap T_{s_{2}}|}{\binom{k}{j}}<7\frac{\varepsilon+\eta}{1+\gamma}\right]\leqslant\frac{4(1+\gamma)}{\varepsilon+\eta}\exp\left(-\frac{\eta}{192}\left\lfloor\frac{k}{j}\right\rfloor\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{4}.

∎

Proof of
Claim
8.2
.

Fix
v\in X{\left(j\right)}
and let
D^{(v)}_{d,k}
denote the conditional distribution over
(s_{1},t,s_{2})\sim D_{d,k}
such that
v\subset t
. Note that by construction,
D^{(v)}_{d,k}
(after removing
v
from each face) is distributed exactly as the down-up walk
D_{d-j,k-j}
within the link of
v
. Partition the vertices of this graph into sets
S^{(v)}_{\sigma}\coloneqq\{s|\penalty\ f_{s}(v)=\sigma\}_{\sigma\in\Sigma}
by the alphabet value they assign
v
, and observe that since the marginals of
D^{(v)}_{d,k}
are distributed as
X_{v}{\left(d-j\right)}
we have

1-\ProbOp_{{v\subset s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]=\ProbOp_{v\subset s}[f_{s}(v)=G(v)]=\E_{{v\sim{X{\left(j\right)}}}}\left[\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\left\{\ProbOp\left[S^{(v)}_{\sigma}\right]\right\}\right],

where the inner probability is over the
v
-conditioned marginal.

The idea, which is fairly standard (see e.g.
[
DK17
, Claim 5.2]
), is now to relate this maximum partition size to the
local
disagreement of
\mathcal{F}
using expansion. In particular, for each
v\in X{\left(j\right)}
define the
v
-local disagreement of
\mathcal{F}
as:

\varepsilon_{v}\coloneqq\ProbOp_{(s_{1},s_{2})\sim D_{d,k}^{(v)}}[f_{s_{1}}(v)\neq f_{s_{2}}(v)].

It is easy to see that
\E_{{v}}\left[\varepsilon_{v}\right]\leqslant\varepsilon+\eta
. This follows from observing that sampling a random
v\in X{\left(j\right)}
, then
(s_{1},t,s_{2})\sim D_{d,k}^{(v)}
is equivalent to
first
sampling
(s_{1},t,s_{2})\sim D_{d,k}
, then sampling
v
uniformly from
t
. The bound is clear from the latter interpretation since the tester rejects with probability at most
\varepsilon
, and otherwise agrees on all but an
\eta
fraction of the
j
-sets in
t
. It is therefore enough to prove

\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\left\{\ProbOp\left[S^{(v)}_{\sigma}\right]\right\}\geqslant 1-\frac{\varepsilon_{v}}{1-\nu},

as taking expectation on both sides gives the result.

Toward this end, define
E=E^{(v)}
to be the set of edges
(s_{1},s_{2})\sim D^{(v)}_{d,k}
that cross the alphabet partition. Since the marginal is distributed as the down-up walk in
v
’s link, this graph is a
\nu
-spectral expander by assumption. Writing
S_{\sigma}=S^{(v)}_{\sigma}
for simplicity of notation, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{v}

\displaystyle=\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2})}}\left[s_{1}\in S_{\sigma},s_{2}\notin S_{\sigma}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\langle{\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}},D^{(v)}_{d,k}({\mathbf{1}}-{\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}})\rangle

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\langle{\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}},D^{(v)}_{d,k}{\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}}\rangle

(8.4)

where
{\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}}
is the indicator of
S_{\sigma}
and
{\mathbf{1}}
is the all-ones function and the final equality is from by stochasticity of
D^{(v)}_{d,k}
. Let
f_{\sigma}={\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}}-\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]{\mathbf{1}}
be the projection of
{\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}}
onto
{\mathbf{1}}^{\perp}
. By orthogonality it holds that
\lVert f_{\sigma}\rVert^{2}=\lVert{\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}}\rVert^{2}-\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]^{2}\lVert{\mathbf{1}}\rVert^{2}=\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]-\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]^{2}
. Therefore exploiting the expansion of
D^{(v)}_{d,k}
we have

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}},D^{(v)}_{d,k}{\mathbf{1}}_{S_{\sigma}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]^{2}\langle{\mathbf{1}},{\mathbf{1}}\rangle+\langle f_{\sigma},D^{(v)}_{d,k}f_{\sigma}\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]^{2}+\nu\lVert f_{\sigma}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\nu\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]+(1-\nu)\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]^{2}.

Inserting this back to (
8.4
) we have that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{v}

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\left(\nu\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]+(1-\nu)\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=(1-\nu)\left(1-\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\ProbOp\left[{S_{\sigma}}\right]^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-\nu)\left(1-\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\left\{\ProbOp[S_{\sigma}]\right\}\right).

Re-arranging gives the desired result.
∎

8.2.1
Testing all the intersection

We note that one could also consider the standard V-test where
s
and
s^{\prime}
are still drawn from the down-up walk, but we test agreement over
t=s_{1}\cap s_{2}
(whereas in the definition of the test
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
may have some elements in their intersection not in
t
). The success in these tests are related by the following claim.

Claim 8.3
.

Let
\eta>0
. Let
D_{1}
be the test above. Let
D_{2}
be the test with a similar distribution to
D_{1}
, with the distinction that
t=s_{1}\cap s_{2}
. Then for any
\mathcal{F}
,
Agree_{\eta,D_{2}}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
implies that
Agree_{2\eta,D_{1}}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon-2^{-\Omega_{\gamma}(\eta^{2}k/j)}
.

Proof of
Claim
8.3
.

Let
s_{1},s_{2}
be a pair supported by
D_{2}
such that
\mathcal{F}
passes the
D_{2}
-test on the pair. Let
A
be the set of
j
-faces contained in
s_{1}\cap s_{2}
on which
f_{s_{1}},f_{s_{2}}
disagree on. By assumption
|A|\leqslant\eta|s_{1}\cap s_{2}|
. By the sampling properties of the complete complex
Claim
A.1
the fraction of
t\subseteq s_{1}\cap s_{2}
such that
|A\cap t|\geqslant 2\eta|t|
is
2^{-\Omega_{\gamma}(\eta^{2}k/j)}
. Thus the probability of
failing
the
D_{1}
test is at most the probability of failing the
D_{2}
test plus
2^{-\Omega_{\gamma}(\eta^{2}k/j)}
.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

When
\eta\lesssim\varepsilon
, the ‘additive’ error
\exp(-\Omega(d))
can be removed from
Theorem
8.1
by interpolating the result with a standard averaging argument over
Claim
8.2
. This technique stops working as soon as
\eta
is much larger than
\varepsilon
. While the additional
\exp(-\Omega(d))
error is not harmful in most applications, it is nevertheless interesting to ask whether the term can be removed for general
\eta
.

8.2.2
Agreement Testing for the non-Lazy Down-Up Walk

Using a similar technique, we can also prove an agreement test for the following distribution based on the swap walks. Define the test distribution
(s_{1},s_{2},t)\sim A_{d,k}
by the following procedure:

1.

Sample
t\in X{\left(k\right)}
.

2.

Sample
r_{1},r_{2}\in X{\left(d-k\right)}
in the swap-walk inside
X_{t}
.

3.

Output
s_{i}=t\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}r_{i}
and
t
.

The resulting edge distribution over
(s_{1},s_{2})
is sometimes called the ‘partial’ swap walk
[
AJT19
]
. Note this walk has the property that the intersection is fixed. The following claim shows that when the local swap walks expand, an agreement theorem for this walk follows as well.

Claim 8.5
.

Let
j,d
be integers, let
\gamma,\eta,c,\lambda,\nu<1
. Let
k=\gamma d
and suppose
j<k
,
\eta\lfloor\frac{k}{j}\rfloor\geqslant 192\log\frac{16}{\eta}
and
\nu<1
. Let
X
be a
(2d-k)
-uniform
c
-locally nice complex such that for every
v\in X{\left(j\right)}
,
\lambda(A_{d-j,k-j}(X_{v}))\leqslant\nu
. Suppose additionally that for every
t\in X{\left(k\right)}
,
S_{d-k,d-k}(X_{t})\leqslant\lambda
.
Then the following holds for the set system
SS(X,\Sigma,j,k,d)
; for every alphabet
\Sigma
, family
\mathcal{F}
, and
\varepsilon>0

Agree_{\eta}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon\quad\implies\quad\dist_{8\frac{\eta+\varepsilon}{1-\nu}}(\mathcal{F},g_{\text{maj}})\leqslant\varepsilon^{O_{\gamma,C}(1)}+\lambda^{O_{\gamma,C}(1)}.

(8.5)

where
g_{\text{maj}}\coloneqq\underset{s\in X{\left(d\right)}:s\supset v}{\text{plurality}}\{f_{s}(v)\}
is the plurality decoding.

Proof of
Claim
8.5
.

As in the proof of
Theorem
8.1
, we define

B\coloneqq\left\{s\in S\left|\;\vphantom{s\in S\ProbOp_{{v\in s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]>\frac{8(\varepsilon+\eta)}{1-\nu}}\right.\ProbOp_{{v\in s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]>\frac{8(\varepsilon+\eta)}{1-\nu}\right\}.

and

A\coloneqq\left\{s\in S\left|\;\vphantom{s\in S\ProbOp_{{v\in s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]\leqslant\frac{4(\varepsilon+\eta)}{1-\nu}}\right.\ProbOp_{{v\in s}}\left[f_{s}(v)\neq G(v)\right]\leqslant\frac{4(\varepsilon+\eta)}{1-\nu}\right\}.

As in the proof of
Theorem
8.1
,
\ProbOp[A]\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
by an argument similar to
Claim
8.2
and Markov’s inequality. More precisely, we need to repeat the proof of
Claim
8.2
, only instead of using the down-up walk conditioned on
v
, we use
A_{d,k}
conditioned on
v\in t
. This is just
A_{d-j,k-j}
inside
X_{v}
.

In addition, as in the proof of
Theorem
8.1
, it also holds that the relative fraction of edges crossing between
A
and
B
is small, i.e.
\ProbOp\left[{E_{A,B}}\right]\leqslant 2\varepsilon
.

Our goal is to show that

\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\leqslant\max\{\varepsilon^{\Omega_{\gamma,c}(1)},\exp(-\Omega_{\gamma,c}(d)),\lambda^{\Omega_{\gamma,c}(1)}\}
.

Assume otherwise, and that in particular reverse hypercontractivity for indicators applies for sets of size
\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]
. By
Theorem
6.4

\ProbOp_{{t\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{s\supseteq t}}\left[A\right]\cdot\ProbOp_{{s\supseteq t}}\left[B\right]\geqslant(\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\ProbOp\left[{B}\right])^{O_{\gamma,c}(1)}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{O_{\gamma,c}(1)}.

Let us denote by
G
the set of
t
satisfying the above condition.
Suppose we sampled
t\in G
in the first step of our agreement distribution. Recall that now we sample and edge by going in the swap walk in the link of
t
. The probability we sampled an edge between
A
and
B
is therefore

\ProbOp_{{r_{1},r_{2}\sim S(X_{t})}}\left[t\cup r_{1}\in A,t\cup r_{2}\in B\right]\geqslant(\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\ProbOp\left[{B}\right])^{O_{\gamma,c}(1)}-\lambda=p\cdot\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{O_{\gamma,c}(1)}-\lambda

by the expansion of the swap walk and the expander mixing lemma (for some constant
p
).
Thus if
p\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{O_{\gamma,c}(1)}\geqslant 2\lambda
then we have that

\Omega(\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{O_{c,\gamma}(1)})\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{G}\right]\ProbOp\left[s_{1}\in A,s_{2}\in B\left|\;\vphantom{s_{1}\in A,s_{2}\in BG}\right.G\right]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{E_{A,B}}\right]\leqslant 4\varepsilon

and the claim follows.
∎

8.3
Low Soundness and the Z-Test

In this section we will apply
Theorem
8.1
and reverse hypercontractivity to prove soundness of
[
IKW12
]
’s Z-test in the
1\%
-regime under certain stronger assumptions on the complex. Before moving on, we briefly comment on the notation in this subsection. Unlike before, we will use the convention that
s,t
are
\frac{k}{2}
-sets (and not
k
-sets as in the previous subsections). We will typically denote
k
-sets by a capital letter instead. We first recall their test:

The Z-test on
X{\left(k\right)}
:

1.

Draw
t\in X{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
, and
s,s^{\prime}\in X_{t}{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
independently

2.

Draw
s^{\prime\prime}\sim X_{s^{\prime}}{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}

3.

Accept if:

f_{t\cup s}=f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{s^{\prime}\cup t}=f_{s^{\prime}\cup s^{\prime\prime}}

We prove soundness for complexes which are ‘
\lambda
-global’, a slight strengthening of
\ell_{\infty}
-independence
[
KKS21
]
:

Definition 8.6

(Global Complex)
.

A
k
-uniform complex
X
is called
\lambda
-global if
\forall t\in X{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
:

\lVert S_{t}-\pi_{\frac{k}{2}}\rVert_{TV}\leqslant\lambda

where
S_{t}
is the distribution over neighbors of
t
in
S_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}
and
\pi_{\frac{k}{2}}
is the induced distribution on
X{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
.

Globality of
X
essentially promises that every
\frac{k}{2}
-set in
X
‘sees’ most other
\frac{k}{2}
-sets in
X
, and is equivalent to bounding the matrix infinity norm of the swap walk from its stationary operator
\frac{1}{2}\lVert S_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}-\Pi_{\frac{k}{2}}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant\lambda
. We note that the theorem below really only relies on a somewhat weaker average-case bound on the
1
-norm of the rows of
S_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}-\Pi_{\frac{k}{2}}
. However, we are not aware of any complexes satisfying such a notion that are not already global in the defined sense, so we stick with this more standard requirement below.

Theorem 8.7
.

\forall\lambda,\eta>0
and large enough
k
, let
X
be a
k
-uniform,
\lambda
-global,
c
-locally nice complex. Then for any
\delta\in(8\lambda+e^{-\Omega_{c}(\eta k)},\frac{1}{8})
if
Agree_{0}^{Z}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant\delta
:

\exists g:X{\left(0\right)}\to\Sigma,\;\;\ProbOp_{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}[f_{s}\overset{\eta}{\approx}g(s)]\geqslant\delta/8.

A couple of remarks are in order. First, we mention that it is well known that no tester can do better than
\exp(-\Omega(k))
soundness
[
DG08a
,
DL17
]
, so our bound is essentially optimal for small
\lambda
. Second, we remark that many (dense) complexes of interest are indeed
\lambda
-global HDX, including basic examples such as the complete complex and random complexes, and more involved examples such as skeletons of various Ising models, independent sets, list-colorings, or more generally essentially any of the myriad complexes studied in the approximate sampling literature (see e.g.
[
ALG21
,
Liu21
,
CLV21
,
Fen+22
,
Bla+22
]
among many others).
Theorem
8.7
shows the Z-test is sound on these families in a blackbox fashion, even up to
\exp(-\eta k)
for the appropriate parameters. We elaborate on these examples further in the end of this section.

The proof of
Theorem
8.7
largely follows the strategy of
[
IKW12
,
DL17
]
. The main difference lies in replacing certain ad-hoc arguments for the complete complex with more general methods based on reverse hypercontractivity and globality.

8.3.1
A Local Agreement Theorem

The core of
Theorem
8.7
is a
local
structure theorem for the V-test on high dimensional expanders. Stating this requires a few definitions. We remark that these are standard notions in the literature, and we follow the notation of
[
DL17
]
. We first define
restrictions
.

Definition 8.8

(Restriction)
.

A
restriction
is a pair
\tau=(t,\sigma)
such that
t\in X{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
and
\sigma:t\to\Sigma
is an assignment to
t
.

Given a family of functions as above, there is a natural distribution over restrictions that comes from sampling a random function and a random restriction of it. More formally:

1.

Sample
t\in X{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
and
s\in X_{t}{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
.

2.

Output
\tau=(t,f_{s\cup t}|_{t})
.

Whenever we mention a distribution over restrictions
\tau
we always mean
\tau=(t,f_{s\cup t}|_{t})
sampled as above. Given such a restriction
\tau
, it will also be useful to have notation for the faces in
X
that are
consistent
with
\tau
.

Definition 8.9

(Consistent Strings)
.

Given a restriction
\tau=(t,\sigma)
, denote by
\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
the set of faces
s\in X_{t}{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
consistent with
\tau
:

\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\coloneqq\left\{s\in X_{t}{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}:f_{s\cup t}|_{t}=\sigma\right\}

A restriction is called
good
if it has many consistent faces.

Definition 8.10

(Good Restriction)
.

A restriction
\tau=(t,\sigma)
is called
\delta
-good if
\ProbOp_{{s\in X_{t}}}\left[\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\right]\geqslant\frac{\delta}{2}
.

When
\delta
is clear from context, we just call such restrictions good. Finally, a restriction is called ‘DP’ if its consistent strings agree with a global function (‘DP’ is for ‘direct product’).

Definition 8.11

(DP-Restriction)
.

A restriction
\tau=(t,\sigma)
is called
(\eta,\delta)
-DP if there exists
g_{\tau}:X_{t}{\left(1\right)}\to\Sigma
such that:

\ProbOp_{s\sim X_{t}}\left[f_{t\cup s}\overset{\eta/4}{\not\approx}g_{\tau}\penalty\ \bigg|\penalty\ s\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\right]\leqslant\delta^{2}

As before, when
\eta,\delta
are clear from context we just write ‘DP’.
We can now state the local agreement theorem for high dimensional expanders.

Theorem 8.12

(The Local Agreement Theorem)
.

For any
\eta>0
and
k
sufficiently large, let
X
be a
k
-uniform
c
-locally nice complex. Then for all
\delta>e^{-\Omega(\eta k)}
, if
Agree^{V}_{0}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant\delta
:

1.

Many restrictions are good:

\ProbOp_{\tau}[\text{$\tau$ is good}]\geqslant\frac{\delta}{2}

2.

Almost all ‘good’ restrictions are DP:

\ProbOp_{\tau}[\text{$\tau$ is DP $|$ $\tau$ is good}]\geqslant 1-\delta^{2}

We remark that while
Theorem
8.12
does not give a true agreement tester in its own right, it is a powerful tool independent of
Theorem
8.7
. It roughly states that good agreement implies a non-negligible fraction of good restrictions,
and
that if
f_{s}
agrees with a restriction of a face
t
it is almost certainly a restriction of a global assignment on the link
X_{t}
. This tool is a critical part of many of the previous works on agreement testing
[
DG08a
,
DS14
,
IKW12
,
DL17
]
.

We prove
Theorem
8.12
in the next section. We first show it implies the main theorem under the additional assumption of
\lambda
-globality. The argument is a simple adaptation of
[
IKW12
]
.

Proof of
Theorem
8.7
.

As in
[
IKW12
]
, we replace the standard Z-test in
Figure
8.3
with a related proxy test where we first sample
(t,s)
as before, but then draw
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})

independent of

(t,s)
. If
s^{\prime}\in X_{t}
we continue as in the Z-test, and if
s^{\prime}\notin X_{t}
we accept. The probability of passing the new test is only greater or equal to the probability of passing the original test, but by
\lambda
-globalness, the swap walk starting at
t
is
\lambda
-close to the stationary distribution hence the probability that
s^{\prime}
misses the link of
t
is at most
\lambda
.

With this in mind, let
\tau
denote the restriction
(t,f_{s\cup t}|_{t})
and observe that if
\tau
is not good, the new Z-test accepts with probability at most
\delta/2+\lambda\leqslant\delta
. Thus it must be the case that conditioned on
\tau
being good, the test still accepts with probability at least
\delta
. Condition on this event, and observe that by
Theorem
8.12
,
\tau
is also a DP-restriction except with probability at most
\delta^{2}\leqslant\delta/8
(recall that
\delta<\frac{1}{8}
).

Fix such a DP restriction
\tau
, and denote by
B
the set of faces in
\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
on which
\mathcal{F}
is
\eta/4
-close to the DP function
g_{\tau}
promised by
Theorem
8.12
(if there is more than one, take any such function).
Assume toward contradiction that
\mathcal{F}
is not
(\eta,\delta/8)
-close to
g_{\tau}
. Let
S=s^{\prime}\cup s^{\prime\prime}
and let
H
denote the event that
f_{S}\overset{\eta}{\approx}g_{\tau}
.
13
13

13

Formally here we extend
g_{\tau}
to a function on
X{\left(1\right)}
by arbitrarily assigning values to any vertex in
X{\left(1\right)}\setminus X_{t}{\left(1\right)}
.
By assumption
\ProbOp\left[{H}\right]\leqslant\frac{\delta}{8}
, because
S
is drawn as in the distribution of
X{\left(k\right)}
. We define four events, outside which the test rejects:

1.

E_{1}
: The event that
s^{\prime}\notin X_{t}
,

2.

E_{2}
: The event that
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\setminus B
,

3.

E_{3}
: The event that
S\in H
,

4.

E_{4}
: The event that
S\notin H
, and
f_{S}|_{s^{\prime}}\overset{\eta/2}{\approx}g_{\tau}|_{s^{\prime}}
.

We first argue the test rejects if none of the
E_{i}
occurs. In particular, note by
\overline{E_{1}}\cap\overline{E_{2}}
,
s^{\prime}
must either be in
B
, or in
X_{t}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
. In the latter case, the first query of the proxy Z-test rejects since
s^{\prime}\in X_{t}
but
f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}\neq f_{t\cup s}
. On the other hand, in the former case
s^{\prime}\in B
and therefore
f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}|_{s^{\prime}}\overset{\eta/4}{\approx}g_{\tau}|_{s^{\prime}}
. However, by
\overline{E_{3}}\cap\overline{E_{4}}
, we have
S\notin H
and, moreover, that the restriction to
s^{\prime}
has disagreement at least
f_{S}|_{s^{\prime}}\overset{\frac{\eta}{2}}{\napprox}g_{\tau}|_{s^{\prime}}
. Thus there must be a vertex on which
f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}
and
f_{S}
disagree and the second query rejects.

Finally by a union bound it is enough to prove
\sum\ProbOp[E_{i}]\leqslant\delta/2
. The first event occurs with probability at most
\delta/8
by
\lambda
-globality where
\lambda<\frac{\delta}{8}
. The second event occurs with probability at most
\delta^{2}\leqslant\delta/8
by
Theorem
8.12
. The third event occurs with probability at most
\delta/8
by assumption. The final event occurs with probability at most
e^{-\Omega(\eta k)}\leqslant\delta/8
by Chernoff, since
f_{S}

\eta
-disagrees with
g_{\tau}
and
s^{\prime}
is a random subset, which completes the proof.
∎

8.3.2
Proof of the Local Agreement Theorem

Let us begin by proving the first property in
Theorem
8.12
.

Claim 8.13
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem
8.12
,
\ProbOp_{{\tau}}\left[\tau\text{ is good}\right]\geqslant\frac{\delta}{2}
.

Proof.

Let
p=\ProbOp_{{\tau}}\left[\tau\text{ is good}\right]
.
Since
Agree^{V}_{0}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant\delta
and we have

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\leqslant Agree^{V}_{0}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle=p\ProbOp_{{s,t,s^{\prime}}}\left[f_{t\cup s}=f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}\left|\;\vphantom{f_{t\cup s}=f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}(t,f|_{s\cup t})\text{ is good}}\right.(t,f|_{s\cup t})\text{ is good}\right]+(1-p)\ProbOp_{{s,t,s^{\prime}}}\left[f_{t\cup s}=f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}\left|\;\vphantom{f_{t\cup s}=f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}(t,f|_{s\cup t})\text{ is not good}}\right.(t,f|_{s\cup t})\text{ is not good}\right].

By definition
\ProbOp_{{s,t,s^{\prime}}}\left[f_{t\cup s}=f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}\left|\;\vphantom{f_{t\cup s}=f_{t\cup s^{\prime}}(t,f|_{s\cup t})\text{ is not good}}\right.(t,f|_{s\cup t})\text{ is not good}\right]\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2}
so
\delta\leqslant p+\frac{\delta}{2}
which implies the claim.
∎

The rest of this section is dedicated to proving the second property in
Theorem
8.12
, which is more complicated. The proof has two main steps. First, we will need a variant of the notion of an
excellent restriction
, that appeared in
[
DG08a
,
IKW12
,
DL17
]
. Roughly, an excellent restriction is a restriction
(t,\sigma)
so that if
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathcal{V}_{t}
, the probability that
f_{t\cup s_{1}}|_{s_{1}\cap s_{2}}\approx f_{t\cup s_{2}}|_{s_{1}\cap s_{2}}
is
1-\poly(\delta)
. The probability in which we choose
s_{1},s_{2}\in X_{t}
is stated below. We show most good restrictions are in fact excellent.

Second, we show that excellent restrictions are
DP
. Here we use a variant of the “smoothing” technique introduced by
[
DL17
]
in order to get an ensemble of local functions
\left\{\tilde{f}_{s}:s\to\Sigma\left|\;\vphantom{\tilde{f}_{s}:s\to\Sigma s\in X_{t}{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}}\right.s\in X_{t}{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}\right\}
that agree with high probability
1-\poly(\delta)
and use our
99\%
-tester (
Theorem
8.1
) to show the restriction is
DP
.

Formally we require some additional notation to properly define these notions. First, denote the
\frac{k}{4}
-step down-up walk in the link of
t
as
N_{t}
and denote by
(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}
the distribution where
s,s^{\prime}
are chosen according to
N_{t}
and
t^{\prime}
is the intermediate set chosen in the first down step (we note that
t^{\prime}\subseteq s\cap s^{\prime}
but equality doesn’t necessarily hold). Second, let
N_{t}^{3}
denote three steps of
N_{t}
. Let
(s_{1},t_{1},s_{2},t_{2},s_{3},t_{3},s_{4})\sim N_{t}^{3}
be the full walk sampled. Finally, let
(s_{1},t_{2},t^{\prime},s_{4})\sim N_{t}^{3}
be such that
s_{1},t_{2},s_{4}
are the marginals as before, and
t^{\prime}=t_{1}\cap t_{2}\cap t_{3}
the vertices that are in all sets in the sampled walk.

Fix some sufficiently small constant
c_{1}>0
. We can now define excellence.

Definition 8.14

(Excellent Restriction)
.

A good restriction
\tau=(t,\sigma)
is called excellent if:

1.

\ProbOp_{(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}}\left[(s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau})\land\left(f_{s}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta/1500}{\not\approx}f_{s^{\prime}}|_{t^{\prime}}\right)\right]\leqslant e^{-c_{1}\eta k}
and,

2.

\ProbOp_{(s_{1},t_{2},t^{\prime},s_{4})\sim N_{t}^{3}}\left[(s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau})\land\left(f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta/1500}{\not\approx}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime}}\right)\right]\leqslant e^{-c_{1}\eta k}.

where we’ve abused notation and written
f_{s}
as shorthand for
f_{s\cup t}
.

We note that this definition is slightly cumbersome due to the fact that we don’t measure the agreement of
f_{s},f_{s^{\prime}}
(respectively
f_{s_{1}},f_{s_{4}}
) on the intersection of
s\cap s^{\prime}
(respectively
s_{1}\cap s_{4}
), but instead on a fixed subset. This is for technical reasons and we encourage the readers to think of the case of
N_{t}
where
t^{\prime}=s\cap s^{\prime}
(and analogously for
N_{t}^{3}
).

For notational convenience, let
\mu\coloneqq e^{-c_{1}\eta k}
and
\eta^{\prime}=\eta/1500
. The following lemma (or variants thereof) is fairly standard
[
Imp+08
,
IKW12
,
DL17
]
:

Lemma 8.15

(Good restrictions are excellent)
.

There exists
c_{2}>0
such that

\ProbOp_{{\tau=(t,f_{s\cup t}|_{t})}}\left[\tau\text{ is good but not excellent}\right]\leqslant e^{-c_{2}\eta k}.

Proof.

We only prove that
N_{t}^{3}
case, which is somewhat less standard. The
N_{t}
proof is analogous. It is sufficient to show that over a random restriction
\tau=(t,f_{s_{0}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}t}|_{t})
:

\ProbOp_{\tau=(t,f_{s_{0}\cup t}|_{t})}\left[\ProbOp_{(s_{1},t_{2},t^{\prime},s_{4})\sim N_{t}^{3}}\left[s_{1},s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{t}\land f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime}}\right]>e^{-c\eta k}\right]\leqslant e^{-\Omega(\eta k)}

(8.6)

where
s_{1},t_{2},t^{\prime},s_{4}
is chosen independently of
s_{0}
. In particular if this holds then, by claim
Claim
8.13
, conditioning on being good can increase the probability of this event to hold to at most
\frac{2e^{-\Omega(\eta k)}}{\delta}\leqslant e^{-c_{2}\eta k}
for the right choice of constants.

The key to
(
8.6
)
is to observe that this process can be sampled by the following equivalent method: sample
T\in X{\left(\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k}{4}\right)}
, and randomly partition it into
T=t\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}t_{2}
so that
|t|=\frac{k}{2}
and
|t_{2}|=\frac{k}{4}
. The faces
s_{1}
,
t_{2}
,
t_{3}
, and
s_{4}
are now drawn independently from the "up-down-up" walk
U_{\frac{k}{4},\frac{k}{2}}D_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{4}}U_{\frac{k}{4},\frac{k}{2}}
within
X_{t}
starting from
t_{2}
.

Observe that
|t^{\prime}|\geqslant\frac{k}{32}
with probability
e^{-\Omega(k)}
by a standard Chernoff bound.
Conditioning on this event, observe that if
s_{1},s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
and
f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime}}
, then it must be the case that

1.

t_{2}
has at least
\frac{\eta^{\prime}k}{32}
elements on which
f_{s_{1}}\neq f_{s_{4}}

2.

t
has no elements on which
f_{s_{1}}|_{t}\neq f_{s_{4}}|_{t}
.

However,
t
and
t_{2}
are a uniformly random partition of
T
, so such a split occurs with probability at most
e^{-\Omega(\eta k)}
by Chernoff as desired.
∎

To proving excellent restrictions are DP, we’ll use a variant of Dinur and Livni-Navon’s ‘smoothing’ operation that spreads the consistent strings
\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
over the entire link. Instead of using the noise operator as in their work, we use the down-up walk which avoids a number of technical complications.

Definition 8.16

(Smoothed Assignment)
.

For every
\tau=(t,f_{t\cup s_{0}}|_{t})
, define the smoothed assignment
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}=\{\tilde{f}_{s}\}_{s\in X_{t}}
as

\tilde{f}_{s}(v)=\underset{(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}(s):s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau},t^{\prime}\ni v}{\text{Plurality}}\{f_{s^{\prime}}(v)\}.

We break ties arbitrarily. If the plurality is not well defined (i.e. there are no such
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
that contain
v
) we define it to be
\tilde{f}_{s}(v)=\bot
.

The main idea is to show that on excellent restrictions,
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}
is 1) highly consistent with the original family
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
, and 2) has very high agreement on the
entire
link. This reduces the problem to an (approximate) 99%-regime test within the link of
t
, which we can solve via our tester from the previous section. More formally, the following lemmata suffice to prove
Theorem
8.12
.

Lemma 8.17
.

Every excellent
\tau=(t,\sigma)
satisfies:

\ProbOp_{s\sim X_{t}}\left[f_{s}\overset{10\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}\tilde{f}_{s}\penalty\ \Bigg|\penalty\ s\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\right]\leqslant\frac{\delta^{2}}{2},

that is,
\mathcal{F}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
are close on
\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
(note
f_{s}\overset{10\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}\tilde{f}_{s}
is over just the vertices of
s
, not
s\cup t
)

Lemma 8.18
.

There exists
c_{3}>0
such that every excellent
\tau=(t,\sigma)
satisfies

\ProbOp_{(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}}\left[\tilde{f}_{s}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{10\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}\tilde{f}_{s^{\prime}}|_{t^{\prime}}\right]\leqslant e^{-c_{3}\eta k},

that is,
\mathcal{\widetilde{F}}
is highly agreeing on
X_{t}
.

Proof of
Theorem
8.12
.

The first property is proved in
Claim
8.13
. Toward the second, we will prove that every excellent
\tau
is a
DP
-restriction. Then by
Lemma
8.15
we have that

\ProbOp_{{\tau}}\left[\tau\text{ is DP}\left|\;\vphantom{\tau\text{ is DP}\tau\text{ is good}}\right.\tau\text{ is good}\right]\geqslant\ProbOp_{{\tau}}\left[\tau\text{ is excellent}\left|\;\vphantom{\tau\text{ is excellent}\tau\text{ is good}}\right.\tau\text{ is good}\right]\geqslant 1-\delta^{2}

where we’ve used the assumption that
\delta\geqslant e^{-\Omega(\eta k)}
.
Fix any excellent
\tau=(t,\sigma)
. One can check these satisfy the conditions of
Theorem
8.1
with
\nu=1/2
. Together with
Lemma
8.18
and
Claim
8.3
that translates between the agreement guarantee in
Lemma
8.18
to that of
Theorem
8.1
, we have that there exists a global function
g:X_{t}{\left(0\right)}\to\Sigma
such that

\ProbOp_{s\sim X_{t}}[\tilde{f}_{s}\overset{320\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}g|_{s}]\leqslant e^{-c_{4}\eta k}

where
c_{4}
is some constant depending on
c_{3}
,
\eta
and the
c
-local niceness of
X
.
We note that after conditioning over
s\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
, we still have that this is at most
\frac{2e^{-c_{4}\eta k}}{\delta}\leqslant\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}
, where this holds by assumption that
\delta
is large enough.

Combining this with
Lemma
8.17
, we then have

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{s\sim X_{t}}}\left[f_{s}\overset{\eta/4}{\not\approx}g(s)\left|\;\vphantom{f_{s}\overset{\eta/4}{\not\approx}g(s)s\in\mathcal{V}_{t}}\right.s\in\mathcal{V}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp_{s\sim X_{t}}\left[f_{s}\overset{10\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}\tilde{f}_{s}\penalty\ \Bigg|\penalty\ s\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\right]+\ProbOp_{{s\sim X_{t}}}\left[\tilde{f}_{s}\overset{320\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}g(s)\left|\;\vphantom{\tilde{f}_{s}\overset{320\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}g(s)s\in\mathcal{V}_{t}}\right.s\in\mathcal{V}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta^{2}

as desired.
∎

It is left to prove the key lemmata. This is the main place we use reverse hypercontractivity (other than the application of
Theorem
8.1
in the proof of
Theorem
8.12
). The proofs for both properties follow the strategy of
[
DL17
]
adapted to our setting.

Proof of
Lemma
8.17
.

We define two families of ‘bad’ sets outside of which
f
and
\tilde{f}
approximately agree. First, we look at the set of ‘lonely’ strings that don’t sufficiently see
\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
.

L\coloneqq\left\{s\in X_{t}:\ProbOp_{s^{\prime}\sim N_{t}(s)}[s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}]\leqslant\delta^{\prime}\right\}

for
\delta^{\prime}=e^{-c_{5}\eta k}
for some
c_{5}>0
sufficiently small. We will assume below that
\delta^{\prime}\leqslant\delta^{4}
(which is possible due to the assumptions on
\delta
).
We record the following properties of
L
that we prove at the end of the subsection.

Claim 8.19
.

There exists a sufficiently small constant
c_{5}>0
such that the following holds for
\delta^{\prime}=e^{-c_{5}\eta k}
.

1.

\ProbOp_{{s\in X_{t}}}\left[L\right]\leqslant\frac{\delta^{\prime 2}}{2}
.

2.

For any
s\notin L
, the fraction of vertices
v\in s
s.t.
\ProbOp_{{(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}(s)}}\left[s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\left|\;\vphantom{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}t^{\prime}\ni v}\right.t^{\prime}\ni v\right]\leqslant\delta^{\prime}/3
is at most
\eta^{\prime}
.

Second, we look at the family of sets within
\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
with strong disagreement:

B\coloneqq\left\{s\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}:\ProbOp_{(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}(s)}\left[s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\land f_{s}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s^{\prime}}|_{t^{\prime}}\right]\geqslant\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{30}\right\}.

We claim that both
L
and
B
have measure at most
\delta^{2}/2
within
\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
. The former follows from the first item of
Claim
8.19
since
\ProbOp[L|\mathcal{V}_{\tau}]\leqslant\frac{\delta^{\prime 2}}{\delta}\leqslant\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}
. For the latter we have
\ProbOp[B]\leqslant\frac{30\mu}{\delta^{\prime}}\leqslant\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}
from Markov’s inequality and excellence of
\tau
(here we also need that the
c_{5}
in the definition of
\delta^{\prime}
is sufficiently smaller than the
c_{1}
in the definition of
\mu
).

It is left to observe that
f_{s}\overset{10\eta^{\prime}}{\approx}\tilde{f}_{s}
for any face
s\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\setminus(L\cup B)
. Fix such an
s
, and define
D_{0}
to be the set of vertices on which
f
and
\tilde{f}
disagree:

D_{0}\coloneqq\left\{v\in s:f_{s}(v)\neq\tilde{f}_{s}(v)\right\}.

We need to argue
|D_{0}|\leqslant 10\eta^{\prime}|s|
. Assume toward contradiction otherwise. Let
D^{\prime}
denote the vertices in
s
such that
\ProbOp_{{(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}(s)}}\left[s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\left|\;\vphantom{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}t^{\prime}\ni v}\right.t^{\prime}\ni v\right]\leqslant\delta^{\prime}/3
and write
D=D_{0}\setminus D^{\prime}
. By
Claim
8.19
, we have
|D|\geqslant 9\eta^{\prime}|s|
.

Consider now the following probabilistic experiment. Sample
v\in D
uniformly, then some
t^{\prime},s^{\prime}\sim N_{t}(s)
conditioned on
v\in t^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
. We denote this distribution by
\mathcal{D}
. Note that for every fixed
v\in D
, this is exactly the distribution over which the plurality in the definition of
\tilde{f}_{s}(v)
was defined.

With this in mind consider the event
P=\{f_{s}(v)\neq f_{s^{\prime}}(v)\}
and observe that
\ProbOp_{{\mathcal{D}}}\left[P\right]\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
because for every fixed
v\in D
, by construction
f_{s}(v)
disagrees with the plurality vote on
v
. On the other hand, we show toward contradiction that
P
is contained in events whose total probability is strictly less than
\frac{1}{2}
. Namely:

1.

E_{1}
: the event
\{f_{s}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\napprox}f_{s^{\prime}}|_{t^{\prime}}\}

2.

E_{2}
: the event
|t^{\prime}\cap D|<6\eta^{\prime}|t^{\prime}|

3.

E_{3}
: the event
P\setminus(E_{1}\cup E_{2})
.

Obviously
P\subseteq E_{1}\cup E_{2}\cup E_{3}
so it is enough to bound the probability of the three events, starting with
E_{3}
. In this case we have that
f_{s},f_{s^{\prime}}
disagree on at most an
\eta^{\prime}
-fraction of
t^{\prime}
, but
|t^{\prime}\cap D|\geqslant 6\eta^{\prime}|t^{\prime}|
. Conditioned on
t^{\prime}
,
v
is drawn uniformly from
|D\cap t^{\prime}|
, so the probability that
f_{s}(v)\neq f_{s^{\prime}}(v)
is at most
\frac{1}{6}
.

Moving on to
E_{2}
, notice that if
D
is an
9\eta^{\prime}
-fraction of
s
, a random
t^{\prime}\subset s
contains
less than a

6\eta^{\prime}
-fraction of
D
with probability
\exp(-\Omega(\eta^{\prime 2}k))
by Chernoff. While the distribution
\mathcal{D}
doesn’t sample
t^{\prime}
uniformly (since it conditions on
v\in t^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
), we do know for any fixed
v\in D
,
v\in t^{\prime}
with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
, and because
s\notin L
and
v\notin D^{\prime}
the probability that
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
is at least
\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{3}
even after conditioning on
v
. Hence, even after conditioning on these events the probability that
|t^{\prime}\cap D|<6\eta^{\prime}|t^{\prime}|
-fraction of
D
is at most
2\delta^{-1}\exp(-\Omega(\eta^{\prime 2}k))<\frac{1}{10}
for large enough
k
.

Finally, we bound the probability of
E_{1}
by similar reasoning.

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{\mathcal{D}}}\left[E_{1}\right]=\ProbOp_{{v\sim D,(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}(s)}}\left[f_{s}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s^{\prime}}|_{t^{\prime}}\left|\;\vphantom{f_{s}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s^{\prime}}|_{t^{\prime}}s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau},v\in t^{\prime}}\right.s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau},v\in t^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{3}{\delta^{\prime}}\ProbOp_{{v\sim D,s^{\prime}\sim N_{t}(s)}}\left[s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\land f_{s}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s^{\prime}}|_{t^{\prime}}\left|\;\vphantom{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\land f_{s}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s^{\prime}}|_{t^{\prime}}v\in t^{\prime}}\right.v\in t^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{s\notin B}{\leqslant}\frac{3}{\delta^{\prime}}\cdot\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{30}\cdot\ProbOp\left[{v\in t^{\prime}}\right]^{-1}\leqslant\frac{1}{5}.

Thus
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{P}\right]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{E_{1}}\right]+\ProbOp\left[{E_{2}}\right]+\ProbOp\left[{E_{3}}\right]<\frac{1}{2}
and a contradiction is reached.
∎

Proof of
Lemma
8.18
.

The proof is similar to the first property. Let
L
be as in
Lemma
8.17
. This time we define a set of bad
triples
as follows. For a set
(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}
we consider the following quantity

F(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\coloneqq\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},t_{1},s_{2},t_{2},s_{3},t_{3},s_{4})\sim N_{t}^{3}}}\left[s_{1},s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\land f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\left|\;\vphantom{s_{1},s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\land f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}s_{2}=s,t_{2}=t^{\prime},s_{3}=s^{\prime}}\right.s_{2}=s,t_{2}=t^{\prime},s_{3}=s^{\prime}\right]

where
t^{\prime\prime}=t_{2}\cap t_{3}\cap t_{4}
. We define
B
to be the set of triples with large
F
-value:

B\coloneqq\left\{(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime}):F(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\geqslant\frac{\delta^{\prime 2}}{80}\right\},

and keep
L
as in the proof of
Lemma
8.17
. By excellence of
\tau
and Markov’s inequality
\ProbOp_{(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}}[B]\leqslant\frac{80\mu}{\delta^{\prime 2}}\leqslant\exp(-\Omega(\eta k))
. Since
s
and
s^{\prime}
are both marginally distributed as
X_{t}{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
, we also have by
Claim
8.19
and a union bound that either one of
s\in L
or
s^{\prime}\in L
with probability at most
\delta^{\prime 2}=e^{-2c_{5}\eta k}
. It is therefore enough to show
\tilde{f}_{s}|_{t^{\prime}}\overset{10\eta^{\prime}}{\approx}\tilde{f}_{s^{\prime}}|_{t^{\prime}}
under the assumption that
(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\notin B
and
s,s^{\prime}\notin L
.

Toward this end, fix
(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})
and consider the set of disagreeing vertices on the smoothings at
s
and
s^{\prime}
:

D_{0}\coloneqq\left\{v\in t^{\prime}:\tilde{f}_{s}(v)\neq\tilde{f}_{s^{\prime}}(v)\right\}.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that
|D_{0}|\geqslant 10\eta^{\prime}|t^{\prime}|
. Similar to before, we actually consider
D=D_{0}\setminus D^{\prime}
where
D^{\prime}
is the set of vertices in
t^{\prime}
such that
\ProbOp_{(t_{3},s_{4})\sim N_{t}(s^{\prime})}[s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}|t_{3}\ni v]
(likewise for
s_{1},t_{1}\sim N_{t}(s)
). By
Claim
8.19
, both
s
and
s^{\prime}
have at most
\eta^{\prime}|s|
such vertices so
|D|\geqslant 6\eta^{\prime}|t^{\prime}|
.

We now consider the following probabilistic experiment. Sample
v\in D
uniformly and
(s_{1},t_{1},s_{2},t_{2},s_{3},t_{3},s_{4})
conditioned on
s_{2}=s,t_{2}=t^{\prime},s_{3}=s^{\prime}
. We then condition this experiment on:

1.

v\in t^{\prime\prime}=t_{1}\cap t_{2}\cap t_{3}
.

2.

s_{1},s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
.

Note that for a fixed
v\in D
, the marginals
(s_{1},t_{1},s_{2}=s)
and
(s_{3}=s^{\prime},t_{3},s_{4})
are precisely the distributions that were used in the definition of
\tilde{f}_{s}(v),\tilde{f}_{s^{\prime}}(v)
respectively. As before, define the event
P=\{f_{s_{1}}(v)\neq f_{s_{4}}(v)\}
and observe that
\ProbOp_{{\mathcal{D}}}\left[P\right]\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
because for every
v\in D
the plurality labeling for
v
over
N_{t}
under our conditioned process is different at
s
and
s^{\prime}
.

We now define events whose union contains
P
which occur with total probability less than
\frac{1}{2}
. These are

1.

E_{1}
: the event
\{f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\napprox}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\}

2.

E_{2}
: the event
|t^{\prime\prime}\cap D|\leqslant 5\eta^{\prime}|t^{\prime\prime}|
.

3.

E_{3}
: the event
P\setminus(E_{1}\cup E_{2})
.

By the same analysis as
Lemma
8.17
we have
\ProbOp\left[{E_{3}}\right]<\frac{1}{5}
and
\ProbOp\left[{E_{2}}\right]\leqslant\frac{1}{10}
(we omit the repeated details). As for
E_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{\mathcal{D}}}\left[E_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=\ProbOp_{{v\in D,s_{1},t_{1},t_{3},s_{4}}}\left[f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\left|\;\vphantom{f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}s_{1},s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau},v\in t^{\prime\prime}}\right.s_{1},s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau},v\in t^{\prime\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{9}{\delta^{\prime 2}}\ProbOp_{{v\sim D,s^{\prime}\sim N_{t}(s)}}\left[s_{1},s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\land f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\left|\;\vphantom{s_{1},s_{4}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\land f_{s_{1}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}\overset{\eta^{\prime}}{\not\approx}f_{s_{4}}|_{t^{\prime\prime}}v\in t^{\prime\prime}}\right.v\in t^{\prime\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(s,t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\notin B}{\leqslant}\frac{9}{\delta^{\prime 2}}\cdot\frac{\delta^{\prime 2}}{80}\cdot\ProbOp\left[{v\in t^{\prime}}\right]^{-1}\leqslant\frac{1}{5}.

We reach a similar contradiction as before.
∎

Proof of
Claim
8.19
.

Let us begin with the first item. Recall under the assumptions of
Theorem
8.7

N_{t}
is reverse hypercontractive for indicators. Namely let
c_{6}
