be the constant promised by
Theorem
6.4
, and observe that by assumption
\ProbOp_{{X_{t}}}\left[\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\right]\geqslant\delta/2\geqslant e^{-c_{6}\eta k}
. If
\ProbOp_{{X_{t}}}\left[L\right]\leqslant 2e^{-2c_{5}\eta k}
we are done, so assume otherwise. Then by definition of
L
and
Theorem
6.4
we have the chain of inequalities:

\ProbOp[L]\delta^{\prime}\geqslant\ProbOp_{(s,s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}}[s\in L,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}]\geqslant 2^{-q-1}\ProbOp[L]^{q}\delta^{q}.

where
q
is the constant given in
Theorem
6.4
. Re-arranging gives
\ProbOp[L]\leqslant(2^{q+1}\delta^{\prime}\delta^{-q})^{\frac{1}{q-1}}\leqslant\exp(-c_{7}\eta k)\leqslant\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}
for the appropriate choice of constant
c_{7}>0
. Thus if
c_{5}
is sufficiently small we get the first item.

For the second item, let
D^{\prime}
denote the set of
v\in s
such that
\ProbOp_{(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}(s)}[s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\penalty\ |\penalty\ t^{\prime}\ni v]\leqslant\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{3}
. Define
g:\binom{s}{|t^{\prime}|}\to[0,1]
to be
g(t_{0})=\ProbOp_{{(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\sim N_{t}(s)}}\left[s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}\left|\;\vphantom{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}t^{\prime}=t_{0}}\right.t^{\prime}=t_{0}\right]
. Observe that
\E[g]\geqslant\delta^{\prime}
since by assumption
s\notin L
. Moreover by construction we have
v\in D^{\prime}
if and only if
\E_{{t_{0}\ni v}}\left[g(t_{0})\right]\leqslant\frac{1}{3}\E[g(t_{0})]
.

The problem is now reduced to sampling on the complete complex. In other words let
L=\binom{s}{|t^{\prime}|}
,
R=s
and
v\sim t
if
v\in t
. By Chernoff for every
\alpha
, this graph is an
(\alpha,\beta,\frac{1}{2})
-multiplicative sampler for
\beta=\exp(-\Omega(\alpha k))
. By
Claim
4.8
, this implies that the opposite graph is a
(\frac{6\beta}{\alpha},2\alpha,\frac{2}{3})
-multiplicative sampler. We take
\alpha=\eta^{\prime}/2
, and the constant
c_{5}>0
defined above small enough so that
\frac{6\beta}{\alpha}\leqslant\delta^{\prime}
. As such we have for every function
g:L\to[0,1]
of expectation at least
\delta^{\prime}
the fraction of vertices
v\in s
such that
\E_{{t^{\prime}\subseteq s,v\in t^{\prime}}}\left[g\right]<\frac{1}{3}\E_{{t^{\prime}\subseteq s}}\left[g(t^{\prime})\right]
is at most
\eta^{\prime}
as desired.
∎

8.4
Examples of Global Complexes

We conclude the section with several examples of ‘global HDX’ to which
Theorem
8.7
applies in a blackbox manner. We start with a few basic direct examples and then argue a vast array of complexes studied in the approximate sampling literature give rise to global HDX. These are:

1.

The complete complex.

2.

Erdos-Renyi Hypergraphs.

3.

Skeletons of the full linear matroid over
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d}
.

4.

Skeletons of
\ell_{\infty}
-independent complexes, including many classic spin systems.

5.

Faces complexes of any of the above.

The first and simplest example of global complexes (other than the complete complex) come from the classical random model of Linial and Meshulam
[
LM06
]
.

Definition 8.20

(Erdos-Renyi Hypergraphs)
.

The Erdos-Renyi hypergraph
X\sim G_{k}(n,p)
is a random
k
-uniform simplicial complex whose
(k-1)
-skeleton is complete, and such that every
k
-face is sampled into
X
with probability
p
independently.

The second direct example we’ll give is based on the full linear matroid over
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d}
, whose faces consist of linearly independent vectors over
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d}
:

X{\left(i\right)}=\left\{\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i}\}{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i}}\text{ are independent}}\right.{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i}}\text{ are independent}\right\},

endowed with the uniform distribution.

Claim 8.21

(Global complexes)
.

The following families are global and HDX:

1.

The
k
-uniform complete complex on
n
vertices for large enough
n
is:

–

(one-sided)
0
-local-spectral HDX

–

n^{-\Omega(k)}
-global

2.

The Erdos-Renyi hypergraph
G_{k}(n,p)
for large enough
n
is w.h.p:

–

o_{n}(1)
-local-spectral HDX

–

(1-p+o_{n}(1))
-global

3.

The
k
-skeleton of the full linear matroid over
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{d}
for large enough
q,d
is :

–

(one-sided)
0
-local-spectral HDX

–

q^{-\Omega(d)}
-global

Proof.

We cover each case individually:

Complete Complex

All links of the complete complex are complete and therefore
0
-one-sided local spectral expanders. For globalness, observe that the only
\frac{k}{2}
-sets which do not appear in the link of some
t
are those that intersect it, which are an
1-\frac{\binom{n-k}{k}}{\binom{n}{k}}\leqslant n^{-\Omega(k)}
fraction.

Random Hypergraphs

It is well known for fixed
p
that this complex is an
o_{n}(1)
-local-spectral expander with high probability so long as
p\gg\frac{k\log(n)}{n}
(the top links are simply random
G(n,p)
graphs). Since all other links are at least connected with high probability,
Theorem
4.23
implies the local-spectral bound. Toward globality, fix any
\frac{k}{2}
-set
t\subset\binom{[n]}{k/2}
. By Chernoff the probability that more than
(1-p+o_{n}(1))
-fraction of
\frac{k}{2}
-sets disjoint from
t
fail to appear in
X
is much less than
n^{-\Omega(k)}
for large enough
n
. Thus union bounding over
t
, the worst-case total variation of
S_{t}
from uniform over
{\binom{n}{\frac{k}{2}}}
is at most
1-p+o_{n}(1)
as desired.
14
14

14

Formally, distance here should be measured from
\pi_{\frac{k}{2}}
, but this is within
o_{n}(1)
of uniform in TV with high probability.

Linear Matroid

All matroids (and therefore their skeletons) are
0
-local spectral expanders
[
Ana+19
]
(for this matroid specifically, it is folklore). Toward globality, fix
\frac{k}{2}
linearly independent vectors
B_{1}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k/2}\}
. The set
B_{2}=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{k/2}\}
only fails to appear in the link of
B_{1}
if
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are linearly dependent, which happens with probability roughly
\frac{q^{k+(k-1)d}}{q^{dk}}\leqslant q^{-\Omega(d)}
(ignoring low-order terms). Since
\Pi_{k/2}
is uniform over linearly independent sets this gives the result.
∎
The sampling literature is rife with examples of “
\ell_{\infty}
-independent” complexes (a term formally coined in
[
KKS21
]
), which are closely related to global complexes. We’ll consider the variant implicit in
[
CLV21
]
. Let
\mu
be a distribution over
[q]^{d}
, and observe
\mu
induces a natural
d
-partite simplicial complex
X_{\mu}
with

X_{\mu}{\left(d\right)}\coloneqq\{(1,a_{1}),\ldots,(d,a_{d}):(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})\in Supp(\mu)\}

and
\pi_{X}((1,a_{1}),\ldots,(d,a_{d}))=\mu(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})
. Let
S\subset[d]
and
z\in[q]^{S}
be such that
\ProbOp_{x\sim\mu}[x_{S}=z]>0
(we call such configurations ‘feasible’). The
(S,z)
-
influence matrix

\Psi_{z\to S}
has entries

\Psi_{z\to S}((u,i),(v,j))=\begin{cases}\ProbOp_{x\sim\mu}[x_{i}=u|x_{j}=v,x_{S}=z]-\ProbOp_{x\sim\mu}[x_{i}=u|x_{S}=z]&\text{if $j\neq i$}\\
0&\text{if $j=i$}.\end{cases}

A complex is called
\ell_{\infty}
-independent if
\lVert\Psi\rVert_{\infty}
is bounded for all feasible configurations.

Definition 8.22

(
\ell_{\infty}
-independence)
.

Fix
q,d\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mu
be a distribution over
[q]^{d}
.

\mu
is called
D
-
\ell_{\infty}
-independent if for all feasible
S\subset[d]
and
z\in\{0,1\}^{S}
:

\lVert\Psi_{S\to z}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant D.

We remark that in this regime, one typically thinks of
q
as fixed or small and the uniformity
d
as going to infinity. We show any sufficiently low-dimensional skeleton of an
\ell_{\infty}
-independent complex is a global HDX:

Proposition 8.23
.

Let
\mu
be a
D
-
\ell_{\infty}
-independent distribution over
[q]^{d}
and
3\leqslant k\leqslant d
. Then the
k
-skeleton
X_{\mu}^{\leqslant k}
satisfies:

1.

X_{\mu}^{\leqslant k}
is a
\frac{D+q}{d+3-k}
-two-sided local-spectral expander

2.

X_{\mu}^{\leqslant k}
is
\frac{k^{2}}{8}\cdot\frac{D+q}{d+3-k}
-global.

We remark that (tighter) spectral variants of both items above are well known (see e.g.
[
ALG21
,
AP23
]
).
Proposition
8.23
implies essentially every spin-system studied in the recent breakthrough line of work on approximate sampling through spectral independence admits optimal 1% agreement testers (taking
k\leqslant\sqrt{\log(d)}
skeletons).
15
15

15

Note while spectral independence is weaker than what we require, almost all known methods for spin systems actually bound
\ell_{\infty}
-independence.
See
[
KKS21
, Section 3]
for an overview including distributions with the stochastic covering property, independent sets, various Ising/Potts models, list colorings, and more.

The proof of
Proposition
8.23
relies on an intermediate notion of
[
Hop22
]
called local-
\ell_{\infty}
-expansion.

Definition 8.24

(
(\lambda,\infty)
-expansion)
.

A simplicial complex
X
is called a
(\lambda,\infty)
-local expander if for every
\tau\in X
with
|\tau|\leqslant d-2
:

\lVert A_{\tau}-\Pi_{\tau}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant\lambda

where
A_{\tau}
is the weighted adjacency matrix of
X_{\tau}
’s 1-skeleton, and
\Pi_{\tau}
is its corresponding stationary operator.

We require two lemmas regarding this notion. First, we note that up to dependence on the alphabet
q
(typically thought of as constant in this regime)
\ell_{\infty}
-expansion is essentially equivalent to
\ell_{\infty}
-independence.

Lemma 8.25
.

Let
\mu
be a
D
-
\ell_{\infty}
-independent distribution over
[q]^{d}
. Then for every feasible
S\subset[d]
and
x_{S}\in\{0,1\}^{S}
:

\lVert A_{x_{S}}-\Pi_{x_{S}}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant\frac{D+q}{d-|S|}.

Proof.

We prove the case where
S=\emptyset
. The general statement follows from applying this argument to the links of
X_{\mu}
. With this in mind, observe that by definition:

\displaystyle\lVert A_{\emptyset}-\Pi\rVert_{\infty}=

\displaystyle\max_{(u,i)}\sum\limits_{(v,j)\in X_{(u,i)}}\left|\frac{1}{d-1}\ProbOp[x_{i}=u|x_{v}=j]-\frac{1}{d}\ProbOp[x_{i}=u]\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{d}\max_{(u,i)}\left(\sum\limits_{(v,j)\in X_{(u,i)}}\left|\ProbOp[x_{i}=u|x_{j}=v]-\ProbOp[x_{i}=u]\right|+\frac{1}{d-1}\ProbOp[x_{i}=u|x_{j}=v]\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{q}{d}+\frac{1}{d}\max_{(u,i)}\sum\limits_{(v,j)\in X_{(u,i)}}\left|\ProbOp[x_{i}=u|x_{j}=v]-\ProbOp[x_{i}=u]\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{d}+\frac{1}{d}\max_{(u,i)}\sum\limits_{(v,j)\in X_{(u,i)}}\left|\ProbOp[x_{i}=u|x_{j}=v]-\ProbOp[x_{i}=u]\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{D+q}{d}

∎

Second, we’ll need the slightly more involved fact from
[
Hop22
]
that any
\ell_{\infty}
-local expander is global. We reproduce the proof here for completeness.

Lemma 8.26
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform
\lambda
-
\ell_{\infty}
-local expander. Then
X
is
\frac{d^{2}}{8}\lambda
-global.

Proof.

Recall that it is sufficient to bound the infinity norm:

\lVert S_{\frac{d}{2},\frac{d}{2}}-\Pi_{\frac{d}{2},\frac{d}{2}}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant\frac{d^{2}}{4}\lambda.

We prove a slightly stronger statement by induction. For any
\tau\in X
and feasible
i,j
both the
\infty
-norm and
1
-norm are bounded by:

\lVert S^{\tau}_{i,j}-\Pi^{\tau}_{i,j}\rVert_{1},\lVert S_{i,j}-\Pi_{i,j}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant ij\lambda.

We induct on
i+j
. For the base case
i=j=1
, first observe the
\infty
-norm is exactly the
\lambda
-
\ell_{\infty}
-expansion of the
1
-skeleton. A classical consequence of Hölder duality states that for any Hölder conjugates
(p,q)
and operator
M

\lVert M\rVert_{p}=\lVert M^{*}\rVert_{q},

where
M^{*}
is
M
’s adjoint. Since
S_{1,1}-\Pi_{1,1}
is self-adjoint we therefore have

\lVert S^{\tau}_{0,0}-\Pi^{\tau}_{0,0}\rVert_{1}=\lVert S^{\tau}_{0,0}-\Pi^{\tau}_{0,0}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant\lambda

as desired.

Assume now by induction that, for some fixed
i+j>0
, all
i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}<i+j
and
\tau\in X
satisfy

\lVert S^{\tau}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}-\Pi^{\tau}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant i^{\prime}j^{\prime}\lambda.

Let
p\in\{1,\infty\}
and let
\bar{p}
denote the complement of
p
. We first argue we may assume without loss of generality that
i>1
. This is again by Hölder duality since

\lVert S_{i,j}-\Pi_{i,j}\rVert_{p}=\lVert S_{j,i}-\Pi_{j,i}\rVert_{\bar{p}}.

Thus we are done if we show the result for both
p\in\{1,\infty\}
just for the case
i\geqslant j
(and thus
i>1
).

Toward this end, fix any
f:X{\left(j\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
. As is typically the case, the idea is to localize the input by first drawing an
(i-1)
-face, then a vertex from its link:

\displaystyle\lVert S_{i,j}f-\Pi_{i,j}f\rVert_{p}=\Bigg|\Bigg|\lVert(S_{i,j}f)|_{s}-(\Pi_{i,j}f)|_{s}\rVert_{p,v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}\Bigg|\Bigg|_{p,s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}

where for any
g\in X{\left(i\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
,
g|_{s}(v)=g(s\cup v)
denotes the localization of
g
to the link of
s
. For concreteness, we write out the above explicitly for the
p=1
case in slightly less compact notation. The
p=\infty
case is the same replacing expectations with absolute maxima:

\displaystyle\lVert S_{i,j}f-\Pi_{i,j}f\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle=\underset{t\in X{\left(i\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[|S_{i,j}f(t)-\Pi_{i,j}f(t)|]

\displaystyle=\underset{{s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\underset{{v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}}{\mathbb{E}}[|S_{i,j}f(s\cup v)-\Pi_{i,j}f(s\cup v)|]\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\underset{s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\underset{v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[|S_{i,j}f|_{s}(v)-\Pi_{i,j}f|_{s}(v)|]\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}_{v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}[|S_{i,j}f|_{s}(v)-\Pi_{i,j}f|_{s}(v)|]\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}\Bigg[\Bigg|\mathbb{E}_{v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}[|S_{i,j}f|_{s}(v)-\Pi_{i,j}f|_{s}(v)|]\Bigg|\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\Bigg|\lVert(S_{i,j}f)|_{s}-(\Pi_{i,j}f)|_{s}\rVert_{p,v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}\Bigg|\Bigg|_{p,s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}.

We stick to the more compact norm notation for the remainder of the proof. The trick is now to observe that as a function of
X_{s}{\left(1\right)}
,
S_{i,j}f|_{s}
is exactly
S_{1,j}^{s}f^{s}
, where
f^{s}:X_{s}{\left(j\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
is the restriction
f^{s}(\tau)=f(\tau)
. Then by adding and subtracting the corresponding local stationary operator we have:

\displaystyle\lVert S_{i,j}f-\Pi_{i,j}f\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\Bigg|\lVert S^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}-\Pi^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}+\Pi^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}-(\Pi_{i,j}f)|_{s}\rVert_{p,v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}\Bigg|\Bigg|_{p,s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Bigg|\Bigg|\lVert S^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}-\Pi^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}\rVert_{p,v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}\Bigg|\Bigg|_{p,s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}+\Bigg|\Bigg|\lVert\Pi^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}-(\Pi_{i,j}f)|_{s}\rVert_{p,v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}\Bigg|\Bigg|_{p,s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}

by the triangle inequality. The first term is now bounded by the inductive hypothesis applied in the link of
s
:

\Bigg|\Bigg|\lVert S^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}-\Pi^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}\rVert_{p,v\in X_{s}{\left(1\right)}}\Bigg|\Bigg|_{p,s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}\leqslant\Bigg|\Bigg|j\lambda\lVert f^{s}\rVert_{p,X_{s}{\left(j\right)}}\Bigg|\Bigg|_{p,s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}=j\lVert f\rVert_{p,X{\left(j\right)}}

Toward analyzing the second term, observe that
\Pi^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}=S_{i-1,j}f(s)
and
(\Pi_{i,j}f)|_{s}(v)=\Pi_{i-1,j}f(s)
so:

\Bigg|\Bigg|\lVert\Pi^{s}_{1,j}f^{s}-(\Pi_{i,j}f)|_{s}\rVert_{p,v\in X_{s}{\left(0\right)}}\Bigg|\Bigg|_{p,s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}=\lVert S_{i-1,j}f-\Pi_{i-1,j}f\rVert_{p,s\in X{\left(i-1\right)}}\leqslant(i-1)j\lambda\lVert f\rVert_{p,X{\left(j\right)}}

by the inductive hypothesis. Altogether this gives

\lVert S_{i,j}f-\Pi_{i,j}f\rVert_{p}\leqslant\lambda j+\lambda(i-1)j=ij\lambda

as desired.
∎

The proof of
Proposition
8.23
is now essentially immediate.

Proof of
Proposition
8.23
.

The first fact is immediate from
Lemma
8.25
and the fact that
\lVert A_{\tau}-\Pi_{\tau}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\lVert A_{\tau}-\Pi_{\tau}\rVert_{\infty}
. The second fact is an immediate consequence of combining
Lemma
8.25
and
Lemma
8.26
.
∎

We conclude the subsection by the observation that if a complex
X
is global, then so is its faces complex (
Definition
5.8
). Since the faces complex also inherits the two-sided expansion of the original complex up to factors in uniformity (c.f. the proof of
Theorem
5.1
), this shows that one can also take as examples faces complexes of any of the examples above.

Claim 8.27
.

Let
X
be a
k
-uniform and
\lambda
-global complex. Let
\ell
be such that
\ell
divides
k
, then the
\ell
-faces complex
F^{\ell}_{X}
is
\lambda
-global.

Proof.

Let
m=\frac{k}{\ell}
for notational convenience. Fix
t=\{s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{\frac{m}{2}}\}\in F^{\ell}_{X}(\frac{m}{2})
. Observe that the set of neighbors of
t
with respect to the swap walk in
F^{\ell}X
are exactly those
t^{\prime}
such that
\cup t
and
\cup t^{\prime}
are neighbors in the swap walk on
X{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}
(here
\cup t=s_{1}\cup s_{2}\cup\dots\cup s_{\frac{m}{2}}
and similarly for
t^{\prime}
). Moreover, one samples a neighbor of
t
by sampling a random neighbor
\tau
of
\cup t
, and then sampling a uniform at random partition of
\tau
to
t^{\prime}=\{s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime},\dots,s_{\frac{m}{2}}\}
(the partition is chosen independent of
t
).

Thus one observes that the
TV
-distance between
S_{t}
and
\Pi
can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t^{\prime}\in F^{\ell}X{\left(\frac{m}{2}\right)}}|\ProbOp_{{S_{t}(F^{\ell}X)}}\left[t^{\prime}\right]-\ProbOp_{{\Pi}}\left[t^{\prime}\right]|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\tau\in X{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}}\sum_{t^{\prime}:\cup t^{\prime}=\tau}|\ProbOp_{{S_{t}(F^{\ell}X)}}\left[t^{\prime}\right]-\ProbOp_{{\Pi}}\left[t^{\prime}\right]|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\tau\in X{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}}\sum_{t^{\prime}:\cup t^{\prime}=\tau}p|\ProbOp_{{S_{\cup t}(X)}}\left[\tau\right]-\ProbOp_{{\Pi(X)}}\left[\tau\right]|

where
p
is one over the number of partitions of
\tau
to
t^{\prime}
. Obviously this is equal to

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\tau\in X{\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)}}\ProbOp_{{S_{\cup t}(X)}}\left[\tau\right]-\ProbOp_{{\Pi(X)}}\left[\tau\right]=d_{TV}(S_{\cup t}(X),\Pi(X))\leqslant\lambda

as desired.
∎

9
Analytic, Geometric, and Combinatorial Applications

In this section, we cover several brief applications of sampling and reverse hypercontractivity in well-studied combinatorial and analytic settings.

9.1
New Double Samplers

Double samplers are a strengthened notion of sampling introduced in
[
DK17
,
Din+18
]
that consist of two “stacked” samplers with additional local sampling properties under ‘closure’ of the top layer. These interesting objects have powerful applications in agreement testing
[
DK17
]
and list-decoding
[
Din+18
]
, and have even seen algorithmic use in the construction of space-efficient data structures for the heavy hitter problem
[
DW22
]
.

Definition 9.1

(Double sampler,
[
Din+18
]
)
.

A double sampler consists of a triple
(V_{2},V_{1},V_{0})
, where
V_{0}
is the ground set,
V_{1}
is a collection of
i
-subsets of
V_{0}
and
V_{2}
is a collection of
k
-subsets of
V_{0}
, where
k>i\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
(V_{2},V_{1},V_{0})
is an
(\varepsilon,\beta,\varepsilon_{0},\beta_{0})
-
double sampler
if

–

The inclusion graphs on
(V_{2},V_{1})
and
(V_{2},V_{0})
are
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-additive samplers and the inclusion graph on
(V_{1},V_{0})
is an
(\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{0},\beta+\beta_{0})
-additive sampler
16
16

16

The weights over edges in
(V_{i},V_{j})
are the marginals of the probabilistic experiment where we first choose
T\in V_{2}
(according to some given weight distribution
\Pi
), and then choose
v\in V_{0}
and
S\in V_{1}
such that
v\in S\subseteq T
, uniformly at random over all such pairs
S,v
.
(recall the inclusion graph is defined by connecting two subsets by an edge if one contains the other).

–

For every
T\in V_{2}
, let
V_{1}(T)=\{S\in V_{1}\;:\;S\subset T\}
be the sets in
V_{1}
that are contained in
T
. Let
G_{|T}
be the bipartite inclusion graph connecting elements in
T
(viewed as elements in the ground set
V_{0}
) to subsets in
V_{1}(T)
. We require that for every
T\in V_{2}
, the graph
G_{|T}
is an
(\beta_{0},\varepsilon_{0})
-additive sampler.

Double samplers are only known to arise from high dimensional expanders. In their original work,
[
DK17
,
Din+18
]
use the Ramanujan complexes
[
LSV05
]
to construct explicit double samplers for all
(\varepsilon,\beta,\varepsilon_{0},\beta_{0})
:

Theorem 9.2

(
[
Din+18
, Theorem 2.11]
)
.

For every
(\varepsilon,\beta,\varepsilon_{0},\beta_{0})>0
, there exists an explicit infinite family of double samplers
\{(V_{0}^{(n)},V_{1}^{(n)},V_{2}^{(n)})\}
such that for all
n
:

\frac{|V_{1}^{(n)}|}{|V_{0}^{(n)}|},\frac{|V_{2}^{(n)}|}{|V_{0}^{(n)}|}=\exp\left(\poly\left(\frac{1}{\beta},\frac{1}{\beta_{0}},\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)\right).

One of the main open questions posed in
[
Din+18
]
is to settle the overhead of double samplers. In particular, while it is known that exponential dependence on
\varepsilon_{i}
is necessary (even for standard samplers), it is plausible the dependence on the ‘failure probability’
\beta_{i}
could be improved, perhaps even to match the corresponding polynomial dependence of optimal standard samplers. Since typical related applications (e.g. for low soundness agreement tests and PCPs
[
IKW12
,
DL17
]
) take
\varepsilon
to be constant (or at most some polylog in
\beta
), this latter dependence is where the crux of the problem lies.

To the best of our knowledge, there have been no lower bounds or improvements over
Theorem
9.2
since
[
Din+18
]
’s original work. Leveraging our concentration bounds and their corresponding optimality (see
Section
11.1
) we take a significant step toward resolving this problem: quasi-polynomial size double samplers exist and, under reasonable assumptions on the underlying complex, are the best possible.

Theorem 9.3

(Quasi-Polynomial Double Samplers)
.

for every
(\varepsilon,\beta,\varepsilon_{0},\beta_{0})>0
, there exists an explicit family of (
\beta,\varepsilon
),
(\beta_{0},\varepsilon_{0})
double samplers
\{(V^{(n)}_{2},V^{(n)}_{1},V^{(n)}_{0})\}
of size at most:

\frac{|V^{(n)}_{1}|}{|V^{(n)}_{0}|},\frac{|V^{(n)}_{2}|}{|V^{(n)}_{0}|}\leqslant\exp\left(\widetilde{O}\left(\min\left\{\frac{\log^{4}\frac{1}{\beta}\log^{4}\frac{1}{\beta_{0}}}{\varepsilon^{8}\varepsilon_{0}^{4}},\frac{\log^{3}\frac{1}{\beta}\log^{3}\frac{1}{\beta_{0}}}{\varepsilon^{16}\varepsilon_{0}^{4}}\right\}\right)\right).

Moreover, for
\varepsilon\in(0,0.01)
, if the underlying complex family
\{X_{n}\}
is
\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
-hitting, vertex-uniform, and non-contracting,
17
17

17

Here we mean 1)
\pi_{0}(v)=|X{\left(1\right)}|^{-1}
for all
v\in X{\left(1\right)}
, and 2)
|X{\left(i\right)}|\geqslant|X{\left(i-1\right)}|
for all
2\leqslant i\leqslant d
.
this is optimal up to polynomial factors in the exponent:

\frac{|V_{2}^{(n)}|}{|V_{0}^{(n)}|}\geqslant\exp\left(\Omega\left(\frac{\log(\frac{1}{\beta})\log(\frac{1}{\beta_{0}})}{\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\right)\right)

Note the above does not fully rule out the existence of simplicial poly-size double samplers. While the assumption on regularity in the lower bound can be easily relaxed, it is unclear to what extent the assumption on hitting-set is an artifact of our proof technique (see
Section
11.1
for details), though it is certainly a natural property for such objects to satisfy. Moreover, our bound strongly relies on the assumption that
|V_{2}|\geqslant\exp(d)|V_{0}|
(implied by
X
being non-contracting), which is not generically true. Nevertheless, high dimensional expanders exhibit both good hitting set behavior (see
Proposition
10.12
) and at least exponential degree (see
Proposition
9.6
), so it is at least accurate to say double samplers arising from HDX are at best quasi-polynomial in
\beta^{-1}
.

The proof of
Theorem
9.3
relies on two further results. The first, toward our upper bound, is Lubotzky, Samuels, and Vishne’s
[
LSV05a
]
classical construction of Ramanujan complexes.

Theorem 9.4

(LSV-Complexes
[
LSV05a
]
)
.

For any
\lambda>0
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an explicit infinite family of
d
-partite one-sided
\lambda
-local-spectral expanders
\{X^{(n)},\Pi^{(n)}\}
with degree
\text{deg}(X)\leqslant\lambda^{-O(d^{2})}
. Moreover, for every
d\geqslant k>\ell\geqslant 0
,
\max\{\pi_{k}\}\leqslant o_{n}(1)
.

The second, toward the lower bound, is the following optimality result we prove in
Section
11.1
.

Corollary 9.5

(Corollary of
Theorem
11.1
)
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,0.01)
, let
X
be a
d
-uniform,
\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
-hitting vertex-uniform complex and
i\leqslant k\leqslant d
. Then there exists a set
A\subset X{\left(i\right)}
such that:

\ProbOp_{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}[|U_{i,k}1_{A}(s)-\mu_{A}|>\varepsilon]\geqslant\exp\left(O\left(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right).

We are now ready to prove
Theorem
9.3
.

Proof of
Theorem
9.3
.

We split into the upper and lower bounds.

Upper Bound

We prove the two bounds within the minimum separately. For the first, we use
d
-dimensional LSV-complexes with
\lambda=\frac{1}{2d}
and
d=O\left(\frac{\log^{2}(1/\beta)\log^{2}(1/\beta_{0})}{\varepsilon^{4}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\right)
for top layer, and set
i=\Theta(\frac{\log(1/\beta_{0})}{\varepsilon_{0}^{2}})
for the middle layer. By
Corollary
7.3
,
(V_{2},V_{1})
is an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler for the right choice of constants. Moreover, every closure graph is simply the
(\frac{\log(1/\beta_{0})}{\varepsilon_{0}^{2}})
-uniform complex on
k
vertices, which is negatively correlated and therefore also a
(\varepsilon_{0},\beta_{0})
-sampler by standard Chernoff-Hoeffding for negatively correlated variables. The overhead bound is then immediate from the degree of LSV complexes in
Theorem
9.4
.

For the latter, we will use
d
-dimensional LSV-complexes with
\lambda=2^{-d}
and
d=O\left(\frac{\log(1/\beta)\log(1/\beta_{0})}{\varepsilon^{8}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\right)
for top layer, and set
i=\Theta(\frac{\log(1/\beta_{0})}{\varepsilon_{0}^{2}})
for the middle layer. The result then follows from
Theorem
5.1
and the above.

Lower Bound

Recall that by definition
V_{0}^{(n)}=X_{n}{\left(1\right)}=[n]
,
V_{1}^{(n)}=X_{n}{\left(i\right)}
, and
V_{2}^{(n)}=X_{n}(k)
for some family of simplicial complexes
\{X_{n}\}
and set sizes
1<i<k
. By
Corollary
9.5
, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,0.01)
,
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is at best an
(\varepsilon,e^{O(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i})})
-sampler, therefore any such double sampler requires:

\frac{k}{i}\geqslant\Omega\left(\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

On the other hand, it is also required for every
T\in X{\left(k\right)}
that the induced graphs
G|_{T}=(V_{2}|_{T},V_{1}|_{T})
are
(\varepsilon_{0},\beta_{0})
-samplers. By standard sampling lower bounds
[
CEG95
]
, this graph is at best a
(\varepsilon_{0},e^{O(\varepsilon_{0}^{2}i)})
-sampler, forcing

i\geqslant\Omega\left(\frac{\log(1/\beta_{0})}{\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\right).

Combining these bounds we have that for any valid choice of
i
the top uniformity
k
of an
(\varepsilon,\beta,\varepsilon_{0},\beta_{0})
-double sampler is at least

k\geqslant\Omega\left(\frac{\log(\frac{1}{\beta})\log(\frac{1}{\beta_{0}})}{\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\right).

It is therefore enough to argue that the overhead is at least exponential in the uniformity
k
:

\frac{|V_{2}|}{|V_{0}|}\geqslant 2^{\Omega(k)}.

This is true for any non-contracting complex. In particular, since
|X{\left(k\right)}|\geqslant|X{\left(k/2\right)}|
, the average degree of every
(k/2)
-face (and hence every vertex) is at least
2^{\Omega(k)}
.
∎
We remark that it may also be possible to construct double samplers with smaller overhead by removing the
simplicial
requirement from the definition (see e.g. the relaxed notion in
[
DW22
]
). It is likely, for instance, that ‘Grassmannian HDX’
[
KT21
,
Gol23
,
DLZ23
]
based on subspace structure lead to double-samplers with different parameters-overhead trade-offs.

9.2
A Degree Lower Bound for HDX

One of the most classical results in the study of expander graphs is the Alon-Boppana Theorem
[
Nil91
]
, roughly stating that any family of bounded-degree
\lambda
-expanders must have degree at least
\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}
. In higher dimensions, the situation is less understood. Not only should degree scale in some way with the local expansion
\lambda
, but dependence on
dimension
becomes a critical parameter in application. In this section, we give (to our knowledge) the first systematic study of the degree of high dimensional expanders. In particular, we prove that various families of hyper-regular HDX require super-exponential degree and prove a threshold-phenomenon exists at the TD-barrier: there exist (reasonably balanced) constructions of
1
-TD complexes achieving exponential degree at every level, while any (reasonably balanced)
\lambda
-TD complex must have degree
2^{-\Omega(k^{2})}
for any level
k\leqslant\sqrt{d}
.

Before applying our stronger concentration bounds, we first look at what one can infer about the degree of HDX directly from spectral methods. The following proposition, though not to our knowledge appearing anywhere in the literature, is the result of applying elementary spectral methods to the problem:

Proposition 9.6

(Spectral Lower Bound)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform
\{\lambda_{i}\}
-two-sided local-spectral expander. Then for every
k<d
, the degree of
X
’s
k
-skeleton is at least:

\deg^{(k)}(X)\geqslant\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}

Proof.

Observe the
k
-th degree of a vertex
v\in X{\left(1\right)}
can be bounded by

\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\min_{t\in X{\left(i\right)}}\{|X_{t}{\left(1\right)}|\}

simply by counting the number of ways a face
\{v_{2},\ldots,v_{k-1}\}\in X_{v_{1}}{\left(k\right)}
can be sampled by choosing
v_{2}\sim X_{v_{1}}{\left(1\right)}
,
v_{2}\in X_{\{v_{1},v_{2}\}}{\left(1\right)}
and correcting for the
\frac{1}{(k-1)!}
possible orderings of the face. The result now follows from the elementary fact (see e.g.
[
Gai+22
, Lemma A.7]
) that any two-sided
\lambda
-expander has at least
\frac{1}{\lambda}
vertices.
∎

We remark that one might reasonably think to use Alon-Boppana
[
Nil91
]
(the classic ‘optimal’ trade-off between expansion and degree) to prove a version of the above for one-sided expansion. This does not work for high dimensional expanders, since their links may have low diameter.

Proposition
9.6
implies that any (hyper-regular)
o(\frac{1}{d})
-two-sided HDX must have super-exponential degree but is essentially trivial for complexes near the TD-barrier, under spectral independence, or even for arbitrarily strong partite one-sided HDX. In these cases, even if one is willing to look e.g. at polynomial size cutoffs (as is quite common in the literature)
Proposition
9.6
only gives a slightly super-exponential lower bound:

\deg^{(k)}(X)\geqslant\exp(\Omega(k\log(k))).

Using concentration, we improve this bound to strongly super-exponential. We start with the partite case.

Theorem 9.7
.

Fix
c\in[0,1]
and let
X
be a
d
-uniform hyper-regular partite
2^{-\Omega(d^{c})}
-HDX. Then either:

1.

X
’s 1-skeleton is dense:
18
18

18

While this condition may seem strange, note it is necessary to handle dense settings. Take, e.g.,
\{0,1\}^{n}
, which is a hyper-regular
0
-local-spectral expander whose
\sqrt{n}
-skeleton has degree
\exp(\sqrt{n}\log(n))
.

\deg^{1}(X)\geqslant\frac{|X{\left(1\right)}|}{2}

2.

X
has super-exponential degree:

\deg^{(d)}(X)\geqslant\exp(\Omega(\max\{d^{2c},d\}))

Second we prove an analogous bound for skeletons of TD and SI-complexes.

Theorem 9.8
.

Let
\lambda,c\in[0,1)
,
\eta>0
, and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. If
X
is a
d
-uniform hyper-regular
\lambda
-TD or
\eta
-SI complex then for any
k\leqslant d^{c}
:

1.

X
’s 1-skeleton is dense:

\deg^{1}(X)\geqslant\frac{|X{\left(1\right)}|}{2}

2.

X
’s
k
-skeleton has super-exponential degree:

\deg^{(k)}(X)\geqslant\exp(\Omega_{\lambda,\eta}(\min\{k^{2},k^{1/c}\}))

The latter setting above is tight in the sense that when
\lambda=1
, that is
at
the TD-barrier, there exist (reasonably balanced) complexes where
\deg^{(k)}(X)=\exp(O(k))
for all
k\leqslant d
. On the other hand, beyond the TD barrier the best known degree upper bound for a hyper-regular (or even reasonably balanced) HDX is
\exp(\exp(d))

[
FI20
]
, leaving a substantial gap with the above. Nevertheless,
Theorem
9.8
is the first to exhibit quantitative threshold behavior for degree at the TD-barrier.

Theorem
9.7
and
Theorem
9.8
leave open two critical questions:

1.

Can we prove super-exponential bounds for the
top
level of a
\lambda
-TD/
\eta
-SI complex?

2.

Can the assumption of hyper-regularity be dropped?

As we will see in the proof of
Theorem
9.8
, the first question would be resolved if one could give
any
asymptotic improvement over exponential concentration for weak HDX. In other words, any bound of the form:

\ProbOp_{s\in X{\left(d\right)}}\left[\frac{1}{d}|s\cap A|-\mu>\frac{1}{2}\right]\leqslant\exp(\omega(\sqrt{d}))

for
X
and its links would imply a super-exponential degree lower bound. If, for instance, we have a bound of
\exp(-\Omega(d))
holds, this would imply a degree lower bound of
\exp(\Omega(d^{2}))
on the top level of
X
.

Regarding the second question, our proof technique extends to any complex whose links are reasonably ‘balanced’ in the sense that there should not be a
2^{-\Omega(\sqrt{d})}
fraction of vertices making up an
\Omega(1)
fraction of the mass. Unfortunately, many classical HDX constructions, e.g. the Ramanujan complexes of
[
LSV05a
]
, actually are highly unbalanced in this sense. The links of these constructions essentially approximate an ‘unbalanced product’: they are partite complexes which are regular on each individual part, but many of the parts are vanishingly small and therefore make up a significant portion of the mass. While known constructions of this type all have minimum part size at least exponential in
d
(and therefore result in degree
\exp(d^{2})
), it is unclear how to rule out a construction whose links look, e.g., like
\{0,1\}^{d}\times[2^{d}]
. Such a complex could potentially satisfy optimal inclusion sampling while maintaining exponential degree.

We leave the possible extension of our lower bounds to the top level of unbalanced complexes (or conversely the construction of highly locally unbalanced exponential-size HDX) as one of the main open problems suggested by this work:

Question 9.9

(Lower Bounds for Locally Unbalanced HDX)
.

Let
\{X_{n}\}
be an infinite family of bounded-degree HDX that are either
\lambda
-TD or
\eta
-SI. Is the degree of every (sufficiently large)
X_{n}
super-exponential:

\text{deg}(X_{n})\geqslant\exp(\omega(d))?

Question
9.9
is critical from the standpoint of applying HDX in other areas of theoretical computer science where degree dependence corresponds to the ‘overhead’ incurred by using the complex as a gadget. For instance, the lower bound in
Theorem
9.3
can be achieved by HDX essentially if and only if
Question
9.9
is false. Similarly, a variant of
Question
9.9
where
d
may depend on
n
is critical to the study of agreement testers whose soundness scale with the
number of vertices
of the system. Constructing polynomial size testers with inverse polynomial soundness is the combinatorial core of the so-called
sliding scale conjecture
in PCP theory. If the above lower bound holds, HDX cannot give such objects:
d
must be taken to be
o(\log(n))
to maintain polynomial size, and the resulting soundness of any HDX-based poly-size agreement tester would be at best
2^{-o(\log(n))}
, missing the inverse polynomial mark.

We now move to the proofs of
Theorem
9.7
and
Theorem
9.8
which are inspired by our
localization
technique for proving concentration that samples
s=\{v_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{d}\}
by iteratively sampling
v_{0}\in X
, then
v_{2}\in X_{v_{1}}
,
v_{3}\in X_{\{v_{1},v_{2}\}}
… and analyzing the local concentration at each step. Upon further inspection, this technique yields the fact that the
1
-skeleton of a complex with good sampling in
0
and
1
-links must itself be an excellent sampler graph.

Lemma 9.10
.

Fix
\varepsilon,\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
. Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex such that:

1.

(X{\left(d\right)},X{\left(1\right)})
is a
(\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\delta_{1})
-additive sampler.

2.

For every
v\in X{\left(1\right)}
,
(X_{v}{\left(d\right)},X_{v}{\left(1\right)})
is an
(\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\delta_{2})
-additive sampler.

Then the partite double cover of
X
’s
1
-skeleton is a
(\varepsilon,\frac{2\delta_{1}}{1-\delta_{2}})
-sampler.

Proof.

Fix
A\subseteq X{\left(1\right)}
. At a high level, the proof follows from observing that if the base graph is a poor sampler, then after drawing the first vertex
v_{1}
of our
d
-set
t=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\}
, there is a non-trivial chance that the neighborhood of
v_{1}
(i.e.
X_{v_{1}}{\left(1\right)}
) ‘sees’
A
in the wrong proportion. However, since the remainder of the face
\{v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}\in X_{v_{1}}{\left(d\right)}
, if
(X_{v_{1}}{\left(d\right)},X_{v_{1}}{\left(1\right)})
is indeed a good sampler it will maintain this incorrect proportion from the first step and violate sampling of
A
in
(X{\left(d\right)},X{\left(1\right)})
.

We now formalize this argument. Given our assumptions we’d like to show:

\ProbOp_{{v\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{u\in{X_{v}{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[A\right]>\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\frac{\delta_{1}}{1-\delta_{2}}.

The lower tail follows from applying this on the complement set. Toward this end, denote by
B
the set of ‘bad’ vertices in
A
that over-sample the base graph:

B=\left\{v\in X{\left(1\right)}\left|\;\vphantom{v\in X{\left(1\right)}\ProbOp_{{u\in{X_{v}{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[A\right]>\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon}\right.\ProbOp_{{u\in{X_{v}{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[A\right]>\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon\right\}.

We need to show
\ProbOp[B]\leqslant\frac{\delta_{1}}{1-\delta_{2}}
. We bound
\ProbOp[B]
by relating it to the set of bad
d
-faces:

C=\left\{t\in X{\left(d\right)}\left|\;\vphantom{t\in X{\left(d\right)}\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in t}}\left[A\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\right.\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in t}}\left[A\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right\},

which has measure at most
\ProbOp[C]\leqslant\delta_{1}
by Condition (2). The key to relate
B
and
C
is to observe we can sample a random
d
-face
t
by first sampling a vertex
v\in X{\left(1\right)}
, then sampling
t
from its link:

\ProbOp\left[{C}\right]=\E_{{t\in{X{\left(d\right)}}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{C}(t)\right]=\E_{{v\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[\E_{{t\in{X_{v}{\left(d\right)}}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{C}(t)\right]\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\E_{{v\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[\E_{{t\in{X_{v}{\left(d\right)}}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{C}(t)\right]\penalty\ \Bigg|\penalty\ v\in B\right].

When
v\in B
, we have
\ProbOp_{{u\in{X_{v}{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[A\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon
by assumption, but then by Condition (1)

\E_{{v\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[\E_{{t\in{X_{v}{\left(d\right)}}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{C}(t)\right]\penalty\ \Bigg|\penalty\ v\in B\right]\geqslant\ProbOp_{{t\in{X_{v}{\left(d\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{At}\right.t\right]>\ProbOp_{{u\in{X_{v}{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[A\right]-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right]\geqslant 1-\delta_{2}.

Putting everything together we have

(1-\delta_{2})\ProbOp\left[{C}\right]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\leqslant\delta_{1}.

and
\ProbOp\left[{C}\right]\leqslant\frac{\delta_{1}}{1-\delta_{2}}
as desired.
∎

Combining this fact with standard degree lower bounds for sampler graphs
[
CEG95
]
gives the following degree lower bound for the 1-skeletons of such complexes:

Corollary 9.11
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex satisfying:

1.

(X{\left(d\right)},X{\left(1\right)})
is a
(\frac{1}{10},\delta_{1})
-sampler

2.

For all
v\in X{\left(1\right)}
,
(X_{v}{\left(d\right)},X_{v}{\left(1\right)})
is a
(\frac{1}{10},\delta_{2})
-sampler.

Then
X
’s
1
-skeleton has high max-degree:

\exists v\in X{\left(1\right)}:\deg^{(1)}(v)\geqslant\min\left\{\frac{|X{\left(1\right)}|}{2},\frac{2(1-\delta_{2})}{5\delta_{1}}\right\}

Proof.

By
Lemma
9.10
the underlying graph of
X
is a
(\frac{1}{5},\delta=\frac{2\delta_{1}}{1-\delta_{2}})
-sampler.
[
CEG95
, Theorem 2]
states the following relation on a graph
G=(L,R,E)
that is an
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-sampler of max degree
t\leqslant\frac{|X{\left(1\right)}|}{2}
.

t|R|\geqslant\frac{|L|(1-2\varepsilon)}{2\delta}.

Here
L=R=X{\left(1\right)}
and this inequality gives
t\geqslant\frac{0.8}{2\delta}=\frac{2(1-\delta_{2})}{5\delta_{1}}
.
∎

Notice this bound is substantially stronger than what can be inferred from spectral expansion alone. For instance, when
X
is a
\lambda=\poly(d^{-1})
-HDX, Alon-Boppana only implies a degree lower bound of
\Omega(\lambda^{-2})=\poly(d^{-1})
, whereas combining the above with
Corollary
7.3
gives degree at least
\exp(-\sqrt{d})
. Given this fact,
Theorem
9.7
and
Theorem
9.8
follows by recursive application of
Corollary
9.11
on each link of the HDX.

Proof of
Theorem
9.7
and
Theorem
9.8
.

Similar to the spectral argument, since
X
is hyper-regular the
k
th degree of any vertex
v_{0}\in X{\left(1\right)}
can be bounded by:

\deg^{(k)}(v_{0})\geqslant\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\min_{t\in X{\left(i\right)}}\left\{\deg^{(1)}(X_{t})\right\}.

It is therefore sufficient to bound the degree of the 1-skeleton of the links of
X
. We split the analysis into the partite and TD/SI cases.

Partite Case

Let
k^{\prime}=\max\{\sqrt{d},d^{c}\}
. By
Theorem
7.8
, there exists a universal constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that for every
t\in X^{\leqslant\frac{d}{2}}
, the link
X_{t}
satisfies:

1.

(X_{t}{\left(d-|t|\right)},X_{t}{\left(1\right)})
is a
(\frac{1}{10},\exp(-c^{\prime}k^{\prime}))
-sampler

2.

For all
v\in X_{t}{\left(1\right)}
,
(X_{t\cup\{v\}}{\left(d-|t|-1\right)},X_{t\cup\{v\}}{\left(1\right)})
is a
(\frac{1}{10},\exp(-c^{\prime}k^{\prime}))
-sampler.

Setting
t\in X{\left(1\right)}
,
Corollary
9.11
implies

\deg^{(1)}(X)\geqslant\min\left\{\frac{|X{\left(1\right)}|}{2},\exp(-c^{\prime}k^{\prime})\right\}.

Assume
\exp(-c^{\prime}k^{\prime})\leqslant\frac{|X{\left(1\right)}|}{2}
(else Condition (1) is satisfied and we are done). Under this assumption, we argue by induction that the max degree of any
i
-link is at least:

\forall i\leqslant\frac{d}{2}:\;\;\min_{t\in X{\left(i\right)}}\{\deg^{(1)}(X_{t})\}\geqslant\frac{1}{2^{i}}\exp(c^{\prime}k^{\prime}).

(9.1)

We remark the restriction on
i
is to enforce that links of this level are still
(\frac{1}{10},\exp(-c^{\prime}k^{\prime}))
-samplers.
Plugging this back into our bound on degree gives
\exp(-\Omega(k^{\prime 2}))\geqslant\exp(-\Omega(\max\{d,d^{2c}\}))
as desired.

To prove
(
9.1
)
, observe the base case
i=1
is given by assumption. The inductive step simply follows from observing that the minimum number of vertices in any
i
-link is exactly the minimum degree of the 1-skeleton of any
(i-1)
-link. Thus applying
Corollary
9.11
, we have:

\forall t\in X{\left(i\right)}:\deg^{(1)}(X_{t})\geqslant\min\left\{\frac{|X_{t}{\left(1\right)}|}{2},\exp(-c^{\prime}k^{\prime})\right\}\geqslant\frac{1}{2^{i}}\exp(c^{\prime}k^{\prime}).

as desired.

TD/SI Case

When
X
is
\lambda
-TD or
\eta
-SI, we’d like to run the same argument as above but run into a slight problem. We can still bound the
k
th degree of a vertex
v
by:

\deg^{(k)}(v)\geqslant\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}\min_{t\in X{\left(i\right)}}\left\{\deg^{(1)}(X_{t})\right\},

but because
X
satisfies only exponential concentration,
(
9.1
)
becomes

\forall i\leqslant\frac{d}{2}:\min_{t\in X{\left(i\right)}}\{\deg^{(1)}(X_{t})\}\geqslant\frac{1}{2^{i}}\exp(c^{\prime}\sqrt{d}).

This bound is now only non-trivial up to
i\leqslant O(\sqrt{d})
levels of
X
, stopping us from proving super-exponential concentration for
X
’s top level. Nevertheless, when
k=d^{c}
, plugging the above into the degree calculation gives
\exp(d)=\exp(k^{1/c})
whenever
c\geqslant 1/2
, and
\exp(k\sqrt{d})\geqslant\exp(k^{2})
whenever
c<1/2
.
∎

9.3
Geometric Overlap

The geometric overlap property is a classical notion of high dimensional expansion (see e.g.
[
Lub18
]
) which promises every embedding of the complex has a point in space hit by at least a constant fraction of the faces.

Definition 9.12
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex and let
k\leqslant d-1
. Let
c>0
. We say that
X
has
(k,c)
-geometric overlap if for every
\rho:X{\left(1\right)}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, there exists a point
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that

\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(d\right)}}}}\left[q_{0}\in conv(\rho(s))\right]\geqslant c.

Here
conv(\rho(s))
is the convex hull of the points of
\rho(s)=\left\{\rho(v)\left|\;\vphantom{\rho(v)v\in s}\right.v\in s\right\}
. While we are not aware of any applications of geometric overlap in computer science, it is a popular topic in geometry and topology and related ideas (e.g. an overlap variant of the Borsuk-Ulam theorem) have seen recent use in the study of algorithmic stability
[
Cha+23
]
.

Boros and Füredi
[
BF84
]
showed that the
2
-dimensional complete complex has geometric overlap, a result which was later generalized to all dimensions by Bárány
[
Bár82
]
. The notion was extended to general simplicial complexes by Gromov
[
Gro10
]
, who asked whether there are bounded degree complexes that have this property. Followup work by Fox, Gromov, Lafforgue, Naor, and Pach
[
Fox+12
]
gave an affirmative answer to this question both via random construction and through
[
LSV05
]
’s Ramanujan complexes. Finally, a series of works
[
PRT16
,
Evr17
,
Opp18
]
extended their ideas to general spectral high dimensional expanders by leveraging high dimensional expander-mixing properties. We note that Gromov also defined a closely related (but stricter) notion of topological overlap
[
Gro10
]
, which was later studied by
[
KKL14
,
EK16
]
among others. We focus only on the geometric setting here.

While prior works have succeeded in showing optimal geometric overlap for
random
bounded-degree complexes
[
Fox+12
, Theorem 1.7]
, it has been open since Gromov’s work to construct such a family
explicitly
. In this subsection, building on the approach of these prior works, we show sufficiently strong high dimensional expanders have optimal overlap, resolving this problem.

To state our result, it is useful to first understand the overlap properties of the complete complex. Let
c_{k,n}
be the largest constant so that
\Delta_{n}{\left(k+1\right)}
has
(k,c_{k,n})
-geometric overlap, and let
c(k)=\lim_{n\to\infty}c_{k,n}
. By
[
Bár82
]
this limit exists and is positive. Similarly, let
c_{d,k,n}^{p}
be the largest constant so that the complete
d
-partite complex with
n
-vertices on every side has
(k,c_{d,k,n}^{p})
-geometric overlap, and let
c_{d,k}^{p}=\lim_{n\to\infty}c_{d,k,n}^{p}
. Existence and positivity of this limit follows from
[
Pac98
]
. The former constant,
c{\left(k\right)}
, is known to be the best possible for bounded degree simplicial complexes
[
Fox+12
, Theorem 1.6]
.

We show that every sufficiently strong (partite) high dimensional expanders has geometric overlap nearly matching the (partite) complete complex:

Theorem 9.13
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
and
k<d\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
\lambda,\lambda_{p}>0
, and
n_{0},n_{0,p}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

1.

Two-sided case:
If
X
is
d
-uniform
\lambda
-two-sided high dimensional expander on at least
n_{0}
vertices so that the measure on the vertices is uniform, then
X
has
(k,c_{k}-\varepsilon)
-geometric overlap.

2.

Partite case:
If
X
is
d
-partite
\lambda_{p}
-one-sided HDX on at least
n_{0,p}
vertices so that the measure on the vertices is uniform, then
X
has
(k,c^{p}_{d,k}-\varepsilon)
-geometric overlap.

We comment that this theorem applies to many of the complexes constructed in
[
LSV05
]
(for the non-partite setting we need to take lower uniform skeletons).

The proof of
Theorem
9.13
combines our concentration bounds with tools developed in
[
Fox+12
]
. We have not tried to find the optimal dependence on
\lambda
or on the degree of the complex. Finally before moving to the proof, we remark that the result really only relies on the weaker ‘high-dimensional expander-mixing’ type inequality which actually holds for the more general family of splitting-trees such as expander walks (see
Section
10.1
). Thus it is possible to obtain explicit complexes with geometric overlap approaching that of the complete (partite) complex through this more general family as well, though we omit the details.

Homogeneous tuples

The core of
[
Fox+12
]
’s proof of geometric overlap is the following partitioning theorem. We follow their definitions and presentation precisely. A tuple of subsets
S_{1},\ldots,S_{k+1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
is said to be
homogeneous
with respect to a point
q\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
if either:

1.

All simplices with one vertex in each of the sets
S_{1},\ldots,S_{k+1}
contain
q
.

2.

None of these simplices contain
q
.

An
equipartition
of a finite set is a partition of the set into subsets whose sizes differ by at most one.

Theorem 9.14

(
[
Fox+12
, Corollary 1.9]
)
.

Let
k
be a positive integer and
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a positive integer
K=K(\varepsilon,k)\geqslant k+1
such that for any
K^{\prime}\geqslant K
the following statement holds. For any finite set
P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
and for any point
q\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, there is an equipartition
P=P_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\dots\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}P_{K^{\prime}}
such that all but at most an
\varepsilon
-fraction of the
(k+1)
-tuples
P_{i_{1}},\ldots,P_{i_{k+1}}
are homogeneous with respect to
q
.

Our proof adapts the idea in
[
Fox+12
, Theorem 1.7]
.

Proof of
Theorem
9.13
.

We split the proof into the two-sided and partite cases.

Two-sided Case

Let
K=K(\varepsilon/16,k)
be the constant promised in
Theorem
9.14
and
K^{\prime}\geqslant K
be the smallest constant so that for any equipartition of the
n
-vertices to
K^{\prime}
parts, the fraction of faces in the complete complex (over the same vertex set) that touch every part at most once is at least
1-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}
.
19
19

19

One can verify as in
[
Fox+12
, Theorem 1.7]
that
K^{\prime}=\max\{K,16(d+1)/\varepsilon\}
for large enough
n
.
Let
z_{0}=\frac{1}{2K^{\prime}}
and let
n_{0}
be such that for any
n\geqslant n_{0}
and sets
A_{1},A_{2},\dots,A_{k+1}\subset[n]
of measure at least
\frac{1}{K^{\prime}}-z_{0}
it holds that

\ProbOp_{{\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k+1}\}\in{\Delta_{n}{\left(k+1\right)}}}}\left[\forall j=1,\dots,k+1:\penalty\ v_{j}\in A_{j}\right]\geqslant\prod_{j=1}^{k+1}\ProbOp\left[{A_{j}}\right]-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}.

Let
X
be as in the statement and
\rho:X{\left(1\right)}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
an embedding. There is a point
q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
so that
q_{0}
is contained in
c_{k}-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}
of the faces of
\Delta_{n}{\left(k+1\right)}
embedded via
\rho
.

Let
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{K^{\prime}}
be the partition we obtain from
Theorem
9.14
with respect to
\rho
and the complete complex. Let
H=\left\{\{P_{i_{1}},P_{i_{2}},\dots,P_{i_{k+1}}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{P_{i_{1}},P_{i_{2}},\dots,P_{i_{k+1}}\}\forall s\in E(P_{i_{1}},P_{i_{2}},\dots,P_{i_{k+1}}),\;q_{0}\in conv(\rho(s))}\right.\forall s\in E(P_{i_{1}},P_{i_{2}},\dots,P_{i_{k+1}}),\;q_{0}\in conv(\rho(s))\right\}
. Here
E(P_{i_{1}},P_{i_{2}},\dots,P_{i_{k+1}})
are the faces
s\in X{\left(k+1\right)}
such that for every
j=1,\dots,k+1
,
\left\lvert s\cap P_{i_{j}}\right\rvert=1
. Recall that at most an
\frac{\varepsilon}{16}
-fraction of the tuples are not homogeneous and that at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{16}
of the faces in
\Delta_{n}{\left(k+1\right)}
touch a part in this partition more than once.
Thus,

\sum_{(P_{i_{j}})_{j=1}^{k+1}\in H}\prod_{j=1}^{k+1}\ProbOp\left[{P_{i_{j}}}\right]\geqslant c_{k}-\frac{3\varepsilon}{16}.

(9.2)

On the other hand,

\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k+1\right)}}}}\left[q_{0}\in conv(\rho(s))\right]\geqslant\sum_{\{P_{i_{1}},P_{i_{1}},\dots,P_{i_{k+1}}\}\in H}{\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k+1\right)}}}}\left[E(P_{i_{1}},P_{i_{2}},\dots,P_{i_{k+1}})\right]}.

(9.3)

By
Proposition
7.9
(applied for indicators of the corresponding functions) for small enough
\lambda=\lambda(K^{\prime},\varepsilon,z_{0})
, for every
k+1
parts
P_{i_{1}},P_{i_{2}},\dots,P_{i_{k+1}}
of size at least
\frac{1}{K^{\prime}}-z_{0}
,

\ProbOp_{{\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k+1}\}\in{X{\left(k+1\right)}}}}\left[\forall j=1,\dots,k+1\;v_{j}\in P_{i_{j}}\right]\geqslant(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})\prod_{j=1}^{k+1}\ProbOp\left[{P_{i_{j}}}\right].

Combining (
9.2
) and (
9.3
) gives

\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k+1\right)}}}}\left[q_{0}\in\rho(s)\right]\geqslant(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})(c_{k}-\frac{\varepsilon}{4})\geqslant c_{k}-\varepsilon
as desired.

Partite Case

The argument is similar. Let
K=K(\varepsilon/16,k)
be the constant promised in
Theorem
9.14
. Let
K^{\prime}\geqslant K
be the smallest constant so that for any equipartition over
K^{\prime}
parts, the fraction of faces in the complete partite complex that touch every part at most once is at least
1-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}
.

Let
z=\frac{1}{2K^{\prime}}
be as before. As before we first identify the points of
X
with the complete partite complex with the same number of vertices in every side. Then we find a point
q_{0}
such that
c_{d,k}^{p}-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}
of the faces of the complete partite complex contain
q_{0}
. We find an equipartition
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{K^{\prime}}
of
\rho(X{\left(1\right)})
to
K^{\prime}
parts such that
1-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}
of the parts are homogeneous with respect to
q_{0}
.

We partition further every
P_{i}
according to the colors of the vertices to
P_{i}^{j}
for
j=1,2,\dots,d
. Then we remove from the partition every subset
P_{i}^{j}
whose relative size is smaller than
\frac{\varepsilon}{16K^{\prime}\cdot d^{2}}
. We note that by taking only the remaining sets in the partition the fraction of vertices we removed from every color is no more than
\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
vertices in any side. This is because for every
P_{i}
, colors
j
that have that
\ProbOp\left[{P_{i}^{j}}\right]\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{16K^{\prime}\cdot d^{2}}
can account for no more than
\frac{\varepsilon}{16K^{\prime}\cdot d}
out of at least
\frac{1}{K^{\prime}}-z=\frac{1}{2K^{\prime}}
. Thus we have not removed more than
\frac{\varepsilon}{8d}
vertices in total, or
\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
-fraction out of each color.

We now have subsets
P_{i}^{j}
that such that
\frac{\varepsilon}{16K^{\prime}d^{2}}\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{P_{i}^{j}}\right]\leqslant\frac{3}{2K^{\prime}}
. The proof now follows as in the two-sided case.
∎

9.4
Separating MLSI from Reverse Hypercontractivity

As discussed in
Section
1
, prior techniques for establishing reverse hypercontractivity relied on tensorization or bounded (Modified) Log-Sobolev constant (MLSI). To our knowledge, all known reverse-hypercontractive objects prior to this work also have bounded MLSI. In this section, we give the first separation between these analytic notions in the case where the leading hypercontractive constant
C
may be greater than
1
(or, alternatively, for the indicator variant). To start, recall the definition of MLSI.

Definition 9.15

(Modified Log-Sobolev Inequality)
.

Let
M
be a reversible markov chain with stationary distribution
\pi_{s}
. The modified log-sobolev constant of
M
is:

\rho_{\text{\tiny{MLSI}}}=\inf_{f>0}\frac{\langle f,(I-M)\log f\rangle}{2\text{Ent}_{\pi}(f^{2})}

MLSI is classically used to bound the
mixing time
of its associated Markov chain,

T_{\text{mix}}(M,\varepsilon)=\min_{t}\max_{\pi_{init}}\lVert\pi_{init}^{T}M^{t}-\pi_{s}\rVert_{TV}\leqslant\varepsilon,

due to the following upper bound:

Fact 9.16
.

The mixing time of a reversible Markov chain
M
with stationary distribution
\pi
is at most:

T_{\text{mix}}(M,\varepsilon)\leqslant\frac{1}{\rho_{\text{\tiny{MLSI}}}}\log\left(\frac{\log\pi_{*}^{-1}}{\varepsilon}\right),

where
\pi_{*}
is the minimum stationary probability.

High dimensional expanders (at least in the bounded degree setting), are
slow-mixing
, so they cannot have small MLSI constant. In particular, applying this fact to the Ramanujan complexes gives an infinite family of reverse-hypercontractive operators with at least inverse logarithmic dependence on the domain size.

Corollary 9.17
.

There exist constants
\ell>1
and
C>0
and an infinite family
\{T_{\rho}^{(n)}\}
of
(\frac{1}{\ell},\frac{\ell}{1-\ell},C)
-reverse hypercontractive operators with at most inverse logarithmic MLSI:

\rho_{MLSI}\leqslant C_{2}\frac{\log^{3}\log(\pi_{*})}{\log\pi_{*}}

Proof.

Set
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
, let
c
be as in
Theorem
6.3
, and let
\{X^{(n)}\}
be the family of
k=k(n)
-uniform LSV complexes promised in
Theorem
9.4
for
k=\log\log n
and
\lambda=2^{-k}
, and recall that

1.

The degree of every vertex is at most
2^{C_{2}k^{3}}
for some universal constant
C_{2}>0

2.

The stationary distribution of
T_{1/2}
on
X
is uniform

By
Theorem
6.3
, there exist (universal) constants
C,\ell
such that the noise operator
T_{1/2}
is
(\frac{1}{\ell},\frac{\ell}{1-\ell},C)
-reverse hypercontractive for every complex in the family. On the other hand, we will argue that:

T_{\text{mix}}\left(T_{\rho},\frac{1}{2}\right)\geqslant\min\left\{\frac{2^{k}}{4},\frac{C^{\prime}_{2}\log(n)}{k^{3}}\right\},

which combined with
Fact
9.16
completes the result.

Define the
\varepsilon
-support of a distribution

\text{supp}^{\geqslant}_{\varepsilon}(\pi)\coloneqq\{x:\pi(x)\geqslant\varepsilon\}.

Setting
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{4n}
, observe that any distribution
\pi
with
|\text{supp}^{\geqslant}_{\varepsilon}(\pi)|<\frac{n}{4}
has
\lVert\pi-\pi_{s}\rVert_{TV}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
. We will argue this holds for
\pi_{\text{init}}T_{\frac{1}{2}}^{t}
whenever
\pi_{\text{init}}
is any indicator and
t
is sufficiently small.

To see this, we separate out the bounded-degree and stationary components of
T_{\frac{1}{2}}
as:

T_{\frac{1}{2}}^{+}\coloneqq T_{\frac{1}{2}}-2^{-k}\Pi

where
\Pi
is the stationary operator, and note that
T_{\frac{1}{2}}=T_{\frac{1}{2}}^{+}+2^{-k}\Pi
. Observe that after
t
steps, we can write any initial distribution
\pi
as:

\pi^{T}T_{\frac{1}{2}}^{t}=\pi^{T}(T^{+}_{\frac{1}{2}}+2^{-k}\Pi)^{t}=\pi^{T}(T^{+}_{\frac{1}{2}})^{t}+(1-(1-2^{-k})^{t})\pi_{s}

For large enough
k
, as long as
t\leqslant 2^{-k/4}
, the latter term contributes strictly less than
\frac{1}{4}
to each coordinate. On the other hand for
\pi
an indicator, the first term contributes positive weight to at most
deg(T_{\frac{1}{2}^{+}})^{t}
coordinates, so setting
t
such that
deg(T_{\frac{1}{2}^{+}})^{t}\leqslant\frac{n}{4}
completes the result.
∎

9.5
It Ain’t Over Till It’s Over

Friedgut and Kalai’s ‘It Ain’t Over Till It’s Over’ Theorem is a classical result in social choice which states that even after taking a significant random restriction, any balanced function still has some uncertainty with high probability (in other words, it is a tail bound on the random restriction operator). The precursor to this Theorem was a version for the noise operator proved in
[
MOO05
, Appendix C]
. We prove the result holds for any LUS, which implies the same for any
c
-nice complex.

Theorem 9.18
.

For any
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\tau>0
, let
X
be a
d
-uniform
\tau
-LUS for
d
sufficiently large. Then there exists a constant
q=q(\rho,\tau)
such that for any
\delta>0
and
f:X{\left(d\right)}\to[0,1]
with density
\mu=\mathbb{E}[f]
:

\ProbOp[T_{\rho}f>1-\delta]\leqslant c_{\mu,1}\delta^{q}

where
c_{\mu,1}=\left(\frac{8(1+\mu)^{q+1}}{(1-\mu)^{q+2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
, and

\ProbOp[T_{\rho}f<\delta]\leqslant c_{\mu,2}\delta^{q}

where
c_{\mu,2}=\left(\frac{8(2-\mu)^{q+1}}{\mu^{q+2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
.

Proof.

We prove the former. The latter follows from considering
1-f
. Let
T
of the set of elements whose neighborhoods are dense in
f
:

T\coloneqq\{x:T_{\rho}f(x)\geqslant 1-\delta\},

and consider the indicator cut-off
h=\mathbf{1}_{f\leqslant\frac{1+\mu}{2}}
. We will argue that on the one hand the correlation of
T
and
h
cannot be large, since when
x\in T
,
y\sim_{\rho}x
must be mostly above the cutoff. On the other hand, the correlation is at least some power of
\mu(T)
by reverse hypercontractivity, so
\mu(T)
must be small.

Formally, observe that for any
x\in T
, Markov’s inequality gives that
T_{\rho}h(x)\leqslant\frac{2\delta}{1-\mu}
so

\mathbb{E}[1_{T}T_{\rho}h]\leqslant\mu(T)\delta\frac{2}{1-\mu}.

On the other hand, we also have by Markov that
\mathbb{E}[h]\geqslant\frac{1-\mu}{1+\mu}
, so reverse hypercontractivity for indicators (
Theorem
6.4
) implies there exists some
\ell
such that:

\mathbb{E}[1_{T}T_{\rho}h]\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\left(\mu(T)\frac{1-\mu}{1+\mu}\right)^{\ell}

Combining the equations and setting
q=\ell-1
gives the desired result.
∎

We note we have made no attempt to optimize the constants, and it may be possible to improve dependence on
\mu
.
[
MOO05
]
combine a variant of
Theorem
9.18
with the invariance principle to prove the classical form of the conjecture with respect to random restrictions. It is not clear what the correct form of this conjecture should be for HDX, or even for product spaces.
20
20

20

We remark it should be possible to give a version of the result for suitably ‘global’ functions via tools of
[
Kee+21
,
GLL22
,
Baf+22a
]
, but it is not clear such a notion would have significant qualitative meaning
We leave this as an open question.

9.6
A Frankl–Rödl Theorem

The Frankl–Rödl Theorem
[
FR87
]
is a powerful result from extremal combinatorics that states that the independence number of the graph whose vertices are
\{0,1\}^{n}
and whose edges are given by fixed weight intersection
k
(typically for some
k=\Theta(n)
) is at most
\exp(-\Omega(k))|V|
. Benabbas, Hatami, and Magen
[
Ben12
]
proved a variant of this Theorem via reverse hypercontractivity on the cube, later leading to several applications in hardness of approximation
[
Kau+14
]
. Their method, which also bounds classical properties such as the chromatic and dominating set numbers, is based on the following claim:

Claim 9.19
.

Let
p_{0},q_{0}>0
and let
G=(V,E)
be a weighted graph with minimum vertex weight
q_{0}
such that for every
A,B\subset V
of relative size at least
p_{0}
:

\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2})\sim E}}\left[s_{1}\in A,s_{2}\in B\right]>0.

Then the maximal independent set in
G
has size at most
2p_{0}+q_{0}
, the minimal dominating set has relative size at least
1-(2p_{0}+q_{0})
, and the chromatic number is at least
\frac{1}{2p_{0}+q_{0}}
.

As a corollary we have that the down-up walks in any of the discussed LUS-complexes in
Section
6.3
have maximal independent sets of relative size at most
\exp(-\Omega(k))
, a chromatic number of at least
\exp(\Omega(k))
and minimal dominating sets of size at least
1-\exp(-\Omega(k))
. As discussed in the introduction, we remark that these graphs are not quite the correct analog of Frankl–Rödl, since they have edges with intersection up to some fixed
\gamma k
instead of
\gamma k
exactly. Benabbas, Hatami and Magen
[
Ben12
]
handle this by analyzing the closeness of the exact intersection versus noise operator on the cube, using tools from Fourier analysis. It would be interesting to see if a variant of this result holds on HDX. If this could be shown generally, then our reverse hypercontractivity theorem would recover Frankl–Rödl up to constants in the exponent.

Proof of
Claim
9.19
.

We prove the statement for independent sets. The chromatic number statement follows since every coloring is a partition to independent sets, and the statement for dominating sets follows since the complement of any dominating set is an independent set. Let
I\subseteq G
be an independent set and assume toward contradiction that
\ProbOp\left[{I}\right]\geqslant 2p_{0}+q_{0}
. We partition
I
into
I_{1},I_{2}
such that
\ProbOp\left[{I_{1}}\right],\ProbOp\left[{I_{2}}\right]\geqslant p_{0}
(we can do so by greedily adding vertices to
I_{1}
until its probability is between
p_{0}
and
p_{0}+q_{0}
). Then by assumption
\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2})\sim E}}\left[s_{1}\in I_{1},s_{2}\in I_{2}\right]>0
, which gives the desired contradiction.
∎

Remark 9.20
.

Although the graphs we discussed in this paper have self loops. The claim above continues to hold after removing these self loops, as is evident from the proof.

10
Codes and Splitting Trees

Error correcting codes, and in particular the powerful notions of
list-decoding
and
local-testability
, are among the earliest successful applications of inclusion samplers
[
Imp+08
,
IJK09
,
IKW12
]
and HDX
[
Din+18
,
Ale+20
,
JST21
,
PK22
,
Din+22
]
. In this section we explore the implications of our tools and related ideas over the weaker family of ‘splitting trees’ in this classical setting. Our main application is the first construction of constant rate codes over large alphabets which simultaneously have 1) near-optimal distance 2) list-decodability and 3) local-testability. At the end of the section we introduce a (conjectural) HDX-based approach toward ‘lossless’ distance-amplification of LTCs that experiences no decay in soundness.

10.1
Splitting Trees

We start with a fairly substantial detour into the world of splitting trees, which are a weakening of high dimensional expanders introduced in
[
AJT19
]
which only requires certain patterns of swap walks to expand. We will prove a high-dimensional expander mixing lemma and some basic sampling properties for general splitting trees that will be useful for our applications to coding theory.

10.1.1
Splitting Preliminaries

Given a binary tree
T
, let
\mathcal{L}(T)
denote
T
’s leaves and
\mathcal{I}(T)
its internal nodes. We drop
T
from the notation when clear from context. For every internal node
v\in\mathcal{I}
, denote the left and right children of
v
by
\ell_{u}
and
r_{u}
respectively.

Definition 10.1

(Ordered Binary Tree)
.

A
d
-uniform ordered binary tree is a pair
(T,\rho)
where
T
is a rooted binary tree with
d
leaves, and
\rho:T\to[d]
is a labeling such that:

1.

The label of every leaf
u\in\mathcal{L}
is
\rho(u)=1
.

2.

For every interior
u\in\mathcal{I}
, it holds that
\rho(u)=\rho(\ell_{u})+\rho(r_{u})
.

We call such a
\rho
well-ordered with respect to
T
.

A complex is called splittable if it can be decomposed by an ordered tree where every non-leaf node corresponds to an expanding swap walk.

Definition 10.2

(Splitting tree)
.

Let
\gamma>0
. A
d
-uniform
\gamma
-splitting tree
is a triple
(X,T,\rho)
where
X
is a
d
-uniform simplicial complex and
(T,\rho)
is an ordered binary tree such that for every
u\in\mathcal{I}(T)
:

\lambda(S_{\rho(\ell_{u}),\rho(r_{u})})\leqslant\gamma,

We will also work with the corresponding partite notion of splitting trees.

Definition 10.3

(Partite Ordered Binary Tree)
.

A
d
-uniform partite ordered binary tree is a pair
(T,\rho)
where
T
is a rooted binary tree with
d
leaves, and
\rho:T\to\mathbb{P}\{[d]\}\setminus\{\emptyset\}
is a labeling such that:

1.

\rho|_{\mathcal{L}}
is a bijection to the singletons of
[d]
.

2.

For every interior
u\in\mathcal{I}
, it holds that
\rho(u)=\rho(\ell_{u})\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\rho(r_{u})
.

We remark that when convenient, we may use a general set
S
of size
d+1
as the domain of
\rho
(generally corresponding to a labeling of the "parts" of an associated
d
-uniform partite complex).

Definition 10.4

(Tuple splitting tree)
.

Let
\gamma>0
. A
d
-uniform
\gamma
-tuple splitting tree
is a triple
(X,T,\rho)
where
X
is a
d
-uniform simplicial complex and
(T,\rho)
is an ordered partite binary tree such that for every non-leaf vertex
u\in T
:

\lambda(S_{\rho(\ell_{u}),\rho(r_{u})})\leqslant\gamma

Since all swap walks on two-sided HDX expand, these give rise to a basic family of splitting trees.

Example 10.5

(
[
AJT19
,
DD19
,
GLL22
]
)
.

Let
\lambda\geqslant 0
and let
X
be a
d
-uniform two-sided
\lambda
-high dimensional expander. Then for any binary tree
T
and labeling
\rho
that satisfies the first and second item in
Definition
10.2
,
(X,T,\rho)
is a
d\lambda
-splitting tree.

An easy consequence of
[
DD19
]
is that partite one-sided HDX also give rise to tuple-splitting trees.

Example 10.6
.

Let
\lambda\geqslant 0
and let
X
be a
d
-uniform two-sided
\lambda
-high dimensional expander. Then for any binary tree
T
and labeling
\rho
that satisfy the first and second item in
Definition
10.4
,
(X,T,\rho)
is a
d^{2}\lambda
-tuple splitting tree.

Finally,
[
Ale+20
, Corollary 9.18]
also prove that walks on expander graphs are splittable.
Let us define the complex formally. Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
W_{G}
be the following
k
-partite simplicial complex

W_{G}{\left(1\right)}=V\times[k],

W_{G}{\left(k\right)}=\left\{\{(v_{1},1),(v_{2},2),\ldots,(v_{k},k)\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{(v_{1},1),(v_{2},2),\ldots,(v_{k},k)\}(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})\text{ is a walk in }G}\right.(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})\text{ is a walk in }G\right\}.

We choose a face in
W_{G}{\left(k\right)}
by choosing a vertex
v_{1}\in V
according to the stationary distribution over vertices in
G
and then taking a
(k-1)
-length random walk on that vertex.

Example 10.7
.

Let
G
be a
\lambda
-expander and
T,\rho
an ordered partite tree. If every
u\in\mathcal{I}
satisfies:

\max\rho(\ell_{u})<\min\rho(r_{u}),

then
(W_{G},T,\rho)
is a
\lambda
-splittable tree.

10.1.2
Operations on splitting trees

We will need several further operations on splitting trees for our analysis: a
pruning
method that allow us to analyze higher dimensionalT faces, and a
partitification
technique that allows us to reduce standard splitting trees to the tuple setting.

We start with the latter. Recall
X
’s partitification is the complex

P{\left(k\right)}\coloneqq\left\{(s,\pi)\coloneqq\left\{(s_{\pi(1)},1),(s_{\pi(2)},2),\ldots,(s_{\pi(k)},k)\right\}\left|\;\vphantom{(s,\pi)\coloneqq\left\{(s_{\pi(1)},1),(s_{\pi(2)},2),\ldots,(s_{\pi(k)},k)\right\}s\in X{\left(k\right)},\pi\in S_{k}}\right.s\in X{\left(k\right)},\pi\in S_{k}\right\},

This notion extends naturally to splitting trees.

Definition 10.8
.

Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a
d
-uniform
\lambda
-splitting tree, and
\rho^{\prime}:T\to[d]
a well-ordered labeling such that
|\rho^{\prime}(u)|=\rho(u)
for every
u\in T
. The
\rho^{\prime}
-partitification
of
(X,T,\rho)
is defined to be the tuple splitting tree
(P,T,\rho^{\prime})
.

Any un-ordered splitting tree can be embed into a tuple splitting tree by taking any partitification.

Claim 10.9
.

Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a
\lambda
-splitting tree. Every
\rho^{\prime}
-partitification of
(X,T,\rho)
is a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree.

Proof.

It suffices to show for every internal node
u\in\mathcal{I}
with
\rho^{\prime}(\ell(u))=A
and
\rho^{\prime}(r(u))=B
that
S_{A,B}(P)
is a
\lambda
-bipartite expander. This follows from the observation that
S_{A,B}(P)
is isomorphic to the bipartite graph with left vertices
L=X{\left(|A|\right)}\times[|A|!]
, right vertices
R=X{\left(|B|\right)}\times[|B|!]
, and edges
(s,i)\to(t,j)
for all
i\in A,j\in B
, and
(s,t)
such that
s\sim t
in
S_{\rho(\ell_{u}),\rho(r_{u})}
.
21
21

21

Formally, the edge
(s,i)\to(t,j)
also inherits the weight
\frac{\ProbOp(st)}{|A||B|}
from
S_{\rho(\ell_{u}),\rho(r_{u})}
.
It is a standard fact that such a bipartite construction inherits its expansion from
S_{\rho(\ell_{u}),\rho(r_{u})}
, and is therefore a
\lambda
-bipartite expander.
∎

Finally, we note that tuple splitting trees can be pruned to create new splitting trees. Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a tuple splitting tree with
k
leaves. Let
u\in\mathcal{I}
be an internal node whose label is
F=\rho(u)
.
Let
P=P(X,F)
be the partite complex with vertices
P{\left(1\right)}=X^{[k]\setminus F}{\left(1\right)}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}X[F]
and top level faces
t\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{s\}
such that
t\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s\in X{\left(k\right)}
, inheriting the corresponding densities. In other words, we replace the vertices of
X
in
X^{F}{\left(1\right)}
with the set of faces
X[F]
.

Let
T(u)
be the sub-tree of
T
rooted by
u
. Let
T^{\prime}=(T\setminus T(u))\cup\{u\}
. Let
\rho^{\prime}:T^{\prime}\to([k]\setminus F)\cup\{F\}
be the labeling that replaces the subset
F
with a single replacement index
f
:

\rho^{\prime}(v)=\begin{cases}(\rho(v)\setminus F)\cup\{f\}&F\subseteq\rho(v)\\
\rho(v)&otherwise\end{cases}.

Definition 10.10

(Pruning Trees)
.

Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree with
k
leaves. Let
u\in T
be a non-leaf node whose label is
F=\rho(u)
. The
u
-pruning of the tree is the triple
(P(X,F),T^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
.

Claim 10.11
.

Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree. Let
u\in T
be a non-leaf node. Then the
u
-pruning is also a
\lambda
-splitting tree.
∎

The proof follows directly from the definitions and we therefore omit it.

Finally, it will be convenient to introduce notation for
sequential pruning
, that is given a collection of disjoint color sets
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{m}\}
, we denote the sequential pruning of
X
by
\mathcal{F}
as
P(X,\mathcal{F})\coloneqq P(...P(X,F_{1}),F_{2}),F_{m})
. Note that the resulting complex is independent of the order of
\mathcal{F}
, so
P(X,\mathcal{F})
is well-defined. One can similarly define a sequentially pruned splitting tree as above. It is immediate from
Claim
10.11
that these are also
\lambda
-tuple splitting.

10.1.3
Hitting set and Bias amplification

In this section, we prove two basic ‘sampling-type’ properties for splitting trees: hitting set and bias amplification. We start with the former. Recall a complex is
(\gamma,i)
-hitting if for any
A\subset X{\left(0\right)}
:

\ProbOp_{\sigma\in X{\left(i\right)}}[\sigma\subset A]\leqslant\mu(A)^{i}+\gamma

Proposition 10.12

(Hitting Set)
.

Any depth
D

\lambda
-(tuple) splitting tree with
k
leaves is
(3^{D}\lambda,k)
-hitting.

Splitting trees also exhibit strong bias amplification, used to build balanced error correcting codes.

Proposition 10.13

(Bias-Amplification)
.

Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree for
\lambda<\frac{1}{16}
. For any
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{4}
and family of mean
\varepsilon
functions
\{f_{i}:X[i]\to\{\pm 1\}\}_{i\in[k]}
,
\prod f_{i}
is a
\{\pm 1\}
-valued function with bias at most:

\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(a_{i})\right]\right\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon^{k}+2\lambda

(10.1)

While
Proposition
10.12
and
Proposition
10.13
are elementary, to the authors’ knowledge they do not appear in the literature. Prior works on HDX (e.g.
[
DK17
,
Ale+20
]
) use weaker variants of these lemmas which require super-exponential overhead to achieve a given distance or bias, while the above only requires quasi-polynomial overhead. We discuss this in greater detail in
Section
10
.

These results are immediate corollaries of a functional variant of
[
DD19
]
’s “high dimensional expander-mixing lemma” (HD-EML) extended to splitting trees. We give the statement here and include a proof in the appendix for completeness along with the proofs of
Proposition
10.12
and
Proposition
10.13
.

Theorem 10.14

(high dimensional expander Mixing Lemma)
.

Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a depth
D

\lambda
-tuple splitting tree with
k
leaves. Denote by
d_{i}
the depth of the leaf labeled
i
. Then for any family of functions
\{f_{i}:X[i]\to\mathbb{R}\}_{i\in[k]}
:

\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(a_{i})\right]-\prod_{i=1}^{k}\E_{{a_{i}\in X[i]}}\left[f_{i}(a_{i})\right]\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{k}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}.

(10.2)

If
(X,T,\rho)
is instead a depth
D
standard
\lambda
-splitting tree, we take
\{f_{i}:X{\left(1\right)}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{i\in[k]}
and have:

\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k\right)}},\pi\in S_{k}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(a_{\pi(i)})\right]-\prod_{i=1}^{k}\E_{{a_{i}\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[f_{i}(a_{i})\right]\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{k}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}.

(10.3)

In the future, we state our results only for the partite setting unless there is a substantial difference in proof or parameters for the results. Otherwise, we collect analog statements for the unordered case at the end of
Appendix
D
for completeness.

We close the subsection with a few remarks. First it should be noted that for the special case of expander walks, better hitting set and bias amplification methods are known (indeed one can achieve polynomial overhead vs our quasipolynomial bound above). Nevertheless, it is useful to have these lemmas on structures like HDX that admit stronger properties like agreement tests. We remark it is also actually possible to use the high dimensional expander mixing lemma for splitting trees directly to prove a different variant of Chernoff-Hoeffding by reduction to the complete complex. However, this results in exponentially worse parameters than our arguments in
Section
5
, and we will not need the result in the following.

10.2
Coding Preliminaries

A
q
-ary error correcting code
C
over a finite size-
q
alphabet
\Sigma
is a subset
C\subset\Sigma^{n}
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}
is called the
block length
. We say a code is
linear
if
\Sigma=\mathbb{F}_{p}
for prime power
p
and
C
is a subspace, and
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linear
if
\Sigma=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
C
is a subspace of
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k})^{n}
.

The
rate
of a code
C
, which measures its overhead, is
r=\frac{\log_{|\Sigma|}(|C|)}{n}
, and its
distance
, which captures the codes error correction capability, is the minimum normalized hamming distance between any two codewords

d\coloneqq\min_{c_{1},c_{2}\in C}\left\{\frac{|\{i\in[n]:c_{1}(i)\neq c_{2}(i)\}|}{n}\right\}.

We remark that for any
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linear code, the distance is the minimum weight of a non-zero codeword. For ease of notation, we will call a
q
-ary code with rate
r
and distance
d
an
(r,d)_{q}
-code.

We will be interested in so-called “good” families of codes, which are infinite families with growing block length and constant rate and distance. We will focus in particular in this section on building large distance
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-linear codes over
\Sigma=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
. In this regime there is a classical upper bound due to McEliece, Rodemich, Rumsey, and Welch
[
McE+77
]
stating that good
q
-ary codes have distance at most
1-\frac{1}{q}
, or more exactly:

Theorem 10.15

(MRRW Bound
[
McE+77
]
)
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
, any family of
q
-ary codes with distance
1-\frac{1}{q}-\varepsilon
has rate at most:

r\leqslant O\left(\varepsilon^{2}\log(1/\varepsilon)\right).

Given a code
C
, a
unique decoding algorithm
with radius
\gamma\in[0,1/2]
is a (possibly randomized) algorithm
Dec:\Sigma^{n}\to C
which given any
w\in\Sigma^{n}
such that
dist(w,C)\leqslant\gamma
outputs the uniquely closest
y\in C
to
w
. Note that unique decoding is only possible up to radius
\lfloor\frac{nd-1}{2n}\rfloor\leqslant 1/2
, even with large alphabet, as there may be no unique closest codeword beyond this point.

List-decoding
is the natural extension of unique decoding beyond the
\frac{1}{2}
barrier. A code is said to be list-decodable with radius
t
if there is an algorithm which outputs a
list
of all codewords within radius
t
of the given word. We first give the combinatorial definition, which simply bounds the number of codewords around any fixed point.

Definition 10.16

(Combinatorial List-decoding)
.

For
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
L\in\mathbb{N}
, a code
C
is said to be
(\gamma,L)
list-decodable if for every
z\in Y
, there are at most
L
codewords
x\in C
such that
dist(x,z)<\gamma
.

In this section, we will be interested in
efficient algorithms
