for list-decoding, typically a much more challening task than the existential definition.

Definition 10.17

(Efficient List-decoding)
.

For
\delta>0
,
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
L\in\mathbb{N}
, a family of codes
\{C_{n}\}
is said to be efficiently
(\gamma,L)
list-decodable with confidence
\delta
if it is combinatorially
(\gamma,L)
list-decodable and there exists a (randomized) polynomial time
22
22

22

Here we mean polynomial in the block length.
algorithm
Dec
:\Sigma^{n}\to P(C)
such that for every word
w\in\Sigma^{n}
:

\textsc{Dec}(w)=\{c\in C:\text{dist}(c,w)<\gamma\}

except with probability
\delta
.

Another classical approach to handling the high error regime is
local testability
. A code
C
is said to be locally testable if there is a (randomized, non-adaptive) algorithm
\mathcal{T}_{C}
which on input of a word
w\in\Sigma^{n}
, queries constantly many symbols of
w
and rejects with high probability if it is far from the code. In practice, if one receives such a word, instead of trying to list decode they could simply request re-transmission. More formally, we consider the following standard notion of ‘strong’ local testability:

Definition 10.18

(Locally Testable Code)
.

For any
s>0
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
, a code
C
of blocklength
n
is said to be
(q,s)
-locally testable if there exists a function
\mathcal{T}_{C}:[n]^{q}\times\Sigma^{q}\to\{0,1\}
and a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
[n]^{q}
such that on any word
w\in\Sigma^{n}
, the pair
(\mathcal{T}_{C},\mathcal{D})
(called the tester) satisfies:

1.

Completeness:
If
w\in C
, then
\ProbOp_{J\sim\mathcal{D}}[\mathcal{T}_{C}(J,w_{J})=1]=1

2.

Soundness:

\ProbOp_{J\sim\mathcal{D}}[\mathcal{T}_{C}(J,w_{J})=0]\geqslant s\cdot\text{dist}(w,C)

We call
q
the locality, and
s
the soundness.

Typically we will simply think of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
as a randomized algorithm and write
\mathcal{T}_{C}(w)
to denote its application on the word
w
, dropping the distribution of the tester when clear from context. We will work with testers that have one additional constraint: their queries should be marginally uniform.

Definition 10.19

(Uniform LTC)
.

(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{D})
is called
uniform
if every marginal of
\mathcal{D}
is uniform over
[n]
.

We remark that focusing on uniform LTCs is not particularly restrictive—since codes do not typically have ‘preferred’ coordinates, most natural constructions are uniform.

Finally, though not strictly necessary, we will assume throughout that all complexes in this section are “homogenous” subsets
X\subset[n]^{k}
, meaning that their marginal distribution over each part is uniform over
[n]
. We remark that in the context of high dimensional expanders, any partite ‘type-regular’ complex can be homogenized in this fashion while maintaining expansion and bounded degree (see
[
FI20
]
for details). As most known constructions of HDX are type-regular, this is not a particularly restrictive assumption, and it is also known to hold for other classical splitting trees such as expander walks
[
Ale+20
]
.

10.3
The ABNNR Construction

In the previous section, we proved several ‘agreement theorems’ on simplicial complexes. These well-studied tests are actually very closely related to both local testability and list decoding of a particular family of codes called
direct product codes
, corresponding exactly to the set of ‘global functions’ discussed in
Section
8
. At a broader level, these are themselves a special case of a well-studied family of codes introduced in the seminal work Alon, Bruck, Naor, Naor and Roth
[
Alo+92
]
called the ‘ABNNR’-construction. We focus on the case of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
for simplicity, though our results extend naturally to larger fields.

Definition 10.20

(the ABNNR Construction)
.

Given a right
k
-regular bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
and a base code
C\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{L}
, the “ABNNR-Encoding” of
C
with respect to
G
is the code
\text{Im}(E_{G}(C))
where
E_{G}
is an “encoding” map
E_{G}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|L|}\to(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k})^{|R|}
defined by setting for each
c\in C
and
v\in R
:

E_{G}(c)_{v}=c|_{N(v)}.

In other words, each right vertex concatenates the symbols of its neighbors to create a new code over
R
. One can check that in
Section
8
, the set of global functions is exactly the ABNNR encoding over the inclusion graph
(X{\left(j\right)},X{\left(k\right)})
. For a general
k
-uniform complex
X
and
j\leqslant i\leqslant k
, we write
E^{(j,i)}_{X}
to mean the ABNNR-encoding on the vertex inclusion graph
(X{\left(i\right)},X{\left(j\right)})
. In the special case where
i=k
and
j=1
, we write just
E_{X}
to denote the ABNNR-encoding over the inclusion graph
([n],X{\left(k\right)})
, where inclusion is viewed taking
X{\left(k\right)}\subseteq[n]^{k}
as ‘ordered tuples’ of
[n]
, rather than as faces of a simplicial complex. This is a minor difference, but is a bit more convenient in the setting of amplifying base codes.

Recent years have seen a great deal of work on instantiating the ABNNR construction on high dimensional expanders and splitting trees
[
DK17
,
Din+18
,
Ale+20
,
JST21
,
BD22
,
JST23
]
, in particular in the context of efficient list-decoding. Building on tools from these works, we give the first family of large alphabet codes with near-optimal distance that are simultaneously locally testable and list-decodable.

Theorem 10.21
.

For all large enough
k\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an explicit family of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-linear ABNNR-Codes that are simultaneously:

1.

(\varepsilon^{O(k)},1-2^{-k}-\varepsilon)_{2^{k}}
-codes

2.

(O(1),\frac{O(1)}{k\log(1/\varepsilon)})
-locally testable.

3.

(1-2^{-\Omega(k)},2^{O(k)})
-efficiently list-decodable with confidence
2^{-\Omega(n-k)}
whenever
\varepsilon\leqslant 2^{-\Omega(k)}
.

We remark that very recently, Jeronimo, Srivastava, and Tulsiani
[
JST23
]
gave a direct list-decoding algorithm for the LTCs of
[
Din+22
]
(which have some small constant distance
d\ll\frac{1}{2}
). To our knowledge, these are the only other examples of codes that are simultaneously
c^{3}
-LTC and efficiently list-decodable.

The remainder if this section is split into three parts focused respectively on distance amplification, local-testability, and list-decoding of the ABNNR construction, and a short fourth section devoted to a conjectural approach to removing the soundness decay in
Theorem
10.21
. Each section is independent, containing both the corresponding component of
Theorem
10.21
as well as more general results within the framework. To facilitate this structure we first give a brief proof overview of
Theorem
10.21
that doubles as a roadmap for the section.

Proof Overview

The high level idea behind
Theorem
10.21
is simple: starting with a binary locally testable ‘base code’
C
with distance near
\frac{1}{2}
, we will argue that the ABNNR-encoding of
C
on any
k
-partite splitting tree
23
23

23

Formally for list-decoding actually require a slightly stronger notion called ‘complete’ splittability, c.f.
Definition
10.31
.
has distance near
1-2^{-k}
(
Corollary
10.24
), is list-decodable (
Theorem
10.32
), and maintains local testability (
Theorem
10.25
). Instantiating this framework on
expander walks
(the sparsest known family of splitting trees) gives codes with the claimed parameters. We give a brief description of each step.

Distance amplification
on splitting trees is fairly elementary, and follows from the hitting-set lemma. This is a standard argument: one observes that the two encoded symbols
E(x),E(x^{\prime})
differ in any face which hits the vertex set
1_{x\neq x^{\prime}}
. Since by assumption the base code has distance roughly
1/2
, the hitting set lemma promises this set is hit except with roughly
2^{-d}
probability (see
Corollary
10.24
).
Theorem
10.21
actually uses a slightly stronger distance amplification lemma for expander-walks and is given in
Corollary
10.30
. The weaker amplification lemma (in particular its bias variant) appears later in the proof as a sub-component of the list-decoding algorithm.

List-Decoding
the ABNNR construction, despite being heavily studied on HDX and splitting trees, is surprisingly tricky. Roughly speaking there are two main issues. First, known list-decoding algorithms for this setting are actually either for an
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-valued variant of ABNNR called the
direct sum
construction
[
Ale+20
,
JST21
,
BD22
]
, or only hold on
two-sided
HDX
[
Ale+20
,
Din+18
]
(which have exponentially worse rate). To handle this, we build a reduction from list-decoding the ABNNR-encoding on
X
to list-decoding the direct sum encodings of its projected sub-complexes
X^{F}
. The second issue with this strategy is that known list-decoding algorithms for direct sum
[
JST21
,
BD22
]
actually requires the base code to be
\varepsilon
-biased
, a stronger condition than simply having distance near
\frac{1}{2}
. We will discuss how to construct such base codes shortly. We instantiate this framework in
Corollary
10.35
to prove the list-decoding component of
Theorem
10.21
.

Local Testability
of the ABNNR construction, to our knowledge, has not been studied outside the special case of agreement testing. Our test follows a very simple strategy. We first check whether the given word
f
is a direct product (in the sense of
Section
8
) via a simple 2-query agreement test: pick a random vertex
v\in[n]
, and independently
s,s^{\prime}\supset v
, and check whether
f_{s}
and
f_{s^{\prime}}
match on
v
. If this test passes, we then simulate the tester on the ‘direct product decoding’ of
f
. That is, for every location the base tester queries, we pick a random face including that vertex and feed the tester its value in the word
f
. We prove this procedure is sound in
Theorem
10.25
, and instantiate the process in
Corollary
10.30
to prove the local testability component of
Theorem
10.21
.

The Base Code
for our construction, as discussed above, must be an
\varepsilon
-biased
c^{3}
-LTC.
c^{3}
-LTCs with distance near
\frac{1}{2}
are now known due to the breakthrough works of
[
Din+22
,
PK22
]
combined with LTC distance-amplification techniques of
[
Kop+17
]
, but the latter amplification causes several core issues that make the tecnique largely useless for our setting: 1) the resulting codes make
\poly(1/\varepsilon)
queries, 2) may not be
\varepsilon
-biased, and 3) are heavily randomized. We remedy these issues via the ABNNR-construction itself. In particular, starting with the base LTC of
[
Din+22
]
, we encode it into
\log(1/\varepsilon)
-uniform walks on an expander graph using ABNNR, and then concatenate with the Hadamard code. This code is
\varepsilon
-biased roughly because concatenating with the perfectly balanced Hadamard code converts the good distance of the ABNNR-encoding into good bias, and the resulting code is locally testable using the Hadamard’s classical local decoding and local testing algorithms. We formalize this step in
Proposition
10.29

Finally we summarize the construction and proof below:

Code underlying
Theorem
10.21
:

1.

Start with the base
c^{3}
-LTC
C
of
[
Din+22
]

2.

Amplify
C
’s distance via expander walks

3.

Reduce alphabet and bias by concatenating with Hadamard

4.

Re-amplify the resulting code via a second set of expander walks.

Proof of
Theorem
10.21
.

Bias, rate, and local-testability are proven in
Corollary
10.30
. List-decodability is proved in
Corollary
10.35
.
∎

10.4
Distance Amplification

The ABNNR construction is one of the most classical distance amplification methods in coding theory. The standard result to this effect is that instantiating the framework on a (bounded degree) additive sampler amplifies distance while maintaining rate up to a constant factor.

Lemma 10.22

(
[
Alo+92
]
)
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a right
k
-regular bipartite graph such that
(R,L,E)
is an
(\alpha,\beta)
-sampler. If
C\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|L|}
is a code of distance
\alpha
and rate
r
, then
E_{G}(C)
is a
(r\frac{|L|}{|R|},1-\beta)_{2^{k}}
-code.

This observation was used in
[
DK17
,
Din+18
]
to give distance amplification for codes defined on HDX. Our sampling lemmas imply an exponential improvement in the rate-distance trade-off of such codes. We choose a fairly arbitrary setting of our parameters for simplicity, but any setup with good sampling works:

Corollary 10.23

(Distance Amplification on HDX)
.

For any
\alpha,r,c>0
, let
C
be a base code of distance
\alpha
and rate
r
. Let
X
be a
d
-uniform
c
-nice complex. Then for any
i<j\leqslant d
and
G=(X{\left(i\right)},X{\left(j\right)})
the ABNNR code
E^{i,j}_{X}(C)
is an
(r^{\prime},d^{\prime})_{|\Sigma|}
-code for

r^{\prime}=r\frac{|X{\left(i\right)}|}{|X{\left(j\right)}|},\quad\quad d^{\prime}=1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\exp\left(\Omega_{c}\left(\alpha^{8}\frac{j}{i}\right)\right),\quad\quad|\Sigma|=2^{\binom{j}{i}}.

Note that when
i=1
,
Corollary
10.23
has alphabet size
|\Sigma|=2^{j}
and distance
1-\poly(|\Sigma|^{-1})
, optimal up to polynomial factors by
Theorem
10.15
. However, in this special case, it is actually possible achieve optimal amplification directly via the hitting set lemma (
Proposition
10.12
).

Corollary 10.24

(Distance Amplification for Splitting Trees)
.

For any
\alpha,r>0
, let
C
be a base code of distance
\alpha
and rate
r
. If
X
is a
k
-uniform
\lambda
-(tuple)-splitting tree of depth
D
, then
E_{X}(C)
is an
(r^{\prime},d^{\prime})_{|\Sigma|}
-code for

r^{\prime}=r\frac{n}{|X{\left(k\right)}|},\quad\quad d^{\prime}=1-(1-\alpha)^{k}-3^{D}\lambda,\quad\quad|\Sigma|=2^{k}

Moreover when
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}(1-\varepsilon)
, then
d^{\prime}\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{2^{k}}(1+\varepsilon)^{k}-3^{D}\lambda
.

Proof.

Rate and alphabet size are immediate from construction. Let
x,x^{\prime}\in C
be any two distinct non-zero codewords. We wish to bound the distance between their encodings
E_{X}(x)
and
E_{X}(x^{\prime})
, which is exactly the hitting probability of
1_{x\neq x^{\prime}}
in the inclusion graph
([n],X{\left(k\right)})
. By the distance of
C
we are promised that
\mu(1_{x=x^{\prime}})\leqslant 1-\alpha
, so
Proposition
10.12
gives the desired result.
24
24

24

Proposition
10.12
is formally for
(X{\left(1\right)},X{\left(k\right)})
, but because
X
is homogenous the bound carries over by simply setting each partite component of
A
to be
A_{i}=1_{x=x^{\prime}}
.
Plugging
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}(1-\varepsilon)
into the resulting bound gives the final line.
∎

10.5
Local Testability of ABNNR

In this section, we prove the ABNNR encoding of a locally testable code is locally testable.

Theorem 10.25

(Local Testability of ABNNR)
.

For any uniform
(q,s)
-LTC of blocklength
n
and
(k_{L},k_{R})
-regular bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
on
|L|=n
vertices,
E_{G}(C)
has a uniform
(q+2,\frac{cs}{qk_{R}})
-local tester.

Our proof relies on the following elementary agreement test. Given a word
f
, denote by
\mathcal{T}_{G}(f)
the following process:

1.

Pick a uniformly random vertex
v\in L

2.

Pick two uniformly random neighbors
w,w^{\prime}\in N(v)

3.

Accept if and only if
f_{w}(v)=f_{w^{\prime}}(v)

In the agreement notation of the previous section, we’d write the word
f
as an ensemble
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{w}:N(w)\to\mathbb{F}_{2}\}_{w\in R}
and denote the acceptance probability of this test as
Agree_{0}(\mathcal{F})
. We will move between these two viewpoints interchangeably.
\mathcal{T}_{G}
is clearly complete. Its soundness follows from a general result of
[
GC19
]
:

Lemma 10.26

(
[
GC19
, Lemma 5.2]
)
.

There exists a universal constant
c\geqslant 1
such that for all
\varepsilon>0
and any ensemble
\mathcal{F}
:

Agree_{0}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon\quad\implies\quad dist(F,g_{\text{maj}})\leqslant ck_{R}\varepsilon

where for
v\in L
we define
g_{\text{maj}}(v)\coloneqq\text{plurality}_{w\in N(v)}\{f_{w}(v)\}
.
25
25

25

Ties can be broken arbitrarily.

With this in mind, we prove
Theorem
10.25
via the following natural tester: given a word
f\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{R}})^{R}
, run the DP-tester on
w
, and the base code tester on the plurality decoding of
f
if it passes:

1.

Run
\mathcal{T}_{G}
on
f

2.

For every
v\in L
that
\mathcal{T}_{C}
would query:

–

Sample a random neighbor of
v
:
w\sim N(v)

–

Denote the ‘decoded’ plurality as
g_{\text{dec}}(v)=f_{w}(v)

3.

Run
\mathcal{T}_{C}
on
g_{\text{dec}}

Proof of
Theorem
10.25
.

Query complexity and completeness are immediate from construction. The tester is uniform since the base code is uniform, and picking a uniformly random vertex
v\in L
and random
w\in N(v)
is uniformly distributed over
R
.

Toward analyzing soundness, fix any
\delta\in[0,1]
and
f
some word at distance
dist(f,E_{X}(C))=\delta
. We need to show the test
rejects
on
f
with probability at least
\delta c^{\prime}\frac{s}{qk_{R}}
for some
c^{\prime}\geqslant 0
. Observe that we may assume
\mathcal{T}_{G}(f)
passes with probability at least
1-\delta\frac{1}{4c}\frac{s}{qk_{R}}
, since otherwise Step (1) rejects with probability at least
\delta\frac{1}{4c}\frac{s}{qk_{R}}
and we are done. We claim that conditioned on this event, the following two properties hold:

1.

g_{\text{dec}}
matches
g_{\text{maj}}
with high probability:

\ProbOp_{S\sim\mathcal{T}_{C}}[\exists v\in S:g_{\text{dec}}(v)\neq g_{\text{maj}}(v)]\leqslant\frac{s}{4ck_{R}}\delta

2.

g_{\text{maj}}
is far from the base code:

dist(g_{\text{maj}},C)\geqslant\frac{1}{2k_{R}}\delta.

Soundness is then immediate: as long as the base tester
\mathcal{T}_{C}
receives the ‘correct’ symbols from
g_{\text{maj}}
, it rejects with probability
s\cdot dist(g_{\text{maj}})\geqslant\frac{s}{2k_{R}}\delta
by
Item
2
. Since the former occurs with probability at least
\frac{s}{4k_{R}}\delta
by
Item
1
, the total rejection probability is at least
\frac{s}{2k_{R}}\delta-\frac{s}{4k_{R}}\delta=\frac{s}{4k_{R}}\delta
as desired.

It is left to prove the claimed properties.

Proof of
Item
1

Observe that since the queries are marginally uniform, by a union bound it is enough to show that the decoding of a uniformly random vertex succeeds except with probability
\frac{s}{4cqk_{R}}\delta
. The proof is essentially a simpler variant of
Claim
8.2
. For a fixed
v\in L
define the set of ‘bad’ neighbors as those whose labeling disagrees with the plurality decoding as:

B_{v}\coloneqq\{w\in N(v):f_{w}(v)\neq g_{\text{maj}}(v)\}.

The failure probability of our single-query decoding procedure is exactly
\underset{v\in L}{\mathbb{E}}[B_{v}]
, so it is enough to show

\mathbb{E}[B_{v}]\leqslant\frac{s}{4cqk_{R}}\delta.

This follows from the fact that the test
\mathcal{T}_{G}
can be written as an expectation over complete local distributions. In particular, observe that we can decompose
\mathcal{T}_{G}
as:

Agree_{0}(f)=\ProbOp_{v\sim L,w,w^{\prime}\sim N(v)}[f_{w}(v)=f_{w^{\prime}}(v)]\geqslant 1-\frac{s}{4cqk_{R}}\delta.

This implies that the expected probability that
f_{w}(v)\neq f_{w^{\prime}}(v)
in the local distribution is at most:

\underset{v\sim L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ProbOp_{w,w^{\prime}\sim N(v)}[f_{w}(v)\neq f_{w^{\prime}}(v)]\right]\leqslant\frac{s}{4cqk_{R}}\delta.

Since
w
and
w^{\prime}
are independent neighbors of
v
, the inner term is at least

\ProbOp_{w,w^{\prime}\sim N(v)}[f_{w}(v)\neq f_{w^{\prime}}(v)]=2\mathbb{E}[B_{x}]\mathbb{E}[\overline{B_{v}}]\geqslant\mathbb{E}[B_{v}]

since
\mathbb{E}[\overline{B_{v}}]\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
by construction, which completes the proof.

Proof of
Item
2

The proof follows from the fact that conditioned on Step (1) (the agreement test) passing,
E_{X}(g_{\text{maj}})
is close to a DP-codeword. Thus if
g_{\text{maj}}
itself is too close to
C
, it’s encoding will be close to
E_{G}(C)
violating our original assumption on the distance. Formally, assume for the sake of contradiction that
g_{\text{maj}}
is not
\frac{\delta}{2k_{R}}
-far from
C
. Then there exists
x\in C
such that
dist(g_{\text{maj}},x)<\frac{\delta}{2k_{R}}
. Since
G
is bi-regular, this further implies a bound on the distance of the
encodings
of
g_{\text{maj}}
and
x
:

\displaystyle dist(E^{k}_{G}(g_{\text{maj}}),E_{G}^{k}(x))

\displaystyle=\ProbOp_{w\sim R}[E_{G}(g_{\text{maj}})(w)\neq E_{G}(x)(w)]

\displaystyle\leqslant k_{R}\ProbOp_{w\sim R,v\sim N(w)}[E_{G}(g_{\text{maj}})(w)_{v}\neq E_{G}(x)(w)_{v}]

\displaystyle=k_{R}dist(g_{\text{maj}},x)

\displaystyle<\frac{\delta}{2}.

where the second equality follows from the fact that since
G
is bi-regular, sampling a uniformly random vertex
v\in R
and a random
s\in N(v)
is equidistributed with sampling a random
w\in L
. Finally closeness of the encoded words contradicts the fact that
f
is
\delta
-far from
E_{G}(C)
, since
E_{G}(x)
is a codeword and by the triangle inequality:

dist(f,E_{G}(x))\leqslant dist(f,E_{G}(g_{\text{maj}}))+dist(E_{G}(g_{\text{maj}}),E_{G}(x))<\delta,

where we have used the fact that since
Agree_{0}(f)\geqslant 1-\frac{s}{4cqk_{R}}
by assumption,
f
is
\frac{s}{4q}\delta
-close to
E_{G}(g_{\text{maj}})
by
Lemma
10.26
.
∎
We remark that it is likely possible to reduce the query complexity of the above argument to just
q+1
by re-using the first query of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
for the agreement test and performing a more careful conditioned analysis on the latter passing. Since
q
for us is some small constant, the difference between
q+1
and
q+2
is negligible so we only give the simpler independent version above.

To prove
Theorem
10.21
, we need a good explicit family of binary linear LTCs with near optimal distance to amplify via
Theorem
10.25
. In fact, as discussed in the proof overview we will need base codes with the stronger property of having small
bias
:

bias(C)\coloneqq\max_{c\in C\setminus\{0\}}\left\{\left|\mathbb{E}_{i\in[n]}[(-1)^{c_{i}}]\right|\right\}.

Note that an
\varepsilon
-biased linear code always has distance at least
\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}
since for every
c\in C
,
\left\lvert\E_{{i\in{[n]}}}\left[(-1)^{c_{i}}\right]\right\rvert=1-2\dist(c,0)
. Our base codes start with the breakthrough construction of
c^{3}
-LTCs by Dinur, Evra, Livne, Lubotzky, and Moses
[
Din+22
]
:

Theorem 10.27

(
[
Din+22
]
)
.

There exists an explicit infinite family of binary, linear, uniform LTCs with constant rate, distance, locality, and soundness, and a linear time unique decoder up to constant radius.

We remark that while
[
Din+22
]
do not argue their testers are uniform, it is easy to check from their construction that this is the case. As discussed in the overview, it is possible to use the distance amplification techniques of
[
Kop+17
]
to achieve
c^{3}
-LTCs with distance near
1/2
(even hitting the GV bound), but these codes may not be
\varepsilon
-biased and incur a heavy
\poly(\varepsilon^{-1})
cost in the query complexity. We will take a different amplification strategy that avoids these issues almost entirely at the cost of a small polynomial blowup in rate. The idea is to concatenate our amplification procedure in
Theorem
10.25
with the
Hadamard code
:

Had:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{k}},\;\alpha\overset{Had}{\mapsto}\langle\alpha,\cdot\rangle

We denote the resulting code (the image of this map), by
C_{\text{Had}}
.

The Hadamard code comes with three properties critical to our construction. First, it is easy to check that the code is binary, linear, and perfectly balanced (
0
-biased). Second, it is a classical result that the Hadamard code is an excellent LTC:

Theorem 10.28

(
[
BLR90
]
)
.

The Hadamard code is a uniform
(3,1)
-LTC.

Finally, it is well-known folklore (and an easy exercise) to show the Hadamard code is
locally decodable
. We will not go into the formal details of locally decodable codes, but it suffices to say there is a randomized algorithm
Dec:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{k}}\times[k]\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
which given a word
y
such that
d(y,C_{\text{Had}})<\frac{1}{4}
and a coordinate
i
, queries only
2
bits of
y
and outputs the correct decoding with probability proportional to its distance from the code:

\ProbOp[Dec(y,i)\neq x_{i}]\leqslant 2d(y,C_{\text{Had}})

(10.4)

where
x=Had^{-1}(y)
is the pre-image of the unique closest codeword to
y
.

Note that this is essentially the same property we used in
Theorem
10.25
in decoding the ABNNR-encoding. We will show that concatenating with the Hadamard code similarly allows us to amplify the bias and distance of the base code while only incurring constant blow-up in query complexity and logarithmic loss in soundness.

Proposition 10.29

(The Base Code)
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
there is an explicit infinite family of uniform,
\varepsilon
-biased, linear
(\poly(\varepsilon),\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)_{2}
-codes that are
(O(1),\log^{-1}(1/\varepsilon)))
-locally testable. Moreover, there exists
t<1
such that these codes are uniquely decodable up to radius
t
in time
n\exp(\poly(\varepsilon^{-1}))
.

Proof.

We apply
Theorem
10.25
to
[
Din+22
]
’s LTCs using the complex
X=W_{G}
generated by length
k=O(\log(1/\varepsilon))
random walks on any constant degree, regular
\frac{1}{2}
-spectral expander
G
(such expanders are known to exist for any
n
, see e.g.
[
Alo21
]
). This results in a
\poly(\varepsilon)
-rate code with distance at least
1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
by the classical expander hitting set lemma
[
HLW06
]
which states that for any set
T\subset X{\left(1\right)}
of measure
\rho

\ProbOp_{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in X{\left(k\right)}}[|\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\}\cap T|=0]\leqslant\rho\left(\frac{1+\rho}{2}\right)^{k-1}.

(10.5)

The argument is then as in
Corollary
10.24
. Finally by
Theorem
10.25
this encoding has a uniform
(c,\frac{c^{\prime}}{\log(1/\varepsilon)})
-local tester for some universal constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
.

The key step is now to concatenate this resulting family with the Hadamard code:

\mathcal{C}\coloneqq Had(E_{X}(C)),

where the Hadamard encoding is applied pointwise to each symbol in
E_{X}(C)
. This encoding maintains linearity and decreases the rate by a factor of at most
2^{k}=\poly(\varepsilon)
. To compute the bias, observe that since the Hadamard code is perfectly balanced, every non-zero symbol of a word
w\in E_{X}(C)
contributes 0 to the bias so in the worst case we have

\displaystyle bias(\mathcal{C})\leqslant\max_{c\in E_{X}(C)\setminus\{0\}}\{wt(c)\cdot 0+(1-wt(c))\cdot 1\}\leqslant 1-d(E_{X}(C))\leqslant\varepsilon,

where
wt(c)
denotes the normalized hamming weight (fraction of non-zero elements in
c
).

We now argue that this concatenation has a uniform local tester. Write
q=c
and
s=\frac{c^{\prime}}{\log(1/\varepsilon)}
and recall
E_{X}(C)
has a uniform
(q,s)
-local tester (which we’ll refer to as the “base tester”) which requests only a single bit of each queried symbol. We’ll denote these requests as pairs
(x,i)\in X{\left(k-1\right)}\times[k]
. Our testing procedure follows a similar form as in
Theorem
10.25
. Namely given a potential word
y
:

1.

Draw a uniformly random
x\in X
, and run the Hadamard test on
y(x)
.

2.

For each
(x,i)
requested by the base tester, run the Hadamard decoder on
y(x)
at coordinate
i
.

3.

Run the base tester on the output

The test is complete by construction, and makes at most
2q+3
queries (two for each run of the local decoder, and three for the initial Hadamard test). Soundness follows along the same lines as
Theorem
10.25
. We first argue that the word
y
must be close to a cartesian product of Hadamard codewords (else Step 1 rejects). We then argue that the decoding succeeds with high probability, and the decoded word must be far from the base code (else
y
itself would be close to
\mathcal{C})
, so the base tester rejects with good probability.

Formally, let
y\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{k}})^{X}
satisfy
dist(y,\mathcal{C})=\tau
. We argue that the above procedure rejects with probability at least
\frac{s\tau}{4q}
. To see this, first observe we can assume the word
y
is
1-\frac{s\tau}{4q}
close from being a product of Hadamard codewords. Otherwise Step 1 rejects with probability at least
\frac{s\tau}{4q}
since

\ProbOp[\text{Step 1 Rejects}]\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{x}[dist(y(x),C_{Had})]=dist\left(y,\prod_{X}C_{Had}\right).

Fix some
y_{\text{had}}\in\prod_{X}C_{Had}
which minimizes the distance from
y
and denote the set of ‘corrupted’ coordinates of
y
to be

B\coloneqq\left\{x\in X:dist\left(y(x),C_{\text{Had}}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\right\}.

Note that the measure of
B
is at most
\frac{s\tau}{q}
, since the closeness of
y
to
\prod C_{\text{Had}}
is violated otherwise.

Define set of ‘valid’ decodings of
y
to be strings in
(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k})^{X}
whose encodings match
y_{\text{had}}
on all uncorrupted coordinates:

Y_{\text{dec}}\coloneqq\left\{y_{\text{dec}}\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k})^{X}:\forall x\in\bar{B},Had(y_{\text{dec}}(x))=y_{\text{had}}\right\}.

Note that when we
re-encode
any
y_{\text{dec}}\in Y_{\text{dec}}
, it will be at distance at most
\frac{s\tau}{q}
from
y
since:

\displaystyle dist(Had(y_{\text{dec}}),y)

\displaystyle\leqslant dist(Had(y_{\text{dec}}),y_{\text{had}})+dist(Had(y_{\text{had}}),y)

\displaystyle\leqslant(\ProbOp[B]\cdot\frac{1}{2}+\ProbOp[\bar{B}]\cdot 0)+\frac{s\tau}{4q}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{s\tau}{q}.

We now argue that Step 2 correctly decodes to some
y_{\text{dec}}\in Y_{\text{dec}}
with high probability. Since decoding always ‘succeeds’ on coordinates in
B
(since any decoded value is accepted), the worst case is when
B=\emptyset
and
y_{dec}
is the pre-image of the unique closest word in every coordinate. In this case, local decodability of the Hadamard code
(
10.4
)
promises the probability any fixed query
(x,i)
fails to decode to
y_{dec}(x)_{i}
is at most

\mathbb{E}_{x}[2dist(y(x),C_{Had})]\leqslant 2dist\left(y,\prod_{X}C_{\text{Had}}\right)\leqslant\frac{s\tau}{2q}.

Union bounding over all
q
queries, we decode to
y_{\text{dec}}
except with probability
\frac{s\tau}{2}
.

Finally, we argue any
y_{\text{dec}}\in Y_{\text{dec}}
must be
\frac{3}{4}\tau
-far from
E_{X}(C)
, in which case the base tester rejects with probability at least
\frac{3}{4}s\tau-\frac{s\tau}{2}=\frac{s\tau}{4}
by a union bound. This simply follows from the fact that if
y_{\text{dec}}
were
\frac{3}{4}\tau
-close to
E_{X}(C)
, then there exists
w\in E_{X}(C)
such that

\displaystyle dist(Had(w),y)

\displaystyle\leqslant dist(Had(w),Had(y_{\text{dec}}))+dist(Had(y_{\text{dec}}),y)

\displaystyle\leqslant dist(w,y)+\frac{s\tau}{q}

\displaystyle\leqslant\tau

where the second inequality comes from observing that the encodings of
w
and
y_{\text{dec}}
are the same on any symbol on which they agree, and can only improve in distance when they differ.
26
26

26

Technically we’ve also used that
q\geqslant 4
and
s\leqslant 1
here, which follow from the parameters of
[
Din+22
]
.

It is left to show our concatenated family has an efficient unique decoder. It is a classical fact that concatenating codes with efficient unique decoding at radii
t_{\text{inner}}
and
t_{\text{outer}}
gives a code with an efficient unique decoder at radius
t_{\text{inner}}t_{\text{outer}}
simply by running the inner decoder on every inner symbol, then running the outer decoder on the result
[
For65
]
. In particular decoding at this distance is successful since if a true word
m
is corrupted in at most a
t_{\text{inner}}t_{\text{outer}}
fraction of its coordinates, no more than a
t_{\text{outer}}
fraction of inner codewords can have a
t_{\text{inner}}
fraction of corruptions. Thus running the inner decoder results in the correct word except in at most a
t_{\text{outer}}
fraction of outer coordinates, which is then successfully decoded by the outer decoder.

We can decode over each Hadamard symbol up to radius
t^{\prime}<\frac{1}{4}
by brute force in
\exp(\poly(\varepsilon^{-1}))
time (and otherwise output some fixed word, say 0, beyond this radius).
E_{X}(C)
can be efficiently decoded up to some constant radius
t
by taking the majority vote over each coordinate, then running the base decoder on
C
(see e.g.
[
Din+18
]
). This process takes time
n\exp(\poly(\varepsilon^{-1}))+O(n)
, for a total time of
n\exp(\poly(\varepsilon^{-1}))
as desired.
∎

Finally, we argue these base codes can be amplified to large alphabet LTCs with near optimal distance.

Corollary 10.30

(Large Distance c3-LTCs)
.

For all large enough
k\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an infinite family of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-linear
(r,d)_{|\Sigma|}
-codes that are
(q,s)
-locally testable for:

r=\varepsilon^{O(k)},\quad d=1-2^{-k}-\varepsilon,\quad|\Sigma|=2^{k},\quad q=O(1),\quad s=O\left(\frac{1}{k\log(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})}\right)

Proof.

We start with the explicit family of base codes given in
Proposition
10.29
with
q=O(1)
queries, distance
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
, and rate
\poly(\varepsilon)
. For a given code
C
in the family of blocklength
n
, let
X
be the collection of length-
k
walks on any explicit degree
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
-expander of degree
O(\varepsilon^{-2})
on
n
vertices (such graphs exist for any large enough
n

[
Alo21
]
). The size of
X{\left(k-1\right)}
is
O(\frac{n}{\varepsilon^{2k}})
, and the expander hitting-set lemma (Equation
(
10.5
)
) ensures the distance of
E_{X}(C)
is at least

1-\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{k}\geqslant 1-2^{-k}-\varepsilon.

Finally, since the base code’s tester is uniform, local testability follows from
Theorem
10.25
.
∎

It is worth comparing
Theorem
10.25
to the distance amplification for LTCs of Kopparty, Meir, Ron-Zewi, and Saraf
[
Kop+17
]
, who also analyzes local testability of distance-amplified codes via a related construction of Alon and Luby
[
AL96
]
. Their work largely focuses on achieving near-optimal
rate-distance
trade-off: they achieve the singleton bound over large alphabet codes and the GV-bound over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. In comparison
Theorem
10.25
has worse rate than their construction, but makes up for this with exponentially better alphabet size and local testability. Quantitatively, to achieve distance
1-\beta
,
[
Kop+17
]
’s codes require alphabet size
2^{\poly(1/\beta)}
and lose
\poly(1/\beta)
factors in the testing parameters, while ours have the optimal
\beta^{-1}
-dependence in the former and lose only
logarithmically
in the latter.

10.6
List-Decoding ABNNR

In this section, we show that the codes in
Corollary
10.30
are efficiently list-decodable, completing the proof of
Theorem
10.21
. Efficient list-decoding of the ABNNR construction was first considered by Dinur, Harsha, Kaufman, Livni-Navon, and TaShma
[
Din+18
]
, who gave an algorithm for the construction instantiated on HDX. After
[
Din+18
]
’s work, Alev, Jeronimo, Quintana, Srivastava, and Tulsiani
[
Ale+20
]
showed it possible to efficiently list decode a closely related construction called a
direct sum code
over some relaxed families of splitting trees (including HDX and expander walks). Using a reduction to direct sum, they also recover the parameters of
[
Din+18
]
’s ABBNR-codes on HDX as well.

In this section, we give an efficient list-decoding algorithm for the ABNNR-construction on complexes satisfying a slightly strengthened variant of splittability that ensures the complex can be “split” at any coordinate. We refer to this notion as complete splittability.

Definition 10.31

(Complete Splittability)
.

A
d
-uniform partite complex
X
is called completely
\lambda
-splittable if for all
i\in[d]
:

\lambda_{2}(S_{[1,i],[i+1,d]})\leqslant\lambda

where we recall
S_{[1,i],[i+1,d]}
is the ‘swap walk’ adjacency operator between
X[{[1,i]}]
and
X[{[i+1,d]}]
.

Complete splittability sits in-between tuple-splitting trees and partite HDX, and is closely related to notions of splittability defined in more recent works on direct-sum decoding
[
JST21
,
BD22
]
(in particular it is equivalent to the definition introduced in the former, see
Appendix
F
for details). We can now state the main result of the subsection:

Theorem 10.32

(List Decoding on Completely Splittable Complexes)
.

For any
t<1/2
, let
C_{0}
be a binary code with blocklength
n
,
\text{bias}(C_{0})\leqslant 1-2t
, and which is unique-decodable to radius
t
in time
\mathcal{T}_{0}
. Let
X\subset[n]^{k}
be a homogenous, completely
\lambda
-splittable complex, and
C=E_{X}(C_{0})
its ABNNR encoding. Let
d(C)=1-\varepsilon
and
\tau>0
be any value satisfying

\tau\geqslant\max\left\{\sqrt{\varepsilon},4\sqrt{(1-2t)^{i}+2\lambda},\sqrt{2^{21}\lambda k^{3}},8\left(1-t\right)^{i/2}\right\}

for
i=\frac{k}{2}-\sqrt{k\log(\frac{2}{\tau})}
. There exists a randomized algorithm, which given
\tilde{y}\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k})^{X}
recovers the list

\mathcal{L}_{\tau}(\tilde{y})=\left\{y\in C:d(\tilde{y},y)\leqslant 1-\tau\right\}

with probability
1-\frac{\tau-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon(\tau^{2}-\varepsilon^{2})}2^{-\Omega(n/t^{2})}
in time
\tilde{O}(\frac{\tau-\varepsilon}{\tau^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}\exp(\log(1/t)\exp(k^{3}/\tau^{2}))(|X|+\mathcal{T}_{0}))
. Moreover, the number of such codewords is at most:

|\mathcal{L}_{\tau}(\tilde{y})|\leqslant\frac{\tau-\varepsilon}{\tau^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}.

The proof of
Theorem
10.32
goes via reduction to direct sum decoding. The
direct sum
encoding of a code
C
on a complex
X
simply concatenates the ABNNR-encoding with
XOR:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
, that is
dsum_{X}(C)\coloneqq XOR(E_{X}(C))
(where XOR applied pointwise, similar to in the Hadamard concatenation in the prior section). Most of the work in proving
Theorem
10.32
is already done via
[
JST21
,
BD22
]
’s direct sum decoder for completely splittable complexes:

Theorem 10.33

(List Decoding Direct Sum
[
JST21
,
BD22
]
)
.

For any
t<1/2
, let
C_{0}
be a binary code with blocklength
n
,
\text{bias}(C_{0})\leqslant 1-2t
, and which is unique-decodable to radius
t
in time
\mathcal{T}_{0}
. Let
X\subset[n]^{k}
be a homogenous, completely
\lambda
-splittable complex, and
C=\text{dsum}_{X}(C_{0})
be its direct sum encoding. Let
\varepsilon=bias(C)
and
\beta>0
be such that

\beta\geqslant\max\left\{\sqrt{\varepsilon},\sqrt{2^{20}\lambda k^{3}},2\left(1-t\right)^{k/2}\right\}.

There exists a randomized algorithm which given
\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{X}
recovers the list

\mathcal{L}^{X,(sum)}_{\beta}(\tilde{y})=\left\{x\in C_{0}:d(\tilde{y},dsum_{X}(x))\leqslant\frac{1}{2}-\beta\right\}

with probability
1-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}2^{-\Omega(n/t^{2})}
in time
\tilde{O}(\exp(\log(t)\exp(\frac{k^{3}}{\beta^{2}}))|X|+\mathcal{T}_{0})
. Moreover, the list has at most

|\mathcal{L}^{X,(sum)}_{\beta}(\tilde{y})|\leqslant\frac{1}{\varepsilon}

codewords.

We remark that the requirement on the unique decoder radius in
[
JST21
,
BD22
]
is slightly more strict as stated, but one can easily modify parameters in their proof to achieve the above. We now prove
Theorem
10.32
by reduction to
Theorem
10.33
.

Proof of
Theorem
10.32
.

We appeal to a simple variant of the reduction of
[
Ale+20
]
. Given a potential word
\tilde{y}\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k})^{X}
at distance
d(\tilde{y},C)=1-\tau
, we claim the following process is an efficient list-decoder.

1.

For every
F\subseteq[d]
, apply
Theorem
10.33
to
XOR(\tilde{y}|_{F})
on
dsum_{X^{F}}(C)
with
\beta=\tau/4
to get:

\mathcal{L}^{pot}_{\beta}(\tilde{y})\coloneqq\bigcup_{F\subseteq[d+1]}\mathcal{L}^{X^{F},(sum)}_{\beta}(\tilde{y})

2.

Prune any decodings that are far from
\tilde{y}
:

\mathcal{L}_{\tau}(\tilde{y})\coloneqq\left\{E_{X}(x):x\in\mathcal{L}_{\beta}^{pot}(\tilde{y}),\penalty\ d(E_{X}(x),\tilde{y})\leqslant 1-\tau\right\}.

To prove this procedure indeed gives a valid list-decoding we will use the fact that any projection of a splittable complex, in particular each
X^{F}
above, is also splittable.

Claim 10.34
.

Let
X\subset[n]^{d}
be a homogeneous, completely
\lambda
-splittable complex. Then for any
F\subseteq[d]
,
X^{F}
is homogeneous and completely
\lambda
-splittable.

The proof follows from the inheritance of expansion under projection, and is defered to
Appendix
F
.

We now prove the output list
\mathcal{L}_{\tau}
is exactly the set of codewords at distance at most
1-\tau
from
C
. Recall that our base code is assumed to be at most
(1-2t)
-biased. By bias amplification lemma for splitting trees (
Proposition
10.13
) and
Claim
10.34
, we have that for any
X^{F}
, the bias of
dsum_{X^{F}}(C)
is at most
(1-2t)^{|F|}+2\lambda
. As such, our choice of
\beta=\tau/4
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem
10.33
whenever
i\leqslant|F|\leqslant k
(for
i
as in the Theorem statement). Let
y\in E_{X}(C)
be a codeword at distance at most
1-\tau
from
\tilde{y}
, then we have

\ProbOp[\tilde{y}_{s}=y_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{s}\left[\mathbb{E}_{F\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}[\chi_{F}(\tilde{y}_{s}+y_{s})]\right]\geqslant\tau,

where
\chi_{F}(y)=(-1)^{\langle F,y\rangle}
are the standard characters. By averaging and Chernoff, there must exist
|F|\geqslant\frac{k}{2}-\sqrt{k\log(2/\tau))}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{s}\left[\chi_{F}(\tilde{y}_{s})\chi_{F}(y_{s})\right]\geqslant\tau/2,

which is equivalent to the statement that the distance of
\tilde{y}|_{F}
from
y|_{F}
is at most
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\tau}{4}=\frac{1}{2}-\beta
in the direct sum encoding on
X^{F}
. Since
X^{F}
is homogeneous and completely
\lambda
-splittable, by
Theorem
10.33
we have that the decoding of
y
appears in
\mathcal{L}_{pot}(\tilde{y})
except with probability
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\exp(n/t^{2})
. By the Johnson bound (see e.g.
[
Din+18
]
[Theorem 2.15]), there can be at most
\frac{\tau-\varepsilon}{\tau^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}
such codewords
y
close to
\tilde{y}
, so union bounding over these gives that all valid codewords are in the list with the desired probability. Finally, all extraneous decodings are removed in the second step, which completes correctness of the procedure.

Running time follows immediately from the fact that we have run the direct sum decoder at most
2^{k}
times, and the resulting potential list has at most
\exp(k)
potential codewords by the Johnson bound and our choice of
\beta=\tau/4
, each of which can be pruned in
O(k|X|)
time.
∎

Finally, we instantiate this result on expander walks to complete the proof of
Theorem
10.21
.

Corollary 10.35
.

The family of codes in
Corollary
10.30
is
(1-2^{-\Omega(k)},\frac{1}{2^{O(k)}})
efficiently list-decodable with confidence
2^{-\Omega(n-k)}
.

Proof.

Since
[
JST21
]
prove that walks on an
\lambda
-expander are homogeneous and completely
\lambda
-splittable, it is enough to ensure the parameter
\tau
in
Theorem
10.32
can be set to
2^{-\Omega(k)}
. Recalling that the base code of this construction is
\varepsilon
-biased and uniquely decodable in linear time up to constant radius, and that the encoding has distance at least
1-2^{-k}-\varepsilon
, as long as
\varepsilon\leqslant\exp(-\Omega(k))
one can easily check it is possible to take
\tau=\exp(-\Omega(k))
and
\frac{\tau-\varepsilon}{\tau^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}=2^{-O(k)}
satisfies all conditions of
Theorem
10.32
.
∎

10.7
Toward Lossless Amplification from HDX?

Our expander-based construction experiences polylogarithmic decay in soundness with the alphabet size. This is exponentially better than prior methods, but it is plausible the decay could be avoided entirely. In this section, we propose an approach toward such ‘lossless’ amplification via the ABNNR-Encoding on an HDX.

Instantiating
Theorem
10.25
directly on an HDX does actually slightly improve soundness over
Theorem
10.21
, but the loss of
\frac{1}{k}
in the dimension of the complex is inherent to our approach, no matter the instantiation. The reason is that our method only simulates the base tester a single time. As a result, the rejection probability will only scale with the distance of the decoded word from the
base
code. On the other hand, because
X
amplifies this distance by a factor of
k
, this results in an unavoidable loss in soundness.

There is a natural approach to fixing this issue: simulate the base tester “
k
times in parallel
,” analogous to Raz’s parallel repetition theorem
[
Raz95
]
and its de-randomized variants in agreement testing
[
IKW12
,
DM11
,
DD23a
,
BM23a
]
. Indeed on the complete complex, this approach can actually be carried out successfully:

Theorem 10.36

(Parallel LTC Amplification)
.

Let
C
be a uniformly
(q,s)
-locally testable
(r,\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2})_{2}
-code of blocklength
n
and
X=\binom{[n]}{k}
. Then
E_{X}(C)
is a uniformly
(q^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-locally testable
(r^{\prime},d^{\prime})_{|\Sigma|}
-code for:

r^{\prime}=\frac{r}{n^{k-1}},\quad d^{\prime}=1-\frac{1}{2^{k}}\left(1+\varepsilon\right)^{k}-o_{n}(1),\quad|\Sigma|=2^{k},\quad q^{\prime}=q+2,\quad s^{\prime}=cs

where
c>1
is some universal constant (independent of
k
).

Proof.

Rate and alphabet are immediate from construction, and distance follows from (near)-independence of
\binom{[n]}{k}
. For local testability, we rely on the following agreement theorem of Dinur and Steurer:

Claim 10.37

(
[
DS14
]
)
.

There exists a two query agreement test on
\binom{[n]}{k}
and a constant
c\geqslant 1
such that
27
27

27

We remark that formally Dinur and Steurer consider
[n]^{k}
, but their techniques transfer to this setting (formally, see e.g.
[
DD19
]
for the quoted result).

Agree_{0}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon\quad\implies\quad dist(\mathcal{F},g_{\text{maj}})\leqslant c\varepsilon

With this in hand the proof is similar to
Theorem
10.25
. For
k
independent runs of the base tester
\mathcal{T}_{C}
, denote the requested symbols by
\{x^{(i)}_{j}\}_{j\in[q],i\in[k]}
and write
s_{j}\coloneqq\{x_{j}^{(1)},\ldots,x_{j}^{(k)}\}\in\binom{[n]}{k}
. Given a word
f
, our tester performs the following two-step procedure:

1.

Run the Dinur-Steurer test on
f

2.

For every
i\in[q]
:

–

For each
j\in[k]
, denote the ‘decoded’ plurality function at
x^{(i)}_{j}
as
g_{\text{dec}}(x_{j}^{(i)})=f_{s_{j}}(x_{j}^{(i)})

–

Run
\mathcal{T}_{C}
on
g_{\text{dec}}(x^{(i)}_{1}),\ldots,g_{\text{dec}}(x^{(i)}_{q})

The tester is complete and makes
q+2
queries by construction. The proof of soundness is very similar to
Theorem
10.25
, so we give a shortened explanation here. Assuming
dist(f,E_{X}(C))=\delta
, we need to show our test rejects with probability
\Omega(\delta)
. Assume Step (1) passes with probability at least
1-\frac{s\delta}{8cq}
(else we are done). Since the base tester is marginally uniform and the
k
runs are independent, each face
s_{j}\in X
is marginally uniform, and therefore by
Claim
10.37
exactly matches plurality except with probability
\frac{s\delta}{8q}
. Union bounding over the
q
faces gives that
g_{\text{dec}}
matches
g_{\text{maj}}
on all queried points except with probability
\frac{s}{8}\delta
. By the same argument as in
Theorem
10.25
,
g_{\text{maj}}
must be at least
\frac{\delta}{2k}
-far from
C
, so conditioned on receiving the correct values, at least one of the base testers rejects with probability
1-(1-\frac{s\delta}{2k})^{k}\geqslant\frac{s\delta}{4}
for a total rejection probability of
\frac{s\delta}{4}-\frac{s\delta}{8}=\frac{s\delta}{8}
as desired.
∎

The issue with
Theorem
10.36
is that the resulting code has very poor rate. It is natural to conjecture that, as in the single query variant, this can be resolved by replacing
\binom{[n]}{k}
with a high dimensional expander. Indeed it is known that the agreement testing step (the analog of
Claim
10.37
) works in this setting
[
DK17
,
DD19
,
KM20
]
. Note that this is
not
the case for other splitting trees such as expander walks, which would lose a factor of
k
. The challenge then mainly lies in extending the parallel simulation. Below, we discuss the current challenges toward porting this second half of the proof to HDX.

The first difficulty in parallel simulation is that the HDX and base code must ‘match’ in the sense that there is a natural distribution over
q
-tuples of faces that corresponds to
k
valid
q
-query tests for the base code. Recently, Golowich
[
Gol23
]
showed
how to construct HDX from certain (modified) locally testable codes, and Dinur, Liu, and Zhang
[
DLZ23
]
built LTCs (of sub-constant rate) that sit on the triangles of an HDX. It is not clear a priori how to fit either of these constructions into our framework, but it is plausible they or modifications thereof do.

The second difficulty in simulating the parallel tester is that the
k
runs of the base test will be inherently correlated. There is a long history in the HDX literature of handling this type of behavior (e.g.
[
DK17
,
DD19
,
KM20
,
GK23a
,
DD23a
,
BM23a
]
), including the agreement tests we discuss in
Section
8
. Very recently, building upon tools in this work,
[
DD23a
,
BM23a
]
prove that certain strong families of high dimensional expanders admit
1\%
-regime agreement tests, which are closely related to parallel repetition
[
IKW12
,
DM11
]
. As a start, it would be interesting to extend
Theorem
10.36
even to dense complexes known to admit such tests, such as the Grassmann
[
IKW12
]
or spherical buildings
[
DD23a
]
. To summarize, we give the following informal conjecture:

Conjecture 10.38

(Parallel LTC Amplification)
.

There exists a family of high dimensional expanders and locally testable codes which admit a “parallel” variant of
Theorem
10.25
, that is:

1.

There is a
q
-step random walk or distribution on
X
generating
k
valid ‘parallel’ tests

2.

The resulting parallel tester has no (asymptotic) decay in soundness.

11
Concentration Lower Bounds

In this section we prove our results are essentially optimal with respect to their dependence on the sampling parameters and quantitative expansion of the complex. We further show one cannot hope to achieve our more involved applications such as reverse hypercontractivity using classical samplers such as expander walks.

11.1
Optimality for Inclusion Samplers

In
Section
5.2
, we proved that the if
X
is a high dimensional expander, then the inclusion graphs
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
are roughly
(\varepsilon,\exp(-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}))
-additive samplers. Inspecting the proof of
Theorem
5.1
, it is easy to show that for any set
A
of density
\mu
and strong enough local-spectral expansion
\lambda\ll\mu
, one can give the following improved bound:
28
28

28

Though formally when
i=\Omega(\frac{k}{\log k})
one needs to assume
\varepsilon
is a large enough to subsume the
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
factor in front of the exponent.

\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{r\in X{\left(i\right)}}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Ar\subseteq s}\right.r\subseteq s\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]}\frac{k}{i}\right)\right).

In this section we prove that for most
i\leqslant k
, this is essentially optimal. Recall that a complex
X
is called
(\gamma,i)
-hitting if for any
A\subseteq X{\left(1\right)}
,
\ProbOp_{{\sigma\in{X{\left(i\right)}}}}\left[\sigma\subseteq A\right]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{i}+\gamma
. We say that
X
is
(\gamma,i)
-hitting for sets of size
\mu
if
\ProbOp_{{\sigma\in{X{\left(i\right)}}}}\left[\sigma\subseteq A\right]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{i}+\gamma
holds for any
A\subseteq X{\left(1\right)}
of size
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\mu
.

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,0.1)
. Let
i<k
be two integers satisfying
\frac{k}{i}\geqslant 3\varepsilon^{-2}
and let
n\geqslant ke^{k}
.
Let
X
be a
k
-uniform simplicial complex such that every vertex in
X
has the same probability
\frac{1}{n}
. If
X
is
(i,\frac{\varepsilon}{3})
-hitting, then
(X(k),X(i))
is not an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-additive sampler for
\beta<\exp(-O(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}))
.

In particular, for any
\mu\in(0,0.2)
, if
X
is
(i,\frac{\min(\varepsilon,\mu)}{3})
-hitting for sets of size
\frac{\mu}{i}
there exists
E\subseteq X{\left(i\right)}
with
\frac{1}{2}\mu\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]\leqslant\mu
such that

\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{r\in X{\left(i\right)}}}\left[E\left|\;\vphantom{Er\subseteq s}\right.r\subseteq s\right]>\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]+\varepsilon\right]\geqslant\exp(-O(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\mu}\frac{k}{i})).

While the assumption that
X
is
(i,\frac{\varepsilon}{3})
-hitting is not completely general, it follows from
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
being a modest sampler. We prove this at the end of the subsection.

Claim 11.2
.

Let
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
be an
(\frac{1}{10i},\frac{\varepsilon}{6i})
-additive sampler. Then
X
is a
(i,\frac{\varepsilon}{3})
-hitting for all sets of size at least
\frac{0.2}{i}
.

This results in the variant of the bound stated in the introduction.

Corollary 11.3
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,0.1)
. Let
k
and
n
be large enough (as a function of
\varepsilon
).
Let
X
be a
k
-uniform simplicial complex such that every vertex in
X
has the same probability
\frac{1}{n}
. If
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is a
(\frac{1}{10i},\frac{\varepsilon}{6i})
-additive sampler, then
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is not a
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-additive sampler for any
\beta<\exp(-O(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}))
.

The assumption that the vertices all have the same probability is also not general, see the discussion in the end of this subsection.

11.1.1
Proof of
Theorem
11.1

For the rest of the section we restrict our attention to “one-sided” additive sampling, that is:

\ProbOp_{{s}}\left[\ProbOp_{{v\sim s}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{As}\right.s\right]>\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon\right].

This is no larger than the probability that
\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{v\sim s}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{As}\right.s\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert<\varepsilon
and is more convenient to handle.

Let us introduce some notation, similar to the notation in
Section
7.2
(but for one-sided sampling in different levels of the complex). For a
k
-uniform simplicial complex,
i\leqslant k
and constants
\mu,\varepsilon>0
let

\pi(X,k,i,\mu,\varepsilon)=\max\left\{\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{r\in X{\left(i\right)}}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Ar\subseteq s}\right.r\subseteq s\right]>\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon\right]\left|\;\vphantom{\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{r\in X{\left(i\right)}}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Ar\subseteq s}\right.r\subseteq s\right]>\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon\right]A\subseteq X{\left(i\right)},\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\mu}\right.A\subseteq X{\left(i\right)},\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\mu\right\}.

That is the best one-sided sampling bound for when restricting to sets of relative size
\mu
.
29
29

29

Here let us assume for simplicity that there exists sets of this probability. We note that this assumption is justified because as
n
grows larger the possible sizes of sets,
\{\frac{k}{n}\}_{k=0}^{n}
, becomes dense in
[0,1]
. The use of this claim later on is to find a set of size
\approx\mu
anyway, so we neglect dealing with this minor point.

For general sampler graphs
G=(L,R,E)
we use similar notation:

\pi(G,\varepsilon,\mu)=\max\left\{\ProbOp_{{v\in R}}\left[\ProbOp_{{u\in L}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Av\sim u}\right.v\sim u\right]>\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon\right]\left|\;\vphantom{\ProbOp_{{v\in R}}\left[\ProbOp_{{u\in L}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Av\sim u}\right.v\sim u\right]>\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon\right]A\subseteq L,\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\mu}\right.A\subseteq L,\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\mu\right\}.

We also denote by
\Delta_{n}
the complete complex on
n
vertices.

Proof of
Theorem
11.1
.

We start by stating a general bound on
all
samplers (not just inclusion samplers), via reduction to the sampling properties of the complete complex.

Claim 11.4
.

Let
k,n>0
. Let
G
be a bipartite graph such that
|L|=n
and such that it is
k
-right regular. Then

\pi(G,\varepsilon,\mu)\geqslant\pi(\Delta_{n},k,1,\varepsilon,\mu).

We defer the proof, and first argue this implies
Theorem
11.1
.

Fix
\mu
as in the theorem statement. By
Claim
11.4
there exists
A\subseteq X{\left(1\right)}
of measure
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\frac{\mu}{i}
such that

\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{As}\right.s\right]>\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\frac{10\varepsilon}{i}\right]\geqslant\pi(\Delta_{n},k,1,\frac{\mu}{i},\frac{10\varepsilon}{i}).

Let
E=N_{i}(A)
be the set of all
i
-faces that hit
A
and let
\mu^{\prime}=\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]
. We claim that

\mu=i\ProbOp[A]\geqslant\ProbOp[E]\geqslant\frac{i}{2}\ProbOp[A]=\frac{\mu}{2}

The lower bound follows from the hitting property, namely:

\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]\geqslant 1-(1-\frac{\mu}{i})^{i}-\frac{\mu}{3}\geqslant\frac{\mu}{2}=\frac{i}{2}\ProbOp[A]

since
\mu<0.2
.

The upper bound follows because the only way to sample a vertex in
A
is to sample an
i
-face in
E
, then subsample a vertex in
A
in that
i
-face. The probability of doing so (conditioned on landing in
E
) is at least
\frac{1}{i}
.

The proof will follow if we can show that

\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{r\in X{\left(i\right)}}}\left[E\left|\;\vphantom{Er\subseteq s}\right.r\subseteq s\right]>\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]+\varepsilon\right]\geqslant\pi(\Delta_{n},k,1,\frac{\mu}{i},\frac{10\varepsilon}{i})

(11.1)

since Chernoff’s bound is tight on the complete complex.

Lemma 11.5

(Reverse Chernoff, e.g.
[
KY99
, Lemma 5.2]
)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\mu
and
k
be such that
\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime 2}}{\mu^{\prime}}k\geqslant 3
, and let
n\geqslant ke^{k}
, then
\pi(\Delta_{n},k,1,\mu^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})\geqslant\exp(-10\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime 2}}{\mu^{\prime}}k)
.
30
30

30

Technically the lemma in
[
KY99
]
is for sampling with replacement, instead of sampling without replacement (which is the complete complex case), but for
n\geqslant ke^{k}
the two are close enough in
TV
-distance so we get essentially the same theorem.

Applying this lemma with
\mu^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{\mu}{i}
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{10\varepsilon}{i}
and using the fact that
\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime 2}}{\mu^{\prime}}k\geqslant 3
by assumption, implies

\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{r\in X{\left(i\right)}}}\left[E\left|\;\vphantom{Er\subseteq s}\right.r\subseteq s\right]>\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]+\varepsilon\right]\geqslant\pi(\Delta_{n},k,1,\frac{\mu}{i},\frac{10\varepsilon}{i})\geqslant\exp(-O(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\mu}\frac{k}{i})).

Toward proving (
11.1
) observe that for a fixed
s\in X{\left(k\right)}
, the probability of
E
inside
s
depends only on the probability of
A
inside
s
. Assume that
\ProbOp[A|s]-\ProbOp[A]>\frac{10\varepsilon}{i}
. Then

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{r\in X{\left(i\right)}}}\left[E\left|\;\vphantom{Er\subseteq s}\right.r\subseteq s\right]

\displaystyle>1-(1-\ProbOp\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{As}\right.s\right])^{i}

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-(1-\ProbOp[A]-\frac{10\varepsilon}{i})^{i}

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-(1-\ProbOp[A])^{i}+2\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp[E]+\varepsilon

where the first inequality is by observing the choice of
r\subseteq s
subsamples
i
random vertices (without replacement). The probability of hitting
A
in the latter is then at least
1-(1-\ProbOp\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{As}\right.s\right])^{i}
, the hitting probability with replacement. The third inequality is true when
i\geqslant 1
and
\varepsilon<0.1
.

Combining this with our original guarantee on sampling
A
itself we have

\displaystyle\pi(X,k,i,\mu^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{r\in X{\left(i\right)}}}\left[E\left|\;\vphantom{Er\subseteq s}\right.r\subseteq s\right]>\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]+\varepsilon\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp_{{s\in{X{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Av\in S}\right.v\in S\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\frac{10\varepsilon}{i}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\pi(\Delta_{n},k,1,\frac{\mu}{i},\frac{10\varepsilon}{i})

as desired.
∎

It remains to prove
Claim
11.4
. The proof is basically repeating the lower bound in
[
CEG95
]
. The latter show any degree-
k

(\varepsilon,\delta)
-additive sampler satisfies
\delta\geqslant\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}k))
. Similar to our strategy above, their proof can be interpreted as showing
(\Delta_{n}{\left(k\right)},\Delta_{n}{\left(1\right)})
containment graph is the optimal sampler in terms of right-degree, then reducing the general case to
\Delta_{n}
. We give a refinement of their argument for sets of specific size, though our definition of samplers is less general than theirs.

Proof of
Claim
11.4
.

Let
D_{\mu}
be the uniform distribution over all sets
A\subseteq L
of probability
\mu
. Then

\pi(G,\varepsilon,\mu)\geqslant\ProbOp_{{r\in R,A\sim D_{\mu}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Av\sim r}\right.v\sim r\right]>\mu+\varepsilon\right].

Observe that since we randomize over
A
and
G
is right regular, the choice of
r\in R
doesn’t affect the inner probability. As such, we may fix any
r_{0}\in R
and instead consider

\ProbOp_{{A\sim D_{\mu}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Av\sim r_{0}}\right.v\sim r_{0}\right]>\mu+\varepsilon\right],

that is the probability (over
A
) that
A\cap r_{0}
contains
\geqslant(\mu+\varepsilon)k
elements.

We claim this is equal to
\pi(\Delta_{n},k,1,\varepsilon,\mu)
. To see this, assume without loss of generality that
L=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and
r_{0}=\{1,2,\dots,k\}
. Since we have assumed the vertex weights are uniform,
A
is a uniform sample of
\mu n
points out of
L
. It is convenient to think of
A
as being sampled via the following procedure:

1.

Draw a random permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}

2.

Set
A=\sigma(A_{0})
, where
A_{0}=\{1,2,\dots,\mu n\}

Observe
|r_{0}\cap A|\geqslant(\mu+\varepsilon)k
if and only if
|\sigma^{-1}(r_{0})\cap A_{0}|\geqslant(\mu+\varepsilon)k
. But note that the random variable
\sigma^{-1}(r_{0})
is exactly a random choice of a set
\Delta_{n}{\left(k\right)}
. Therefore, this is equal to

\ProbOp_{{s\in{\Delta_{n}{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{v\in[n]}}\left[A_{0}\left|\;\vphantom{A_{0}v\in s}\right.v\in s\right]>\mu+\varepsilon\right].

Since every set of vertices of equal measure has the same sampling in
\Delta_{n}
by symmetry, this is exactly
\pi(\Delta_{n},k,1,\varepsilon,\mu)
as desired.
∎

11.1.2
Sampling implies hitting

We briefly prove
Claim
11.2
.

Proof of
Claim
11.2
.

Let
A\subset X{\left(1\right)}
be a set of size
\frac{\mu}{i}
and
E\subseteq X{\left(i\right)}
its set of neighbors. We need to show

\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]\geqslant 1-(1-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right])^{i}-\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

Let
f:X{\left(i\right)}\to[0,1]
be
f(s)=\frac{|s\cap A|}{i}
and observe that
\E_{{s\in{X{\left(i\right)}}}}\left[f(s)\right]=\frac{\mu}{i}
. Let
B
be the set of bad
k
-faces that over-samples
f
:

B=\left\{t\in X{\left(k\right)}\left|\;\vphantom{t\in X{\left(k\right)}\E_{{s\subseteq t}}\left[f(s)\right]\geqslant\frac{\mu}{i}+\frac{1}{10i}}\right.\E_{{s\subseteq t}}\left[f(s)\right]\geqslant\frac{\mu}{i}+\frac{1}{10i}\right\},

then by our sampling guarantee
\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]<\frac{\varepsilon}{6i}
.

Fix
t\notin B
. Then it holds that
\ProbOp_{{v\in X{\left(1\right)}}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Av\in t}\right.v\in t\right]\leqslant\frac{\mu+1}{i}\leqslant\frac{1.1}{i}
because
\ProbOp_{{v\in X{\left(1\right)}}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{Av\in t}\right.v\in t\right]=\E_{{s\subseteq t}}\left[f(s)\right]
. Moreover, by the hitting properties of the complete complex, we have that
\ProbOp\left[E\left|\;\vphantom{Et}\right.t\right]\geqslant 1-(1-\ProbOp\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{At}\right.t\right])^{i}
(using the fact that inside the complete complex, the sampling is negatively correlated). Thus

\displaystyle\ProbOp\left[{E}\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{{t}}\left[\ProbOp\left[E\left|\;\vphantom{Et}\right.t\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{\neg B}\right]\E_{{t\notin B}}\left[\ProbOp\left[E\left|\;\vphantom{Et}\right.t\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{6})(1-\E_{{t\notin B}}\left[(1-\ProbOp\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{At}\right.t\right])^{i}\right])

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{6})(1-\E_{{t\notin B}}\left[(1-\ProbOp\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{At}\right.t\right]-\frac{\varepsilon}{6i})^{i}\right])

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{6})(1-(1-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right])^{i}-\frac{\varepsilon}{6})

\displaystyle=1-(1-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right])^{i}-\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

∎

11.1.3
Discussion

We conclude the section with a few remarks.

Remark 11.6
.

1.

Theorem
11.1
doesn’t follow from the tight bounds depending on degree or on
\frac{|X{\left(k\right)}|}{|X{\left(i\right)}|}

[
CEG95
]
. The degree of a
k
-face in
(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(i\right)})
is
\binom{k}{i}
, and the degree of a set in
X{\left(i\right)}
can be arbitrarily large, as in the complete complex case. The lower bounds in
[
CEG95
]
, imply a lower of
\exp(-O(\binom{k}{i}))
instead of
\exp(-O(\frac{k}{i}))
. In addition, the ratio
\frac{|X{\left(k\right)}|}{|X{\left(i\right)}|}
, which is another source for lower bounds in
[
CEG95
]
, can be arbitrarily large. That being said, we will
reduce
to the bound in
[
CEG95
]
(only for small sets).

2.

While most high dimensional expanders can be symmetrized to satisfy the assumed vertex uniformity
[
FI20
]
, it is worth noting the condition can likely be relaxed to a bound on
\max_{s\in X{\left(k\right)}}\ProbOp_{{v\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[v\in s\right]
with some additional effort. Further, some assumption of this sort is necessary. To see this, take the complete
k
-partite complex
X
where
X[i]=\{x_{i}\}
are singletons for all
i<k
, and
X[k]=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n}\}
. That is
X{\left(k\right)}=\left\{\{x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1}\}\cup\{y_{j}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1}\}\cup\{y_{j}\}j=1,2,\dots,n}\right.j=1,2,\dots,n\right\}
are all possible faces between the different parts. For any
f:X{\left(1\right)}\to[0,1]
we note that
\E[f]=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}f(x_{0})}{k}+\frac{1}{k}\E_{{y_{i}\in X{\left(k\right)}}}\left[f(y_{i})\right]
. Thus for every
s
,
\left\lvert\E_{{v\subseteq s}}\left[f(v)\right]-\E[f]\right\rvert\leqslant\frac{1}{k}
. In particular, for every
\varepsilon>\frac{1}{k}
, the inclusion graph is an
(\varepsilon,0)
-sampler.

3.

We note that a similar bound can be achieved by essentially the same proof assuming only
\frac{\mu}{3}
-hitting, albeit at the cost of worse dependence on
\varepsilon
. In particular, assuming only that
X
is
\Theta(1)
-hitting, one can still prove a lower bound against
(\varepsilon,\exp(-O(\varepsilon\frac{k}{i})))
-sampling.

11.2
High Dimensional Expansion

Having shown that the concentration bounds of
Theorem
5.1
cannot be quantitatively improved, we turn to understanding the extent to which our requirements on the
expansion
of the underlying complex are necessary for strong concentration. We give two lower bounds to this effect. First, we argue that concentration is not implied by local-spectral expansion at or below the TD-barrier.

Proposition 11.7

(Lower Bounds at the TD-Barrier)
.

For every
\beta<1
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a family of
1
-TD complexes
\{X_{n}\}
such that
(X_{n}{\left(d\right)},X_{n}{\left(1\right)})
is not a
(\frac{1}{2},\beta)
-additive sampler.

Thus in this sense our results giving concentration bounds for
\lambda
-TD complexes for any
\lambda<1
are tight. Note that by
Lemma
4.15
, the above also implies failure of sampling for these complexes for any (large enough)
k\leqslant d
(this can also be shown directly by the same method).

The proof of
Proposition
11.7
is based on the ‘product’ complexes of Golowich
[
Gol21
]
, whose construction we quickly describe. Given a weighted graph
G
defines the product-complex
X_{G}
as

X_{G}(d)=\left\{\{(v_{1},s_{1}),\ldots,(v_{d},s_{d})\}:\{v_{i}\}_{i\in[d]}\in E,\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[d]}\in\binom{[n]}{d}\right\}.

The measure of each face
\sigma=\{(v_{1},s_{1}),\ldots,(v_{d},s_{d})\}
corresponding to an edge
\{s,t\}
is proportional to
w(s,t)\cdot f(j)
, where
f
is some weighting function dependent only on the number
v_{i}=s
.

Proof of
Proposition
11.7
.

Let
G=K^{(1)}_{n/2}\amalg K^{(1)}_{n/2}\amalg\{e\}
, that is two disjoint copies of
K_{n/2}
with an additional edge
e
passing between them, and take the weights of every edge to be uniform.
[
Gol21
]
shows that there exists a weighting function
f
such that
X_{G}
is connected and has expansion
\frac{1}{d}
in every
(d-2)
-link. By construction, the 1-skeleton of
X
is a
(d+1)
-cover of
G
with edges
(\{v_{0},s_{0}\},\{v_{1},s_{1}\})
whenever
\{v_{0},v_{1}\}
is an edge in
E
, and
s_{0}\neq s_{1}
. With this in mind, define the set
A
to be the indicator of the first clique in each part:

A\coloneqq\{(v,s):v\in K^{(1)}_{n/2}\}

A
clearly has measure
\frac{1}{2}
by construction, while on the other hand any
\sigma\in X{\left(d\right)}
corresponding to an edge
e^{\prime}\neq\{e\}
is either entirely contained in or entirely misses
A
. Since all edges are evenly weighted, the probability that a
d
-face corresponds to
\{e\}
is at most
O(n^{-2})
, which gives the desired bound for large enough
n
.
∎

The above proof extends immediately to a lower bound on sampling of
X_{G}
under any weight function and any graph
G
simply by taking
A
to be any (nearly) balanced subset in
G
with expansion (near)
\frac{1}{2}
. It is an easy exercise to show that choosing
A
randomly suffices (and namely that such a set always exists).

Our second lower bound studies a slightly different regime.
1
-TD complexes typically have only constant local-spectral expansion at low level links, so
Proposition
11.7
does not, for instance, rule out showing
o_{d}(1)
-local-spectral HDX satisfy strong concentration. Using similar ideas to our degree lower bounds, we show at least some inverse polynomial local-spectral expansion is required to have strong concentration.

Proposition 11.8
.

For any
c<\frac{1}{2}
and large enough
d\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a family
\{X_{n}\}
of
d^{-c}
-two-sided local spectral expanders such that for any
\beta<1
,
(X_{n}{\left(d\right)},X_{n}{\left(1\right)})
is not a
(d^{-c},\beta)
-additive sampler.

The proof of
Proposition
11.8
relies on another construction of Golowich
[
Gol23
]
building inverse polynomial HDX from Cayley complexes.

Theorem 11.9

(
[
Gol23
]
)
.

For every
c<\frac{1}{2}
and large enough
d\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a family of
d
-uniform,
2^{\Omega_{d}(\sqrt{\log(n)})}
-degree,
\frac{1}{d^{c}}
-two-sided local spectral expanders
\{X_{n}\}
whose
1
-skeletons are Cayley graphs over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
with two-sided expansion
\lambda=\Theta(\frac{1}{d^{c}})
.

We can now use
Lemma
9.10
and the Cayley structure of
X
’s 1-skeleton to prove
Proposition
11.8
.

Proof of
Proposition
11.8
.

Since the
1
-skeleton of
X
is a Cayley graph over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
, there is an eigenvector
g_{0}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\to\{\pm 1\}
whose eigenvalue is
\lambda
. Let us consider the indicator
g
of the set
A=\left\{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\left|\;\vphantom{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}g_{0}(v)=1}\right.g_{0}(v)=1\right\}
whose measure is
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\frac{1}{2}
. This indicator is equal to
g=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}g_{0}
. We note that for every
v
,

\ProbOp_{{u\sim v}}\left[A\right]=\E_{{u\sim v}}\left[g(u)\right]=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\E_{{u\sim v}}\left[g_{0}(u)\right].

The function
g_{0}
is an eigenvalue so
\frac{1}{2}\E_{{u\sim v}}\left[g_{0}(u)\right]=\frac{\lambda}{2}g_{0}(v)=\pm\frac{\lambda}{2}
. Thus,
\ProbOp_{{u\sim v}}\left[A\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\pm\frac{\lambda}{2}
so for every
\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{\lambda}{2}
and any
\beta<1
, the underlying graph
is not
a
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler. By
Lemma
9.10
, this implies that either the containment graph is not a
(2\varepsilon,\beta)
-sampler, or the containment graph of some link of a vertex is not a
(2\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2})
-sampler. Since the vertex links of
\{X_{n}\}
also give an infinite family of
\frac{1}{d^{c}}
-two-sided local spectral expanders this implies the result.
∎

This counterexample poses the following question: For
\varepsilon\in[d^{-c},d^{-c/2}]
what is the worst
\lambda
which still promises that the containment graph is a
(\varepsilon,\exp(-d^{c}))
-sampler for some
c>0
?

11.3
Expander-Walks

Finally we take a step back from concentration itself and look at necessity of
local
concentration for our applications (that is concentration of the links of
X
and in particular the locally nice property). We argue this is at least in some sense necessary: classical samplers such as expander walks which behave poorly under restriction fail reverse hypercontractivity. Recall the expander walk complexes
W_{G}
defined in
Section
10.1
.

Proposition 11.10
.

For any
0<\gamma<1
and
\lambda>0
, there are infinitely many pairs
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
with a corresponding
\lambda
-expanders
G=([n],E)
and balanced subset
A\subset W_{G}(k)
satisfying:

\ProbOp_{{t,t^{\prime}\sim UD_{k,\gamma k}}}\left[t^{\prime}\in X{\left(1\right)}\setminus A\;\hbox{and}\;t\in A\right]\leqslant 2^{-\Omega_{\gamma}(k)}

Proof.

Take
k
odd and
n
even. It suffices to take any regular graph
G
with girth
(G)>k
. Define
A
to be the set of walks whose center vertex is in
\{1,2,\dots,\frac{n}{2}\}
. Notice that
A
is indeed a balanced function, as every element is the center of the same number of random walks in a regular graph (see e.g.
[
Ale+20
]
for details).

On the other hand, it is easy to see
A
has very poor expansion due to the girth of
G
. In particular, the only way to leave
A
(resp.
X{\left(1\right)}\setminus A
) is if the walk re-samples all of
[k/2+1]
, or all of
[k]\setminus[k/2]
. If neither event occurs, there exist indices
i<\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil<j
which were not re-sampled, and the girth of the graph ensures there is only one option for the
\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil
th element (namely its starting position). One can compute directly the probability either event occurs is at most
2^{-\Omega_{\gamma}(k)}
.

To complete the proof, we need an infinite family of regular
\lambda
-expanders for any
\lambda>0
with super-constant girth. Many such constructions exist in the literature, including the classical Ramanujan graphs of
[
LPS88
]
.
∎

Note that for any fixed
\gamma,C,\ell>0
, taking
k
in
Proposition
11.10
sufficiently large means we can always find subsets
A,B\subset X{\left(k\right)}
such that

\ProbOp_{t,t^{\prime}\sim UD_{k,\gamma k}}[t\in A\land t^{\prime}\in B]\leqslant C\ProbOp[A]^{\ell}\ProbOp[B]^{\ell},

violating reverse hypercontractivity for all parameter settings. We remark that the failure of expander walks in agreement testing is well known, and can be derived e.g. from
[
GC19
]
.

Appendix A
Concentration for the Complete Complex

In this section we show near-optimal concentration bounds for the complete complex.

Claim A.1
.

Let
X
be the complete complex on
n
vertices, that is
X{\left(k\right)}=\binom{[n]}{k}
. Let
\alpha,\delta>0
. Then for any
\ell\leqslant k
, the containment graph
G=(X{\left(k\right)},X{\left(\ell\right)})
is an
(\alpha,\frac{4}{\alpha\delta}\exp(-\frac{\delta^{2}}{12}\alpha\lfloor\frac{k}{\ell}\rfloor),\delta)
-multiplicative sampler and
(\varepsilon,\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8}\lfloor\frac{k}{\ell}\rfloor))
-additive sampler for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

Fix and
A\subseteq X{\left(\ell\right)}
such that
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant\alpha
. We apply
Lemma
5.10
in the following distribution. The distribution we use samples
(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m},t)\sim D
such that
(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m})
are independent and
t\in X{\left(k\right)}
is a uniform face conditioned on containing all
s_{i}
. Obviously,
(s_{i},t)
is a uniform pair of
\ell
and
k
faces where
s_{i}\subseteq t
. Moreover, by Chernoff’s bound for independent sampling

\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m},t)\sim D}}\left[\left\lvert\frac{|s_{i}\in A|}{m}-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>\frac{\delta}{2}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right]\leqslant 2\exp(-\frac{\delta^{2}}{12}\alpha m).

The claim follows from
Lemma
5.10
. The additive bound follows by the same argument applying Hoeffding’s inequality.
∎

Note that we do not restrict the number of vertices
n
in this claim. Since the
k
-skeleton of the complete complex is only an
\frac{1}{n-k}
-two sided spectral expander, this claim does not follow from
Theorem
5.1
.

Appendix B
Concentration for the Swap Complex

In this appendix we prove
Theorem
7.14
, which we restate here for convenience.

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem
7.14
)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
i\leqslant k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
C=C_{i,k,n}
for
n\geqslant\frac{1153k\log\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon^{5}}
. For any
f:C(1)\to[0,1]
:

1.

Upper tail
:
\ProbOp[U_{1,\frac{k}{i}}f-\mathbb{E}[f]>\varepsilon]\leqslant\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}))

2.

Lower tail
:
\ProbOp[U_{1,\frac{k}{i}}f-\mathbb{E}[f]<-\varepsilon]\leqslant\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\frac{k}{i}))

The main technical claim we prove is the following. We reparametrize
k=im
.

Claim B.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
i,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
n\geqslant\frac{1153im\log\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon^{5}}
, and denote
C=C_{i,im,n}
. Then for any
A\subseteq C(1)
:

\ProbOp_{{t=\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m}\}\in C(m)}}\left[\ProbOp\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{At}\right.t\right]\geqslant\ProbOp[A]+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{1}{9}\varepsilon^{2}m\right).

The same lower bound applies when replacing
\geqslant\ProbOp[A]+\varepsilon
with
\leqslant\ProbOp[A]-\varepsilon
.

Theorem
7.14
directly follows from
Claim
B.1
together with
Claim
4.11
.

We will prove this claim by reducing to the following result of Panconesi and Srinivasan.

Theorem B.2

(
[
PS97
, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{m}
be functions on a probability space with
\mu=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\E[f_{i}]
, and such that
a_{\ell}\leqslant f_{\ell}\leqslant b_{\ell}
. Assume that for every
n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{m}\in\mathbb{N}

\E\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{\ell}^{n_{\ell}}\right]\leqslant\eta\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\E[f_{\ell}^{n_{\ell}}].

Then

\ProbOp\left[{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{\ell}\geqslant\mu+t}\right]\leqslant\eta\exp\left(\frac{-2t^{2}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}(b_{\ell}-a_{\ell})^{2}}\right).

Proof of
Claim
B.1
.

We prove the bound for
\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon
; the
\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]-\varepsilon
follows from the original inequality applied on the complement
1{\left(0\right)}\setminus A
. We also assume without loss of generality that
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
, and prove that

\ProbOp_{{\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m}\}\in C(m)}}\left[\frac{\left\lvert s_{\ell}\in A\right\rvert}{m}\geqslant\mu+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\varepsilon^{2}m\right).

(B.1)

This suffices since, by our assumption that
n\geqslant\frac{1153}{\varepsilon^{5}}
, for any
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
we may always find
B\supseteq A
with
\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\in\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\frac{2\varepsilon}{3}\right)
and deduce

\ProbOp_{{\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m}\}\in C(m)}}\left[\frac{\left\lvert s_{\ell}\in A\right\rvert}{m}\geqslant\mu+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\ProbOp_{{\{s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m}\}\in C(m)}}\left[\frac{\left\lvert s_{\ell}\in B\right\rvert}{m}\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{9}m\right)

where we’ve applied
(
B.1
)
to the appropriately sized set
B
.

Intuitively, we wish to prove
(
B.1
)
by applying the theorem of
[
PS97
]
where each
f_{\ell}:C(m)\to\{0,1\}
is the indicator that
s_{\ell}\in A
(ignoring the rest of the coordinates),
t=\varepsilon m
, and
\eta=\exp(\varepsilon^{2}m)
. Then since
a_{\ell}=0
and
b_{\ell}=1
, we’d have

\ProbOp\left[\frac{|s_{\ell}\in A|}{m}\geqslant\mu+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\eta\exp(-2\varepsilon^{2}m)=\exp(-\varepsilon^{2}m)

as desired.

Unfortunately, since
C(m)
is unordered, this is not quite well-defined. Instead, we will apply
Theorem
B.2
as above to the
partification
of
C(m)
, denoted
\widetilde{C}(m)
, the distribution over
ordered

m
-tuples
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})
such that each
s_{\ell}\in\binom{[n]}{i}
and all
s_{\ell}
