are pairwise disjoint. Since the
\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{\ell}=|s_{\ell}\in A|
is independent of the ordering of the face, proving
(
B.1
)
for the partification clearly implies the desired unordered bound as well.

Moving back now to the statement of
Theorem
B.2
, for any
I\subseteq[m]
, let
s_{I}\in A
be shorthand for the event that
\forall\ell\in I,s_{\ell}\in A
. The expectations
\E\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{\ell}^{n_{\ell}}\right]
in our setting are then of the form
\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m})\in\widetilde{C}(m)}}\left[s_{I}\in A\right]
, so we need to show
\forall I\subseteq[m]
:

\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m})\in\widetilde{C}(m)}}\left[s_{I}\in A\right]\leqslant\exp(\varepsilon^{2}m)\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{|I|},

We will prove the slightly stronger bound by induction on
|I|
:

\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m})\in\widetilde{C}(m)}}\left[s_{I}\in A\right]\leqslant(1+\varepsilon^{2})^{|I|}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{|I|}.

The base case
|I|=1
clearly holds since the marginals
s_{\ell}\sim\binom{[n]}{i}
are uniformly random. Assume then the result holds for all sets of size
r
. Let
I
be a set of size
r+1
and write
I=J\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{\ell\}
.

The idea is to split the analysis based on whether or not the marginal
s_{J}
samples
A
well. Toward this end, define the set of
s_{J}
that over-sample
A
as

T_{J}\coloneqq\left\{s_{J}:\ProbOp_{s_{I}}[s_{i}\in A|s_{J}]\geqslant(1+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\}.

We can bound the probability that
s_{I}\in A
by

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m})\in\widetilde{C}(m)}}\left[s_{I}\in A\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp\left[T_{J}\right]+\ProbOp_{{(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m})\in\widetilde{C}(m)}}\left[\overline{T_{J}}\;\hbox{and}\;s_{I}\in A\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{T_{J}}\right]+(1+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\cdot\ProbOp\left[{s_{J}\in A}\right]

(Def of
T_{J}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{T_{J}}\right]+(1+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2})(1+\varepsilon^{2})^{|J|}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{|J|+1}

(Ind. Hypothesis)

Thus it remains to argue that
\ProbOp\left[{T_{J}}\right]\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})^{|J|}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{|J|+1}
. In particular, by our assumption that
\ProbOp[A]\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
, it suffices to show

\ProbOp\left[{T_{J}}\right]\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{m+2}}{2^{m+1}}.

We prove this by reducing analysis of
\ProbOp\left[{T_{J}}\right]
to the probability of mis-sampling
A
on a higher level of the complete complex, an event we’ve already shown has a subgaussian tail in
Appendix
A
.

Toward this end, let
t=\bigcup_{j\in J}s_{j}
denote the disjoint union of the
\ell
-sets in
s_{J}
, viewed as an element in
\binom{[n]}{i|J|}
, and write
\bar{t}=[n]\setminus t
. Observe that the conditional distribution of
s_{\ell}
given
s_{J}
is simply uniform over
\binom{\bar{t}}{i}
— in other words, it depends
only
on the union
t
. As such, the event
\{s_{j}\}_{j\in J}\in T_{J}
also depends only on the union, namely
\{s_{j}\}_{j\in J}\in T_{J}
if and only if

\ProbOp_{{s_{\ell}\cup s_{J}}}\left[s_{\ell}\in A\left|\;\vphantom{s_{\ell}\in As_{\ell}\subseteq\overline{t}}\right.s_{\ell}\subseteq\overline{t}\right]>(1+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right].

Since the distribution over
\bar{t}
generated as above is uniform over
\binom{[n]}{n-i|J|}
, we may rewrite the condition as:

\ProbOp[T_{J}]=\ProbOp_{{\overline{t}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{s_{\ell}}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{As_{\ell}\subseteq\overline{t}}\right.s_{\ell}\subseteq\overline{t}\right]>(1+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right].

The righthand side is now exactly the probability of (multiplicatively) mis-sampling
A
in the inclusion graph of the complete complex over
n
vertices
(\Delta_{n}(n-i|J|),\Delta_{n}(\ell))
. In
Claim
A.1
, we proved this graph is a
(\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\frac{16}{\varepsilon^{3}}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{5}}{96}\lfloor\frac{|\overline{t}|}{i}\rfloor\right),\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2})
-multiplicative sampler. Since
\ProbOp[A]\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
by assumption, we therefore have

\ProbOp[T_{J}]=\ProbOp_{{\overline{t}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{s_{\ell}}}\left[A\left|\;\vphantom{As_{\ell}\subseteq\overline{t}}\right.s_{\ell}\subseteq\overline{t}\right]>(1+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right]\leqslant\frac{16}{\varepsilon^{3}}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{5}}{192}\frac{|\overline{t}|}{i}\right).

The size of
\overline{t}
is at least
n-im\geqslant\frac{1152im\log\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon^{5}}
, so

\ProbOp\left[T_{J}\right]\leqslant\frac{16}{\varepsilon^{3}}\exp\left(6m\log\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)\right)\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{m+2}}{2^{m+1}}

for all
m\geqslant 1
as desired.
∎

Appendix C
Outstanding Proofs on Samplers and Concentration

C.1
Sampling

Claim C.1

(
Claim
4.8
Restated)
.

Let
\beta,\delta>0
, let
\delta^{\prime}>\delta
and
\alpha<\frac{\min\{\delta,0.5\}}{1+\delta}
. Then for every
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-sampler
G=(L,R,E)
, it holds that
G_{op}\coloneqq(R,L,E)
is a
(\frac{1-\alpha(1+\delta)}{\alpha(\delta^{\prime}-\delta)}\beta,2\alpha,\delta^{\prime})
-sampler.

Proof of
Claim
4.8
.

Let
A\subseteq L
be such that
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant\frac{1-\alpha(1+\varepsilon)}{\alpha(\varepsilon^{\prime}-\varepsilon)}\beta
. Let

B_{S}=\left\{v\in R\left|\;\vphantom{v\in R\ProbOp_{{u\in L}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]<(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]}\right.\ProbOp_{{u\in L}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]<(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\}

and let

B_{B}=\left\{v\in R\left|\;\vphantom{v\in R\ProbOp_{{u\in L}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]>(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]}\right.\ProbOp_{{u\in L}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]>(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\}.

Showing that
\ProbOp\left[{B_{B}}\right],\ProbOp\left[{B_{S}}\right]\leqslant\alpha
will prove the claim. Let us begin with
B_{S}
. Assume toward contradiction that
\ProbOp\left[{B_{S}}\right]>\alpha
. We denote by
{\mathbf{1}}_{A}:L\to\{0,1\}
and
{\mathbf{1}}_{B_{S}}:R\to\{0,1\}
the indicators of
A,B_{S}
respectively. On the one hand

\E_{{uv\in E}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{A}(u){\mathbf{1}}_{B_{S}}(v)\right]<\ProbOp\left[{B_{S}}\right](1-\varepsilon^{\prime})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right],

(C.1)

since the product of indicators is one iff
v\in B_{S}
, in which case, there are at most a
(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]
fraction of
u\in A
adjacent to
v
by the definition of
B_{S}
. On the other hand, by the sampling properties of
(L,R,E)
there is at most a
\beta
-fraction of
u\in L
such that
\ProbOp_{{v\in R}}\left[v\in B_{S}\left|\;\vphantom{v\in B_{S}v\sim u}\right.v\sim u\right]<(1-\varepsilon)\ProbOp\left[{B_{S}}\right]
. Hence

\E_{{uv\in E}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{A}(u){\mathbf{1}}_{B_{S}}(v)\right]\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{B_{S}}\right](1-\varepsilon)(\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]-\beta).

(C.2)

Combining (
C.1
) with (
C.2
) yields

\ProbOp\left[{B_{S}}\right]\ProbOp\left[{A}\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}-\varepsilon)<\beta\ProbOp\left[{B_{S}}\right](1-\varepsilon)

or

\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]<\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon^{\prime}-\varepsilon}\beta\overset{(\alpha<\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon})}{\leqslant}\frac{1-\alpha(1+\varepsilon)}{\alpha(\varepsilon^{\prime}-\varepsilon)}\beta,

a contradiction to the lower bound on the size of
A
.

Let us bound
B_{B}
. Let
{\mathbf{1}}_{B_{B}}:R\to\{0,1\}
be the indicator of
B_{B}
. Similar to (
C.1
) we have that

\E_{{uv\in E}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{A}(u){\mathbf{1}}_{B_{S}}(v)\right]>\ProbOp\left[{B_{B}}\right](1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\ProbOp\left[{A}\right].

(C.3)

By the sampling properties of
(L,R,E)
there is at most a
\beta
-fraction of
u\in L
such that
\ProbOp_{{v\in R}}\left[v\in B_{B}\left|\;\vphantom{v\in B_{B}v\sim u}\right.v\sim u\right]>(1+\varepsilon)\ProbOp\left[{B_{B}}\right]
. So similar to (
C.2
) we have that

\E_{{uv\in E}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{A}(u){\mathbf{1}}_{B_{S}}(v)\right]\leqslant\beta+\ProbOp\left[{B_{B}}\right](1+\varepsilon)(\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]-\beta).

(C.4)

Combining (
C.3
) with (
C.4
) yields

\ProbOp\left[{B_{B}}\right]\ProbOp\left[{A}\right](\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime})<\beta\left(1-\ProbOp\left[{B_{B}}\right](1+\varepsilon)\right)

or

\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]<\frac{1-\ProbOp\left[{B_{B}}\right](1+\varepsilon)}{\ProbOp\left[{B_{B}}\right](\varepsilon^{\prime}-\varepsilon)}\beta\leqslant\frac{1-\alpha(1+\varepsilon)}{\alpha(\varepsilon^{\prime}-\varepsilon)}\beta,

where the last inequality is because the function
x\mapsto\frac{1-(1+\varepsilon)x}{x(\varepsilon^{\prime}-\varepsilon)}\beta
is monotone increasing when
x\in[0,\frac{0.5}{1+\varepsilon}]
and
\alpha
is in this interval.
This is a contradiction to the lower bound on the size of
A
.
∎

Claim C.2

(
Claim
4.9
Restated)
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a bipartite graph.

1.

If
G
is a
(\beta,\delta)
-additive sampler then
G
is a
(C\delta,\beta,\frac{1}{C})
-multiplicative sampler for any
C>1
.

2.

If
G
is a
(\alpha,\beta,\delta)
-multiplicative sampler for
\alpha\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Then
G
is a
(\beta,\delta)
-additive sampler, where
\delta=\max\{\delta,(1+\delta)(\alpha+p)\}
and
p=\max_{v\in R}\ProbOp\left[{v}\right]
.

Proof of
Claim
4.9
.

The first item follows immediately from the definition of multiplicative samplers. If
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant C\varepsilon
then
\frac{1}{C}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant\varepsilon
so by the promise of additive sampling it holds that

\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>\frac{1}{C}\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right]\leqslant\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\beta.

We turn to the second item. Let
A\subseteq R
. For every
u\in L
it holds that

\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert=\left\lvert 1-\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]-(1-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right])\right\rvert=\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in R\setminus A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in R\setminus Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]-\ProbOp\left[{R\setminus A}\right]\right\rvert,

so we assume that without loss of generality
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. We need to show that

\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>\delta\right]\leqslant\beta.

(C.5)

If
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\geqslant\alpha
then this holds from the multiplicative sampling guarantee. Hence it suffices to show that this inequality
always holds
for sets of relative size smaller that
\delta
.

First we note that if
u\in L
is such that
\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]<\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]
then

\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\leqslant\delta

so

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>\delta\right]=\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]>\delta+\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right].

In this case we find a subset
B\supseteq A
such that
\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\leqslant\alpha+p
(we can do so by adding vertices to
A
one-by-one which is where
p
comes into play). Then

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]>\delta+\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in B\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Bu\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]>\delta+\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in B\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Bu\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]>(1+\varepsilon)\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta.

The claim follows.
∎

One can also remove the dependence on
\delta
in the second item.

Claim C.3

(
Claim
4.10
Restated)
.

Let
\beta,\alpha_{0}>0
. If for every
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
it holds that
G
is an
(\alpha,\beta,\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\sqrt{\alpha}})
-multiplicative sampler, then
G
is a
(\beta,2(\alpha+p))
-additive sampler where
p=\max_{v\in R}\ProbOp\left[{v}\right]
.

Proof of
Claim
4.10
.

The proof is the same as the proof of the second item in
Claim
4.9
. the only difference is that for
large
sets, we still have that

\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{u\in R}}\left[u\in A\left|\;\vphantom{u\in Au\sim v}\right.u\sim v\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right\rvert>2\alpha_{0}+p\right]\leqslant\beta

(C.6)

because if
\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]=\alpha
then
\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\alpha\leqslant\alpha_{0}
.
∎

Claim

(Restatement of
Claim
4.11
)
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be an
(\varepsilon,\beta)
-additive sampler such that every
r\in R
has degree at least
k
and every
v\in L
has probability at most
\frac{1}{k}
. Assume that
\exp(-0.01\varepsilon^{2}k))<\frac{1}{4}
. Then
G
is also a
(4\beta,2\varepsilon)
-function additive sampler.

Proof of
Claim
4.11
.

Fix
f:L\to[0,1]
. We prove a
(2\beta,2\varepsilon)
upper tail bound. The lower bound is similar. Let
A\sim\mathcal{P}(L)
be a random subset where every vertex is inserted into
A
independently with probability
p_{v}=f(v)
. Since
G
is a sampler, for every possible outcome
A
we have
\ProbOp_{{r\in R}}\left[\ProbOp_{{v\sim r}}\left[v\in A\right]-\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]>\varepsilon\right]<\beta
. Let
{\mathbf{1}}(A,r)
be the indicator for this event. In particular, we have that
\E_{{A,r\sim R}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}(A,r)\right]\leqslant\beta
. By Markov, the fraction of
r_{0}\in R
such that
\E_{{A}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}(A,r_{0})\right]>\frac{1}{2}
is at most
2\beta
. On the other hand, we will show that if

\E_{{v\sim r_{0}}}\left[f(v)\right]\geqslant\E_{{v\in L}}\left[f(v)\right]+2\varepsilon

then
\E_{{A}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}(A,r_{0})\right]>\frac{1}{2}
. Indeed, fix such an
r_{0}
. Observe that if
\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[A\right]<\E_{{v\in L}}\left[f\right]+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon
and
\ProbOp_{{v\sim r_{0}}}\left[A\right]>\E_{{v\sim r_{0}}}\left[f(v)\right]-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon
, then

\ProbOp_{{v\sim r_{0}}}\left[A\right]>\E_{{v\sim r_{0}}}\left[f(v)\right]-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\geqslant\E_{{v\in L}}\left[f(v)\right]+\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon\geqslant\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]+\varepsilon.

It is direct to check that
\E_{{A}}\left[\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]\right]=\E[f]
and that
\E_{{A}}\left[\ProbOp_{{v\sim r_{0}}}\left[A\right]\right]=\E_{{v\sim r_{0}}}\left[f(v)\right]
. Hoeffding’s inequality therefore bounds the probability that either
\ProbOp_{{v\in L}}\left[A\right]<\E_{{v\in L}}\left[f\right]+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon
or
\ProbOp_{{v\sim r_{0}}}\left[A\right]>\E_{{v\sim r_{0}}}\left[f(v)\right]-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon
by
2\exp(-0.01\varepsilon^{2}k)<\frac{1}{2}
and
\E_{{A}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}(A,r_{0})\right]>\frac{1}{2}
follows as desired.
∎

Finally we prove the basic Chebyshev-type sampling bound from expansion.

Claim

(Restatement of
Claim
7.10
)
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a
\lambda
-bipartite expander and let
A
be its bipartite adjacency operator. Let
f:L\to[0,1]
be a function with
\E[f]\geqslant\mu
and let
0<\varepsilon<\mu
. Then

\ProbOp_{u\in R}[Af(u)<\mu-\varepsilon]<\frac{\lambda^{2}\mu}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Proof of
Claim
7.10
.

Dinur and Kaufman observe the following bound

\ProbOp_{u\sim R}[|Af(u)-\mu|>\varepsilon]<\frac{\lambda^{2}Var(f)}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Let
f^{\perp}=f-\mu
. Then
Af(v)-\mu=Af^{\perp}
. By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\ProbOp\left[{T}\right]\leqslant\frac{\E[(Af^{\perp})^{2}]}{\varepsilon^{2}}

and by
\lambda
-expansion
\E[(Af^{\perp})^{2}]\leqslant\lambda^{2}\E[(f^{\perp})^{2}]=\lambda^{2}Var(f)
. The claim follows.
Now by the above we can write

\ProbOp\left[{Af(u)<\mu-\varepsilon}\right]\leqslant\ProbOp\left[{\left\lvert Af(u)-\E[f]\right\rvert>\E[f]-\mu+\varepsilon}\right]\leqslant\frac{\lambda^{2}Var(f)}{(\varepsilon+(\E[f]-\mu))^{2}}.

Noting that
Var(f)\leqslant\E[f]=\mu+(\E[f]-\mu)
and denoting
x=\E[f]-\mu
, this is at most
\frac{\lambda^{2}(\mu+x)}{(\varepsilon+x)^{2}}\leqslant\frac{\lambda^{2}\mu}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.
∎

C.2
Concentration

Lemma C.4

(
Lemma
4.15
Restated)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex,
k\leqslant d
, and
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to[0,1]
a function satisfying

1.

Upper Tail
:
\underset{X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}[f-\mathbb{E}[f]>t]\leqslant f_{up}(t)

2.

Lower Tail
:
\underset{X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}[f-\mathbb{E}[f]<-t]\leqslant f_{low}(t)
.

for some functions
f_{up},f_{low}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to[0,1]
. Then the
d
-lift
U_{k,d}f:X{\left(d\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies:

1.

Upper Tail
:
\underset{X{\left(d\right)}}{\ProbOp}[U_{k,d}f-\mathbb{E}[f]>t]\leqslant f_{up}(\frac{t}{2})(1-\pi_{low}^{d,k,f}(\frac{t}{2}))^{-1}

2.

Lower Tail
:
\underset{X{\left(d\right)}}{\ProbOp}[U_{k,d}f-\mathbb{E}[f]<-t]\leqslant f_{low}(\frac{t}{2})(1-\pi_{up}^{d,k,f}(\frac{t}{2}))^{-1}
.

Proof.

We give the argument for the upper tail. The argument for the lower tail is analogous. Assume for the sake of contradiction that

\underset{X{\left(d\right)}}{\ProbOp}\left[U_{k,d}f-\mu>2t\right]>f_{up}\left(t\right)\left(1-\pi_{low}^{f,k,d}\left(t\right)\right)^{-1}.

Since a
k
-face can be drawn by first drawing a
d
-face, then a uniformly random
k
-subface, we can use concentration of the complete complex to derive a contradiction. In particular, let
E_{1}
denote the event that
U_{k,d}f-\mu>2t
. Then:

\displaystyle\underset{\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{k}\}\in X{\left(k\right)}}{\ProbOp}\left[U_{k,d}f-\mu>t\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp[E_{1}]\ProbOp_{r\subset s}[f(r)-\mu>t\penalty\ |\penalty\ s\in E_{1}]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp[E_{1}](1-\ProbOp_{r\subset s}[f(r)-\mathbb{E}[f]<t\penalty\ |\penalty\ s\in E_{1}])

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp[E_{1}](1-\ProbOp_{r\subset s}[f(r)-U_{k,d}<-t\penalty\ |\penalty\ s\in E_{1}])

\displaystyle>f_{up}(t)

∎

Lemma C.5

(
Lemma
4.16
Restated)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-uniform simplicial complex and
k\leqslant d
. Assume there exist functions
f_{up}(t,\nu)
and
f_{low}(t,\nu)
such that any
\nu
-Lipschitz
f:X{\left(d\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies:

1.

Upper Tail
:
\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]>t]\leqslant f_{up}(t,\nu)

2.

Lower Tail
:
\ProbOp[f-\mathbb{E}[f]<-t]\leqslant f_{low}(t,\nu)
.

Then any function
f^{\prime}:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\nu
-bounded difference satisfies:

1.

Upper Tail
:
\ProbOp[f^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}]>t]\leqslant f_{up}(\frac{t}{2},\frac{k}{d}\nu)+e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{4\nu}}

2.

Lower Tail
:
\ProbOp[f^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}]<-t]\leqslant f_{low}(\frac{t}{2},\frac{k}{d}\nu)+e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{4\nu}}
.

Proof.

Let
f:X{\left(k\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function with
\nu
-bounded difference and
g
its
d
-lift
g=U_{k,d}f
. Since a face
r\in X{\left(k\right)}
can equivalently be sampled by first drawing a face
s\in X{\left(d\right)}
, then subsampling
r
uniformly from
s
, we can bound the concentration of
f
as:

\displaystyle\ProbOp[f(r)-\mathbb{E}[f]\geqslant t]

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp_{r\subset s}[g(s)-\mathbb{E}[f]\geqslant t/2\lor f(r)-g(s)\geqslant t/2]

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp_{r\subset s}[g(s)-\mathbb{E}[g]\geqslant t/2]+\ProbOp_{r\subset s}\left[f(r)-\underset{r\subset s}{\mathbb{E}}[f(r)]\geqslant t/2\right]

where we have used the fact that
\mathbb{E}[g]=\mathbb{E}[f]
. Informally, the idea is then to argue the first term is small due to exponential concentration of
X{\left(d\right)}
and the fact that the lift
g
is itself more concentrated than
f
, and the second term is small by subgaussian concentration of the complete complex.

We first argue that if
f
has
\nu
-bounded difference,
g
is
\frac{k}{d}\nu
-Lipschitz. Given
s\in X{\left(d\right)}
and
i\in[d]
, let
s^{(i)}
denote a copy of
s
with the
i
th coordinate re-sampled. Expanding
\nu_{g}
we have:

\displaystyle\nu_{g}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\underset{s,s^{(i)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(g(s)-g(s^{(i)})\right)_{+}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\underset{s,s^{(i)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\underset{t\subset s}{\mathbb{E}}[f]-\underset{t\subset s^{(i)}}{\mathbb{E}}[f]\right)_{+}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\binom{d}{k}^{2}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\underset{s,s^{(i)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\sum\limits_{T\subseteq[d]}f(s_{T})-f(s^{(i)}_{T})\right)_{+}^{2}\right]

where we recall
s_{T}
is the
k
-face consisting of the elements of
s
indexed by
T
. Observe that the inner term is non-zero only when
i\in T
, since otherwise
s_{T}=s^{(i)}_{T}
. Thus by Cauchy-Schwarz, we have:

\displaystyle\nu_{f}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\binom{d-1}{k-1}}{\binom{d}{k}^{2}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\underset{s,s^{(i)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{T\subseteq[d]}\left(f(s_{T})-f(s^{(i)}_{T})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\binom{d-1}{k-1}}{\binom{d}{k}^{2}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\underset{s,s^{(i)}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{i\in T\subseteq[d]}\frac{\nu}{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{d}\nu

where we have used the fact that
s(T)
and
s^{\prime}_{(i)}(T)
are neighboring
k
-faces in the down-up walk on level
k
. We can now bound the first term of our upper tail by assumption as

\ProbOp_{r\subset s}[g(s)-\mathbb{E}[g]\geqslant t/2]\leqslant f_{up}\left(\frac{t}{2},\frac{k}{d}\nu\right).

For the second term, observe that since
f
has
\nu
-bounded difference as a function of
X{\left(k\right)}
, the restriction of
f
to
\binom{s}{k}\subset X{\left(k\right)}
also has
\nu
-bounded difference (since any edge
(r,r^{\prime})
in the down-up walk restricted to
s
is also an edge in the walk on
X{\left(k\right)}
). Thus using subgaussian concentration for Lipschitz functions on the complete complex (see e.g.
[
CGM19
, Corollary 2]
) we also have

\ProbOp_{r\subset s}\left[f(r)-\underset{r\subset s}{\mathbb{E}}[f(r)]\geqslant t/2\right]\leqslant e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{4\nu}}.

Combining the two gives the result.
∎

Appendix D
High Dimensional Expander-Mixing Lemma

In this section, we give the proof of our variant of the high-dimensional expander mixing lemma and its various corollaries.

Theorem D.1

(high dimensional expander Mixing Lemma (
Theorem
10.14
Restated))
.

Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a depth
D

\lambda
-tuple splitting tree with
k
leaves. Denote by
d_{i}
the depth of the leaf labeled
i
. Then for any family of functions
\{f_{i}:X[i]\to\mathbb{R}\}_{i\in[k]}
:

\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k-1\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(a_{i})\right]-\prod_{i=1}^{k}\E_{{a_{i}\in X[i]}}\left[f_{i}(a_{i})\right]\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{k}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}.

If
(X,T,\rho)
is instead a depth
D
standard
\lambda
-splitting tree, we take
\{f_{i}:X{\left(1\right)}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{i\in[k]}
and have:

\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k-1\right)}},\pi\in S_{k}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(a_{\pi(i)})\right]-\prod_{i=1}^{k}\E_{{a_{i}\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[f_{i}(a_{i})\right]\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{k}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}.

Proof.

We prove the partite case directly, then argue
(
10.3
)
follows from partitification. It is convenient to introduce the following notation. Let
\bar{f}=(f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{k})
such that
f_{i}:X[i]\to\mathbb{R}
. Let
S\subseteq[k]
. Let
(T,\rho)
be an ordered tree. Without loss of generality we identify every leaf
u\in\mathcal{L}
with its label
\rho(u)
.

–

Denote by
\bar{f}_{S}
the sub-tuple containing only functions whose index is in
S
.

–

Let
\pi_{\bar{f}_{S}}:X[S]\to\mathbb{R}
be the product of functions in
\bar{f}_{S}
, that is
\pi_{\bar{f}_{S}}(s)=\prod_{i\in S}f_{i}(a_{i})
(where
s=\{a_{i}\}_{i\in S}
). We note that when
S_{1},S_{2}
are disjoint then
\pi_{\bar{f}_{S_{1}}}\cdot\pi_{\bar{f}_{S_{2}}}=\pi_{\bar{f}_{S_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}S_{2}}}
.

–

Let
E_{\bar{f}_{S}}=\prod_{i\in S}\E[f_{i}]
.

–

Let
T_{L}
and
T_{R}
be the sub-trees rooted by the left and right children of the root of
T
, respectively. Let
L,R
be the leaves of
T_{L},T_{R}
respectively.

–

For
T,T_{L},T_{R}
and tuple of functions
\bar{f}
we denote by

\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{[k]},T)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}},\;\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{L},T_{L})=\prod_{i\in L}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}-1}},\;\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{R},T_{R})=\prod_{i\in R}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}-1}}.

In this notation we need to show:

\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}]-E_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{[k]},T).

The proof is by induction on the depth
D
. The base case (depth
1
) is trivial. Assume that the theorem holds for any set of functions on any tree of depth
\leqslant D-1
. By adding and subtracting
\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\cdot\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]
, observe that

\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}]-E_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}\right\rvert\leqslant\underbrace{\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}]-\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\cdot\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]\right\rvert}_{\text{I}}+\underbrace{\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\cdot\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]-E_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}\right\rvert}_{\text{II}}

(D.1)

by the triangle inequality. We bound I and II separately, starting with I. Since the swap walk
S_{L,R}
is a
\lambda
-bipartite expander, applying (
4.2
) to
\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}},\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}
we get that the first term in the right-hand side of (
D.1
) is

\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}]-\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]\right\rvert=\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}\cdot\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]-\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]\right\rvert\leqslant\lambda\lVert\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}\rVert_{2}\lVert\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\lambda\prod_{i}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}

where we have used the fact that

\lVert\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\prod_{i\in L}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}},\quad\text{and}\quad\lVert\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\prod_{i\in R}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}

(D.2)

by repeatedly applying Cauchy-Schwarz along the splitting trees of L and R respectively.

It remains to bound II. By the triangle inequality, we can factor this term into components dependent on the left and right subtrees:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]\cdot\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]-E_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\cdot\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]-\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\cdot E_{\bar{f}_{R}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\cdot E_{\bar{f}_{R}}-E_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}\right\rvert

(D.3)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\right\rvert\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]-E_{\bar{f}_{R}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert E_{\bar{f}_{R}}\right\rvert\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]-E_{\bar{f}_{L}}\right\rvert.

Since
(X^{L},T_{L},\rho|_{T_{L}}),(X^{R},T_{R},\rho|_{T_{R}})
are both
\lambda
-tuple splitting trees of depth
\leqslant D-1
,
32
32

32

Recall that
X^{L},X^{R}
are the simplicial sub-complexes induced by the faces in
X[L]
and
X[R]
respectively.
the inductive hypothesis implies

\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]-E_{\bar{f}_{R}}\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D-1}\lambda\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{R},T_{R}),\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]-E_{\bar{f}_{L}}\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D-1}\lambda\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{L},T_{L})

so we can upper bound (
D.3
) by

\displaystyle 3^{D-1}\lambda\left(\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\right\rvert\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{R},T_{R})+\left\lvert E_{\bar{f}_{R}}\right\rvert\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{L},T_{L})\right).

Finally, observe that

\left\lvert E_{\bar{f}_{R}}\right\rvert=\prod\limits_{i\in R}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[f_{i}]\right\rvert\leqslant\prod\limits_{i\in R}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\prod\limits_{i\in R}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}

by monotonicity of expectation norms and similarly

\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\right\rvert\leqslant\lVert\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\prod\limits_{i\in L}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}

by (
D.2
). Altogether, this gives an upper bound of

\text{I}+\text{II}\leqslant\lambda\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{[k]},T)+2\cdot 3^{D-1}\lambda\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{[k]},T)\leqslant\lambda 3^{D}\mathcal{E}(\bar{f}_{[k]},T)

as desired.

For the unordered case, take an arbitrary partitification
(X^{\prime},T,\rho^{\prime})
as in
Definition
10.8
.
(X^{\prime},T,\rho^{\prime})
is a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree by
Claim
10.9
. Define
\tilde{f}_{i}:X[\rho^{\prime}(u_{i})]\to\mathbb{R}
by
\tilde{f}_{i}((v,j))=f_{i}(v)
. Viewed as random variables,
\tilde{f}_{i}
and
f_{i}
are equidistributed, so
\mathbb{E}[f_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}_{i}]
and
\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}=\lVert\tilde{f}_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}
. Moreover, by construction

\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k-1\right)}},\pi\in S_{k}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(a_{\pi(i)})\right]=\E_{{(a,\pi)\in{X^{\prime}{\left(k-1\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{f}_{i}(a_{\pi(i)},\pi(i))\right],

so can write

\displaystyle\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k\right)}},\pi\in S_{k}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(a_{\pi(i)})\right]-\prod_{i=1}^{k}\E_{{a_{i}\in{X{\left(1\right)}}}}\left[f_{i}(a_{i})\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\E_{{(a,\pi)\in{X^{\prime}{\left(k\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{f}_{i}((a,\pi)_{i})\right]-\prod_{i=1}^{k}\E_{{(a,\pi)_{i}\in X^{\prime}{\left(i\right)}}}\left[\tilde{f}_{i}(a_{i},i)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\lVert\tilde{f}_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}

\displaystyle=3^{D}\lambda\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}}

as desired.
∎

We now record a few useful corollaries from which hitting set and bias amplification are essentially immediate.

Corollary D.2
.

Let
X
be as in
Theorem
10.14
and
A_{i}\subseteq X[i]
. Then

\left\lvert\ProbOp_{{a\in{X{\left(k-1\right)}}}}\left[\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}\in A_{i}\right]-\prod_{i=1}^{k}\ProbOp\left[{A_{i}}\right]\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{k}\ProbOp\left[{A_{i}}\right]^{2^{-d_{i}}}\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda.

(D.4)

Corollary D.3
.

Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree. Let
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{\ell}\in T
be nodes such that for every
i\neq j
,
u_{i}
is not an ancestor of
u_{j}
, and let
d_{i}
be the depth of
u_{i}
in
T
. Let
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{\ell}
be functions such that
f_{i}:X{\left(\rho(u_{i})\right)}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then

\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k-1\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}f_{i}(a_{\rho(u_{i})})\right]-\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\E[f_{i}(a_{\rho(u_{i})})]\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}},

(D.5)

where
D=\max\{d_{i}\}
.

Proof.

We sequentially prune the tuple-splitting tree using nodes
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{\ell}
as in
Definition
10.10
. If there are nodes of depth greater than
D
we also prune their depth
D
ancestor. By
Claim
10.11
, this results in a pruned
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree
(X^{\prime},T^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
where by construction
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{\ell}
are leaves in
T^{\prime}
corresponding to
X^{\prime}[i]=X[\rho(u_{i})]
(we note
X^{\prime}
may have other parts not associated with these leaves as well). Setting
f_{j}=1
for the latter, applying
Theorem
10.14
to
(X^{\prime},T^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
gives the desired bound.

∎

Proposition D.4

(Hitting Set (
Proposition
10.12
Restated))
.

Any depth
D

\lambda
-(tuple) splitting tree with
k
leaves is
(3^{D}\lambda,k)
-hitting.

Proof.

Any subset
A\subset X{\left(1\right)}
can be divided into partite components
A=A_{1}\amalg\ldots\amalg A_{k}
where
A_{i}\subset X[i]
.
Corollary
D.2
then implies

\ProbOp_{\sigma\in X{\left(k\right)}}[\sigma\subset A]\leqslant\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mu(A_{i})+3^{D}\lambda\leqslant\mu(A)^{k}+3^{D}\lambda

where the righthand inequality follows from the fact that
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu(A_{i})=\mu(A)
.
∎

Proposition D.5

(Bias-Amplification (
Proposition
10.13
Restated))
.

Let
(X,T,\rho)
be a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree for
\lambda<\frac{1}{16}
. For any
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{4}
and family of mean
\varepsilon
functions
\{f_{i}:X[i]\to\{\pm 1\}\}_{i\in[k]}
,
\prod f_{i}
is a
\{\pm 1\}
-valued function with bias at most:

\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k-1\right)}}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(a_{i})\right]\right\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon^{k}+2\lambda

(D.6)

Proof.

The proof is similar to
Theorem
10.14
, and we adopt the same notational conventions. The base case
k=1
is trivial. For the inductive step, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}[\pi_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}]|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lvert\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{[k]}}]-\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\cdot\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]\right\rvert+\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{L}}]\cdot\E[\pi_{\bar{f}_{R}}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda+(\varepsilon^{|L|}+2\lambda)(\varepsilon^{|R|}+2\lambda)

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{k}+2\lambda

by our assumptions on
\lambda
and
\varepsilon
.
∎

Finally we list for completeness the unordered analogs of these results, which follow immediately from partitification as in

.

Let

\lambda

(D.7)

.

Let

u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{\ell}\in T

,

, and let

in

. Let
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{\ell}

. Then

\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k-1\right)}},\pi\in S_{k}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}f_{i}(a_{\rho(u_{i})})\right]-\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\E[f_{i}(a_{\rho(u_{i})})]\right\rvert\leqslant 3^{D}\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{2^{d_{i}}},

.

.

Let

. For any
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{4}
and family of mean

,
\prod f_{i}
is a

\left\lvert\E_{{a\in{X{\left(k-1\right)}},\pi\in S_{k}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(a_{\pi(i)})\right]\right\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon^{k}+2\lambda

(D.9)

Appendix E
Reverse Hypercontractivity: From Boolean to All Functions

We now give a generic reduction from reverse hypercontractivity for general functions to the Boolean case.

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem
6.12
)
.

For every
\ell\geqslant 1
and
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\kappa^{\prime}\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{5}\right)^{2\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{18+\frac{12}{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2(\ell-1)}
such that the following holds. Let
V
be a finite probability space and
D
a monotone linear operator such that for every
A,B\subseteq V
,

\langle 1_{A},D1_{B}\rangle\geqslant\kappa\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{\ell}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{\ell}.

(E.1)

Then for every two
arbitrary
functions
f_{1},f_{2}:V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
it holds that

\langle f_{1},Df_{2}\rangle\geqslant\kappa\kappa^{\prime}\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{\frac{1}{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}}\lVert f_{2}\rVert_{\frac{1}{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}}.

(E.2)

That is,
D
is
(\frac{1}{\ell(1+\varepsilon)},\frac{1}{1-\ell(1+\varepsilon)},\kappa\kappa^{\prime})
-reverse hypercontractive.

Before moving directly to the proof, we give a brief overview of the main idea. Starting with arbitrary
f_{1},f_{2}
, our goal is to build discrete approximations of the
f_{i}
that remain close to the original functions in expectation and don’t take too many unique values. Once this is the case, we can write the functions as a weighted sum over indicators for each value, and apply the boolean result without too much loss.

The key to the proof really lies in these approximations, which we build by discretizing the functions to powers of 2 and applying a careful iterative zero-ing procedure to components that fall too far from the mean. Naively this approach seems problematic, as
f_{i}
is arbitrary and may not have a well-behaved tail to truncate. We handle this by exploiting the fact that no function can have more than a small constant fraction of its mass beyond its expectation times (an appropriate power of) its
(1{\to}(1+\varepsilon))
-norm.

More formally, the proof of
Theorem
6.12
is split into two intermediate reductions, corresponding to the ‘approximation step’ and the ‘indicator step’ above. The first of these is a reduction from general reverse hypercontractivity to a slightly richer set of functions that are
balanced
and
discrete
.

Definition E.1

(Nice Functions)
.

For any
\alpha\in[0,1]
, and
\beta\in[1,\infty)
, we say the function
f:V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
is (
\alpha,\beta
)-nice if it is:

1.

Balanced:
all non-zero outputs of
f
satisfy

\alpha\mathbb{E}[f]\leqslant f(x)\leqslant\beta\mathbb{E}[f].

2.

Discrete:
the range of
f
is entirely contained in
\{0\}\cup\{2^{j}:j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

To state the reduction, we require a setting of parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
based on the
(1{\to}(1+\varepsilon))
-norm of the functions in question due to the tail truncation strategy described above. In particular, for a fixed non-negative function
f_{i}
, define

\eta_{i}=\frac{\E[f_{i}^{1+\varepsilon}]}{\E[f_{i}]^{1+\varepsilon}}=\lVert f_{i}\rVert^{1+\varepsilon}_{1{\to}(1+\varepsilon)}.

With this in mind, we state our two intermediate reductions and prove
Theorem
6.12
.

Proposition E.2

(From General to Nice)
.

Let
\ell,\varepsilon,\kappa,V
, and
D
be as in
Theorem
6.12
. Suppose that for any two functions
f_{1},f_{2}:V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
that are
(\frac{1}{16\eta_{i}},(25\eta_{i})^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}})
-nice

\langle f_{1}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)},Df_{2}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\rangle\geqslant\kappa\E[f_{1}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\E[f_{2}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}.

(E.3)

Then for every two functions
f_{1},f_{2}:V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
it holds that

\langle f_{1}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)},Df_{2}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\rangle\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{5}\right)^{2\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\kappa\E[f_{1}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\E[f_{2}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}.

(E.4)

The ‘indicator step’ then reduces reverse hypercontractivity for nice functions to the Boolean case.

Proposition E.3

(From Nice to Boolean)
.

Let
\ell,\varepsilon,V
, and
D
be as in
Theorem
6.12
. If for every
A,B\subseteq V
,

\langle 1_{A},D1_{B}\rangle\geqslant\kappa\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{\ell}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{\ell},

(E.5)

then every two
(\frac{1}{16\eta_{i}},(25\eta_{i})^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}})
-nice functions
f_{1},f_{2}:V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
satisfy

\langle f_{1}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)},Df_{2}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\rangle\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{18+\frac{12}{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2(\ell-1)}\kappa\E[f_{1}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\E[f_{2}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}.

(E.6)

The proof of
Theorem
6.12
is now essentially immediate.

Proof of
Theorem
6.12
.

We have by assumption that all
A,B\subseteq V
satisfy
\langle 1_{A},D1_{B}\rangle\geqslant\kappa\ProbOp\left[{A}\right]^{\ell}\ProbOp\left[{B}\right]^{\ell}
,
so we may apply
Proposition
E.3
to get that every two
(\frac{1}{16\eta_{i}},(25\eta_{i})^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}})
-nice functions
f_{1},f_{2}:V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
satisfy

\langle f_{1}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)},Df_{2}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\rangle\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{18+\frac{12}{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2(\ell-1)}\kappa\E[f_{1}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\E[f_{2}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}.

Applying
Proposition
E.2
then implies
arbitrary
non-negative
g_{1},g_{2}
satisfy

\langle g_{1}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)},Dg_{2}^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\rangle\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{5}\right)^{2\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{18+\frac{12}{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2(\ell-1)}\kappa\E[g_{1}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\E[g_{2}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}.

Finally, for
f_{1},f_{2}
arbitrary non-negative functions applying the above to
g_{i}=f_{i}^{\frac{1}{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}}
gives the result.
∎

We now move to the formal proofs.

Proof of
Proposition
E.2
.

Given arbitrary
f_{1},f_{2}:V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
we will construct corresponding
f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime}
satisfying

1.

0\leqslant f_{i}^{\prime}\leqslant f_{i}

2.

\mathbb{E}[f_{i}^{\prime}]\geqslant\frac{1}{5}\mathbb{E}[f]

3.

f_{i}^{\prime}
is
(\frac{1}{16\eta_{i}},(25\eta_{i})^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}})
-nice

Combining the above with monotonicity of
D
and reverse hypercontractive inequality for nice functions gives

\displaystyle\langle f_{1},Df_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant\langle f^{\prime}_{1},Df^{\prime}_{2}\rangle

(Item (
1
) and Monotonicity)

\displaystyle\geqslant\kappa\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}_{1}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}_{2}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}

(Item (
3
) and Equation (
E.3
))

\displaystyle\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{5}\right)^{2\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\kappa\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}_{1}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\mathbb{E}[f^{\prime}_{2}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}

(Item (
2
))

as desired.

We construct
f_{i}^{\prime}
in two separate steps: a simple discretization procedure, and a more involved balancing procedure that iteratively zeroes out unbalanced parts of the function. Starting with the former, given a non-negative
f
define its
rounding down

\tilde{f}
by setting for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
:

\tilde{f}(x)=\begin{cases}2^{j}&\text{for }f(x)\in[2^{j},2^{j+1})\\
0&\text{if }f(x)=0.\end{cases}

Note that, by construction,
\frac{f_{i}}{2}\leqslant\tilde{f}_{i}\leqslant f_{i}
.

We now define an iterative balancing procedure outputting a sequence of functions
\tilde{f}_{i}=f_{i}^{(0)}\geqslant f_{i}^{(1)}\geqslant\ldots\geqslant f_{i}^{(k_{i})}=f^{\prime}_{i}
for
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
some finite stopping time. For these (soon-to-be-defined) functions, define
\eta_{i}^{(j)}=\frac{\E[(f_{i}^{(j)})^{1+\varepsilon}]}{\E[(f_{i}^{(j)})]^{1+\varepsilon}}
and denote the set of ‘balanced’ inputs of
f^{(j)}_{i}
as:

B_{i}^{(j)}=\left\{x\left|\;\vphantom{x\frac{1}{4\eta_{i}^{(j)}}\E[f_{i}^{(j)}]\leqslant f_{i}^{(j)}(x)\leqslant\left(4\eta_{i}^{(j)}\right)^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}\E[f_{i}^{(j)}]}\right.\frac{1}{4\eta_{i}^{(j)}}\E[f_{i}^{(j)}]\leqslant f_{i}^{(j)}(x)\leqslant\left(4\eta_{i}^{(j)}\right)^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}\E[f_{i}^{(j)}]\right\}.

If
f_{i}^{(0)}
is already
(\frac{1}{16\eta_{i}},(25\eta_{i})^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}})
-nice, set
k_{i}=0
and output
f^{\prime}_{i}=\tilde{f}_{i}
. Otherwise for
j=1,2,\ldots
define
f_{i}^{(j)}
by zeroing out all non-balanced inputs:

f_{i}^{(j)}:=f_{i}^{(j-1)}\cdot{\mathbf{1}}_{B_{i}^{(j-1)}},

and define the stopping time
k_{i}
to be the first index
j
such that
\eta_{i}^{(j)}\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\eta_{i}^{(j-1)}
. This procedure terminates (i.e.
k_{i}
is finite) since each
\eta_{i}^{(j)}\geqslant 1
by convexity. Every step the process doesn’t stop decreases
\eta_{i}^{(j)}
by a factor of
4
, so there can be a total of
O(\log(\eta_{i}^{(0)}))
steps.

It is immediate from definition that
0\leqslant f_{i}^{\prime}\leqslant f_{i}
, so we just need to show
Item
2
and
Item
3
. The key is to observe that our balancing process cannot remove too much mass in each step:

\E[f_{i}^{(j-1)}]\geqslant\E[f_{i}^{(j)}]\geqslant\left(1-\frac{1}{2\eta_{i}^{(j-1)}}\right)\E[f_{i}^{(j-1)}].

(E.7)

We defer the proof, and first show
Item
2
and
Item
3
given this assumption.

Proof of
Item
2

The proof is essentially immediate by the exponential decay of
\eta_{i}^{(j)}
. Namely by iterated application of (
E.7
) we have

\frac{\E[f_{i}^{(k)}]}{\mathbb{E}[f_{i}^{(0)}]}\geqslant\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{2\eta_{i}^{(k-1-j)}}\right)\geqslant\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{1}{2\cdot 4^{j}}\right)\geqslant\frac{2}{5}

since by construction
\eta_{i}^{(j)}>4^{k-1-j}\eta_{i}^{(k-1)}\geqslant 4^{k-1-j}
. Combining with the fact
\tilde{f}_{i}=f_{i}^{(0)}\geqslant\frac{f_{i}}{2}
gives the result.

Proof of
Item
3

Recall that for any
j
we have by construction the following ‘pseudo’-balance condition:

\forall f_{i}^{(j)}(x)\neq 0:\frac{1}{4\eta_{i}^{(j-1)}}\E[f_{i}^{(j-1)}]\leqslant f_{i}^{(j)}(x)\leqslant\left(4\eta_{i}^{(j-1)}\right)^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}\E[f_{i}^{(j-1)}].

When
j=k_{i}
is the stopping time, observe that by
(
E.7
)
and construction respectively we have

1.

\mathbb{E}[f_{i}^{(k_{i})}]\leqslant\mathbb{E}[f_{i}^{(k_{i}-1)}]\leqslant 2\mathbb{E}[f_{i}^{(k_{i})}]

2.

4\eta_{i}^{(k_{i})}\geqslant\eta_{i}^{(k_{i}-1)}
.

Substituting into the above gives

\frac{1}{16\eta_{i}^{(k_{i})}}\E[f_{i}^{(k_{i})}]\leqslant f_{i}^{(k_{i})}(x)\leqslant\left(25\eta_{i}^{(k_{i})}\right)^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}\E[f_{i}^{(k_{i})}]

as desired.

Proof of (
E.7
)

The lefthand inequality is by construction. Toward the righthand, fix
f=f_{i}^{(j-1)}
and
\eta=\eta^{(j-1)}_{i}
for notational simplicity and define the set of ‘terrible’ inputs zeroed in the
j
th step as:

T_{1}\coloneqq\left\{x\left|\;\vphantom{xf(x)<\frac{1}{4\eta}\E[f]}\right.f(x)<\frac{1}{4\eta}\E[f]\right\}\quad\quad\quad\quad T_{2}\coloneqq\left\{x\left|\;\vphantom{xf(x)>(4\eta)^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}\E[f]}\right.f(x)>(4\eta)^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}\E[f]\right\}

Since
T_{1}\cup T_{2}=V\setminus B_{i}^{(j-1)}
and
f_{i}^{(j)}={\mathbf{1}}_{B_{i}^{(j-1)}}f
, it is enough to show that

\E[f\cdot{\mathbf{1}}_{T_{1}}],\E[f\cdot{\mathbf{1}}_{T_{2}}]\leqslant\frac{1}{4\eta}\E[f].

(E.8)

It is obvious that
\E[f\cdot{\mathbf{1}}_{T_{1}}]\leqslant\frac{1}{4\eta}\E[f]
since
f
is upper bounded by this value within
T_{1}
. To bound the upper tail we use Hölder and Markov:

\displaystyle\E[f\cdot{\mathbf{1}}_{T_{2}}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{1+\varepsilon}\ProbOp\left[{f\geqslant(4\eta)^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}\E[f]}\right]^{\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}}

(Hölder’s inequality)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{1+\varepsilon}\frac{1}{16\eta^{2}}

(Markov’s inequality)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{4}\eta^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}}\right)\frac{1}{4\eta}\E[f]

(
\lVert f\rVert_{1+\varepsilon}=\eta^{\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}}\E[f]
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{4\eta}\E[f]

(
\eta^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}}\leqslant 1
)

as desired.
∎

Finally we prove reverse hypercontractivity for ‘nice’ functions (
Proposition
E.3
).

Proof of
Proposition
E.3
.

Since
f_{i}
is
(\frac{1}{16\eta_{i}},(25\eta_{i})^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}})
-nice, it can attain at most

n_{i}=\left(3+\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)\log\eta_{i}+\frac{10}{\varepsilon}+15

non-zero values. With this in mind, decompose
f_{i}=\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{j}\mathbf{1}_{f_{i}(x)=2^{j}}
into level sets and observe that, by linearity of
D
, we can decompose the inner product itself into boolean sub-components and apply our assumed reverse hypercontractive inequality (Equation
(
E.6
)
):

\displaystyle\langle f_{1}^{(1+\varepsilon)\ell},Df_{2}^{(1+\varepsilon)\ell}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{(1+\varepsilon)\ell j}2^{(1+\varepsilon)\ell i}\langle{\mathbf{1}}_{f_{1}(x)=2^{j}},D{\mathbf{1}}_{f_{2}(x)=2^{i}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant\kappa\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{(1+\varepsilon)\ell j}2^{(1+\varepsilon)\ell i}\ProbOp\left[{f_{1}(x)=2^{j}}\right]^{\ell}\ProbOp\left[{f_{2}(x)=2^{j}}\right]^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\kappa\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(2^{(1+\varepsilon)j}\ProbOp\left[{f_{1}(x)=2^{j}}\right])^{\ell}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(2^{(1+\varepsilon)j}\ProbOp\left[{f_{2}(x)=2^{j}}\right])^{\ell}\right).

Now since the sums have only
n_{1},n_{2}
non-zero terms respectively, applying
(\ell,\frac{\ell}{\ell-1})
-Hölder’s inequality lower bounds this quantity by

\displaystyle\kappa(n_{1}n_{2})^{1-\ell}\left(\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{(1+\varepsilon)j}\ProbOp\left[{f_{1}(x)=2^{j}}\right]\right)^{\ell}\cdot\left(\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{(1+\varepsilon)j}\ProbOp\left[{f_{1}(x)=2^{j}}\right]\right)^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa n_{1}^{1-\ell}\E[f_{1}^{1+\varepsilon}]^{\ell}\cdot n_{2}^{1-\ell}\E[f_{2}^{1+\varepsilon}]^{\ell}

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\kappa\cdot(n_{1}^{1-\ell}\eta_{1}^{\ell})\cdot(n_{2}^{1-\ell}\eta_{2}^{\ell})\cdot\E[f_{1}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}\E[f_{2}]^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)}.

It can be checked directly that

\min_{\eta_{i}\geqslant 1}n_{i}\eta_{i}\geqslant\frac{1}{18+\frac{12}{\varepsilon}}

which gives the desired result.
∎

Appendix F
Splittability

In this section we discuss the relations between our notion of complete splittability, the splittability notion of
[
JST21
]
, and the "
\tau
-sampling" gaurantee of
[
DW22
]
, and the inheritance of splittability under projection. We start by defining the latter two notions.

Definition F.1

(JST-Splittability
[
JST21
]
)
.

X\subset[n]^{d}
is said to be
\lambda
-JST-splittable if for all
1\leqslant a\leqslant t\leqslant b\leqslant d
:

\lambda_{2}(S_{[a,t],[t+1,b]})\leqslant\lambda

In their later work,
[
DW22
]
introduced the weaker notion of
\tau
-sampling which suffices for list-decoding.

Definition F.2
.

X\subset[n]^{d}
is said to be
\tau
-sampling if for
t\in[d]
,
S\subset[n]
and
W\subset[n]^{t}
:

\underset{w\sim X}{\text{Cov}}\left(1[w_{t+1}\in S],1[w_{[t]}\in W]\right)\leqslant\tau.

Before arguing relations between these notions, we give the key lemma that underlies all proofs in this section: the expansion of swap walks is inherited under projection.

Lemma F.3

(Swap Walk Inheritence)
.

Let
X
be a partite
d
-uniform complex, and
A
and
B
disjoint subsets of
[d]
, and
F
any subset of
[d]
. Then:

\lambda_{2}(S_{A\cap F,B\cap F})\leqslant\lambda_{2}(S_{A,B})

Proof.

By the variational characterization it is enough to show

\max_{\mathbb{E}[f^{F}]=0}\frac{\lVert S_{A\cap F,B\cap F}f^{F}\rVert}{\lVert f^{F}\rVert_{2}}\leqslant\lambda_{2}(S_{A,B})

Given any such function
f^{F}:X[A\cap F]\to\mathbb{R}
define its extension to
X[A]
by
f(x)\coloneqq f^{F}(x_{F})
. Here
x_{F}=\{v\in x:col(v)\in F\}
is the projection into
X^{F}
. and observe that this extension satisfies:

1.

\mathbb{E}_{X[A]}[f]=\mathbb{E}_{X[A\cap F]}[f^{F}]=0

2.

\lVert f\rVert_{2,X[A]}=\lVert f^{F}\rVert_{2,X[A\cap F]}
.

The result then follows from convexity, in particular:

\displaystyle\lVert S_{A\cap F,B\cap F}f^{F}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\underset{y\sim X[A\cap F]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\underset{x\in X{\left(d\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[f^{F}(x_{B\cap F})|x_{A\cap F}=y]\right)^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\underset{y\sim X[A\cap F]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\underset{x\in X{\left(d\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[f(x_{B})|x_{A\cap F}=y]\right)^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\underset{y\sim X[A\cap F]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\underset{y^{\prime}\sim X_{y}[A\setminus F]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{x\in X{\left(d\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[f(x_{B}|x_{A\cap F}=y\land x_{A\setminus F}=y^{\prime}]\right]\right)^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\underset{y\sim X[A]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\underset{x\in X{\left(d\right)}}{\mathbb{E}}[f(x_{B}|x_{A}=y]\right)^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

(Jensen’s Inequality)

\displaystyle=\lVert S_{A,B}f\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{2}(S_{A,B})\lVert f\rVert_{2}

(
\mathbb{E}[f]=\mathbb{E}[f^{F}]=0
)

\displaystyle=\gamma\lVert f^{F}\rVert_{2}

∎

With this in mind, we prove the following relations between complete splittability, JST-splittability, and
\tau
-sampling.

Proposition F.4
.

X\subset[n]^{t}
is
\lambda
-JST-splittable if and only if it is completely
\lambda
-splittable. Moreover any completely
\lambda
-splittable complex is
\lambda
-sampling.

Proof.

If
X
is
\lambda
-JST-splittable it is clearly completely
\lambda
-splittable setting
a=1,b=d
. If
X
is completely
\lambda
-splittable, then for any
1\leqslant a\leqslant i\leqslant b\leqslant d
we have:

S_{[a,i],[i+1,b]}\leqslant S_{[1,i],[i+1,d]}\leqslant\lambda

as desired. Finally, if
X
is completely
\lambda
-splittable, then for any
S\subset[n]
and
X\subset[n]^{t}
write
1_{S}\coloneqq\mathbf{1}[w_{t+1}\in S]
and
1_{X}\coloneqq\mathbf{1}[w_{[t]}\in X]

\displaystyle\text{Cov}_{w\sim X}\left(1_{S},1_{X}\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{y\sim X^{t+1}}{\mathbb{E}}[1_{S}(y)S_{t+1,[t]}1_{X}(y)]-\mathbb{E}[1_{S}]\mathbb{[}E][1_{X}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{2}(S_{t+1,[t]})\lVert 1_{S}\rVert\lVert 1_{X}\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda

where we have used that by Hölder duality and
Lemma
F.3

\lambda_{2}(S_{t+1,[t]})=\lambda_{2}(S_{[t],t+1})\leqslant\lambda_{2}(S_{[t],t+1})\leqslant\lambda
.
∎

Finally, we show that both complete splittability and standard tuple splittability are inherited under projections. Recall that given a
d
-partite complex
X
and
F\subseteq[d]
,
X^{F}
is the projection of
X
given by drawing a
d
-face from
X
, and projecting the resulting face onto the parts in
F
.

Proposition F.5

(
Claim
10.34
Restated)
.

Let
X\subset[n]^{d}
be a homogeneous, completely
\lambda
-splittable complex. Then for any
F\subseteq[d]

X^{F}
is homogeneous and completely
\lambda
-splittable.

Proof.

The proof is essentially immediate from definition and
Lemma
F.3
. Recall
X
is homogeneous if the projection onto each part is uniform on
[n]
. Since projecting onto
X^{F}
and then a coordinate
i\in F
is the same as just projecting onto
i
, homogeneity is inherited. Toward splittability, write
F=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|F|}\}
where
i_{j}<i_{j+1}
. Then for any
i_{j}
, applying
Lemma
F.3
gives

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}(S^{X^{F}}_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\},\{i_{j+1},\ldots,i_{|F|}}\})

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{2}(S^{X}_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\},\{i_{j+1},\ldots,i_{|F|}}\})

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{2}(S_{[i_{j}],[i_{j}+1,d]})\leqslant\lambda

where we have used the fact that
\lambda_{2}(S^{X^{F}}_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\},\{i_{j+1},\ldots,i_{|F|}\}})=\lambda_{2}(S^{X}_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\},\{i_{j+1},\ldots,i_{|F|}\}})
(indeed they are the same operator).
∎

Finally, while not strictly necessary for the results in this work, we show for completeness that inheritance of splittability also holds in the setting of general tuple-splitting trees as well.

Proposition F.6

(Projected Splitting Trees)
.

Let X be any
d
-uniform partite complex with a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree of depth
D
. For every
F\subseteq[d]
,
X^{F}
also has a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree of depth at most
D
.

The proof of
Proposition
F.6
relies on a simple inductive algorithm for projecting partite orderered binary trees
(T,\rho)
onto a subset of their coordinates. In particular, given such a tree and a subset
F\subseteq[d]
, we define the projection operation via the following inductive algorithm that modifies the tree
T
in place starting from a specific node
v\in T
.

Algorithm 1

Project
(T,\rho,F,v)

Result:

Projected partite ordered binary tree
(T_{v}^{F},\rho_{v}^{F})
rooted at
v

1

if

v
is a leaf

then

2

return

3

else if

\rho(\ell_{v})\cap F=\emptyset

then

4

Delete(
T(\ell_{v})
);

5

Merge(
v
,
r_{v}
)
\the\algocf@printnl@i{{{7}}}footnote
\the\algocf@printnl@i{{{7}}}footnote
\algocf@printnl@i{{{7}}}footnote:

\the\algocf@printnl@i{{{7}}}footnote

8

Merge(
v
,
r_{v}
) deletes the node
v
and replaces
r_{v}
as the respective child of
v
’s parent.
;

6

Project
(T,\rho,F,r_{v})

7

else if

\rho(r_{v})\cap F=\emptyset

then

8

Delete(
T(r_{v})
);

9

Merge(
v
,
\ell_{v}
);

10

Project
(T,\rho,F,\ell_{v})

11

else

12

Project
(T,\rho,F,r_{v})
;

13

Project
(T,\rho,F,\ell_{v})

In other words,
Algorithm
1
is the result of intersecting
\rho
with
F
and deleting the resulting nodes with empty or repeated labelings. We define the
F
-projection of a tree by applying this process at the root.

Definition F.7

(Projected Trees)
.

Let
(T,\rho)
be a
d
-uniform partite ordered binary tree and
F\subset[d+1]
any coordinate subset. The
F
-projection of
(T,\rho)
, denoted
(T^{F},\rho^{F})
is given by

T^{F}=\text{Project}(T,\rho,F,\text{root}(T)),\quad\quad\rho^{F}=(\rho\cap F)|_{T^{F}}.

We are now ready to prove
Proposition
F.6
.

Proof of
Proposition
F.6
.

We will show that
(X^{F},T^{F},\rho^{F})
is a
\lambda
-tuple splitting tree of depth at most
D
. We first argue that
(X^{F},T^{F},\rho^{F})
is a partite ordered binary tree of depth at most
D
. First, observe that every operation in
Algorithm
1
maps binary trees to binary trees, and cannot increase depth. In particular, the only modification of the tree structure occurs when a branch is deleted and the relevant root is contracted with its other child, maintaining the invariant that every internal node has one parent and two children, and that the depth is at most
D
. To see that the leaves are in bijection with
F
, observe that all leaves labeled by
F
survive the projection, all leaves labeled by
[d]\setminus F
are deleted, and no new leaves are introduced by construction. The first two claims hold since a node
v
is deleted if and only if either
\rho(v)\cap F=\emptyset
, or the labeling is repeated later down the tree. Finally, to see that the children of any internal node in
T^{F}
partition its labeling in
\rho^{F}
, observe that this property holds trivially for
T
under the intersected labeling
\rho\cap F
(albeit the partition may be trivial), and that this invariant is preserved by every operation in
Algorithm
1
.

Finally we argue the projected tree inherits the splittability of
X^{F}
. By
Lemma
F.3
, we have that for any internal node
u
:

\lambda_{2}(S_{\rho^{F}(\ell_{u}),\rho^{F}(r_{u})})=\lambda_{2}(S_{\rho(\ell_{u})\cap F,\rho(r_{u})\cap F})\leqslant\lambda_{2}(S_{\rho(\ell_{u}),\rho(r_{u})})\leqslant\lambda

as desired.
∎
